# About This Spoken Edition

This edition is prepared for text-to-speech narration in ElevenLabs Studio.
Exact source-code blocks, visual diagrams, badges, raw web addresses, and the visual table of contents are omitted because reading their punctuation aloud makes the lesson harder to follow.
All explanatory prose, conceptual comparisons, invariants, warnings, workflows, and chapter ordering are retained.
Use the companion Markdown edition whenever a chapter refers to an implementation or code example.

Note. A dependency-ordered computer-science curriculum from mathematical reasoning to CPUs,
memory, operating systems, language runtimes, networks, browsers, graphics, embedded
systems, and reverse engineering—with Rust libraries used as executable models of the
underlying ideas.

Note. ElevenLabs editions: use the
chaptered E pub for Studio, or
the plain-text fallback.
The spoken editions retain the lessons but omit exact code and visual diagrams;
return to this Markdown edition when you want the implementation.

# How to Use These Notes

These notes are a dependency-ordered computer-science curriculum first and a
reference second. On a first read, move from top to bottom. Each later chapter assumes
the representations, invariants, and machine boundaries developed earlier.

The document uses Rust libraries as executable models of computer-science ideas:

- Library or project: Pest. Use it to understand: tokenization, P E G recognition, ordered choice, parse-tree construction.
- Library or project: hosted-languages and Lua interpreters. Use it to understand: values, environments, call frames, bytecode, garbage collection.
- Library or project: Inkwell / L L V M. Use it to understand: I R, control-flow graphs, S S A, A B I lowering, verification.
- Library or project: Rust collections and patterns. Use it to understand: ownership, representation, invariants, error models.
- Library or project: Actix. Use it to understand: actors, capabilities, mailboxes, backpressure, supervision.
- Library or project: small T C P. Use it to understand: layered protocols, explicit buffers, timers, T C P state machines.
- Library or project: Quinn. Use it to understand: encrypted transport, multiplexing, loss recovery, stream independence.
- Library or project: win it / S D L. Use it to understand: event loops, OS windows, input, timing, presentation.
- Library or project: W G P U. Use it to understand: G P U queues, resources, validation, pipelines, asynchronous completion.
- Library or project: Waz-em bind-gen. Use it to understand: F F I, host-owned objects, identity, lifetime across runtimes.
- Library or project: Diesel. Use it to understand: relational modeling, typed queries, transactions, schema boundaries.
- Library or project: Bevy. Use it to understand: ECS, data-oriented storage, scheduling, change detection.
- Library or project: Tauri. Use it to understand: process boundaries, capabilities, native/web I P C.

Note. Library rule: learn the model before the convenience A P I. For every example,
ask what state exists, how it is represented, which transition the call requests,
what invariant the library checks, and what failure or backpressure reaches the
caller.

## Fast Concept-Reading Loop

Read each concept in six passes:

1. Definition — say what the concept is in one precise sentence.
2. Problem — identify the constraint that makes the concept necessary.
3. Representation — find the bytes, types, nodes, buffers, or tables that store it.
4. Mechanism — trace one input through each state transition to an output.
5. Invariant and failure — state what must remain true and how it can break.
6. Transfer — find the same model in a compiler, OS, network, browser, database, or
embedded device.

Code is evidence for the mechanism, not the lesson by itself. Comments explain why a
field or transition exists, which assumption it encodes, and what observable behavior
results.

## Recommended Top-to-Bottom Reading Order

- Phase: 1 · Reasoning foundations. Build understanding in this order: concept map leads to sets/logic/proofs/probability/graphs/linear algebra. Dependency it establishes: vocabulary for state, relations, change, and proof.
- Phase: 2 · The physical machine. Build understanding in this order: C P U leads to instructions leads to memory leads to translation levels. Dependency it establishes: how abstract programs become finite machine state.
- Phase: 3 · Runtime substrate. Build understanding in this order: memory systems leads to OS leads to executables/A B I leads to embedded leads to concurrency. Dependency it establishes: how code receives storage, devices, scheduling, and isolation.
- Phase: 4 · Program construction. Build understanding in this order: Rust ownership/types/errors leads to patterns leads to functional models leads to algorithms leads to ML. Dependency it establishes: how to encode and analyze reliable transformations.
- Phase: 5 · Language implementation. Build understanding in this order: grammar leads to parsing leads to A S T leads to recursion leads to types leads to bytecode leads to runtime leads to native code. Dependency it establishes: how source becomes meaning and execution.
- Phase: 6 · Connected systems. Build understanding in this order: service architecture leads to databases/caches leads to networks leads to cryptography/security leads to T C P/IP leads to quick. Dependency it establishes: how independent state machines communicate under failure.
- Phase: 7 · Interactive systems. Build understanding in this order: graphics/media/G P U leads to Chrome leads to G U I/T U I leads to Web Assembly/D O M leads to S D L leads to applications. Dependency it establishes: how events become state, layout, commands, and pixels.
- Phase: 8 · Read systems backward. Build understanding in this order: x sixty-four leads to memory evidence leads to defensive instrumentation. Dependency it establishes: how to infer hidden structure from observable behavior.
- Phase: 9 · Practice and recall. Build understanding in this order: terminal/tooling leads to automation leads to workflow leads to progression leads to glossary leads to Anki. Dependency it establishes: how to retain and apply the models.

Note. Prerequisite rule: when a later chapter names an unfamiliar earlier concept,
follow the backward link, learn the model, then return. The repetition is deliberate:
the same ideas—representation, state machine, ownership, graph, queue, invariant,
capability, and feedback loop—reappear at larger scales.

## Reading Conventions

- Definition introduces a concept.
- Mental model gives a useful approximation, not a formal guarantee.
- Invariant is a condition that must remain true for an implementation to be
correct.
- Boundary is where data changes representation or trust level.
- Failure mode names what can go wrong and why.
- Rust examples favor clarity and explicit checks over cleverness.

Note. Source rule: these notes distill the linked material into transferable mental
models. For version-sensitive APIs, command flags, ABIs, and hardware details, verify
the current primary source before depending on an example.

# Computer Science as Layers of State and Transformation

The archived
Carl Cheo computer-science overview
groups beginner concepts into algorithms/data structures, artificial intelligence,
architecture, concurrency, security, and development methodology. The analogies are
useful entry points; the tables below tighten them into contracts that transfer to
language implementation and reverse engineering.

## 1. The Major Areas Reinforce Each Other

- Area: Algorithms and data structures. Central question: How is information represented and transformed?. Language/reversing connection: ASTs, symbol tables, graphs, worklists.
- Area: Artificial intelligence. Central question: How can a system search, learn, or choose?. Language/reversing connection: optimizers, heuristics, autotuning.
- Area: Computer architecture. Central question: What physically executes an operation?. Language/reversing connection: instruction sets, caches, A B I, memory layout.
- Area: Concurrency. Central question: What can overlap, and how is shared state protected?. Language/reversing connection: parallel passes, runtimes, event loops.
- Area: Security. Central question: What does an input or caller have permission to do?. Language/reversing connection: parser limits, sandboxing, binary analysis.
- Area: Development methodology. Central question: How is uncertain work divided and validated?. Language/reversing connection: compiler stages, tests, reproducible investigations.

Note. Learning rule: an analogy starts a model; an invariant finishes it. Ask what
the analogy hides—costs, edge cases, ownership, failure, or adversarial behavior.

## 2. Complexity Describes Growth

Big-O notation describes an asymptotic upper bound. It is often used for worst-case
time, but Big-O itself does not mean “worst case”; best, average, and amortized behavior
must be named separately.

- Growth: O(1). Example: indexed array access. What happens as input doubles: roughly unchanged.
- Growth: O(log n). Example: binary search. What happens as input doubles: one extra step.
- Growth: O(n). Example: scan every token. What happens as input doubles: roughly doubles.
- Growth: O(n log n). Example: comparison sort. What happens as input doubles: a little more than doubles.
- Growth: O(n²). Example: compare every pair. What happens as input doubles: roughly quadruples.
- Growth: O(2ⁿ). Example: enumerate all subsets. What happens as input doubles: roughly squares the work.

For a compiler, complexity is only part of the cost:

## 3. Data Structures Encode Operations

- Structure: contiguous array/Vec. Strength: iteration and indexed access. Language-tool example: tokens, bytecode, constant pools.
- Structure: linked structure. Strength: stable links, local insertion. Language-tool example: intrusive runtime lists in rare cases.
- Structure: stack. Strength: last-in-first-out. Language-tool example: parser states, calls, expression eval.
- Structure: queue. Strength: first-in-first-out. Language-tool example: BFS, event processing.
- Structure: hash table. Strength: expected fast key lookup. Language-tool example: symbols, interners, caches.
- Structure: tree. Strength: hierarchy or ordered partition. Language-tool example: syntax, scopes, search indexes.
- Structure: graph. Strength: arbitrary relationships. Language-tool example: control flow, call graph, dependencies.
- Structure: heap/priority queue. Strength: repeatedly select best candidate. Language-tool example: schedulers, Dijkstra, best-first search.

Choose a structure from the operations and invariants, not from its familiarity.

## 4. Search Strategies Trade Certainty for Speed

- Strategy: greedy. Decision rule: take the best immediate choice. Typical weakness: local choice may block global optimum.
- Strategy: hill climbing. Decision rule: move to a better neighboring state. Typical weakness: local maxima and plateaus.
- Strategy: random restart. Decision rule: run local search from several initial states. Typical weakness: more work; still no general guarantee.
- Strategy: simulated annealing. Decision rule: sometimes accept worse moves, less often later. Typical weakness: schedule-sensitive and approximate.
- Strategy: dynamic programming. Decision rule: reuse overlapping subproblem results. Typical weakness: state space may still be too large.
- Strategy: exhaustive search. Decision rule: inspect every candidate. Typical weakness: combinatorial explosion.

Compiler register allocation, instruction selection, inlining, and layout can all use
heuristics because globally optimal solutions may be too expensive. Record when a pass
is exact, approximate, or nondeterministic.

## 5. Computability Is Not Complexity

- Question: Computable?. Meaning: Can any algorithm solve the problem for every valid input?
- Question: Decidable?. Meaning: Can an algorithm always halt with yes/no?
- Question: Tractable?. Meaning: Can it be solved with acceptable resources?
- Question: Verifiable?. Meaning: Can a proposed answer be checked efficiently?

The halting problem shows that no general algorithm can decide whether every
arbitrary program will halt. A practical analyzer can still prove termination for a
restricted language or return unknown when its proof is insufficient.

This three-way result is more honest than treating “not proven” as “false.”

## 6. Concurrency Is Not Parallelism

- Concept: concurrency. Precise idea: tasks have overlapping lifetimes/progress.
- Concept: parallelism. Precise idea: work executes at the same physical instant.
- Concept: race condition. Precise idea: behavior depends on an uncontrolled ordering.
- Concept: mutex. Precise idea: one holder at a time enters a critical section.
- Concept: semaphore. Precise idea: a bounded number of permits guard a resource.
- Concept: deadlock. Precise idea: tasks wait in a cycle that cannot make progress.

A compiler can be concurrent without being parallel—for example, an event loop can
interleave file reads and diagnostics on one thread. Parallel compilation needs actual
simultaneous workers and data dependencies that permit them to run safely.

## 7. Security Is Boundary Engineering

- Principle: least privilege. Concrete question: What is the smallest capability this component needs?
- Principle: defense in depth. Concrete question: Which independent checks contain one failure?
- Principle: complete mediation. Concrete question: Is every sensitive operation authorized?
- Principle: secure defaults. Concrete question: Is the safe behavior selected without extra setup?
- Principle: fail closed. Concrete question: Does an error deny access rather than silently allow it?
- Principle: auditability. Concrete question: Can important decisions be reconstructed later?

Cryptography provides tools for confidentiality, integrity, authentication, and
signatures; it does not automatically make the surrounding parser, protocol, key
storage, randomness, or authorization correct. Use established cryptographic libraries
and protocols rather than inventing production cryptography.

## 8. Methodology Is a Feedback Loop

- Style: sequential plan. Useful when: requirements and interfaces are stable. Main risk: late discovery invalidates earlier work.
- Style: iterative delivery. Useful when: uncertainty is high and feedback is available. Main risk: unmanaged scope and architectural drift.
- Style: prototype. Useful when: testing a risky idea or interface. Main risk: prototype accidentally becomes production.
- Style: experiment. Useful when: comparing measurable hypotheses. Main risk: weak controls produce misleading results.

The compiler pipeline benefits from small, testable increments:

Note. Next: mathematical foundations make the map precise by defining sets,
relations, logic, proof, probability, graphs, linear transformations, and finite
numerical representations.

# Mathematical Foundations for Computer Science

Computer science uses mathematics to describe state, relationships, change,
and limits precisely. The purpose is not to memorize symbols. It is to gain a small
set of models that let you predict what an algorithm, machine, protocol, or program can
do before you run it.

Note. Core mental model: a mathematical model deliberately ignores some physical
detail so one important relationship becomes easier to reason about. An implementation
then chooses finite representations—integers, floats, arrays, graphs, bit fields—and
must account for the details the model ignored.

Use the same six questions throughout this chapter:

- Question: What are the objects?. What it reveals: numbers, bits, sets, nodes, vectors, events.
- Question: What operations are legal?. What it reveals: addition, comparison, traversal, composition.
- Question: Which relationships matter?. What it reveals: order, reachability, dependence, distance.
- Question: What remains invariant?. What it reveals: the fact that proves each transition is safe.
- Question: What is finite on a computer?. What it reveals: width, precision, memory, time, samples.
- Question: How can the model fail?. What it reveals: overflow, approximation, ambiguity, state explosion.

## 1. Mathematical Objects Answer Different Questions

- Tool: sets. Question it answers: Which distinct values belong to a collection?. Where it appears in a computer: type domains, permissions, query results.
- Tool: relations. Question it answers: Which objects are connected or comparable?. Where it appears in a computer: database rows, graphs, dependency edges.
- Tool: functions. Question it answers: How does each valid input determine an output?. Where it appears in a computer: instructions, compiler passes, APIs.
- Tool: logic. Question it answers: Which conclusions follow from known facts?. Where it appears in a computer: branches, circuits, type checking.
- Tool: counting. Question it answers: How many states or arrangements are possible?. Where it appears in a computer: complexity, testing, key spaces.
- Tool: modular arithmetic. Question it answers: How do values behave when they wrap?. Where it appears in a computer: clocks, hashes, ring buffers, cryptography.
- Tool: probability. Question it answers: How should uncertainty be quantified?. Where it appears in a computer: retries, randomized algorithms, inference.
- Tool: graph theory. Question it answers: How does information move through relationships?. Where it appears in a computer: ASTs, calls, networks, build graphs.
- Tool: linear algebra. Question it answers: How do linear quantities and transforms compose?. Where it appears in a computer: graphics, audio, simulation, ML.
- Tool: calculus. Question it answers: How does a quantity change or accumulate?. Where it appears in a computer: motion, optimization, signal processing.

These are not rival descriptions. A single system uses several at once. A network
router has a graph of routes, finite packet buffers, Boolean policy predicates,
probabilistic loss, and counters that wrap.

## 2. Sets, Relations, and Functions Describe Structure

A set is a collection whose members are considered without duplicates. A relation
is a set of ordered pairs. A function is a relation in which each input in the
function's domain maps to exactly one output.

- Idea: membership, x ∈ S. Example: a token kind belongs to the expression-start set. Computer-science meaning: parser decision.
- Idea: subset, A ⊆ B. Example: admin permissions contain reader permissions. Computer-science meaning: authorization.
- Idea: union, A ∪ B. Example: values accepted by either type. Computer-science meaning: sum/union type.
- Idea: intersection, A ∩ B. Example: capabilities supported by both peers. Computer-science meaning: protocol negotiation.
- Idea: Cartesian product, A times B. Example: every state paired with every input. Computer-science meaning: state-machine table.
- Idea: partial function. Example: lookup may have no result. Computer-science meaning: Option of T.
- Idea: fallible function. Example: decoding may reject bytes. Computer-science meaning: Result of T or E.

Rust makes the difference between total, partial, and fallible computation visible:

Note. Library lens: a parser combinator represents languages as sets of accepted
strings and combines them with operations resembling union, sequence, and repetition.
A database query represents relations and transforms them with selection, projection,
joins, grouping, and ordering.

## 3. Logic Turns Facts into Decisions

Propositional logic treats complete statements as true or false. Predicate logic
adds variables and quantifiers such as “for every” and “there exists.” A program's
conditionals, type rules, protocol guards, and hardware gates all implement logical
decisions.

- A: 0. B: 0. A AND B: 0. A OR B: 0. A XOR B: 0.
- A: 0. B: 1. A AND B: 0. A OR B: 1. A XOR B: 1.
- A: 1. B: 0. A AND B: 0. A OR B: 1. A XOR B: 1.
- A: 1. B: 1. A AND B: 1. A OR B: 1. A XOR B: 0.

Two useful equivalences are De Morgan's laws:

They explain why guard clauses can replace deeply nested conditions and why bit masks
can implement sets efficiently.

Logic is only as sound as its premises. If an authorization check trusts an unverified
claim, flawless Boolean algebra still produces the wrong security decision.

## 4. Proofs and Invariants Explain Why a Program Works

A test checks selected examples. A proof explains why a claim follows for every input
covered by its assumptions.

- Reasoning method: direct proof. Shape: assumptions leads to valid steps leads to conclusion. Programming use: derive a bound or type rule.
- Reasoning method: contradiction. Shape: assume the opposite and derive impossibility. Programming use: uniqueness and impossibility results.
- Reasoning method: induction. Shape: base case plus size-preserving step. Programming use: recursion and tree/list algorithms.
- Reasoning method: case analysis. Shape: cover every member of a finite set. Programming use: exhaustive match.
- Reasoning method: loop invariant. Shape: fact true before and after every iteration. Programming use: searching, sorting, parsing.

A binary search is correct because of an invariant, not because it “cuts the array in
half”:

When reading code, look for the invariant each mutation is meant to preserve. Ownership
types, database constraints, protocol sequence numbers, and G P U resource states are all
ways of making invariants executable.

## 5. Counting Explains State Explosion

The sum rule counts mutually exclusive choices. The product rule counts
independent sequences of choices.

- Situation: choose one of n alternatives. Count: n.
- Situation: choose an A and then a B. Count: or A or times or B or.
- Situation: subsets of n independent flags. Count: 2ⁿ.
- Situation: arrangements of n distinct items. Count: n!
- Situation: choose k items without order. Count: n! / (k!(n-k)!).

This is why exhaustive testing becomes impossible quickly. Twenty independent Boolean
options already describe 2²⁰ = 1,048,576 configurations. Protocols and APIs therefore
try to reduce independent flags, eliminate illegal combinations with types, and test
equivalence classes rather than every raw input.

The pigeonhole principle says that placing more than n objects into n boxes
forces at least one collision. A finite hash has more possible inputs than outputs, so
collisions are mathematically unavoidable; a hash table manages them, while a
cryptographic design makes chosen collisions computationally difficult.

## 6. Number Theory and Modular Arithmetic Model Finite Cycles

Two integers are congruent modulo m when they leave the same remainder:

Modular arithmetic models clocks, sequence numbers, ring buffers, integer wraparound,
checksums, and much of public-key cryptography. But machine overflow and mathematical
modulo are not automatically the same contract—signed remainder rules and overflow
behavior vary by language and operation.

Wrapping sequence numbers need a bounded-distance comparison. Ordinary cannot tell
which of two wrapped counters is logically newer without assuming they are less than
half the number space apart.

## 7. Probability Quantifies Uncertainty

A sample space lists possible outcomes. An event is a subset of outcomes. A
random variable maps outcomes to values. The expected value is a
probability-weighted average, not a promise about any one run.

- Concept: independence. Meaning: learning one event does not change the other's probability. Systems example: idealized separate coin flips.
- Concept: conditional probability. Meaning: probability of A given evidence B. Systems example: failure given a warning signal.
- Concept: Bayes' rule. Meaning: update a belief using evidence and base rates. Systems example: alert interpretation.
- Concept: expectation. Meaning: long-run weighted average. Systems example: retries, latency, memory cost.
- Concept: variance. Meaning: spread around the mean. Systems example: jitter and tail instability.
- Concept: distribution. Meaning: probability assigned across outcomes. Systems example: request sizes, packet delay.

For independent attempts with failure probability p, the probability that all n
attempts fail is pⁿ. This explains why a small number of retries can improve success,
but it does not justify unbounded retry storms. Retries also add load and can make
failures correlated.

Bayes' rule highlights the base-rate problem:

A scanner with a low false-positive rate can still produce mostly false alerts when the
condition it detects is extremely rare.

## 8. Information Theory Connects Uncertainty to Encoding

Information content increases as an event becomes less likely. Shannon entropy measures
the average uncertainty of a source:

- Idea: compression. Mechanism: assign shorter representations to more likely patterns.
- Idea: checksum. Mechanism: detect accidental changes; not designed against an attacker.
- Idea: cryptographic hash. Mechanism: compress arbitrary input into a fixed fingerprint with security goals.
- Idea: encoding. Mechanism: choose a representation; does not imply secrecy.
- Idea: encryption. Mechanism: transform data under a key to provide confidentiality.

Lossless compression cannot make every possible input shorter: if all n-bit strings
compressed to fewer than n bits, there would not be enough shorter outputs to
represent them uniquely. Compressors win by exploiting nonuniform structure in actual
data.

## 9. Graph Theory Models Relationships and Reachability

A graph contains vertices and edges. Edges may be directed, undirected,
weighted, labeled, cyclic, or acyclic.

- Graph: A S T. Vertices: syntax nodes. Edges: parent/child relation. Useful operation: traversal and rewriting.
- Graph: call graph. Vertices: functions. Edges: possible calls. Useful operation: reachability and inlining.
- Graph: control-flow graph. Vertices: basic blocks. Edges: possible jumps. Useful operation: data-flow analysis.
- Graph: dependency graph. Vertices: packages/tasks. Edges: “requires”. Useful operation: topological ordering.
- Graph: object graph. Vertices: heap objects. Edges: references. Useful operation: garbage-collection tracing.
- Graph: network. Vertices: machines/routers. Edges: links. Useful operation: routing and fault analysis.

Breadth-first search explores by distance in unweighted edges. Depth-first search
explores one path deeply and is natural for dependency analysis, cycle detection, and
recursive structures. A topological order exists only for a directed acyclic graph.

Note. Deep connection: tracing garbage collection, module dependency resolution,
reachability-based linker elimination, and “find every node reachable from here” are
the same graph problem with different vertex and edge meanings.

## 10. Linear Algebra Describes Composable Transformations

A vector can represent a position, direction, velocity, color, audio frame, or feature
row. A matrix represents a linear transformation. Matrix multiplication composes
transformations, and its order matters.

- Operation: vector addition. Meaning: combine displacements or signals.
- Operation: scalar multiplication. Meaning: change magnitude.
- Operation: dot product. Meaning: measure alignment or projection.
- Operation: matrix-vector product. Meaning: transform one value.
- Operation: matrix-matrix product. Meaning: compose transforms.
- Operation: norm. Meaning: measure magnitude.
- Operation: basis change. Meaning: express one vector in another coordinate system.

Types such as Point3, Direction3, Normal3, Radians, and Degrees distinguish
objects that may share a numeric representation but obey different legal operations.

## 11. Calculus and Difference Equations Model Change

A derivative measures local rate of change. An integral accumulates a quantity
over an interval. A gradient collects partial derivatives and points in the
direction of steepest increase.

Computers usually approximate continuous models with discrete samples:

The approximation has an error model. Smaller time steps usually reduce numerical error
but require more work; floating-point rounding and unstable equations can still
accumulate error.

- Continuous idea: derivative. Discrete implementation: finite difference.
- Continuous idea: integral. Discrete implementation: weighted sum of samples.
- Continuous idea: differential equation. Discrete implementation: recurrence / update rule.
- Continuous idea: continuous signal. Discrete implementation: sampled sequence.
- Continuous idea: gradient descent. Discrete implementation: repeated parameter update.

## 12. Floating Point Is an Approximation Contract

An IEEE-style floating-point value stores a sign, a scaled significand, and an
exponent. This provides large dynamic range, but most real values—including 0.1—
cannot be represented exactly in binary.

- Property: finite precision. Consequence: nearby real values round to the same bit pattern.
- Property: nonuniform spacing. Consequence: large-magnitude values have coarser gaps.
- Property: special values. Consequence: NaN, positive/negative infinity, and signed zero exist.
- Property: non-associativity. Consequence: (a plus b) plus c can differ from a plus (b plus c).
- Property: gradual underflow. Consequence: subnormal values trade precision for range.
- Property: NaN not equals NaN. Consequence: ordinary equality is not a validity test.

Use integers or fixed-point values when exact cents, ticks, sample positions, or wire
fields matter. Use floating point for continuous quantities, but define units,
non-finite handling, tolerances, and determinism requirements.

## 13. Complexity Is a Model of Growth, Not a Stopwatch

Asymptotic notation describes how resource use grows as input size grows:

- Class: O(1). Typical shape: fixed work. Example: array indexing.
- Class: O(log n). Typical shape: repeatedly shrink the remaining space. Example: binary search.
- Class: O(n). Typical shape: visit each item. Example: scan.
- Class: O(n log n). Typical shape: divide plus linear merge/work. Example: comparison sorting.
- Class: O(n²). Typical shape: compare many pairs. Example: simple nested-loop algorithms.
- Class: O(2ⁿ). Typical shape: explore subsets. Example: unrestricted backtracking.

Big-O hides constants, hardware, allocation, cache locality, vectorization, and input
distribution. A complete engineering cost model asks:

- How many bytes move through each memory level?
- Is access contiguous or pointer-chasing?
- How many allocations and system calls occur?
- Is work parallel, serial, blocking, or asynchronous?
- What are the worst-case and tail-latency bounds?
- Which input controls the cost?

Note. Rust/library connection: iterators expose algebraic pipeline composition,
slices expose contiguous representation, Result exposes partial functions, graph
crates expose traversal, Diesel exposes relational operations, and W G P U exposes
linear transformations plus asynchronous resource constraints. The library is useful
here because its A P I turns the mathematical model into enforceable program structure.

Note. Next: hardware turns these abstract objects into finite bit patterns,
instructions, registers, memory transactions, and timed state changes.

# Hardware Fundamentals: How a C P U Changes State

## The C P U

If you want to create a “computer” from scratch, you need to start by defining an
abstract model for your computer. This abstract model is also referred to as
Instruction Set Architecture (I S A)

A C P U is an implementation of an Instruction Set Architecture (I S A) — an
abstract model defining data types, registers, hardware support, and input and output. Together these
make up Machine Language, the lowest-level language of computing.

The C P U continuously runs a fetch-decode-execute loop:

Note. Core loop: fetch an instruction, decode what it means, then
execute its effect.

1. Fetch — Retrieve the instruction addressed by the Instruction Pointer (IP) /
Program Counter (PC).
2. Decode — Interpret the opcode (a unique encoding for an operation — the
"atoms of computing"), plus any operands (arguments) and optional prefix (behavioral
modifier).
3. Execute — The Control Unit (CU) dispatches signals to functional units like
the A L U (Arithmetic Logic Unit), which performs the operation on register values.
Results are written back to memory if applicable.

Note. Modern CPUs accelerate this loop with instruction pipelining and speculative
execution.

## Number Systems: Binary, Decimal, Hexadecimal

CPUs are built from circuits that are either on or off — so at the hardware
level, everything is represented in binary (base-2): only the digits 0 and 1.

- System: Binary. Base: 2. Digits: 0, 1. Example: 1101.
- System: Decimal. Base: 10. Digits: 0–9. Example: 126.
- System: Hexadecimal. Base: 16. Digits: 0–9, A–F. Example: 0xA1D.

Each system is just a different way of writing the same value — e.g. 1101 in binary is
13 in decimal. Binary gets unwieldy fast (the decimal number 250 is 11111010 in
binary), so hexadecimal (prefixed 0x) is the standard shorthand in computing: each
hex digit maps cleanly to exactly 4 binary bits, so 250 is simply 0xFA. This is why
opcodes, memory addresses, and register dumps are almost always shown in hex.

## Opcodes in Practice

Instructions are comprised of instruction code (aka operation code, in short opcode
or p-code), directly executed by the C P U. An opcode can either have operand(s)
or no operand.

In an 8-bit machine where instructions are 8 bits:

- LOAD 0101 leads to 00110101 — the first 4 bits (0011) are the opcode for load, the
second 4 bits (0101) are the operand.
- INCREMENT leads to 1000 — the opcode for increment by 1, no operand needed.

Since opcodes are the atoms of computing, they are presented in an opcode table
(e.g., the x86 opcode reference).

A few opcodes worth knowing by name:

- nop (0x90 on x86, "no operation") — the C P U does nothing and advances to the
next instruction. It's a useful placeholder, and understanding it makes reading
disassembly less intimidating: not every instruction needs to matter.
- cmp — compares two values.
- jmp / je / jne ("jump", "jump if equal", "jump if not equal") —
conditionally redirect execution to a different address instead of continuing
sequentially. This mechanism — compare, then conditionally jump — is called
branching, and it's the opcode-level foundation of every if/else you write in
a high-level language.

## Assembly Language

Because bit-patterns are hard to remember, Assembly Language assigns abstract,
human-readable symbols to opcodes matching their operations by name:

Here, 0011 is abstracted to the symbol LOAD, so 00110101 can be written as
LOAD 0101 — a higher-level, human-readable form of the same instruction.

A utility program called an Assembler translates Assembly back to Machine Language.

Note. Assembly is high-level relative to machine code, but low-level relative to C,
Rust, or Python. High-level and low-level are relative terms that convey the
amount of abstraction involved.

## Memory Layout

When a program is loaded into memory, it creates a process with three key regions:

- Region: Static. Purpose: Global variables and constants.
- Region: Stack. Purpose: Function frames and local variables — auto-managed, LIFO.
- Region: Heap. Purpose: Dynamically allocated data shared across functions and threads.

- Stack analogy: A notepad — write, tear off when done.
- Heap analogy: A whiteboard — write, stays until you explicitly erase it.

Objects live on the heap because they need to outlive the function that created them.

How an OS knows to run a program at all: each OS defines its own executable
format — a file layout the OS knows how to parse and load. On Windows this is the PE
(Portable Executable) format; on Linux it's ELF. Both split the file into named
sections, most notably a.text section (the compiled opcodes — the actual program
code) and a.data section (initial values for global variables). This is why an
executable built for one OS won't run on another: the OS's loader is looking for a
specific format it doesn't recognize.

# Representation and Translation: From Source to Machine State

Informally, a language is structured text with syntax and semantics. Source code
written in a programming language needs:

1. A translator of some sort — converts it to another language/format.
2. An executor of some sort — runs the translated commands to produce output.

## Levels of Abstraction

- Level: Machine Language. Description: Raw bit-pattern opcodes directly executed by the C P U.
- Level: Assembly Language. Description: Human-readable symbols mapped to opcodes; translated by an Assembler.
- Level: I R (Intermediate Representation). Description: Any format between source and assembly; converting between IRs is called lowering.
- Level: Bytecode. Description: An I R emulating a simplified instruction set; executed by a Virtual Machine (V M).

## Classifying Languages

Languages can be described along two independent axes:

- Axis: What it executes on. Options: Compiled vs. Interpreted. Meaning: Compiled code runs directly on the C P U; interpreted code is translated on the fly by another program.
- Axis: How it executes. Options: Imperative vs. Functional. Meaning: Imperative runs instructions top-to-bottom; functional resolves through function declaration and evaluation.

These combine independently — e.g. C is compiled + imperative, Java is interpreted (its
bytecode runs on the JVM) + imperative, Haskell is compiled + functional. An interpreted
language can still be distributed as something that looks compiled, by bundling the
interpreter together with the source/bytecode it runs.

Note. There's no "best" combination — different points on these axes trade off differently
for different problems, and (per the Crash Course discussion) every language
ultimately answers to the same question: how does this text become something the C P U
executes?

## Compilers

A compiler is any program that translates (maps, encodes) Language A leads to Language B.
Its two core components:

- Frontend — Maps source code strings to a structured format called an Abstract
Syntax Tree (A S T).
- Backend (Code Generator) — Translates the A S T to Bytecode, I R, or Assembly.

Most often, when we talk about compiler, we mean Ahead-Of-Time (A O T) compiler where the
translation happens before execution. Another form of translation is Just-In-Time (J I T)
compilation where translation happens right at the time of the execution.

- Translation Type: A O T (Ahead-of-Time). When It Happens: Before execution. Examples: rustc, gcc.
- Translation Type: J I T (Just-in-Time). When It Happens: During execution. Examples: V eight (JS), PyPy (Python).
- Translation Type: Transpiler. When It Happens: Source-to-source. Examples: Python leads to Java.

## Virtual Machines (VMs)

Hardware instructions are vendor-specific — Intel and AMD instructions differ. A
Virtual Machine (V M) abstracts away hardware details so that code compiled to the
V M's language becomes platform-agnostic.

The most famous example: the Java Virtual Machine (JVM). Any valid Java Bytecode
runs on any platform with a Java Runtime Environment (JRE), regardless of where it
was compiled.

# Memory Systems: Representation, Allocation, and Runtime State

Understanding memory is useful in three directions at once:

1. Building a language — you must decide how values, objects, strings, stack frames,
and garbage or ownership metadata are represented.
2. Debugging low-level software — you must connect a source-level value to bytes,
addresses, registers, and instructions.
3. Reversing a language or binary format — you infer those same representation
decisions from observable behavior.

Use these techniques only on software, memory dumps, packet captures, and systems you
own or are explicitly authorized to study. Prefer an isolated V M, disposable test
program, local server, or purpose-built target.

Note. Safety and authorization boundary: low-level visibility is powerful. Practice
on software and systems you own or have explicit permission to inspect.

## 1. Bytes, Addresses, Pointers, and Views

A byte is data. An address is a number naming a location. A pointer is a
typed or untyped value containing an address. The same bytes may be interpreted through
different views:

The bytes do not change; the interpretation does:

- Context: Disassembler. Interpretation applied to the same bytes: Opcodes, operands, and instruction boundaries.
- Context: Executable/packet parser. Interpretation applied to the same bytes: Header fields, lengths, flags, and sections.
- Context: Virtual machine. Interpretation applied to the same bytes: Tagged values, handles, or bytecode instructions.
- Context: Debugger. Interpretation applied to the same bytes: Runtime objects, fields, pointers, and strings.
- Context: File-format reversing. Interpretation applied to the same bytes: Hypothesized records and relationships.

### Endianness

For the integer 0x12345678:

Network protocols traditionally call big-endian network byte order. x86 and x eighty-six sixty-four
normally use little-endian values in memory. Never decode a multi-byte integer without
deciding its width and byte order.

### Pointer arithmetic is really layout arithmetic

If an array element has size S, element i begins at:

For a record or object:

A multi-level pointer path repeatedly reads an address and then applies the next offset:

Do not confuse a stable relationship with a stable absolute address. A S L R, allocator
behavior, and different runs can move stack, heap, libraries, and sometimes the main
executable.

## 2. Memory Regions and Object Lifetimes

- Region: Code /.text. Typical contents: Machine instructions. Lifetime: Loaded image.
- Region: Read-only data. Typical contents: Constants, string literals. Lifetime: Loaded image.
- Region: Static data. Typical contents: Globals. Lifetime: Process.
- Region: Stack. Typical contents: Call frames, local values, saved state. Lifetime: Function or scope.
- Region: Heap. Typical contents: Dynamic objects, collections, closures. Lifetime: Explicit or managed.
- Region: Memory-mapped region. Typical contents: Files, shared libraries, device memory. Lifetime: Mapping.

This table is a model, not a promise that every compiler and OS lays out every value
exactly this way. Optimizers may keep a local variable only in a register or remove it
entirely.

### Stack frame mental model

Calling conventions define where arguments and return values go, which registers a
callee must preserve, and how the stack is aligned. A compiler backend must obey the
target A B I when calling external functions.

### Heap lifetime hazards

- Hazard: Leak. What went wrong: Unreachable memory is never released.
- Hazard: Double free. What went wrong: One allocation is released twice.
- Hazard: Use after free. What went wrong: A stale pointer outlives its allocation.
- Hazard: Out-of-bounds access. What went wrong: An index or pointer escapes the allocation.
- Hazard: Uninitialized read. What went wrong: Bytes are interpreted before receiving a valid value.
- Hazard: Data race. What went wrong: Shared state is accessed without required synchronization.

These hazards explain the trade-offs among manual allocation, garbage collection,
reference counting, region/arena allocation, and Rust-style ownership.

### Arena allocation for compilers

A S T nodes often share one phase lifetime: they are all created during parsing and
discarded together. An arena makes that lifetime explicit:

This can simplify ownership and improve locality, but references into an arena must
never outlive it.

## 3. Layout, Alignment, Padding, and repr

CPUs often prefer or require values at aligned addresses. A u32 commonly has alignment
4, so a compiler may insert padding:

Key Rust rules:

- Default repr(Rust) does not promise a stable C-compatible field layout.
- number sign [repr(C)] requests C A B I layout and is useful at an F F I boundary.
- number sign [repr(u8)] or another integer representation can make an enum discriminant
representation explicit.
- number sign [repr(packed)] removes some padding but can create unaligned fields. Creating a
normal reference to an unaligned field is invalid; specialized unaligned reads are
required.
- A Rust struct is not automatically a serialized file or wire format. Serialize each
field explicitly.

For your own language, decide whether object layout is:

- specified as part of the language/A B I;
- implementation-defined but documented by one compiler;
- opaque, accessible only through generated accessors.

Opaque layouts give the runtime freedom to reorder fields or change garbage collectors.
Specified layouts make F F I and reversing easier but reduce implementation freedom.

## 4. Safe Binary Parsing in Rust

A binary parser should treat all offsets, sizes, tags, and counts as untrusted. Prefer
slices and checked arithmetic over raw pointers.

- Parser concern: Lengths/counts. Defensive requirement: Bound them before allocating or looping.
- Parser concern: Arithmetic. Defensive requirement: Use checked addition, multiplication, and slicing.
- Parser concern: Tags/enums. Defensive requirement: Reject unknown mandatory tags and invalid values.
- Parser concern: Trailing bytes. Defensive requirement: Define whether they are permitted.
- Parser concern: Structural limits. Defensive requirement: Cap recursion depth and decompressed output.
- Parser concern: Input delivery. Defensive requirement: Never assume one read contains a complete file or message.
- Parser concern: Diagnostics. Defensive requirement: Preserve byte offsets in errors.
- Parser concern: Robustness testing. Defensive requirement: Fuzz with arbitrary byte strings.

The same parser architecture works for bytecode, object files, save files, image
formats, and network frames.

## 5. Memory Inspection as a Scientific Method

The most general-purpose lesson is not a particular tool; it is the repeated workflow:

1. Identify a value or behavior you can control.
2. Change one variable while keeping other conditions stable.
3. Observe memory, registers, instructions, or packets.
4. Form a hypothesis about representation or control flow.
5. Repeat with a different input or a fresh run.
6. Validate by predicting an observation you have not yet seen.

For a safe toy example, pretend a process snapshot is just a byte vector:

This demonstrates differential scanning without touching another process. The same
reasoning is useful for debugging your V M's heap, comparing serialized ASTs, and
locating a field in a captured memory image.

### Static, dynamic, and forensic views

- View: Static. Evidence: File bytes, symbols, strings, disassembly. Best for: Possible behavior and structure.
- View: Dynamic. Evidence: Registers, memory, breakpoints, system calls. Best for: Actual behavior for one run.
- View: Forensic. Evidence: A saved memory image or capture. Best for: Reconstructing prior runtime state.

Memory-forensics workflow can be generalized as:

1. preserve the original image;
2. identify the OS, architecture, and capture context;
3. inventory processes, mappings, handles, and connections;
4. narrow to relevant artifacts;
5. corroborate findings across multiple sources;
6. record offsets, hashes, and tool versions so results are reproducible.

Strings are clues, not proof. A string may be unused, encoded, copied, stale, or
unrelated.

## 6. Defensive Memory Safety and Hardening

Defensive memory-safety material is most useful when it helps you recognize what your
compiler or runtime must prevent.

- Failure: Buffer overflow. Root cause: Write exceeds allocation. Language/runtime defense: Slice bounds, checked APIs.
- Failure: Use after free. Root cause: Lifetime not enforced. Language/runtime defense: Ownership, G C, handles/generations.
- Failure: Double free. Root cause: Ambiguous ownership. Language/runtime defense: Move semantics, unique owner.
- Failure: Null dereference. Root cause: Missing value represented as pointer. Language/runtime defense: Option of T / tagged union.
- Failure: Type confusion. Root cause: Bytes interpreted as wrong type. Language/runtime defense: Tags, validation, safe casts.
- Failure: Integer overflow. Root cause: Size calculation wraps. Language/runtime defense: Checked arithmetic and limits.
- Failure: Data race. Root cause: Unsynchronized shared mutation. Language/runtime defense: Send/sync rules, actors, locks.

- Platform mitigation: Stack canaries. What it hardens: Detect some stack overwrites before return.
- Platform mitigation: NX/D E P. What it hardens: Mark data pages non-executable.
- Platform mitigation: A S L R. What it hardens: Randomize important memory regions between runs.
- Platform mitigation: P I E. What it hardens: Allow the main executable to be relocated.
- Platform mitigation: rel-ro. What it hardens: Make selected relocation data read-only after linking.
- Platform mitigation: Control-flow integrity. What it hardens: Restrict indirect branches to approved targets.

Mitigations are layers, not substitutes for memory-safe design.

Safe C example: bound the read to the actual destination capacity and ensure string
termination.

- Testing layer: Compiler warnings. Recommended practice: Enable them and treat new warnings seriously.
- Testing layer: Native runtime. Recommended practice: Use sanitizers on C/C++ components.
- Testing layer: Input boundaries. Recommended practice: Fuzz lexers, parsers, verifiers, and decoders.
- Testing layer: Numeric limits. Recommended practice: Test zero, maximum, and one-past-maximum values.
- Testing layer: Bytecode. Recommended practice: Verify malformed input before execution.
- Testing layer: Unsafe Rust. Recommended practice: Keep it small and document the relied-upon invariants.

## 7. Reversing a Binary, Bytecode V M, or Language

Source compilation and reverse engineering travel in opposite directions:

- Lost information: Variable/function names. Reverse-engineering consequence: Recovered names are hypotheses or tool-generated labels.
- Lost information: Comments/formatting. Reverse-engineering consequence: Intent and original organization cannot be reconstructed.
- Lost information: Source distinctions. Reverse-engineering consequence: Several source forms may produce identical instructions.
- Lost information: Call boundaries. Reverse-engineering consequence: Inlining can merge a callee into its caller.
- Lost information: Expressions. Reverse-engineering consequence: Constant folding can erase the original computation.
- Lost information: Source types. Reverse-engineering consequence: Only layout and operation clues may remain.

Therefore, decompilation is a model, not restoration of the original source.

### A disciplined reversing notebook

For each discovery, record:

- Field: Location. Example: file offset, virtual address, function symbol.
- Field: Observation. Example: "four-byte big-endian value precedes payload".
- Field: Hypothesis. Example: "value is compressed payload length".
- Field: Evidence. Example: controlled inputs of lengths 1, 5, and 20.
- Field: Confidence. Example: low / medium / high.
- Field: Falsifier. Example: a capture whose value does not match payload length.

### Recovering a bytecode format

Start with the smallest programs:

Then compare outputs:

1. locate a header or magic number;
2. identify version, flags, and section boundaries;
3. find constant pools and encoded strings;
4. change one literal and locate the changed bytes;
5. infer opcode width and operand encoding;
6. trace stack-height changes or virtual registers;
7. build a disassembler before building a decompiler;
8. validate by round-tripping or predicting unseen encodings.

Example of a tiny, safe disassembler:

### Designing for reversibility

- Reversibility feature: Magic number + bytecode version. Benefit: Reliable format identification.
- Reversibility feature: Documented section table. Benefit: Predictable navigation and extension.
- Reversibility feature: Source maps/symbol names. Benefit: Better diagnostics and debugging.
- Reversibility feature: Official dumper/disassembler. Benefit: One canonical inspection path.
- Reversibility feature: Separate verifier. Benefit: Safe validation without execution.
- Reversibility feature: Fail-closed versioning. Benefit: Unknown formats cannot be misinterpreted.
- Reversibility feature: Deterministic output. Benefit: Reproducible builds and meaningful binary diffs.

## 8. Separate Values, Objects, Storage, and Allocations

Memory discussions become confusing when several different concepts are all called “the
value.” Keep the layers separate:

- Term: value. Precise mental model: an abstract member of a type, such as integer 42.
- Term: representation. Precise mental model: the bits/bytes used to encode a value.
- Term: place. Precise mental model: a location expression that can hold a value.
- Term: object. Precise mental model: a typed region of storage with a lifetime.
- Term: allocation. Precise mental model: a reserved storage region managed as one unit.
- Term: address. Precise mental model: a numeric location in an address space.
- Term: pointer. Precise mental model: a value used to locate or refer into storage.
- Term: reference. Precise mental model: a pointer-like value with stronger validity/aliasing promises.
- Term: handle. Precise mental model: an indirect stable identifier resolved through a table.
- Term: lifetime. Precise mental model: the interval during which an object may be used.
- Term: reachability. Precise mental model: whether a root can reach an object through runtime references.

One allocation may contain several objects, and one object may be viewed through several
references:

Conversely, a logical value can span several allocations:

### Lifetime, Scope, and Storage Duration Are Different

- Concept: lexical scope. Question: Where can a source-level name be written?
- Concept: borrow lifetime. Question: For how long may this reference be used?
- Concept: object lifetime. Question: When is this typed object initialized and later destroyed?
- Concept: allocation lifetime. Question: When is the backing storage reserved and released?
- Concept: storage duration. Question: Is storage automatic, static, dynamic, mapped, or managed?

A borrow may end before its variable leaves lexical scope because the compiler sees its
last use. A heap allocation can outlive the function that created it. An arena
allocation can stay reserved after individual objects are logically dead.

Note. Debugging rule: before saying “this pointer is valid,” name the allocation,
object, offset, type, initialization state, ownership path, and time interval that make
the claim true.

## 9. Choose Ownership by the Shape of the Relationship

Rust ownership containers communicate who keeps an allocation alive:

- Type: T. Ownership model: inline, uniquely owned value. Thread use: depends on T. Common fit: ordinary local/field.
- Type: shared reference to T. Ownership model: temporary shared borrow. Thread use: depends on T: Sync. Common fit: read-only view.
- Type: mutable reference to T. Ownership model: temporary exclusive borrow. Thread use: depends on T: Send. Common fit: in-place mutation.
- Type: Box T. Ownership model: unique ownership of an allocation. Thread use: depends on T. Common fit: recursive/large/trait object.
- Type: R C T. Ownership model: non-atomic shared ownership. Thread use: single-threaded. Common fit: A S T/UI/runtime graph.
- Type: Weak T. Ownership model: non-owning observer of R C/Arc. Thread use: matches strong pointer. Common fit: parent/cache/back-reference.
- Type: Arc T. Ownership model: atomic shared ownership. Thread use: cross-thread capable. Common fit: immutable/shared service state.
- Type: Cow 'a, T. Ownership model: borrow until mutation requires a copy. Thread use: depends on owned type. Common fit: normalization and parsing.
- Type: Pin P. Ownership model: restricts moves through pointer P. Thread use: depends on P/target. Common fit: self-referential async/F F I states.

R C clone and Arc clone clone a pointer and increment a strong count; they do not
deep-copy the inner value. Arc T makes ownership counting atomic, but it does not make
arbitrary mutation of T safe.

### Break Ownership Cycles with Weak Edges

- Edge meaning: child cannot exist without parent. Usually strong or weak?: parent owns child.
- Edge meaning: child merely knows its parent. Usually strong or weak?: weak back-reference.
- Edge meaning: cache should not keep item alive. Usually strong or weak?: weak cache entry.
- Edge meaning: task must finish using shared data. Usually strong or weak?: strong task reference.

Weak upgrade returns Option because the strong owners may already be gone.
Reference counting reclaims objects deterministically when the strong count becomes
zero, but strong cycles never reach zero.

### Pinning Is Not “Permanent Memory”

Pinning restricts moving a value through a particular pointer. It does not:

- prevent the value from eventually being dropped;
- make dangling raw pointers valid;
- synchronize concurrent access;
- promise a stable address for every inner field after arbitrary projection; or
- make ordinary self-references safe without a sound construction A P I.

Most types implement Unpin and do not care about address stability. Reach for Pin
only when a documented invariant genuinely connects object identity to its address.

## 10. Pointers Carry More Than a Numeric Address

A useful modern model treats pointer validity as including provenance: which
allocation and access path authorized the pointer, in addition to its numeric address.

Two pointers with the same printed integer are not automatically interchangeable. An
address recovered from stale storage or guessed from an integer does not by itself prove
that dereferencing it is valid.

### References Promise More Than Raw Pointers

For the period it is live, a normal Rust reference promises properties such as:

- Reference: shared reference to T. Key promise: aligned, non-null, initialized, valid shared access to T.
- Reference: mutable reference to T. Key promise: aligned, non-null, initialized, exclusive access to T.
- Reference: asterisk const T / asterisk mut T. Key promise: raw address-like value; dereference still needs proof.

Creating an invalid reference can already violate Rust's rules even if code never reads
through it. Keep questionable states as raw pointers or MaybeUninit T until every
reference invariant is established.

### Interior Mutability Has an Explicit Boundary

UnsafeCell T is the primitive that permits mutation through a shared reference. Safe
types build different policies on top:

- Wrapper: Cell T. Enforcement mechanism: copy/replace values; no references to interior. Typical scope: single thread.
- Wrapper: RefCell T. Enforcement mechanism: runtime shared/exclusive borrow counter. Typical scope: single thread.
- Wrapper: Mutex T. Enforcement mechanism: blocking mutual exclusion. Typical scope: multiple threads.
- Wrapper: RwLock T. Enforcement mechanism: multiple readers or one writer. Typical scope: multiple threads.
- Wrapper: atomic type. Enforcement mechanism: hardware/compiler atomic operations. Typical scope: simple shared values.

UnsafeCell relaxes the immutability assumption of shared reference to T; it does not permit data races
or multiple live mutable reference to T references. The wrapper must enforce the remaining rules.

### Raw-Pointer Review Card

Before every dereference in unsafe code, answer:

1. Which live allocation contains the full access range?
2. How was this pointer derived?
3. Is the address correctly aligned for T?
4. Has a valid T been fully initialized there?
5. Which references or raw accesses may alias it now?
6. Can a callback, signal, interrupt, G C, or another thread invalidate the proof?
7. Who eventually drops the value and deallocates the storage?

## 11. Initialization Is a Type Invariant

“The bytes exist” does not mean “a value of type T exists.” Types restrict valid bit
patterns:

- Type/category: reference. Example invalid state: null, misaligned, dangling, or invalid provenance.
- Type/category: bool. Example invalid state: byte pattern other than a valid boolean value.
- Type/category: enum. Example invalid state: invalid discriminant.
- Type/category: NonZeroUsize. Example invalid state: zero.
- Type/category: String / vector of T. Example invalid state: nonsensical pointer/length/capacity relationship.
- Type/category: custom type. Example invalid state: broken documented semantic invariant.

MaybeUninit T represents storage that may not yet contain a valid T:

- Operation: MaybeUninit uninit. Responsibility: creates storage without claiming a valid T.
- Operation: write(value). Responsibility: initializes without trying to drop old garbage.
- Operation: assume init. Responsibility: caller proves a valid T is fully present.
- Operation: dropping MaybeUninit. Responsibility: does not drop a possibly initialized inner T.

Zero bytes are valid for some types and invalid for others. Never use “zeroed” as a
generic constructor.

### Partial Initialization Needs Cleanup State

When initializing an array or object field-by-field, a failure can leave a prefix
initialized:

A sound implementation tracks how many elements are live and drops exactly that prefix
on failure. This is the low-level version of R A I I: cleanup state must evolve together
with initialization state.

Note. Padding rule: padding may contain unspecified or uninitialized bytes. Do not
compare structs byte-for-byte, hash raw struct storage, or write the storage directly
to disk/network as a serialization format.

## 12. Allocator Mechanics

An allocator receives a request described by a size and alignment. Rust expresses
this with Layout.

The allocator must return a suitably aligned block or report failure. If raw allocation
APIs are used, deallocation must use the corresponding allocator and compatible layout.

### What a General-Purpose Allocator Tracks

- Mechanism: size classes. Purpose: group similarly sized requests.
- Mechanism: free lists/bins. Purpose: find reusable blocks.
- Mechanism: per-thread cache. Purpose: reduce global synchronization.
- Mechanism: allocation metadata. Purpose: record size/state needed for reuse.
- Mechanism: page/span management. Purpose: request larger regions from the OS.
- Mechanism: coalescing. Purpose: combine adjacent free blocks.
- Mechanism: quarantine/debug mode. Purpose: delay reuse to expose stale-pointer bugs.

The requested size, usable size, reserved size, and resident physical memory may all
differ.

### Fragmentation Has Two Forms

- Kind: internal fragmentation. Meaning: unused bytes inside an allocated size class/block.
- Kind: external fragmentation. Meaning: free space exists but not in a suitable contiguous run.

A process can free every logical object and still retain pages in allocator caches or
fragmented spans. That is not necessarily a leak, although it can still be a memory
budget problem.

### Allocation Strategies Encode Lifetime Assumptions

- Strategy: bump arena. Fast path: advance pointer. Best fit: phase-scoped A S T/I R nodes. Main limitation: individual free usually impossible.
- Strategy: pool/slab. Fast path: pop fixed-size slot. Best fit: many same-shaped objects. Main limitation: poor fit for varied sizes.
- Strategy: free-list heap. Fast path: find/split reusable block. Best fit: general dynamic allocation. Main limitation: fragmentation/metadata.
- Strategy: reference count. Fast path: increment/decrement counters. Best fit: shared acyclic ownership. Main limitation: cycles and counter traffic.
- Strategy: tracing G C heap. Fast path: cheap allocation, periodic tracing. Best fit: managed object graphs. Main limitation: pauses/barriers/runtime complexity.
- Strategy: stack/region. Fast path: adjust stack/region pointer. Best fit: nested lifetimes. Main limitation: must obey region lifetime.

For compiler phases, arenas often reduce per-node overhead and improve locality. Use
typed IDs or arena-borrowed references so nodes cannot silently outlive the arena.

### Collections Reserve More Than Their Length

- Quantity: length. Meaning: initialized elements logically in collection.
- Quantity: capacity. Meaning: elements that fit before reallocation.
- Quantity: allocation size. Meaning: backing storage reserved from allocator.

clear() drops/removes elements but normally preserves capacity. shrink to fit() is a
request and may reallocate; it is not a real-time guarantee. Reuse capacity when it
improves performance, and bound it when retained memory matters more.

## 13. Thin Pointers, Fat Pointers, and Dynamically Sized Types

Some references need metadata in addition to a data address:

The pointee may be dynamically sized even though the pointer itself has a known size.
Common examples are slices, str, and trait objects.

- Type: and [T]. Metadata: element count. Boundary question: Is the whole range live and initialized?
- Type: shared reference to str. Metadata: byte length. Boundary question: Are bytes valid U T F eight?
- Type: shared reference to dyn Trait. Metadata: vtable for concrete type. Boundary question: Does data match that vtable/type?
- Type: Box [T]. Metadata: length stored with pointer. Boundary question: Who owns and drops every element?

Never assume a trait object is “just a function pointer.” Its exact A B I/layout is not a
stable cross-language interface unless a specific A B I defines it.

### Niche Optimization

Some types have invalid bit patterns the compiler can reuse to encode an enum variant.
A common example is an optional non-null reference:

The null representation can encode None because a Rust reference cannot be null.
Niche optimizations are useful, but only rely on guarantees documented for the exact
types involved. repr(Rust) remains free to change many layout details.

### Unaligned Data Must Stay Raw Until Read Correctly

Packed or serialized data may place a multi-byte field at an unaligned address. Creating
shared reference to field would first create an invalid reference. Use raw-address formation and an
unaligned read when the format requires it:

For file and network formats, explicit byte decoding is usually clearer and more
portable than mapping bytes onto packed structs.

## 14. Memory Performance Is Mostly About Movement and Locality

C P U execution is much faster than fetching arbitrary data from main memory. Hardware
uses a hierarchy:

- Locality type: temporal locality. Meaning: recently used data is likely to be used again.
- Locality type: spatial locality. Meaning: nearby data is likely to be used soon.
- Locality type: instruction locality. Meaning: nearby/repeated code paths stay hot.

Contiguous iteration usually makes better use of cache lines and prefetching than
pointer chasing through scattered objects.

### Array of Structures Versus Structure of Arrays

- Workload: process every field of one object. Often favorable shape: array of structures.
- Workload: update only x for many objects. Often favorable shape: structure of arrays.
- Workload: polymorphic sparse graph. Often favorable shape: handles/indirection may be necessary.

Do not choose from slogans. Measure the actual traversal, object count, cache behavior,
mutation pattern, and code complexity.

### Working Set and Page Locality

The working set is the memory actively needed during an interval. A program can have
a large virtual address space but a small working set, or a moderate heap with a working
set too large for caches/RAM.

Arena placement, compacting collectors, object splitting, and dense IDs can improve
locality by placing related hot data together.

### False Sharing

Two threads can modify different variables that happen to occupy the same cache line:

The cache-coherence protocol then moves ownership of the line between cores even though
the source variables do not logically overlap. Symptoms include poor scaling and high
coherence traffic. Padding or per-thread aggregation can help, but increases memory use;
measure before changing layout.

## 15. Garbage Collection Is a Graph Algorithm

A tracing collector treats managed memory as a directed graph:

The core invariant is:

Note. Every live managed reference must be discoverable or otherwise protected whenever the
collector may run.

### Collector Families

- Strategy: reference counting. Strength: prompt reclamation, simple local reasoning. Cost/constraint: cycles, count traffic.
- Strategy: mark-sweep. Strength: handles cycles; objects need not move. Cost/constraint: tracing pause and fragmentation.
- Strategy: mark-compact. Strength: improves locality and removes fragmentation. Cost/constraint: updates references; movement cost.
- Strategy: copying/semispace. Strength: fast allocation and compaction. Cost/constraint: extra space; moves live objects.
- Strategy: generational. Strength: focuses work on usually short-lived objects. Cost/constraint: remembered sets and write barriers.
- Strategy: incremental/concurrent. Strength: spreads/reduces pause work. Cost/constraint: complex barriers and synchronization.

“Garbage collected” does not imply objects never move, destructors run immediately, or
memory usage stays below a fixed bound.

### Open, Closed, and Rooted References

For a V M with closures:

The collector must trace the intermediary in both states. Native code that temporarily
holds a managed value also needs a rooting/handle discipline if allocation can trigger
collection.

### Generational Collection Needs a Write Barrier

If an old object starts pointing to a young object, scanning only young roots would miss
the edge:

A write barrier records the old object/card/slot in a remembered set. The next minor
collection scans those recorded old-to-young edges.

- Barrier failure: missed edge. Result: live young object reclaimed.
- Barrier failure: record everything globally. Result: correct but minor collections lose benefit.
- Barrier failure: barrier runs after unsafe publish. Result: collector may observe inconsistent state.

### Safepoints and Stack Maps

A precise moving collector must know where references exist in registers and stack
slots. Compilers can emit stack maps for selected safepoints:

- Safepoint candidate: allocation slow path. Why it is useful: collection is already possible.
- Safepoint candidate: function call. Why it is useful: register/stack state follows a known convention.
- Safepoint candidate: loop back edge. Why it is useful: long-running computation eventually cooperates.
- Safepoint candidate: explicit poll. Why it is useful: runtime can request suspension.

At a safepoint, each live reference location must match the compiler's map. Optimizer
and G C metadata must be generated from the same final liveness facts.

### Finalizers Are Not Ordinary R A I I

Finalization timing may be delayed, reordered, skipped at process exit, or affected by
cycles and resurrection rules. Use explicit resource management for files, locks,
sockets, transactions, and other scarce external resources. Let G C manage memory
reachability, not critical protocol completion.

## 16. Memory Accounting Uses Several Different Numbers

“The process uses 2 GB” is incomplete without naming the measurement:

- Metric: virtual size. Rough meaning: total mapped address ranges.
- Metric: resident set (RSS). Rough meaning: pages currently resident for the process.
- Metric: proportional set (PSS). Rough meaning: shared pages divided among sharers.
- Metric: heap live bytes. Rough meaning: allocations still logically live.
- Metric: allocator active bytes. Rough meaning: blocks/spans currently serving allocations.
- Metric: allocator reserved bytes. Rough meaning: memory retained for future reuse.
- Metric: stack reservation. Rough meaning: address space reserved for thread stacks.
- Metric: mapped-file pages. Rough meaning: file/library mappings, possibly shared/page-cached.
- Metric: peak usage. Rough meaning: high-water mark, not current usage.

The exact availability and definition of these metrics is OS/tool-specific.

### Leak, Retention, Fragmentation, and Cache Are Different

- Pattern: true leak. What keeps memory unavailable: ownership/reference is lost without reclamation.
- Pattern: logical retention. What keeps memory unavailable: reachable collection/cache keeps unneeded objects.
- Pattern: reference cycle. What keeps memory unavailable: ownership counts never reach zero.
- Pattern: fragmentation. What keeps memory unavailable: free pieces cannot satisfy/release useful spans.
- Pattern: allocator retention. What keeps memory unavailable: freed blocks remain cached for later allocation.
- Pattern: page cache/mapping. What keeps memory unavailable: OS keeps file-backed pages or address ranges mapped.

A heap profile dominated by reachable cache entries is a policy problem, not a missing
free. A stable live heap with rising RSS may indicate allocator retention or
fragmentation rather than continued object growth.

### Ask Four Questions

Take comparable measurements after warm-up and after completing the same unit of work.
One snapshot shows composition; two or more snapshots show direction.

## 17. Memory Debugging Needs the Right Observer

- Symptom: invalid native read/write. Useful observer/tool class: AddressSanitizer or memory checker.
- Symptom: use of uninitialized data. Useful observer/tool class: MemorySanitizer/Valgrind-style checking.
- Symptom: leak at process exit. Useful observer/tool class: LeakSanitizer or allocation tracing.
- Symptom: Rust unsafe-code UB. Useful observer/tool class: Miri for supported tests.
- Symptom: unexpected heap growth. Useful observer/tool class: allocation/heap profiler plus retained paths.
- Symptom: stack overflow. Useful observer/tool class: stack trace, recursion/depth accounting.
- Symptom: page-permission fault. Useful observer/tool class: debugger plus memory-map/page information.
- Symptom: race on shared memory. Useful observer/tool class: thread/race sanitizer and synchronization review.
- Symptom: cache/T L B bottleneck. Useful observer/tool class: hardware performance counters/profiler.
- Symptom: wrong V M/G C root. Useful observer/tool class: runtime heap verifier and root/handle dump.

No one tool proves memory correctness. Dynamic tools observe executed paths; type
systems and reviews cover classes of paths; fuzzing/coverage help explore more cases.

### Reproducible Memory Investigation

- Evidence to preserve: exact binary and symbols. Why: optimized layouts differ.
- Evidence to preserve: sanitizer/tool configuration. Why: detection depends on instrumentation/options.
- Evidence to preserve: allocator and environment. Why: retention/size classes vary.
- Evidence to preserve: input and operation count. Why: growth must be normalized by work.
- Evidence to preserve: stack and allocation trace. Why: connects bytes back to ownership decisions.

### Runtime Heap Verification

A custom language can check its own heap in debug builds:

Run verification after collection, after heap growth, and around complicated F F I
transitions. A verifier turns silent corruption into an earlier, localized invariant
failure.

## 18. Memory Design Checklist for Your Own Language

- Area: value representation. Decision to make explicitly: boxed, tagged union, NaN-boxed, handles, or mixed.
- Area: object header. Decision to make explicitly: type/shape, size, mark state, generation, flags.
- Area: alignment. Decision to make explicitly: minimum object/stack/A B I alignment.
- Area: ownership. Decision to make explicitly: unique, reference-counted, tracing, arena, or hybrid.
- Area: roots. Decision to make explicitly: V M stack, registers, globals, native handles, J I T frames.
- Area: movement. Decision to make explicitly: whether objects can move and how references are updated.
- Area: allocation failure. Decision to make explicitly: exception, result, abort, reserve, or G C retry.
- Area: stack policy. Decision to make explicitly: maximum depth, growth, guard pages, overflow behavior.
- Area: finalization. Decision to make explicitly: explicit close versus nondeterministic cleanup.
- Area: F F I. Decision to make explicitly: pin/copy/handle rules and who owns foreign buffers.
- Area: concurrency. Decision to make explicitly: stop-the-world, per-thread heaps, locks, atomics, barriers.
- Area: observability. Decision to make explicitly: heap dump, object IDs, allocation sites, verifier.
- Area: limits. Decision to make explicitly: heap, object, string, collection, recursion, mapped bytes.

Every layer participates in the language's memory model. A safe source language can
still have an unsafe runtime if bytecode verification, root tracking, layout, F F I, or
concurrent mutation breaks the contract.

# Operating Systems: Processes, Virtual Memory, Files, and Scheduling

This section connects the compiler's output to the operating system that actually runs
it. It is based on Computer Science from the Bottom Up,
especially its chapters on the operating system, processes, virtual memory, ELF, and
dynamic linking.

Note. OS mental model: the kernel owns privileged resources and exposes controlled
operations to processes through system calls.

## Kernel Case Study: Booting Into Protected Execution

Writing an OS in Rust is useful here because a tiny kernel
removes the services that a normal Rust program silently inherits. Its code is not just
an operating-system recipe: each step reveals a computer-science contract.

Note. Library lens — Phil Opp's kernel: read each module as an executable model.
The bootloader models representation transfer, an interrupt descriptor table
models event dispatch, paging models address translation, an allocator models
resource management, and a tiny async executor models cooperative scheduling.

### The Boot Path Is a Chain of Representations

Each arrow is a contract boundary. The next stage cannot assume that an ordinary
language runtime already exists.

- Boundary: Firmware leads to bootloader. State that must be agreed on: Boot medium, executable format, entry location.
- Boundary: Bootloader leads to kernel. State that must be agreed on: C P U mode, stack, memory map, framebuffer, page tables.
- Boundary: Kernel leads to driver. State that must be agreed on: Register layout, interrupt route, D M A ownership.
- Boundary: Kernel leads to process. State that must be agreed on: Virtual address space, privilege level, initial registers.
- Boundary: Runtime leads to main. State that must be agreed on: A B I, arguments, environment, initialized language services.

A freestanding Rust binary therefore needs deliberate answers to questions a hosted
program delegates to its operating system:

- Hosted assumption: std can call the operating system. Kernel replacement: Use core, then add only the services that exist.
- Hosted assumption: A stack already exists. Kernel replacement: Boot code or bootloader supplies one.
- Hosted assumption: Panics can print and unwind. Kernel replacement: Install a panic policy and an output path.
- Hosted assumption: Heap allocation exists. Kernel replacement: Map heap pages and install an allocator.
- Hosted assumption: Threads or an executor exist. Kernel replacement: Implement scheduling and wake-up policy.
- Hosted assumption: Exiting returns to a parent process. Kernel replacement: Halt, reset, or report through a machine interface.

This is the same reasoning needed for a language runtime. A V M cannot use garbage
collection, files, exceptions, or native calls until its host has established those
services.
