### C P U Tables Turn Events Into Controlled Transfers

An exception or interrupt is not an ordinary call chosen by the current function. The
processor observes an event, saves enough execution state, changes privilege when
required, and transfers control through an architecture-defined table.

- Event kind: Fault. Cause: Current instruction cannot complete normally. Typical use: Page mapping, access violation.
- Event kind: Trap. Cause: Deliberate synchronous event. Typical use: Breakpoint, system-call entry.
- Event kind: Interrupt. Cause: External or timer event. Typical use: Keyboard, network device, preemption.
- Event kind: Double fault. Cause: Failure while delivering another exception. Typical use: Emergency failure path.

The critical invariant is:

Note. A handler may only return if it has repaired or intentionally accounted for the
state that caused the transfer.

For example, a demand-page handler may install a valid mapping and retry the instruction.
An invalid user pointer should instead terminate or notify the process. Blindly
returning repeats the same fault.

### Paging Separates Names From Storage

Phil Opp's paging implementation makes a general abstraction visible:

The three are not interchangeable. A virtual page can be unmapped, remapped, shared,
read-only, executable, or backed by a file. This indirection enables isolation because
two processes can use the same numeric virtual address without naming the same frame.

The newtypes do not merely improve readability. They prevent accidental arithmetic
between different address spaces. A production kernel also requires alignment checks,
reserved-bit validation, architecture-specific flags, T L B invalidation, and
synchronization with other cores.

Note. Compiler connection: a compiler also separates names from locations. A source
variable, an S S A value, a stack slot, and a machine register can refer to one logical
value at different stages, but they are not the same representation.

### An Allocator Is a Policy Over a Finite Resource

Mapping a heap region answers where allocation may occur. The allocator answers
which free range to choose, how to record it, and how to reuse it.

- Allocator: Bump. State: One next-free offset. Strength: Tiny and fast. Limitation: Individual deallocation is not reclaimed.
- Allocator: Free list. State: Linked free ranges. Strength: Reuses variable-sized blocks. Limitation: Search cost and fragmentation.
- Allocator: Fixed-size classes. State: Free list per size class. Strength: Predictable fast paths. Limitation: Internal waste for size mismatch.
- Allocator: Buddy. State: Power-of-two split/merge tree. Strength: Efficient coalescing. Limitation: Rounding wastes some space.

Every allocator preserves at least these invariants:

- Invariant: Live allocations do not overlap. Failure if violated: Two owners mutate the same bytes.
- Invariant: Returned address satisfies alignment. Failure if violated: Invalid or slow machine access.
- Invariant: Free space and allocated space cover only the managed region. Failure if violated: Memory corruption.
- Invariant: A block is freed at most once. Failure if violated: Free-list corruption and later aliasing.
- Invariant: Metadata remains reachable and consistent. Failure if violated: Leaks or arbitrary allocation results.

Rust can make the safe A P I pleasant, but the allocator implementation is an unsafe proof
boundary because raw addresses and uninitialized storage precede ordinary references.

### A Future Is a Suspended State Machine

The kernel's small async executor is a particularly clear model of cooperative
scheduling. An async function is transformed into a value that stores:

- Stored state: Current suspension point. Why it exists: Determines which part resumes.
- Stored state: Locals live across await. Why it exists: They must survive after the call stack unwinds.
- Stored state: Child future state. Why it exists: The parent cannot complete before dependencies do.
- Stored state: Completion value. Why it exists: Returned once, after the final state.

At the conceptual boundary, an executor repeatedly polls tasks:

Real Rust uses Future poll, Pin, Context, and Waker. A pending future registers
how it should be awakened; otherwise the executor would have to waste C P U repeatedly
polling everything. Pin matters because the compiler-generated future may contain
references into its own stored state, so moving it after polling could invalidate those
relationships.

This reveals a broad principle:

Note. Async does not make work happen by itself. It turns progress into an explicit state
machine and relies on an executor plus an event source to schedule transitions.

The same model appears in G U I event loops, network reactors, actors, browser tasks, and
language coroutines.

### What to Observe While Reading a Tiny Kernel

- Question: Which machine state exists at the entry point?. Concept recovered: Boot A B I.
- Question: Which module contains raw register access?. Concept recovered: Unsafe boundary.
- Question: Which type distinguishes virtual from physical addresses?. Concept recovered: Representation safety.
- Question: What event causes the handler to run?. Concept recovered: Control transfer.
- Question: What state is saved across suspension?. Concept recovered: Continuation/state machine.
- Question: Which allocation strategy chooses a free block?. Concept recovered: Resource policy.
- Question: What happens when a finite table or heap is full?. Concept recovered: Failure semantics.
- Question: Which output path works before drivers exist?. Concept recovered: Minimal observability.

This is also a reversing checklist. When looking at an unfamiliar kernel, firmware
image, hypervisor, or runtime, recover its entry state, tables, ownership boundaries,
event sources, and finite-resource policies before naming individual functions.

## 1. The Kernel as a Privileged Resource Manager

An application cannot safely control the processor, physical memory, disks, and network
devices directly. The kernel owns those shared resources and exposes controlled
operations to user programs.

The boundary changes both privilege and trust:

- Phase: Request. Responsibility: User code supplies an operation and arguments.
- Phase: Transition. Responsibility: The processor enters privileged mode.
- Phase: Validation. Responsibility: Kernel checks pointers, lengths, permissions, handles.
- Phase: Execution. Responsibility: Kernel performs or rejects the operation.
- Phase: Return. Responsibility: Result/error is copied back to user mode.

The exact instruction used to enter the kernel depends on the architecture. Older
systems often used a general software trap; modern processors usually provide a faster
system-call instruction. User code normally reaches it through a standard library
wrapper rather than hand-written assembly.

### System Calls Versus Library Calls

- Kind: Library call. Where it runs: Current process. What it does: Ordinary computation, formatting, buffering.
- Kind: System call. Where it runs: Kernel entry. What it does: Requests a privileged operation.
- Kind: Library wrapper. Where it runs: User + kernel paths. What it does: May make zero, one, or several system calls.

For example, buffered output may append bytes to a user-space buffer many times and
perform a write system call only when the buffer is full or flushed.

This distinction explains why source-level operations do not map one-to-one to kernel
operations.

### A System Call Is Also an A B I Contract

At the lowest boundary, the operation number, argument registers, pointer widths, return
convention, and error encoding are architecture/OS-specific.

- Argument kind: integer/flags. Kernel-side question: Are unused bits clear and combinations valid?
- Argument kind: file descriptor. Kernel-side question: Does it name a live object with required permissions?
- Argument kind: user pointer. Kernel-side question: Is the full range mapped with the required access?
- Argument kind: pointer + length. Kernel-side question: Does arithmetic overflow or cross an invalid mapping?
- Argument kind: structure. Kernel-side question: Which A B I version and exact layout does the caller use?
- Argument kind: output buffer. Kernel-side question: How many bytes may be written back safely?

The kernel cannot trust a pointer merely because it is numerically inside user space.
Another thread may race with mutable memory, mappings can change, and a range may cross
page boundaries. Kernel interfaces therefore copy, pin, validate, or otherwise control
how user memory is accessed.

Note. Reversing connection: a syscall instruction is only the bottom edge. Recover
the wrapper's A B I, argument construction, error translation, and higher-level resource
lifetime before assigning source-level meaning.

## 2. What a Process Contains

A program is an executable representation stored somewhere. A process is a
running instance plus all state required to stop and resume it.

- Process component: Process ID. What it represents: Kernel-visible identity.
- Process component: Virtual address space. What it represents: Code, data, heap, stack, mappings.
- Process component: Register state. What it represents: Instruction pointer, stack pointer, general registers.
- Process component: File-descriptor table. What it represents: Open files, pipes, sockets, devices.
- Process component: Credentials. What it represents: User/group identity and permissions.
- Process component: Signal state. What it represents: Masks, pending signals, handlers.
- Process component: Scheduling state. What it represents: Running, runnable, sleeping, stopped.
- Process component: Accounting data. What it represents: C P U time, memory use, input and output statistics.

The kernel does not need to leave every process's register values in the C P U. During a
context switch it saves the outgoing process's state to memory and restores the incoming
process's saved state.

### Common Process States

Names vary by operating system, but the distinction is important:

- Process state: Running. Meaning: Currently executing on a C P U.
- Process state: Runnable. Meaning: Able to execute but waiting for C P U time.
- Process state: Sleeping/blocked. Meaning: Waiting for an event, timer, lock, or input and output.
- Process state: Stopped. Meaning: Deliberately suspended.
- Process state: Terminated/zombie. Meaning: Finished; exit information may await parent collection.

## 3. File Descriptors: Small Handles to Kernel Objects

On Unix-like systems, a file descriptor is a small process-local integer that indexes a
kernel-maintained table.

- Conventional descriptor: 0. Meaning: Standard input.
- Conventional descriptor: 1. Meaning: Standard output.
- Conventional descriptor: 2. Meaning: Standard error.

The descriptor is not the file itself. It is a handle to an open kernel object with
associated access mode and current state.

This uniform interface is why many different resources support operations resembling
read, write, and close.

- Descriptor consequence: Process-local scope. Practical meaning: Inheritance/transfer requires an explicit OS mechanism.
- Descriptor consequence: Reference semantics. Practical meaning: close releases one reference, not always the object.
- Descriptor consequence: Aliasing. Practical meaning: Multiple descriptors can share one open-file state.
- Descriptor consequence: Number reuse. Practical meaning: A closed descriptor value may later name something else.
- Descriptor consequence: No global identity. Practical meaning: The integer is meaningful only with its process context.

## 4. fork, exec, and Process Creation

Unix process creation is often explained as two separate operations:

1. fork creates a child based on the calling process.
2. exec replaces the current process image with a new program.

Conceptually:

The child does not normally require an eager physical copy of every memory page.
Operating systems use copy-on-write (copy on write):

- Event: Immediately after fork. copy on write behavior: Parent and child map the same read-only frames.
- Event: Read. copy on write behavior: Both processes continue sharing the frame.
- Event: First write. copy on write behavior: A protection fault transfers control to the kernel.
- Event: Fault handling. copy on write behavior: Kernel copies the page and grants private writable access.

This makes fork much cheaper when the child quickly calls exec.

exec does not create a second process ID. It replaces the current address space,
register setup, and executable image while preserving selected process state such as the
process identity and descriptors that are not marked close-on-exec.

## 5. Context Switching and Scheduling

A context switch changes which thread or process owns a C P U:

1. enter the kernel because of a timer, interrupt, system call, or blocking event;
2. save the current execution context;
3. update the current process's state;
4. choose a runnable process;
5. restore its saved context;
6. return to user mode at its saved instruction pointer.

Context switches are necessary but not free:

- Cost source: Register save/restore. Why it costs time: Execution context moves between C P U and memory.
- Cost source: Scheduler work. Why it costs time: Kernel chooses and accounts for the next task.
- Cost source: Cache disruption. Why it costs time: New code/data displaces useful cache lines.
- Cost source: T L B effects. Why it costs time: Address translations may need refilling.
- Cost source: Branch prediction. Why it costs time: Predictor history may no longer match.
- Cost source: Core migration. Why it costs time: Warm state may not exist on the destination C P U.

Preemptive scheduling lets the kernel interrupt a running task. Cooperative
scheduling relies on tasks yielding voluntarily. General-purpose operating systems are
normally preemptive; async runtimes often use cooperative tasks inside a process.

## 6. Signals and Asynchronous Events

A signal is a small asynchronous notification delivered to a process or thread:

- Signal category: Control request. Example meaning: Terminate, stop, or continue.
- Signal category: Memory fault. Example meaning: Invalid address or protection violation.
- Signal category: Arithmetic fault. Example meaning: Invalid numeric operation.
- Signal category: Child notification. Example meaning: A child stopped, continued, or exited.
- Signal category: Timer. Example meaning: Alarm or scheduled notification.
- Signal category: Application event. Example meaning: User-defined signal.

Signal handlers run in an unusual context. Ordinary application invariants may be
temporarily broken, so only operations documented as safe in a signal handler should be
performed there.

- Runtime interaction: Stack overflow. Signal-related use: Detect guard-page faults.
- Runtime interaction: Memory management. Signal-related use: Handle deliberately fault-driven techniques.
- Runtime interaction: Profiling. Signal-related use: Trigger periodic sampling.
- Runtime interaction: Subprocess supervision. Signal-related use: Observe child state changes.
- Runtime interaction: Graceful shutdown. Signal-related use: Request orderly termination.
- Runtime interaction: Debugging. Signal-related use: Implement traps and breakpoints.

Do not use a signal handler as if it were a normal callback.

## 7. Virtual Memory Is Address Translation

Virtual memory is often described as disk pretending to be RAM. That describes swap,
which is only one possible consequence. The central idea is that programs use virtual
addresses, and the operating system plus hardware translate them to physical memory.

- Virtual-memory term: Address space. Meaning: Addresses a process can potentially name.
- Virtual-memory term: Virtual page. Meaning: Fixed-size block in a virtual address space.
- Virtual-memory term: Physical frame. Meaning: Fixed-size block in physical memory.
- Virtual-memory term: Page table. Meaning: Mapping and permissions from pages to frames.
- Virtual-memory term: Mapping. Meaning: Association with memory, a file, or another kernel object.

Two processes can use the same virtual address while mapping it to different physical
frames. Conversely, two virtual pages can intentionally map the same physical frame for
shared memory.

## 8. Page Tables and Page Permissions

If the page size is 2^n, the low n address bits are the offset inside a page and
the remaining bits identify the virtual page.

For a 4 KiB page:

Translation changes the page number but preserves the offset:

A page-table entry commonly includes:

- PTE field category: Presence. What it controls: Whether a valid mapping currently exists.
- PTE field category: Permissions. What it controls: Read, write, and execute access.
- PTE field category: Privilege. What it controls: User-accessible or kernel-only.
- PTE field category: Accessed bit. What it controls: Whether the page has been referenced.
- PTE field category: Dirty bit. What it controls: Whether the page has been modified.
- PTE field category: Cache policy. What it controls: How accesses interact with C P U caches.
- PTE field category: Architecture bits. What it controls: Target-specific translation controls.

These permissions provide isolation and enable mitigations such as non-executable data
pages.

## 9. Multi-Level Page Tables

A flat page table for every possible virtual page would waste large amounts of memory,
especially for sparse address spaces. Multi-level page tables divide the virtual page
number into indexes:

Intermediate tables only need to exist for populated parts of the address space.

This resembles a radix tree: each group of address bits selects the next table. Exact
levels and bit divisions are architecture-specific.

## 10. The T L B

Walking page tables for every memory access would be slow. The processor therefore uses
a Translation Lookaside Buffer (T L B), a small cache of recent virtual-to-physical
translations.

A context switch may require translations to be invalidated, tagged by address-space
identity, or otherwise managed so one process cannot use another process's mappings.

Performance implications:

- poor locality can increase both data-cache and T L B misses;
- large pages cover more memory per T L B entry but reduce allocation flexibility;
- changing mappings requires synchronization with CPUs that may cache the old
translation;
- a compiler's object layout and traversal order can affect page and cache locality.

## 11. Page Faults

A page fault means an address translation or permission check could not complete
normally. It is not automatically a bug.

- Fault cause: Valid file-backed page not loaded. Possible kernel response: Read or map the page, then retry.
- Fault cause: Copy-on-write write. Possible kernel response: Allocate/copy a private frame, then retry.
- Fault cause: Stack growth within allowed range. Possible kernel response: Map another stack page.
- Fault cause: Swapped-out page. Possible kernel response: Restore it from backing storage.
- Fault cause: Write to read-only mapping. Possible kernel response: Deliver an access violation.
- Fault cause: Unmapped address. Possible kernel response: Deliver an access violation.

This distinction matters when debugging: the hardware mechanism is a page fault in all
cases, but the operating system may resolve some transparently and report others to the
process.

## 12. mmap, Shared Memory, and the Page Cache

A memory mapping connects virtual addresses to a backing object.

- Anonymous mapping: zero-initialized memory not backed by a normal file.
- Private file mapping: file content is visible, but writes become private through
copy-on-write.
- Shared file mapping: writes may become visible through the shared mapping and
eventually reach the file.
- Shared anonymous mapping: processes intentionally share frames.

The page cache lets the kernel cache file contents in memory. Ordinary file input and output and
memory-mapped input and output may ultimately interact with the same cached pages.

Note. Page-cache mental model: file input and output and mapped memory can meet in the kernel's
cached view of file contents.

This helps explain why:

- a recently read file may be fast to read again;
- writing does not always mean bytes reached durable storage immediately;
- mapped file accesses can fault pages in lazily;
- executable code and shared libraries can be backed by cached file pages.

## Filesystems: Turning Blocks Into Durable Names

Writing a File System from Scratch in Rust
is a compact demonstration of how layers of metadata turn an unstructured block device
into files and directories.

Note. Filesystem mental model: storage supplies numbered blocks. A filesystem adds
allocation, object identity, names, byte ranges, permissions, and recovery rules.

### A Block Device Does Not Know About Files

At the bottom, a storage interface resembles:

It does not inherently understand /home/notes.md, ownership, directories, or the end
of a file. Those are interpretations imposed by filesystem metadata.

- Layer: Block device. Question it answers: Which fixed-size block should be transferred?
- Layer: Allocation map. Question it answers: Which blocks and object records are free?
- Layer: Inode/object record. Question it answers: Which metadata and data blocks belong to this object?
- Layer: Directory. Question it answers: Which name maps to which object identifier?
- Layer: Path resolver. Question it answers: How is a sequence such as a/b/c traversed?
- Layer: File A P I. Question it answers: Which byte range should a process read or write?
- Layer: Cache and journal. Question it answers: When is the change visible and when is it durable?

This decomposition is important when reversing an unknown disk image: a recognizable
filename is high-level evidence, while the underlying allocation and block pointers tell
you how the bytes are actually reached.

### One Possible On-Disk Layout

The teaching filesystem in the article uses the classic idea of a superblock, bitmaps,
an inode table, and data blocks:

- Region: Superblock. Persistent meaning: Version, block size, counts, and locations of other regions.
- Region: Inode bitmap. Persistent meaning: One bit per object record: free or allocated.
- Region: Block bitmap. Persistent meaning: One bit per data block: free or allocated.
- Region: Inode table. Persistent meaning: Stable object metadata and block references.
- Region: Data blocks. Persistent meaning: File bytes or serialized directory entries.

The superblock is a decoder key. If its version, block size, or region offsets are
misread, every later byte can look plausible while meaning the wrong thing.

The structure used in memory need not be identical to the structure on disk. Production
parsers normally decode fields explicitly so endianness, versioning, padding, and
validation are controlled rather than inherited from a compiler's struct layout.

### Bitmaps Are Compact Sets

A bitmap represents a finite set using one bit per possible member:

For block i:

This is a direct use of quotient and modular arithmetic. Allocation searches for a zero
bit, changes it to one, and persists the result. Freeing does the reverse.

The operation is simple in memory but becomes a transaction on disk. A crash between
marking a block allocated and connecting it to an inode can leak the block. Connecting
it first can make two objects believe they own it after a crash.

### Inodes Separate Object Identity From Names

An inode-like record stores an object's metadata and data-block references. A directory
stores name leads to inode number mappings.

Consequences:

- Consequence: Multiple names can reach one object. Explanation: Hard links store the same inode identifier.
- Consequence: Renaming need not move file bytes. Explanation: A directory mapping changes.
- Consequence: Deleting a name need not delete the object immediately. Explanation: Other links or open handles may remain.
- Consequence: An open file can outlive its path. Explanation: The kernel holds a reference to the object.
- Consequence: A directory is structured data. Explanation: Its contents are mappings, not ordinary prose.

This is another identity-versus-location lesson. A path is a lookup expression, not the
file's permanent identity.

### File Offsets Map Through Several Levels

For a fixed block size:

Small files can keep direct data-block numbers in the inode. Larger files need indirect
blocks that store more block numbers:

- Scheme: Direct. Lookup depth: 1. Capacity effect: A small fixed number of blocks.
- Scheme: Single indirect. Lookup depth: 2. Capacity effect: Pointer count per block.
- Scheme: Double indirect. Lookup depth: 3. Capacity effect: Pointer count squared.
- Scheme: Extents. Lookup depth: Usually shallow. Capacity effect: One record describes a contiguous block range.

The comments identify the invariant at each step: logical bounds, checked conversion,
lookup depth, and physical block selection. Those are exactly the places where a disk
parser should distrust corrupted input.

### Path Resolution Is Repeated Graph Traversal

Resolving /usr/bin/tool means:

1. start at the root directory object;
2. look up usr and verify that the result is a directory;
3. look up bin in that directory;
4. look up tool;
5. apply permissions, link-following policy, and mount transitions along the way.

Symbolic links introduce another path expression. Mount points can replace the
filesystem used for a subtree.. and.. affect traversal. Therefore a safe resolver
needs limits on link depth, path component count, and total expansion.

Note. Security connection: checking a path and opening it later can race with
renames or link changes. Handle-relative APIs and kernel-enforced traversal policies
reduce this time-of-check/time-of-use gap.

### Visibility Is Not Durability

A successful write can mean several different things:

- Event: write accepted bytes. What it normally proves: Kernel owns a copy or mapping of the change.
- Event: Buffered writer flushed. What it normally proves: Bytes reached the operating-system interface.
- Event: Filesystem synchronization completed. What it normally proves: Filesystem requested durable ordering.
- Event: Device reports completion. What it normally proves: Meaning depends on device/cache guarantees.

Atomicity, consistency, isolation, and durability are separate properties. Renaming a
prepared temporary file is a common publication pattern, but crash safety still depends
on syncing the file data and the containing directory in the required order.

### Crash Consistency Is an Ordering Problem

Suppose growing a file requires:

1. reserve a free data block;
2. write new data;
3. attach the block to the inode;
4. update the inode length.

A crash can occur between any two steps. Filesystem designs constrain the possible
post-crash states:

- Technique: Write ordering. Core model: Persist prerequisites before pointers that expose them.
- Technique: Journal / write-ahead log. Core model: Record intended metadata transaction, then apply it.
- Technique: Copy-on-write tree. Core model: Write new nodes, then atomically publish a new root.
- Technique: Checksums. Core model: Detect some torn or corrupted records.
- Technique: Recovery scan. Core model: Reconstruct or repair invariants after an unclean shutdown.

The governing invariant is that every reachable pointer should name initialized,
compatible storage, and each allocated resource should have an intended owner.

This resembles a garbage-collected runtime:

- Filesystem: Inode or tree root. Managed-language runtime: G C root.
- Filesystem: Block pointer. Managed-language runtime: Object reference.
- Filesystem: Reachable block. Managed-language runtime: Live object.
- Filesystem: Unreferenced allocated block. Managed-language runtime: Leak.
- Filesystem: Two mutable owners of one block. Managed-language runtime: Aliasing/corruption.
- Filesystem: Recovery checker. Managed-language runtime: Heap verifier.

### Filesystems in Language Design and Reversing

- Task: Module loader. Filesystem concept that matters: Paths are fallible lookups with encoding and permission rules.
- Task: Compiler cache. Filesystem concept that matters: Content identity, invalidation, atomic publication, durability.
- Task: Package manager. Filesystem concept that matters: Archive traversal, signatures, checksums, rollback.
- Task: V M snapshot. Filesystem concept that matters: Stable serialization, versioning, partial-write recovery.
- Task: Binary reversing. Filesystem concept that matters: Executables may be sparse, mapped, compressed, or deleted-but-open.
- Task: Disk-image reversing. Filesystem concept that matters: Recover superblock, allocation maps, records, then names.

When designing your own language, avoid treating read file(path) as an indivisible
primitive. Decide whether the operation follows links, which encoding paths use, how
large a file may be, whether reads are streaming, how errors are represented, and
whether the runtime exposes synchronous or asynchronous input and output.

When reversing a filesystem, work bottom-up:

Each recovered layer gives constraints that validate the next one. A parser that jumps
straight to filenames has skipped the evidence needed to distinguish a real structure
from coincidental bytes.

## 13. From exec to main

main is usually not the first instruction executed.

The initial stack can contain arguments, environment entries, and an auxiliary
vector with information supplied by the kernel. The runtime startup code uses this
state to initialize the process before calling main.

- Runtime startup responsibility: Heap/G C initialization. Purpose: Prepare managed allocation.
- Runtime startup responsibility: Built-in registration. Purpose: Install core types and native functions.
- Runtime startup responsibility: Thread-local state. Purpose: Establish per-thread runtime context.
- Runtime startup responsibility: Arguments/environment. Purpose: Convert OS input into language values.
- Runtime startup responsibility: Standard streams. Purpose: Connect language input and output to OS handles.
- Runtime startup responsibility: Panic/exception handling. Purpose: Define top-level failure behavior.
- Runtime startup responsibility: Module constructors. Purpose: Initialize global/module state.
- Runtime startup responsibility: Language entry point. Purpose: Call user main or equivalent.
- Runtime startup responsibility: Exit conversion. Purpose: Map the language result to an OS status.

## 14. Bottom-Up Debugging Questions

When a program behaves unexpectedly, walk downward through the abstractions:

1. What source-level operation failed?
2. Which library or runtime function implements it?
3. Did it make a system call?
4. What arguments crossed the kernel boundary?
5. Which descriptor, mapping, or process state did the kernel use?
6. Was the process running, runnable, or blocked?
7. Did an address translation, permission check, or page fault occur?
8. Which executable segment, symbol, or relocation produced the address?

Then walk back upward and explain how the low-level event created the source-level
symptom.

# Executables, Linkers, ABIs, and F F I

Compiling instructions is only part of producing a usable program. The result must also
describe how code and data are arranged, which external names it needs, where it may be
loaded, and how functions exchange values.

Note. Toolchain handoff: the compiler emits code and metadata, the linker resolves
relationships, and the loader creates the running image.

## 1. Source Files, Object Files, and Executables

An object file usually contains:

- Object-file component: Code/static data. Purpose: Instructions and compile-time data.
- Object-file component: Symbol table. Purpose: Defined and unresolved names.
- Object-file component: Relocations. Purpose: Places whose addresses need later repair.
- Object-file component: Debug information. Purpose: Maps instructions and values back to source.
- Object-file component: Target metadata. Purpose: Identifies architecture, A B I, and file format.

The linker combines object files, resolves symbols, lays out sections, applies
relocations, and emits the final executable or library.

## 2. Common Sections

Exact names differ among ELF, PE/COFF, Mach-O, and individual toolchains, but these
categories are common:

- Section: .text. Typical contents: Machine instructions. Usual permissions: Read + execute.
- Section: .rodata. Typical contents: String literals and immutable constants. Usual permissions: Read.
- Section: .data. Typical contents: Initialized writable globals. Usual permissions: Read + write.
- Section: .bss. Typical contents: Zero-initialized globals. Usual permissions: Read + write.
- Section: Symbol/string tables. Typical contents: Names used by linking or debugging. Usual permissions: Read.
- Section: Relocations. Typical contents: Places whose addresses need adjustment. Usual permissions: Loader/linker data.
- Section: Debug sections. Typical contents: Source lines, types, local names. Usual permissions: Not needed to execute.

.bss does not need to store a file full of zero bytes. The executable records the
required size, and the loader supplies zero-initialized memory.

## 3. Symbols and Name Resolution

A symbol associates a name with a function, object, section, or address.

- Symbol kind: Defined. Linker meaning: Provided by the current object/library.
- Symbol kind: Undefined. Linker meaning: Required from another object/library.
- Symbol kind: Local. Linker meaning: Private to one object or translation unit.
- Symbol kind: Global. Linker meaning: Participates in cross-object resolution.
- Symbol kind: Weak. Linker meaning: May be replaced by a stronger definition.

Languages that support overloading or namespaces often use name mangling to encode
more information into a linker-visible name:

An A B I should not rely on an unstable language-specific mangling scheme unless both
sides use compatible toolchains.

## 4. Relocations and Position Independence

An object file often cannot know final addresses. Instead it contains a placeholder plus
a relocation such as:

The linker or loader computes the final value after section placement.

Position-independent code (PIC) avoids assuming one fixed load address. Shared
libraries normally need PIC, and position-independent executables allow the operating
system to randomize the program's base address.

- Coordinate: File offset. Meaning: Position inside the executable file.
- Coordinate: Virtual address (VA). Meaning: Address where bytes appear in a process.
- Coordinate: Relative virtual address. Meaning: Offset from an image or module base.

Never mix these three without an explicit conversion.

## 5. Static and Dynamic Linking

- Model: Static linking. What is included: Required library code is copied into the executable. Main trade-off: Larger files, fewer runtime dependencies.
- Model: Dynamic linking. What is included: Executable refers to libraries loaded at runtime. Main trade-off: Smaller files, version and deployment concerns.

Dynamic linking involves at least three parties:

1. the compiler emits calls and symbol references;
2. the linker records dependencies and creates the required tables;
3. the loader maps libraries and resolves runtime addresses.

This is why "it compiled" does not guarantee "it launches." A binary may still have a
missing library, incompatible A B I, unsupported architecture, or loader configuration
problem.

## 6. Calling Conventions

A calling convention answers:

- A B I question: Arguments. What must be specified: Registers/stack locations by type and position.
- A B I question: Return value. What must be specified: Register, memory, or hidden return pointer.
- A B I question: Caller-preserved. What must be specified: Registers the caller must save if needed.
- A B I question: Callee-preserved. What must be specified: Registers the callee must restore if used.
- A B I question: Stack cleanup. What must be specified: Which side removes argument storage.
- A B I question: Stack alignment. What must be specified: Required alignment at call boundaries.
- A B I question: Variadic arguments. What must be specified: Encoding and retrieval rules.

Conceptual call sequence:

Compiler backends must implement the target convention exactly. A mismatch can look like
random register corruption even when both functions are individually correct.

## 7. A B I Versus A P I

- An A P I describes source-level usage: functions, types, and expected behavior.
- An A B I describes binary-level compatibility: data layout, symbol naming, calling
convention, alignment, and register rules.

Two libraries may have the same conceptual A P I but incompatible ABIs. A B I stability
matters for shared libraries, plugins, operating-system interfaces, and foreign-function
interfaces.

## 8. Rust F F I Boundary

Use an explicit C A B I and C-compatible types when exposing a simple function:

The unsafe operation is small, and its required condition is obvious: the incoming
pointer must either be null or point to a valid writable i32.

- F F I concern: Struct layout. Required practice: Use number sign [repr(C)] for C-shared records.
- F F I concern: Integer widths. Required practice: Use fixed-width types where layout matters.
- F F I concern: Allocation. Required practice: Define who allocates and who frees.
- F F I concern: Unwinding. Required practice: Do not let Rust panics cross a foreign A B I.
- F F I concern: Rust-native types. Required practice: Do not expose references, String, Vec, or trait objects.
- F F I concern: Pointer contract. Required practice: Document nullability, alignment, lifetime, and thread safety.
- F F I concern: Versioning. Required practice: Evolve exported interfaces deliberately.

## 9. ELF Has a Linking View and an Execution View

ELF deliberately describes the same file in two ways:

- View: Linking view. Main structure: Sections and section headers. Consumer: Compiler, linker, debugger. Purpose: Organize code, data, symbols, relocations.
- View: Execution view. Main structure: Segments and program headers. Consumer: Kernel and dynamic loader. Purpose: Map byte ranges with runtime permissions.

One or more sections can contribute to one loadable segment.

This is why "section" and "segment" are related but not interchangeable.

- Program-header field: p type. Meaning: Loadable segment, interpreter, dynamic data, etc..
- Program-header field: p offset. Meaning: Byte offset in the file.
- Program-header field: p vaddr. Meaning: Virtual address after loading.
- Program-header field: p filesz. Meaning: Bytes present in the file.
- Program-header field: p memsz. Meaning: Bytes required in memory.
- Program-header field: p flags. Meaning: Read/write/execute permissions.
- Program-header field: p align. Meaning: Required file/memory alignment relationship.

When p memsz is larger than p filesz, the loader supplies zero-filled memory for the
difference. This is how zero-initialized data can occupy memory without occupying the
same number of bytes in the file.

## 10. ELF Entry Point and Program Interpreter

An ELF header includes an entry-point virtual address. A program header of type
PT INTERP can name the program interpreter, normally the dynamic linker for a
dynamically linked executable.

Conceptually:

The executable is still native machine code. Here, "interpreter" means a loader that
finishes preparing the native image, not a source-language interpreter.

## 11. Dynamic Symbols, Relocations, GOT, and PLT

A shared library can be loaded at different addresses in different processes, so some
addresses cannot be finalized until runtime.

- .dynsym contains symbols needed for dynamic linking.
- .dynstr contains names referenced by dynamic symbol entries.
- dynamic relocation sections describe runtime fixups.
- the Global Offset Table (GOT) holds runtime-resolved addresses used by
position-independent code.
- on architectures/toolchains that use it, the Procedure Linkage Table (PLT)
provides call stubs that lead through resolver-managed slots.

Simplified lazy-resolution path:

Systems may instead resolve symbols eagerly at startup. Details vary by architecture,
A B I, linker, and hardening settings.

### Safe Inspection Commands

For a trusted local ELF file:

Prefer parsers such as readelf and objdump for initial inspection of untrusted
files. Do not execute an unknown binary merely to discover its dependencies.

# Embedded Systems: From Reset Vector to Peripheral input and output

Comprehensive Rust includes
specialized material on bare-metal development, unsafe Rust, and using Rust inside
Chromium. Together they show three versions of the same lesson: make environmental and
safety assumptions explicit at boundaries.

Note. Low-level rule: when the operating system or type system cannot enforce an
assumption, write the assumption down as a contract.

## 1. Hosted Versus Bare-Metal Programs

A hosted program starts inside an operating-system process. The OS and standard library
provide files, sockets, threads, clocks, allocation, arguments, and process exit.

A bare-metal program may start directly at a reset vector with no OS, loader, filesystem,
allocator, or console.

Rust library layers:

- Layer: core. Provides: Language fundamentals, slices, iterators, Option, Result, atomics.
- Layer: alloc. Provides: Box, Vec, String, Arc, and other allocation-backed collections.
- Layer: std. Provides: OS-backed files, networking, threads, clocks, process APIs, and more.

number sign![no std] removes the dependency on std; it does not remove core. The alloc
crate is optional and requires a global allocator supplied by the platform.

## 2. Minimal no std Shape

The exact entry point and linker setup are target-specific, but the source commonly has
this shape:

This is not a complete bootable image:

- Target requirement: Linker layout. Responsibility: Place code, data, stack, and device regions.
- Target requirement: Entry contract. Responsibility: Use the correct reset symbol and calling convention.
- Target requirement: Stack. Responsibility: Establish a valid initial stack.
- Target requirement: Static memory. Responsibility: Copy initialized data and zero.bss.
- Target requirement: Panic strategy. Responsibility: Define terminal failure behavior.
- Target requirement: Build/runner config. Responsibility: Select target, image format, and launch mechanism.
- Target requirement: Hardware initialization. Responsibility: Configure clocks, memory, pins, and peripherals.

Those responsibilities resemble a language runtime's startup sequence. Building
firmware makes the usually hidden path from executable entry point to language-level
main visible.

## 3. Hardware-Abstraction Layers

A useful embedded stack:

Keep application logic testable on the host by depending on small traits:

For real code, return a named error type or the trait's associated error. The important
design idea is that the application owns behavior while the board layer owns volatile
register details.

## 4. Memory-Mapped input and output

Peripherals often expose control and status registers at fixed addresses. Ordinary
loads and stores are insufficient because the compiler may remove or combine them.
Volatile access says the access itself is observable.

The wrapper is only honest if its constructor's contract matches the hardware manual.

- Volatile does not guarantee: Data-race safety. Separate requirement: Atomics, critical sections, or exclusive access.
- Volatile does not guarantee: Multi-register atomicity. Separate requirement: Device-specific transaction protocol.
- Volatile does not guarantee: C P U/device ordering. Separate requirement: Correct memory barriers/fences.
- Volatile does not guarantee: Side-effect-free reads. Separate requirement: Register semantics from the hardware manual.
- Volatile does not guarantee: Peripheral ownership. Separate requirement: A higher-level ownership policy.

Avoid implementing Send or Sync automatically for M M I O wrappers. Decide those
properties from the hardware sharing rules.

## 5. Interrupts and Shared State

An interrupt can occur between ordinary instructions, so code shared with an interrupt
handler is concurrent code.

- Interrupt concern: Small shared values. Preferred approach: Atomics with a justified ordering.
- Interrupt concern: Compound state. Preferred approach: Target-provided critical section.
- Interrupt concern: Data handoff. Preferred approach: Interrupt-safe SPSC queue.
- Interrupt concern: Handler duration. Preferred approach: Record minimal work and return quickly.
- Interrupt concern: Forbidden work. Preferred approach: Avoid blocking, allocation, and unbounded loops.

Acquire/release here publishes work associated with the flag, but the exact design must
match how the sample data itself is stored. volatile is not a substitute for atomics.

## 6. What unsafe Actually Permits

Unsafe Rust enables a small set of operations that the compiler cannot prove safe:

- Unsafe capability: Dereference raw pointer. Proof obligation moved to the programmer: Validity, alignment, initialization, and aliasing.
- Unsafe capability: Mutable static state. Proof obligation moved to the programmer: Synchronization and exclusive access.
- Unsafe capability: Access union field. Proof obligation moved to the programmer: Active representation is valid for the field.
- Unsafe capability: Call unsafe function. Proof obligation moved to the programmer: Every documented precondition is satisfied.
- Unsafe capability: Implement unsafe trait. Proof obligation moved to the programmer: The implementation upholds the trait-wide contract.

unsafe does not disable the borrow checker or turn Rust into C. It transfers proof
obligations from the compiler to the programmer.

A good unsafe abstraction has:

1. a documented safety contract;
2. validation performed in safe code where possible;
3. the smallest practical unsafe block;
4. a safe interface that cannot violate the invariant;
5. tests, fuzzing, and dynamic checking appropriate to its risk.

Use an explicit unsafe block even inside an unsafe fn; the function declares what the
caller must prove, while the block marks where the implementation relies on that proof.
The unsafe op in unsafe fn lint helps enforce this distinction.

## 7. Unsafe Review Questions

- Review area: Invariant. Question: What exact fact makes the operation memory safe?
- Review area: Ownership. Question: Who owns the allocation or device?
- Review area: Lifetime. Question: How long does it remain valid?
- Review area: Pointer validity. Question: Is it aligned and within one allocation?
- Review area: Initialization. Question: Are bytes valid before the typed read?
- Review area: Aliasing. Question: Can mutable access overlap?
- Review area: Concurrency. Question: Can a thread, signal, interrupt, or F F I call mutate it?
- Review area: Layout. Question: Is representation guaranteed by repr or a wire format?
- Review area: Arithmetic. Question: Can an offset/length wrap before validation?
- Review area: Unwinding. Question: Can panic leave foreign code or hardware invalid?
- Review area: Safe A P I. Question: Can any safe caller reach undefined behavior?

Comments should explain the proof, not restate the operation:

## 8. Power, Clocks, Reset, and Watchdogs Create the First State

An embedded processor does not begin at main. Power and clock circuitry first bring
the chip into a state in which instructions can run.

- Mechanism: power-on reset. Why it exists: establish known register and logic state. Failure if misunderstood: execution starts from corrupted state.
- Mechanism: brownout detector. Why it exists: reset when voltage is too low for reliable logic. Failure if misunderstood: silent data or instruction corruption.
- Mechanism: oscillator. Why it exists: generate a timing reference. Failure if misunderstood: C P U and peripherals cannot advance predictably.
- Mechanism: phase-locked loop. Why it exists: multiply/shape clock frequency. Failure if misunderstood: wrong bus and peripheral timing.
- Mechanism: reset controller. Why it exists: reset selected chip domains. Failure if misunderstood: stale peripheral state survives unexpectedly.
- Mechanism: watchdog. Why it exists: reset if software stops making progress. Failure if misunderstood: permanent hang without recovery.

Clock configuration is a dependency graph. A peripheral clock is often derived from a
system clock through dividers and gates. Changing one frequency affects timers, serial
baud rates, flash wait states, power use, and real-time deadlines.

Note. Invariant: code may use a peripheral only after its power domain, clock,
reset state, pin routing, and configuration satisfy that peripheral's hardware
contract.

## 9. Embedded Scheduling Is About Deadlines and Bounded Work

Without a desktop operating system, the program still needs a scheduling policy.
Common models are:

- Model: superloop. How work runs: poll each subsystem repeatedly. Trade-off: simple, but one slow step delays everything.
- Model: interrupt-driven. How work runs: hardware preempts ordinary code. Trade-off: responsive, but shared-state reasoning is harder.
- Model: cooperative executor. How work runs: tasks yield at explicit wait points. Trade-off: compact and predictable if no task blocks.
- Model: preemptive R T O S. How work runs: scheduler switches ready tasks by policy. Trade-off: isolation and priorities with more overhead.

Real-time does not mean “fast.” It means the response is useful only if it occurs before
a deadline. Important quantities include:

- latency — delay before work begins;
- execution time — C P U time the work consumes;
- period — how often periodic work is released;
- deadline — latest acceptable completion time;
- jitter — variation in timing;
- worst-case execution time — a defensible upper bound.

Interrupt handlers should acknowledge the device, capture minimal state, and schedule
bounded follow-up work. Long parsing, allocation, logging, and blocking input and output inside an
interrupt increase latency for every lower-priority event.

## 10. G P I O, you art, S P I, I squared C, and CAN Are Different Communication Models

An electrical connection, a signaling convention, and a software protocol are separate
layers. A driver must satisfy all three.

- Interface: G P I O. Clocking and topology: individual digital pins. Data model: sampled or driven bits. Common use: buttons, LEDs, chip selects.
- Interface: you art. Clocking and topology: asynchronous point-to-point. Data model: framed byte stream. Common use: console, modules, debugging.
- Interface: S P I. Clocking and topology: controller-supplied clock, separate select lines. Data model: full-duplex shifted words. Common use: displays, flash, sensors.
- Interface: I squared C. Clocking and topology: shared clock/data with addressed devices. Data model: half-duplex transactions. Common use: configuration and sensors.
- Interface: CAN. Clocking and topology: shared differential bus with arbitration. Data model: prioritized frames. Common use: vehicles and industrial control.

you art agrees on baud rate and frame shape but carries no address or packet length by
itself. S P I shifts data in both directions on each clock and usually needs a
chip-specific command protocol. I squared C includes addresses and acknowledgements but
requires pull-up resistors and bus arbitration. CAN arbitrates by message identifier
so a numerically dominant priority can continue without corrupting the bus.

The trait is not the hardware. It describes the smallest protocol capability the sensor
driver needs. A HAL implementation translates that capability into register accesses,
D M A requests, interrupts, and pin transitions for one target.

Primary concept references: Embedded Rust memory-mapped registers,
Embedonomicon memory layout,
and Arm C M S I S-Core processor and peripheral model.

## 11. Linker Scripts Are Part of the Program

On a microcontroller, the executable must place bytes where the hardware expects them.
A linker script normally defines memory regions and output sections:

- Linker-script element: MEMORY. Purpose: Names address ranges and their sizes.
- Linker-script element: ENTRY(symbol). Purpose: Selects the executable entry point.
- Linker-script element: SECTIONS. Purpose: Maps input sections into output sections.
- Linker-script element: FLASH / RAM. Purpose: Selects the destination memory region.
- Linker-script element: KEEP(...). Purpose: Prevents garbage collection of hardware-referenced data.
- Linker-script element: EXTERN(symbol). Purpose: Forces the linker to search for a required symbol.
- Linker-script element: /DISCARD/. Purpose: Removes sections the runtime deliberately does not use.

Hardware can reference a vector-table entry without a normal machine-code call. That
means the linker may see no ordinary reference; KEEP or an equivalent mechanism
prevents the required entry from being discarded.

## 12. Reset and Vector-Table Contracts

For Cortex-M, the first vector-table entries include the initial stack pointer and reset
handler. The reset handler has no caller to return to, so its return type is!.

- Attribute: number sign [unsafe(no mangle)]. Required proof: The global symbol name cannot collide.
- Attribute: number sign [unsafe(export name = "...")]. Required proof: The chosen exported name is globally unique.
- Attribute: number sign [unsafe(link section = "...")]. Required proof: The item is valid in the destination memory region.
- Attribute: extern "C". Required proof: The hardware/foreign caller follows that A B I.
- Attribute: -!. Required proof: Control never returns to a nonexistent caller.

The Rust A B I is not a stable hardware boundary. Use the architecture/runtime's required
A B I and inspect the final ELF, not merely the source.

### Reset Must Create the Language's Memory Invariants

Before ordinary Rust code can assume initialized statics, startup code commonly has to
transform the linked image into the runtime image:

- Linker symbol concept: data load start. Meaning during reset: initialized bytes stored in nonvolatile memory.
- Linker symbol concept: data run start/end. Meaning during reset: RAM range that must receive those bytes.
- Linker symbol concept: BSS start/end. Meaning during reset: RAM range that must become all zero.
- Linker symbol concept: stack boundary. Meaning during reset: initial stack location and permitted extent.
- Linker symbol concept: heap boundary. Meaning during reset: optional allocator range that must not overlap data.

The linker defines these addresses; reset code consumes them as raw pointers. The
unsafe proof must cover:

1. source and destination ranges are valid for the required access;
2. byte counts come from ordered, non-overflowing linker symbols;
3. copy regions obey the required overlap rule;
4. startup performs initialization exactly once before safe code reads the statics; and
5. no interrupt or second execution context observes partially initialized state.

Linker assertions can turn layout assumptions into build failures:

Note. Compiler connection: a source-level static X: u32 = 7 becomes bytes, section
placement, load/run addresses, and startup work. The language guarantee exists only
if every handoff preserves it.

## 13. Exception and Interrupt Vector Tables

When an exception occurs, the processor suspends the current flow, saves architectural
state according to its rules, and selects a handler through a vector table.

- Vector-table invariant: Placement. Requirement: Table begins at the architecture's expected address.
- Vector-table invariant: Entry order. Requirement: Each slot corresponds to the documented exception.
- Vector-table invariant: Validity. Requirement: Every usable slot holds a correctly typed handler address.
- Vector-table invariant: Defaults. Requirement: Unimplemented handlers enter a safe diagnostic halt.
- Vector-table invariant: Override. Requirement: Application handlers replace defaults at link time.
- Vector-table invariant: Retention. Requirement: Link-time garbage collection cannot remove the table.

Compile-time defaults can be supplied in the linker script and overridden by an
application-defined symbol. This gives static customization without a runtime lookup.

## 14. Typestate for Peripheral State Machines

The Embedded Rust Book models peripherals as state machines. Consuming a value during a
transition ensures the old state cannot still be used.

Pin Input has no set high method, so that invalid operation fails at compile time.
The marker types are zero-sized and normally disappear after optimization.

- Typestate benefit: Illegal calls disappear. Cost/trade-off: More generic types and impl blocks.
- Typestate benefit: Transitions are explicit. Cost/trade-off: State changes may consume and return values.
- Typestate benefit: No runtime tag needed. Cost/trade-off: Dynamic state may require an enum instead.
- Typestate benefit: Ownership is visible. Cost/trade-off: Shared peripherals need an intentional sharing model.

Use typestate when the state graph is small and correctness-critical. Use a runtime enum
when states genuinely arrive dynamically or must live in one heterogeneous collection.

## 15. HAL Portability

embedded-hal-style traits separate applications and device drivers from a specific
microcontroller implementation:

- Layer: Application. Owns: Product behavior.
- Layer: Driver. Owns: Device protocol, such as a sensor over S P I.
- Layer: HAL trait. Owns: Portable capability contract.
- Layer: Chip HAL. Owns: Pins, clocks, buses, D M A, and peripheral setup.
- Layer: Peripheral access crate. Owns: Register-level representation.
- Layer: Board support package. Owns: Board-specific pin/component wiring.

Generic drivers should depend on the smallest capability trait they need. Avoid leaking
one board's concrete pin or peripheral types into reusable application logic.

## 16. Embedded Concurrency Choices

Interrupt handlers are concurrent with the main loop. A static mut increment is not
automatically atomic: it may compile into load leads to modify leads to store and lose an interrupt's
update.

- Environment: No interrupts/one loop. Appropriate synchronization: Ordinary exclusive ownership.
- Environment: Main + interrupts. Appropriate synchronization: Atomics, interrupt-safe queue, or critical section.
- Environment: D M A + C P U. Appropriate synchronization: Ownership transfer plus required memory barriers.
- Environment: Multiple cores. Appropriate synchronization: SMP-safe atomics/locks; interrupt masking is not enough.
- Environment: Real-time tasks. Appropriate synchronization: Priority-aware framework and bounded operations.

Send means a value can safely move between execution contexts; Sync means a shared
reference can be used safely from multiple contexts. Treat an interrupt handler as a
separate execution context when reasoning about shared state.

Critical sections that merely disable interrupts on one core do not protect against
another core. The proof must match the hardware topology.

## 17. Inspect the Artifact, Not Only the Source

- Question: Is the entry point correct?. Useful inspection: ELF header and symbol table.
- Question: Is the vector table retained?. Useful inspection: Section dump and raw bytes.
- Question: Are code/data in valid regions?. Useful inspection: Program/section headers plus linker map.
- Question: How large is each section?. Useful inspection: size/cargo size-style report.
- Question: What does reset execute?. Useful inspection: Disassembly from the reset symbol.
- Question: Did typestate disappear?. Useful inspection: Compare optimized assembly/code size.
- Question: Does it boot without hardware?. Useful inspection: QEMU or another architecture-appropriate emulator.

Keep the linker map, target specification, disassembly, and exact firmware hash with a
release. They are part of the evidence needed to reproduce a low-level bug.

## 18. Firmware, Bootloaders, Kernels, and Drivers Form a Chain

- Layer: firmware. Primary responsibility: configure a device below the ordinary OS boundary. Typical artifact: flash image, U E F I module.
- Layer: bootloader. Primary responsibility: initialize enough hardware to locate, verify, and start a kernel. Typical artifact: U-Boot, U E F I loader.
- Layer: kernel. Primary responsibility: privileged resource management and isolation. Typical artifact: kernel image plus modules.
- Layer: driver. Primary responsibility: translate a device model into an OS interface. Typical artifact: kernel module or user-mode service.
- Layer: middleware. Primary responsibility: reusable behavior between application and platform. Typical artifact: media, RPC, database layer.
- Layer: backend. Primary responsibility: server-side policy, data, and integrations. Typical artifact: service process.
- Layer: frontend. Primary responsibility: user-facing presentation and interaction. Typical artifact: native/web UI.

These are roles, not guaranteed processes. Firmware may include an R T O S; a
microkernel may run drivers in user space; middleware may exist on the client, server,
or both.

A secure-boot chain verifies the next stage before transferring control. A signature
answers “was this approved key used over these exact bytes?” It does not prove that the
signed code is bug-free.

## 19. A Driver Is a Concurrent Translator

A driver connects two contracts:

- the OS contract: handles, files, sockets, queues, power states, and errors;
- the hardware contract: registers, interrupts, descriptors, timing, and D M A.

The toy state machine is useful even when the final implementation is unsafe: it
separates protocol validity from the tiny adapter that performs volatile reads/writes.

## 20. D M A Lets a Device Access Memory Without C P U Copies

Direct memory access changes the data path:

- Term: descriptor. Meaning: record telling a device where and how much to transfer.
- Term: coherent buffer. Meaning: C P U/device views obey a platform-defined coherence contract.
- Term: streaming mapping. Meaning: temporary mapping for a directional transfer.
- Term: I O M M U. Meaning: translates and restricts device-visible addresses.
- Term: bounce buffer. Meaning: intermediary used when addressing or isolation requires it.
- Term: completion interrupt. Meaning: device reports finished work.

An external D M A-capable device can be powerful because it may operate outside the
ordinary process/page-table path. Defensive design uses an I O M M U, minimal mapped
ranges, short mapping lifetimes, device authorization, signed firmware, and physical
security. These notes do not provide bypass or unauthorized acquisition procedures.

## 21. J tag and S W D Expose Hardware Debug State

J tag began as a boundary-scan interface and is also commonly used to reach processor
debug infrastructure. ARM's S W D provides a lower-pin-count debug transport.

Authorized debugging may expose:

- halt, resume, and single-step;
- registers and memory;
- hardware breakpoints/watchpoints;
- flash programming;
- trace and peripheral access.

That power makes lifecycle policy essential: production devices may authenticate,
limit, or permanently disable debug access. Practice with a development board you own,
document voltage and pinout first, and never assume a connector is electrically safe.

## 22. Real Hardware, Simulator, Emulator, V M, and Container

- Environment: simulator. What is reproduced: selected behavior at an abstract level. Kernel relation: implementation-specific. Best use: circuits, devices, deterministic teaching.
- Environment: emulator. What is reproduced: another machine/I S A or device behavior. Kernel relation: may run a guest kernel. Best use: compatibility and firmware labs.
- Environment: virtual machine. What is reproduced: virtualized hardware for a guest OS. Kernel relation: guest has its own kernel. Best use: OS isolation and testing.
- Environment: container. What is reproduced: isolated host processes. Kernel relation: shares host kernel. Best use: packaging and service isolation.
- Environment: sandbox. What is reproduced: restricted authority, regardless of mechanism. Kernel relation: varies. Best use: limiting untrusted components.
- Environment: real hardware. What is reproduced: actual timing, buses, analog effects. Kernel relation: actual target. Best use: final validation.

Wokwi simulates boards such as Arduino, ESP32, and STM32 and
offers virtual logic analysis, G D B debugging, networking, SD cards, and custom chips.
It is excellent for repeatable firmware experiments, but a simulator cannot fully
guarantee real electrical behavior, performance, radio conditions, or silicon errata.

## 23. Hypervisors and Hyper V

A hypervisor controls privileged execution, second-level address translation, virtual
interrupts, and device access so several guest systems can share hardware.

- Concept: V M exit. Meaning: guest operation transfers control to the hypervisor.
- Concept: guest virtual address. Meaning: address used by code inside the guest.
- Concept: guest physical address. Meaning: “physical” address visible to the guest.
- Concept: host physical address. Meaning: actual machine memory.
- Concept: S L A T / E P T / N P T. Meaning: hardware-assisted guest-physical translation.
- Concept: virtual device. Meaning: emulated or paravirtualized hardware interface.
- Concept: root/parent partition. Meaning: privileged management environment in Hyper V.
- Concept: child partition. Meaning: guest environment managed through the hypervisor.

Memory introspection from a hypervisor can observe a guest outside its ordinary OS
process model, which helps debuggers, incident response, and malware analysis. It also
creates an exceptionally privileged trust boundary. secret.club's hypervisor article
is useful for understanding V M exits and second-level translation; apply those ideas
only to owned guests and defensive laboratories.

Hyper V's architecture
uses a hypervisor plus root and child partitions, with VMBus for synthetic device
communication. Nested virtualization adds another translation and scheduling layer, so
performance measurements need to state the full nesting topology.

## 24. Containers Are Process Isolation, Not Tiny VMs

A Docker container packages a process and its filesystem view while normally sharing
the host kernel. Namespaces isolate selected resource views; cgroups account for and
limit resources; capabilities, seccomp, labels, and read-only mounts reduce authority.

- Boundary: image. Question: What exact files and configuration are supplied?
- Boundary: namespace. Question: Which processes, mounts, network interfaces, and IDs are visible?
- Boundary: capability. Question: Which privileged kernel operations remain allowed?
- Boundary: cgroup. Question: What C P U, memory, and input and output limits apply?
- Boundary: secret. Question: How is sensitive configuration supplied and rotated?
- Boundary: supply chain. Question: Are base images pinned, scanned, and reproducible?

Containers improve packaging and isolation but do not turn unsafe host-kernel exposure
into a security boundary automatically. Compare this with the Docker container model.

## 25. Platform Storage and Machine Identity

- Mechanism: filesystem. Scope: hierarchical byte streams plus metadata. Low-level lesson: names are not identities; links and races exist.
- Mechanism: Windows Registry. Scope: typed hierarchical configuration database. Low-level lesson: views, hives, ACLs, and transaction behavior matter.
- Mechanism: property list (plist). Scope: Apple structured configuration/serialization. Low-level lesson: X M L and binary encodings can represent the same values.
- Mechanism: environment variable. Scope: inherited process string map. Low-level lesson: observable, size-limited, not a secret vault.
- Mechanism: secure credential store. Scope: OS-mediated secret storage. Low-level lesson: access policy and user context matter.
- Mechanism: hardware identifier. Scope: property derived from one or more components. Low-level lesson: can change, collide, or expose privacy.

An hardware I D is not a cryptographic identity. Hardware changes, virtualization, driver
normalization, privacy controls, and collisions make it unsuitable as the sole
authentication factor. If device enrollment is required, generate a key pair in an
OS/hardware-backed keystore, register the public key, rotate it, and provide recovery.

For cross-platform tools, keep a typed configuration model in the core and place
Registry/plist/filesystem adapters at the edge. Do not collect broad fingerprints when
a random installation ID or authenticated device key meets the requirement.

Sources: Linux D M A mapping guide,
Android bootloader overview,
ARM debug access overview,
Wokwi documentation, and
Docker overview.

# Concurrency, Atomics, Actors, and Memory Models

Note. Library lens: Actix is used as one concrete actor model: owned state, typed messages, bounded mailboxes, serialized transitions, lifecycle, backpressure, and supervision.

Concurrency adds a second ordering problem. A compiler already reasons about the order
of expressions; concurrent code must also reason about what other threads are allowed to
observe.

Note. Concurrency rule: shared state is only understandable when ownership,
synchronization, and ordering are all explicit.

## 1. Processes, Threads, Tasks, and Events

- Unit: Process. Memory relationship: Usually isolated address space. Scheduled by: Operating system.
- Unit: Thread. Memory relationship: Shares process memory. Scheduled by: Operating system.
- Unit: Async task. Memory relationship: Shares process memory, cooperatively yields. Scheduled by: Async runtime.
- Unit: Actor. Memory relationship: Communicates through messages. Scheduled by: Runtime or application.

Concurrency means multiple activities make progress over overlapping time.
Parallelism means work literally executes at the same instant on different cores or
processors.

## 2. The Core Shared-State Problem

A source expression that looks like one update:

normally contains several lower-level actions:

Two threads can interleave those actions and lose an update. This is a data race
when unsynchronized conflicting accesses violate the language's memory model.

## 3. Synchronization Tools

- Tool: Mutex. Best mental model: One owner of protected state at a time.
- Tool: Read/write lock. Best mental model: Many readers or one writer.
- Tool: Condition variable. Best mental model: Sleep until protected state may have changed.
- Tool: Semaphore. Best mental model: Finite pool of permits.
- Tool: Channel. Best mental model: Transfer values/events between participants.
- Tool: Atomic. Best mental model: Indivisible primitive operation with chosen ordering.
- Tool: Barrier. Best mental model: Wait for every participant to reach one phase.

Choose the tool based on the invariant, not on familiarity.

Message passing can simplify ownership: the sender transfers a value instead of giving
multiple threads mutable access to it.

## 4. Atomics and Ordering

An atomic operation prevents tearing, but memory ordering controls how operations around
it may be observed.

- Ordering: Relaxed. Mental model: Atomicity only; no cross-thread ordering guarantee.
- Ordering: Acquire. Mental model: Later operations cannot move before the acquire.
- Ordering: Release. Mental model: Earlier operations cannot move after the release.
- Ordering: AcqRel. Mental model: Acquire + release for a read-modify-write operation.
- Ordering: SeqCst. Mental model: Strongest common ordering; one global order for these operations.

Use the weakest ordering only when you can explain the proof. A mutex or channel is
often clearer and fast enough.

This fragment illustrates ordering but is not a complete queue. Correct lock-free
structures also need careful lifetime, progress, and reclamation design.

## 5. Compiler Implications

A language implementation must define:

- Memory-model topic: Atomic operations. Required language rule: Which reads, writes, and updates are indivisible.
- Memory-model topic: Data race. Required language rule: Which conflicting accesses constitute one.
- Memory-model topic: Race semantics. Required language rule: Error, undefined behavior, or defined outcome.
- Memory-model topic: Happens-before. Required language rule: Which synchronization creates ordering.
- Memory-model topic: Transfer. Required language rule: Which values may move between threads.
- Memory-model topic: Shared mutation. Required language rule: Whether shared references permit mutation.
- Memory-model topic: Thread-local lifecycle. Required language rule: Initialization and destructor behavior.

Optimizers rely on these rules. If the language declares data races invalid, the
optimizer may assume they never occur and transform code accordingly.

## 6. Concurrency Failure Modes

- Failure mode: Deadlock. Symptom: Participants wait forever in a dependency cycle.
- Failure mode: Livelock. Symptom: Work continues but no useful progress occurs.
- Failure mode: Starvation. Symptom: One participant repeatedly loses resource access.
- Failure mode: Priority inversion. Symptom: High-priority work waits on lower-priority work.
- Failure mode: Lost wakeup. Symptom: Notification occurs before the waiter observes it.
- Failure mode: ABA problem. Symptom: A leads to B leads to A fools a compare-and-swap equality check.
- Failure mode: False sharing. Symptom: Independent data causes needless cache-line coherence.

Debug concurrent systems with event traces, stable IDs, timeouts, and explicit state
transitions. Adding print statements can change timing and hide the bug.

## 7. Actors: Owned State Behind a Typed Mailbox

The Actix actor documentation presents a concurrent
application as independently executing actors that cooperate through typed messages.
An actor combines four ideas:

Other components hold an address, not a direct reference to the actor. Sending a
message transfers data and asks the actor's context to run the matching handler.

- Actix concept: Actor. Mental model: state and behavior owned by one logical participant.
- Actix concept: Message. Mental model: typed protocol request with a declared result type.
- Actix concept: Handler M. Mental model: transition function for one message type.
- Actix concept: Addr A. Mental model: capability to send supported messages to actor type A.
- Actix concept: Recipient M. Mental model: narrower capability that exposes only message type M.
- Actix concept: Context A. Mental model: mailbox, lifecycle, timers, streams, and spawned work.
- Actix concept: Arbiter. Mental model: single-threaded event-loop environment hosting async work.
- Actix concept: SyncArbiter. Mental model: fixed OS-thread pool containing instances of one actor type.
- Actix concept: Supervisor. Mental model: restarts a supervised actor context after failure.

Note. Actor rule: actors remove direct shared mutation; they do not remove
concurrency. Ordering, overload, cancellation, duplicate work, and failure are now
protocol questions.

### A Typed Symbol-Table Actor

This small actor serializes symbol interning. Each handler receives mutable reference to self, so the
state transition does not need a mutex inside the actor:

Keep handlers short. A real parse, optimize, or code-generation pass may be C P U-bound;
running it directly on an async actor's arbiter blocks other work on that event loop.
Send the job to a bounded worker pool or a deliberately sized SyncArbiter, then send
the result back.

### Addresses Are Capabilities

An address answers two questions at once:

1. Where may this message be sent?
2. Which protocol is the receiver known to implement?

- Send A P I: send(message). Completion/backpressure behavior: returns a future for the handler result or mailbox failure.
- Send A P I: try send(...). Completion/backpressure behavior: attempts immediately; reports a full or closed mailbox.
- Send A P I: do send(...). Completion/backpressure behavior: fire-and-forget; bypasses the mailbox limit and hides send errors.

Recipient M erases the concrete actor type while retaining permission to send only
M. It is useful for subscribers, event sinks, and interfaces that should not gain the
actor's full command surface.

Note. Backpressure warning: a configured mailbox capacity is not a universal memory
limit. do send, context notifications, and work spawned outside the mailbox can
bypass or outlive that queue. Bound message size, producer rate, in-flight requests,
retries, and downstream work as separate resources.

### Lifecycle Is a State Machine

- State: Started. Design question: Which timers, streams, child actors, or resources are attached?
- State: Running. Design question: Which messages are legal, and how is overload handled?
- State: Stopping. Design question: Are queued messages dropped, drained, rejected, or persisted?
- State: Stopped. Design question: Who observes termination and cleans external resources?

An actor can begin stopping when it calls Context stop, when all addresses are
dropped, or when no context event sources remain. If stopping completes, the actor is
dropped. Shutdown therefore needs a protocol: stop accepting new work, finish or
cancel in-flight work, persist required state, then acknowledge termination.

### Arbiter Is Placement; Context Is Actor State

Do not merge these concepts:

- Layer: System. Responsibility: top-level Actix runtime lifecycle.
- Layer: Arbiter. Responsibility: one event loop on one thread.
- Layer: Context A. Responsibility: one actor's mailbox, lifecycle, futures, streams, and timers.
- Layer: actor handler. Responsibility: one typed state transition.

Actors started on one arbiter are tied to it, but their addresses can communicate
across arbiters. Ordinary async arbiters suit short, non-blocking handlers.
SyncArbiter creates multiple instances of one actor type across a fixed thread pool
for synchronous work; its shared address routes messages to available instances, so
instance-local state is not automatically global state.

### Supervision Restarts Execution, Not History

A supervisor can create a new actor context and call the actor's restart hook, but it
cannot guarantee that the message being processed at failure was completed or replayed.

- Restart concern: actor state. Required application decision: reconstruct, reload, reset, or reject restart.
- Restart concern: failed message. Required application decision: retry only if duplicate execution is safe.
- Restart concern: side effects. Required application decision: use idempotency keys or transactional boundaries.
- Restart concern: restart loop. Required application decision: apply limits, backoff, and escalation.
- Restart concern: observability. Required application decision: retain cause, actor identity, message kind, and restart count.

Supervision is not a substitute for durable queues or transactions. If work must
survive process loss, persist it outside the in-memory actor system before acknowledging
acceptance.

### When Actors Fit—and When They Do Not

- Good actor boundary: one compiler/REPL session. Why it fits: stateful commands need per-session ordering.
- Good actor boundary: module registry or symbol service. Why it fits: one owner coordinates shared logical state.
- Good actor boundary: protocol connection. Why it fits: messages map to an explicit connection state.
- Good actor boundary: build/job coordinator. Why it fits: schedules bounded work and records outcomes.
