- Good actor boundary: debugger session. Why it fits: commands mutate one observed-process model.
- Good actor boundary: cache writer. Why it fits: one serialization point protects invariants.

- Poor actor boundary: pure stateless calculation. Better starting point: ordinary function or parallel iterator.
- Poor actor boundary: every tiny A S T node. Better starting point: tree plus traversal; mailbox overhead dominates.
- Poor actor boundary: hot G C pointer update. Better starting point: runtime memory structure and write barrier.
- Poor actor boundary: bulk numeric loop. Better starting point: contiguous data and data-parallel worker pool.
- Poor actor boundary: state requiring multi-actor atomic mutation. Better starting point: database transaction or redesigned ownership.

For your own language, define actor semantics independently of Actix:

Specify mailbox ordering, capacity, cancellation, failure propagation, restart,
location transparency, serialization, and whether messages are processed at-most-once
or may be retried. Those choices become part of the language and runtime contract.

# Rust as a Model of Ownership, Types, and Machine Boundaries

The notes in this section draw on
Idiomatic Rust Snippets, the
Rust Reference, and the Rust patterns catalogue.
The goal is not merely "code that compiles," but APIs whose types explain ownership,
state, and failure.

The code demonstrations also distill the models illustrated by
Ferrous Systems' Rust material,
100 Exercises to Learn Rust,
Rustfinity,
Kobzol's university Rust course,
Comprehensive Rust,
Rust for C Programmers,
Effective Rust, and
High Assurance Rust. These sources are
used for their code-level explanations of ownership, state machines, input and output,
concurrency, unsafe boundaries, and assurance—not as an exercise checklist.

## 1. Ownership as a Runtime Design Lesson

Rust's three basic ownership rules are a useful model for any language:

1. each value has an owner;
2. moving a non-Copy value transfers ownership;
3. the value is dropped when its owner leaves scope.

Borrow when a function only needs temporary access. Take ownership when it must store,
consume, transfer, or transform the value.

For parser APIs:

- use shared reference to str instead of shared reference to String;
- use and [Token] instead of shared reference to vector of Token;
- use String or vector of T when the result owns its data;
- use Cow 'a, str when a transformation may return either borrowed or owned text.

## 2. Make Illegal States Unrepresentable

Do not represent every compiler stage with one giant struct full of optional fields.

The type-state version makes it impossible to call lower on an untyped A S T without
first going through typecheck.

Use enums for closed sets of states or instructions:

Avoid a wildcard arm when adding a new variant should force every semantic decision to
be revisited.

## 3. Newtypes for Offsets, IDs, and Units

Raw you size values are easy to mix up:

This prevents accidentally passing a register number where a file offset is required.
The wrapper normally has no runtime cost.

- Values that look alike: Byte offset / virtual address. Why they need separate types: Different coordinate systems.
- Values that look alike: Source line / source byte. Why they need separate types: Different location units.
- Values that look alike: Token ID / A S T node ID. Why they need separate types: Different entity domains.
- Values that look alike: V M register / local slot. Why they need separate types: Different storage namespaces.
- Values that look alike: Host-order / network-order value. Why they need separate types: Different byte encodings.
- Values that look alike: Compressed / uncompressed size. Why they need separate types: Different allocation and validation limits.

## 4. Errors Are Part of the Language Tool's Interface

Use Option of T for expected absence and Result of T or E for failure with a reason.
Reserve panics for broken internal invariants.

- Diagnostic field: Problem. Question it answers: What went wrong?
- Diagnostic field: Location. Question it answers: Where did it happen?
- Diagnostic field: Expectation. Question it answers: What was valid or expected here?
- Diagnostic field: Observation. Question it answers: What did the compiler actually find?
- Diagnostic field: Recovery. Question it answers: What can the user do next?

## 5. Iterators Express Token and Byte Pipelines

Iterators are lazy and make transformations explicit:

Prefer iterator adapters when they clarify the pipeline. Use a normal loop when control
flow, error recovery, or mutable parser state is easier to read that way. "More
functional" is not automatically "more idiomatic."

## 6. R A I I for Files, Sockets, Locks, and Temporary State

R A I I means a resource is released when its guard or owner is dropped. Rust's File,
TcpStream, mutex guards, and collections already follow it.

A compiler can use a guard to restore temporary state even when a function returns
early:

R A I I is also a language-design option: deterministic destructors are convenient, but
their exact timing affects semantics, optimization, and cycles.

## 7. Keep unsafe Small and Give It a Contract

unsafe does not disable the borrow checker; it permits a small set of operations whose
safety the compiler cannot prove. Every unsafe block should have a written invariant.

Only reach for raw pointers or read unaligned when a safe byte-slice solution cannot
meet the requirement. number sign [repr(packed)] is not a shortcut for parsing arbitrary bytes
into a struct.

- Unsafe concern: Ownership. Review question: Who allocated this memory and who releases it?
- Unsafe concern: Lifetime. Review question: How long is the allocation valid?
- Unsafe concern: Initialization. Review question: Is every byte valid for the interpreted type?
- Unsafe concern: Alignment. Review question: Does the address meet the type's requirement?
- Unsafe concern: Aliasing. Review question: Are simultaneous references permitted?
- Unsafe concern: Concurrency. Review question: Can another thread or interrupt mutate it?
- Unsafe concern: Bounds. Review question: How are the pointer and length proven to be in range?

## Rust as a Language for Stating and Checking Invariants

Rust for C Programmers,
Comprehensive Rust,
Effective Rust, and
High Assurance Rust are most useful when read as four
views of the same model:

- View: C comparison. Question: Which manual machine-level obligation moved into the type system?
- View: Comprehensive Rust. Question: How do ownership, traits, concurrency, bare metal, and F F I compose?
- View: Effective Rust. Question: How should an A P I expose those guarantees idiomatically?
- View: High Assurance Rust. Question: What evidence is still needed after the compiler accepts the code?

### Ownership Is a Static Resource Protocol

In C, a pointer says where a value might be, but usually not who must free it, whether
another pointer aliases it, or how long it remains valid. Rust separates those claims:

- Rust form: T. Permission and obligation: Own the value and eventually run its destructor.
- Rust form: shared reference to T. Permission and obligation: Temporarily read through a shared borrow.
- Rust form: mutable reference to T. Permission and obligation: Temporarily read/write through an exclusive borrow.
- Rust form: Box T. Permission and obligation: Own a heap allocation with one responsible owner.
- Rust form: R C T / Arc T. Permission and obligation: Share ownership through reference counts.
- Rust form: Cell T / RefCell T. Permission and obligation: Move selected borrow checks to controlled runtime operations.
- Rust form: asterisk const T / asterisk mut T. Permission and obligation: Raw address; validity must be proved outside safe Rust.

The important shift is:

This same model applies beyond memory:

- Resource: File. Owner: File value. Borrow or capability: shared reference to File or mutable reference to File. Release: Drop closes handle.
- Resource: Lock. Owner: Mutex. Borrow or capability: Guard value. Release: Guard drop unlocks.
- Resource: Transaction. Owner: Transaction object. Borrow or capability: Scoped operations. Release: Commit or rollback.
- Resource: V M root. Owner: Root handle. Borrow or capability: Borrow into managed heap. Release: Guard drop unregisters.
- Resource: G P U buffer. Owner: Device-associated handle. Borrow or capability: Encoded command use. Release: A P I/device lifetime policy.

R A I I means cleanup follows lexical ownership. It does not prove that an externally
visible operation succeeded: flushing a file, committing a database transaction, or
sending a final network frame often needs an explicit fallible method.

### Borrowing Is an Aliasing Rule, Not Merely a Syntax Rule

The simplified rule is:

This prevents two classes of problems at once:

1. a reference cannot outlive the value it reaches;
2. mutation cannot race with other active access through safe references.

The compiler is not reasoning about the word “header.” It reasons about non-overlapping
regions and lifetimes. The domain name is our job; the aliasing proof is encoded by the
A P I.

Note. Low-level connection: optimizer transformations assume that certain references
do not alias. Rust's borrow rules are therefore simultaneously a safety discipline
and a source of optimization facts.

### Lifetimes Describe Relationships, Not Storage Duration Commands

A lifetime annotation does not keep data alive. It states a relationship that callers
must satisfy:

Read the signature as:

Note. “If the caller lends a packet for lifetime 'packet, a successful result is a view
whose validity is bounded by that same loan.”

This is relational logic embedded in a type signature. It matters when building parsers,
A S T views, packet decoders, database rows, or zero-copy file readers.

### Algebraic Data Types Define the Valid State Space

Effective Rust emphasizes using types to express data structures. An enum is a tagged
sum: the tag selects one variant, and only that variant's fields exist.

A design with unrelated booleans permits nonsense:

The enum's variants instead define a finite state space, and match forces each
transition to account for every currently valid state.

- Type-system tool: enum. State-space effect: Expresses alternatives.
- Type-system tool: struct. State-space effect: Expresses fields that exist together.
- Type-system tool: Option of T. State-space effect: Makes absence explicit.
- Type-system tool: Result of T or E. State-space effect: Separates success from expected failure.
- Type-system tool: Newtype. State-space effect: Prevents mixing equal representations with different meanings.
- Type-system tool: Private fields. State-space effect: Restricts which code can construct or mutate state.
- Type-system tool: Typestate. State-space effect: Makes protocol phase part of the static type.

This is compiler design in miniature. Tokens, A S T nodes, typed expressions, bytecode
instructions, and V M states all benefit from a representation that contains only valid
combinations.

### Safe and Unsafe Form a Proof Boundary

Comprehensive Rust describes unsafe Rust as a small set of extra capabilities, including
raw-pointer dereference, unsafe calls/traits, mutable statics, and union-field access.
unsafe does not mean “ignore correctness.” It means the compiler cannot verify all
preconditions.

Every unsafe abstraction needs two contracts:

- Contract: Caller contract. Audience: Users of an unsafe fn. Content: Preconditions they must establish.
- Contract: Implementation invariant. Audience: Maintainers of a safe wrapper. Content: Facts upheld for every safe call.

The safest unsafe block is the one removed by a better safe primitive. If raw access is
actually necessary, document:

### F F I Joins Two Different Proof Systems

An extern "C" signature agrees on a calling convention. It does not automatically
agree on ownership, unwinding, text encoding, struct layout, or thread safety.

In a production boundary, also decide how errors cross the A B I, prevent unwinding into
foreign code, define allocation/free pairs, version structures, and fuzz all decoding
paths. Chromium's Rust integration is a useful large-system example: Rust code still
enters through build, dependency-review, generated-binding, and C++ interoperability
policies rather than existing as an isolated safe island.

### High Assurance Means Accumulating Independent Evidence

High Assurance Rust makes an essential correction: memory safety eliminates important
bug classes, but it does not prove authentication policy, protocol correctness,
availability, confidentiality, or absence of malicious logic.

- Evidence source: Rust type checker. Finds or prevents: Many lifetime, aliasing, and data-race violations. Cannot establish alone: Business or security policy.
- Evidence source: Unit tests. Finds or prevents: Known examples and local edge cases. Cannot establish alone: All possible values and schedules.
- Evidence source: Property tests. Finds or prevents: Broad input classes and algebraic properties. Cannot establish alone: Complete state-space coverage.
- Evidence source: Fuzzing. Finds or prevents: Crashes and bad states reachable by generated inputs. Cannot establish alone: Absence of bugs.
- Evidence source: Differential tests. Finds or prevents: Disagreement with a reference implementation. Cannot establish alone: Correctness if both share a flaw.
- Evidence source: Static analysis / lints. Finds or prevents: Suspicious patterns. Cannot establish alone: Runtime environment behavior.
- Evidence source: Deductive verification. Finds or prevents: A stated property under a formal model. Cannot establish alone: That the model and property are complete.
- Evidence source: Code review. Finds or prevents: Design errors, intent, maintainability, threat reasoning. Cannot establish alone: Exhaustive execution.
- Evidence source: Reproducible builds and dependency review. Finds or prevents: Supply-chain confidence. Cannot establish alone: Runtime correctness.

Assurance is an argument:

### Threat Modeling Starts at Trust Boundaries

For each boundary, write:

- Question: What enters?. Example: Packet, file, A S T plugin, database row, F F I pointer.
- Question: Who controls it?. Example: Local user, remote peer, dependency, device.
- Question: What must remain true?. Example: Memory bounds, authorization, ordering, availability.
- Question: Which resources can it consume?. Example: C P U, memory, recursion, file handles, logs.
- Question: Which failure is acceptable?. Example: Structured error, dropped request, process restart.
- Question: Which evidence checks the claim?. Example: Parser tests, fuzzing, audit, metrics.

This is directly applicable to a language runtime:

- Runtime boundary: Source parser. Assurance focus: Bounded input, no panics, useful locations.
- Runtime boundary: Bytecode verifier. Assurance focus: Valid opcodes, jumps, stack effects, types.
- Runtime boundary: Native F F I. Assurance focus: Layout, lifetime, ownership, unwinding.
- Runtime boundary: Garbage collector. Assurance focus: Roots, reachability, barriers, no dangling references.
- Runtime boundary: Module loader. Assurance focus: Path policy, signatures, dependency identity.
- Runtime boundary: J I T. Assurance focus: Writable-versus-executable memory, code validation.
- Runtime boundary: Debugger/profiler. Assurance focus: Permission and privacy boundaries.

### A Reusable Assurance Workflow

1. State the claim. Example: “Every accepted bytecode function has balanced stack
effects on all control-flow paths.”
2. Define the representation. Specify instructions, operands, control-flow graph,
and stack-height domain.
3. Write the invariant. Each block has one incoming height; every instruction's
required operands exist.
4. Enforce cheap facts statically. Use enums, newtypes, private fields, and checked
constructors.
5. Validate external data dynamically. Never deserialize bytes directly into a
trusted internal state.
6. Isolate unsafe or foreign operations. Give the boundary a small A P I and explicit
safety comment.
7. Test from several directions. Examples, properties, fuzzing, malformed corpora,
differential execution.
8. Measure failures in deployment. Logs and metrics should reveal violated
assumptions without exposing secrets.
9. Record limitations. The next reviewer must know what remains unproved.

Note. Internalization prompt: for every Rust library you read, identify its
representation, valid states, ownership graph, transitions, failure values, unsafe
boundary, complexity costs, and testing evidence. That turns library documentation
into a computer-science lesson.

## 8. Rust Anti-Patterns to Avoid

- Anti-pattern: Clone to silence a borrow error. Better direction: Model ownership and borrowing deliberately.
- Anti-pattern: Accept shared reference to String or shared reference to vector of T. Better direction: Accept shared reference to str or and [T] when ownership is unnecessary.
- Anti-pattern: Use you size for every numeric domain. Better direction: Introduce newtypes for IDs, offsets, sizes, and slots.
- Anti-pattern: unwrap() input-dependent results. Better direction: Propagate a contextual Result.
- Anti-pattern: Index before checking length. Better direction: Use checked slicing or get.
- Anti-pattern: Return only "failed". Better direction: Include error kind and byte/source offset.
- Anti-pattern: Hold a lock across blocking/.await. Better direction: Shorten the guard's scope.
- Anti-pattern: Make every A S T field pub. Better direction: Expose a small, stable A P I.
- Anti-pattern: Cast bytes directly to a struct. Better direction: Parse layout, alignment, and endianness explicitly.
- Anti-pattern: Recurse over attacker input. Better direction: Enforce a depth limit or use an explicit stack.

## 9. Rust and Python: Similar Task, Different Contract

The
Programming Idioms Rust/Python comparison
is useful because it holds the task constant while changing the language. Do not
translate syntax alone: ownership, errors, integers, strings, iteration, and concurrency
have different contracts.

- Concern: typing. Python tendency: values checked dynamically. Rust tendency: types checked before execution.
- Concern: ownership. Python tendency: garbage-collected object references. Rust tendency: ownership, borrowing, and deterministic drop.
- Concern: errors. Python tendency: exceptions. Rust tendency: Result of T or E and?
- Concern: absence. Python tendency: None, often checked dynamically. Rust tendency: Option of T forces explicit handling.
- Concern: integers. Python tendency: arbitrary precision by default. Rust tendency: fixed-width types; explicit big integers.
- Concern: strings. Python tendency: Unicode string abstraction. Rust tendency: U T F eight String/str; indexing by byte forbidden.
- Concern: iteration. Python tendency: iterable/generator protocol. Rust tendency: zero-cost Iterator adaptors.
- Concern: maps. Python tendency: insertion-ordered dict. Rust tendency: choose HashMap or ordered BTreeMap.
- Concern: mutation. Python tendency: object mutability is runtime behavior. Rust tendency: binding/reference mutability is explicit.

### Iteration with Index and Value

iter() borrows values, iter mut() allows mutation, and into iter() consumes the
collection. That choice is part of the A P I, not incidental syntax.

### Parse, Filter, and Collect Errors

Rust makes the “stop at the first invalid conversion” contract explicit:

Because Result implements FromIterator, collecting stops on the first error. If all
errors matter—such as compiler diagnostics—collect successes and diagnostics into
separate typed outputs instead.

### Unicode Requires a Unit

- Unit: byte. Rust view/A P I: as bytes(), byte ranges. Suitable for: protocols, files, U T F eight validation.
- Unit: Unicode scalar. Rust view/A P I: chars(). Suitable for: code points and many language rules.
- Unit: grapheme cluster. Rust view/A P I: Unicode-segmentation library. Suitable for: user-perceived characters.

Five bytes, five Unicode scalar values, and five visible characters are not equivalent.

### Maps Encode Ordering Expectations

Use a deterministic map or sort keys before emitting compiler artifacts, diagnostics,
snapshots, or protocol output that must be reproducible.

Note. Translation habit: ask “what behavior does this idiom promise?” before asking
“what Rust syntax looks like the Python syntax?”

# Program Design in Rust: State, Errors, and Invariants

Note. Library lens: Rust patterns are treated as executable invariants: builders represent staged construction, enums represent closed state spaces, R A I I represents lifetime-coupled cleanup, and traits represent substitutable capabilities.

Rust Design Patterns separates reusable
Rust knowledge into idioms, design patterns, and anti-patterns. The
Rust Cookbook complements
that vocabulary with small recipes for common tasks.

Note. Selection rule: use a pattern because its trade-off matches the problem, not
because the pattern has a familiar name.

## 1. Idiom, Pattern, or Recipe?

- Kind: Idiom. Purpose: Conventional Rust expression of a common idea. Example: ?, iterators, Default.
- Kind: Design pattern. Purpose: Reusable structure for a recurring design problem. Example: Builder, Command, R A I I guard.
- Kind: Anti-pattern. Purpose: Tempting approach whose costs usually exceed benefits. Example: Clone-to-fix-borrowing.
- Kind: Recipe. Purpose: Focused example that accomplishes one practical task. Example: Stream a file line by line.

Patterns are not automatically abstractions worth keeping. A direct struct constructor
is clearer than a builder with no optional choices; an enum can be clearer than a
trait-object hierarchy with only two closed variants.

## 2. Pattern Selection Table

- Problem shape: Several values share one primitive representation. Rust-oriented pattern: Newtype.
- Problem shape: Construction has many optional settings. Rust-oriented pattern: Builder.
- Problem shape: Cleanup must happen on every exit path. Rust-oriented pattern: R A I I guard.
- Problem shape: Actions must be queued, logged, or undone. Rust-oriented pattern: Command.
- Problem shape: Behavior varies behind one stable interface. Rust-oriented pattern: Trait/generic Strategy.
- Problem shape: Legal operations depend on current state. Rust-oriented pattern: Typestate or an exhaustive enum.
- Problem shape: A closed set of variants has different data. Rust-oriented pattern: Enum + match.
- Problem shape: Heterogeneous extensions are loaded dynamically. Rust-oriented pattern: Trait objects.

## 3. Newtypes Encode Meaning

Newtypes prevent values with the same representation from being mixed:

- Newtype benefit: Domain separation. Result: Offsets, addresses, counts, and IDs cannot be swapped.
- Newtype benefit: Central validation. Result: Constructors can enforce range or formatting rules.
- Newtype benefit: A P I clarity. Result: Signatures explain what a number means.
- Newtype benefit: Future evolution. Result: Representation can change behind the public type.

Implement only operations that make semantic sense. Adding two byte lengths may be
valid; adding two virtual addresses usually is not.

## 4. Builder for Complicated Construction

Rust has no overloaded functions or default arguments. A builder is useful when a
configuration has required fields plus several optional policies:

- Use a builder when...: Many settings are optional. Prefer a constructor when...: Every argument is required.
- Use a builder when...: Defaults should remain centralized. Prefer a constructor when...: There are only a few obvious fields.
- Use a builder when...: Construction needs validation or staged preparation. Prefer a constructor when...: A struct literal is already clear.
- Use a builder when...: Adding an option should not break callers. Prefer a constructor when...: The type is private and construction local.

## 5. R A I I Guards Make Cleanup Structural

An R A I I guard acquires or changes state during construction and restores/finalizes it in
Drop. Cleanup then occurs on normal return, early?, and panic unwinding.

The guard should mediate access to the resource when using the resource after
finalization would be invalid. Common examples are mutex guards, temporary directory
owners, transaction guards, scoped tracing spans, and V M root guards.

Note. Drop is not a general error channel: destructors cannot conveniently return
cleanup errors. Provide an explicit finish, commit, or flush operation when the
caller must observe failure.

## 6. Command Pattern for Compiler Passes

The Command pattern turns an action into a value that can be stored and invoked later.
Trait objects suit an open, heterogeneous pass pipeline:

- Representation: Enum of commands. Best fit: Closed command set; exhaustive dispatch.
- Representation: fn pointers/closures. Best fit: Small stateless actions.
- Representation: Box dyn Command. Best fit: Open plugin-like set with per-command state.
- Representation: Generic command type. Best fit: One command type on a performance-critical path.

Undo requires more than an undo() method name. The command must retain enough prior
state, and failure halfway through a multi-command transaction needs an explicit
rollback policy.

## 7. Strategy: Static or Dynamic Dispatch

- Dispatch form: Generic S: Trait. Benefit: Inlining and static type knowledge. Cost/constraint: One instantiation per concrete type.
- Dispatch form: mutable reference to dyn Trait. Benefit: Runtime-pluggable heterogeneous values. Cost/constraint: Indirect call and object-safety limits.
- Dispatch form: Enum + match. Benefit: Exhaustive, compact closed set. Cost/constraint: Adding a variant changes central code.

Prefer generics at reusable performance-sensitive boundaries and trait objects where
runtime extensibility is the actual requirement.

## 8. Cookbook Mindset

The Rust Cookbook covers practical categories such as algorithms, command-line tools,
encoding, filesystems, concurrency, networking, and asynchronous work.

- Recipe category: Command line. Transferable lesson: Preserve non-U T F eight paths with args os.
- Recipe category: File input and output. Transferable lesson: Stream large input and buffer many small writes.
- Recipe category: Encoding. Transferable lesson: Make byte order and text format explicit.
- Recipe category: Concurrency. Transferable lesson: Select ownership transfer before shared mutation.
- Recipe category: Networking. Transferable lesson: Bound reads, writes, timeouts, and decoded sizes.
- Recipe category: Compression. Transferable lesson: Treat expanded size and archive paths as untrusted.
- Recipe category: Async. Transferable lesson: Bound task counts, channels, and wait time.

Cookbook snippets are starting points. Before adopting a recipe, check current crate
documentation, pin an intentional dependency range, test errors, and add limits suitable
for the input's trust level.

## Rust Demonstration: A Streaming Hash Is a Finite-State Fold

The Rust Cookbook's file and cryptography examples become a computer-science model when
the library call is unpacked. A streaming digest does not keep the whole file. It keeps
a fixed-size algorithm state and folds each input block into it:

- Concept: Incremental algorithm. What the example demonstrates: Each chunk advances a compact internal state.
- Concept: Bounded memory. What the example demonstrates: Space is big O of one with respect to file size.
- Concept: Partial input and output. What the example demonstrates: The returned byte count controls which bytes are valid input.
- Concept: Finalization. What the example demonstrates: Padding and total length complete the digest definition.
- Concept: Determinism. What the example demonstrates: Equal byte sequences under the same algorithm produce equal digests.
- Concept: Domain boundary. What the example demonstrates: A file digest detects change; it is not password storage or a signature.

S H A two fifty-six is fast by design, which is useful for content integrity and addressing but bad
for password verification: an offline attacker could test guesses quickly. Argon2 adds
salt and deliberately expensive memory-hard work for that different problem.

The same fold structure appears in:

- System: Parser. State folded over input: automaton/stack over tokens.
- System: Compression. State folded over input: dictionary/window over bytes.
- System: Network checksum. State folded over input: checksum state over packet chunks.
- System: Database aggregation. State folded over input: accumulator over rows.
- System: Compiler dataflow. State folded over input: facts repeatedly merged across graph edges.
- System: Audio filter. State folded over input: delay/filter state over sample blocks.

The library therefore illustrates a general model—bounded state plus a transition
function over a stream—rather than merely a recipe for hashing one file.

## 9. Stream Large Text Instead of Loading It Whole

- Input shape: Small trusted U T F eight configuration. Preferred approach: fs read to string.
- Input shape: Large line-oriented text. Preferred approach: BufReader lines.
- Input shape: Arbitrary binary input. Preferred approach: Read into a bounded byte buffer.
- Input shape: Many small output writes. Preferred approach: BufWriter + explicit flush.
- Input shape: In-memory test fixture. Preferred approach: Cursor vector of u8.

## 10. Spawn Programs Without Re-parsing a Shell String

Passing arguments separately avoids shell quoting and injection problems. Use a shell
only when shell semantics—pipes, expansion, or redirection—are deliberately required,
and never concatenate untrusted text into a shell program.

## 11. Recipe Adoption Checklist

- Review area: Ownership. Question: Can inputs be borrowed rather than cloned?
- Review area: Errors. Question: Does every failure preserve useful context?
- Review area: Limits. Question: Are memory, recursion, file, task, and output sizes bounded?
- Review area: Portability. Question: Do path, process, newline, and filesystem assumptions hold?
- Review area: Dependencies. Question: Is the crate current, maintained, and necessary?
- Review area: Security. Question: Which bytes, paths, URLs, and arguments are untrusted?
- Review area: Testing. Question: Are success, boundary, partial-failure, and cleanup tested?

# lambda Functional Models: Composition, Effects, and Rust

fp-core.rs presents functional-programming
vocabulary and purely functional data structures for Rust. Most of the underlying ideas
also appear directly in standard Rust through closures, iterators, algebraic data types,
Option, Result, and immutable-by-default bindings.

Note. lambda Mental model: functional programming makes transformation and composition
primary. Rust adds explicit ownership, lifetimes, and controlled mutation to that
model.

## 1. Pure Core, Imperative Shell

A pure function depends only on its arguments and does not mutate external state.
The same input therefore produces the same output.

- Pure core: A S T transformations. Imperative shell: reading files.
- Pure core: type rules. Imperative shell: printing diagnostics.
- Pure core: byte decoding from a supplied slice. Imperative shell: receiving network packets.
- Pure core: optimization over an explicit I R. Imperative shell: timers, randomness, database operations.

Push input and output and nondeterminism to narrow adapters. A pure compiler core is easier to test,
cache, parallelize, and compare with another implementation.

## 2. Higher-Order Functions and Closures

A higher-order function accepts or returns another function. A closure can capture
state from its environment.

The returned closure partially applies minimum: it turns a two-input idea into a
reusable one-input predicate.

## 3. Composition Is Typed Pipelining

- Pipeline stage: trim. Type: String - String.
- Pipeline stage: length. Type: String - you size.
- Pipeline stage: composition. Type: String - you size.

Compiler passes compose similarly, but fallibility and diagnostics often make the real
type Input - Result of Output or Error.

## 4. Algebraic Data Types Carry Meaning

Rust enums are sum types: a value is one variant. Structs and tuples are product
types: a value contains all fields.

This algebra explains exhaustiveness, representation choices, serialization tags, and
why an enum often models runtime or protocol state better than several booleans.

## 5. map, and then, and Context

- Operation: map. Shape: F A + (A - B) leads to F B. Meaning: transform a value in context.
- Operation: and then. Shape: F A + (A - F B ) leads to F B. Meaning: chain a context-producing step.
- Operation: unwrap or. Shape: F A + fallback leads to A. Meaning: leave the context deliberately.

Option carries possible absence; Result carries possible failure. Their combinators
preserve that context without nested control flow.

## 6. Monoids Describe Safe Reduction

A monoid has an associative combine operation and an identity element.

- Type/operation: integers under plus. Identity: 0. Compiler use: instruction counts.
- Type/operation: strings under append. Identity: empty. Compiler use: generated text.
- Type/operation: sets under union. Identity: empty set. Compiler use: data-flow facts.
- Type/operation: lists under concat. Identity: empty list. Compiler use: collected diagnostics.

Associativity permits chunked or parallel reduction, but floating-point addition is not
mathematically associative at machine precision. Deterministic builds must define
reduction order where rounding matters.

## 7. Referential Transparency Enables Reasoning

If an expression can be replaced by its value without changing behavior, it is
referentially transparent. That supports:

- Technique: memoization. Required caution: inputs must capture every dependency.
- Technique: common subexpression elimination. Required caution: operation must be safe to reuse.
- Technique: lazy evaluation. Required caution: effects and resource lifetime change timing.
- Technique: parallel evaluation. Required caution: operations must not depend on hidden ordering.
- Technique: property-based testing. Required caution: generators must respect input invariants.

Functional terminology is useful, but do not force abstractions where ownership becomes
opaque. Prefer ordinary iterators and explicit enums until a more general abstraction
demonstrably improves the code.

Note. Next: algorithms apply these transformation, reduction, and invariant ideas to
concrete problems with measurable resource costs.

# Algorithms and Data Structures Through Rust

Note. Library lens: Rust collections, slices, iterators, ownership, and explicit errors make algorithm state and cost visible. The code illustrates invariants and transitions rather than syntax tricks.

This section expands the algorithm categories from
Idiomatic Rust Snippets:
sorting, searching, graph traversal, and dynamic programming. The emphasis here is on
interfaces and invariants useful in compilers, interpreters, and low-level tools.

Note. Algorithm-selection rule: choose from the contract and constraints, not
from whichever algorithm name is easiest to remember.

## 1. Start with the Contract

Before choosing an algorithm, define its contract:

- Contract dimension: Input. Decision to make: Shape, ownership, and validity.
- Contract dimension: Output. Decision to make: Required result and failure representation.
- Contract dimension: Comparison. Decision to make: Ordering and equality rules.
- Contract dimension: Mutation. Decision to make: Whether the input may be changed.
- Contract dimension: Scale. Decision to make: Expected data size and worst-case limit.
- Contract dimension: Stability. Decision to make: Whether equal items must preserve relative order.
- Contract dimension: Invalid input. Decision to make: Reject, ignore, repair, or return a partial result.

Prefer slices for algorithms that do not need to own or resize data:

and [T] accepts arrays, vectors, boxed slices, and subslices without allocation.

## 2. Complexity Is a Growth Model

- Complexity: big O of one. Typical example: Index an array. Interpretation: Work does not grow with input length.
- Complexity: O( log n). Typical example: Binary search. Interpretation: Repeatedly halves the search space.
- Complexity: big O of n. Typical example: Scan for a token. Interpretation: Visits input once.
- Complexity: O(n log n). Typical example: Comparison sort. Interpretation: Common efficient general sorting bound.
- Complexity: O(n squared). Typical example: Compare every pair. Interpretation: Often acceptable only for small inputs.
- Complexity: O(2^n). Typical example: Enumerate all subsets. Interpretation: Becomes infeasible quickly.

- Performance dimension: Space complexity. Why it matters: Bounds extra memory use.
- Performance dimension: Amortized cost. Why it matters: Spreads occasional expensive work over many operations.
- Performance dimension: Worst-case latency. Why it matters: Matters for services, tools, and real-time paths.
- Performance dimension: Constant factors. Why it matters: Separate practical implementations with similar big-O.
- Performance dimension: Locality. Why it matters: Contiguous access can beat pointer-heavy structures.

## 3. Sorting Choices

- Algorithm: Bubble sort. Average time: O(n squared). Worst time: O(n squared). Extra space: big O of one. Stable?: Yes.
- Algorithm: Quicksort. Average time: O(n log n). Worst time: O(n squared). Extra space: Recursion stack. Stable?: Usually no.
- Algorithm: Merge sort. Average time: O(n log n). Worst time: O(n log n). Extra space: big O of n. Stable?: Yes.
- Algorithm: Heap sort. Average time: O(n log n). Worst time: O(n log n). Extra space: big O of one. Stable?: No.

In application code, use the standard library unless implementing an algorithm for
learning or a specialized constraint:

Rust slice sorting:

- sort and sort by key preserve the relative order of equal elements;
- sort unstable and sort unstable by key may be faster or use less temporary memory
when stability is unnecessary.

### In-Place Quicksort for Study

Note. Partition invariant: values left of the boundary are smaller than the pivot;
unchecked values remain in the middle.

After partitioning, the pivot is in its final position. The two remaining slices can be
sorted independently.

## 4. Linear and Binary Search

Linear search works on any sequence:

Binary search requires sorted input:

For duplicate values, binary search may return any matching position. Use a boundary
search when the first valid insertion point matters:

Note. Loop invariant: the answer always remains inside the current search interval.

- indexes below low contain values less than target;
- indexes at or above high are not part of the remaining uncertainty;
- the answer remains in low..=high.

## 5. Two Pointers: Move Boundaries by a Proven Rule

The two-pointer pattern maintains two positions whose movement discards part of the
search space. It is useful when the input order makes that movement monotonic.

- Shape: sorted pair search. Pointer movement: move left for a small sum, right for a large sum.
- Shape: in-place partition. Pointer movement: scan with one pointer, store boundary with the other.
- Shape: palindrome check. Pointer movement: compare ends, then move inward.
- Shape: merge two sorted sequences. Pointer movement: advance the side containing the next output.
- Shape: remove duplicates in place. Pointer movement: read pointer scans; write pointer marks compact output.
- Shape: linked-list fast/slow scan. Pointer movement: advance by one and two nodes.

Note. Invariant: no discarded index can participate in a valid answer. If you
cannot justify that sentence for each pointer movement, the pattern is not correct.

Two pointers do not automatically improve a nested loop. The improvement comes from
an ordering property—sorted values, a partition boundary, or a linked-list speed
relationship—that prevents revisiting discarded candidates.

## 6. Sliding Windows: Maintain an Aggregate Over a Contiguous Range

A sliding window is a pair of boundaries plus state describing the half-open range
left..right. Instead of recomputing every range, update the state when one item
enters or leaves.

- Window kind: fixed-size. Boundary rule: add right item; remove item now outside size k. Common state: sum, count, rolling hash.
- Window kind: variable. Boundary rule: expand until invalid; shrink until valid/minimal. Common state: sum, frequencies, set.
- Window kind: monotonic. Boundary rule: remove dominated candidates from a deque. Common state: min/max candidates.

This example finds the shortest contiguous range whose sum reaches a target:

Note. Contract matters: this variable-window rule depends on non-negative values.
Negative inputs destroy the monotonic relationship; use prefix sums with a different
data structure or reformulate the problem.

A window is normally contiguous. If the chosen items may be scattered, the problem is
more likely a subsequence, subset, graph, or dynamic-programming problem.

## 7. Linked Lists as an Ownership Laboratory

Learning Rust With Entirely Too Many Linked Lists
uses several list designs to expose how pointer choice determines ownership, mutation,
iteration, destruction, aliasing, and unsafe obligations.

- Representation: Option Box Node T. Ownership shape: one owner per node. Main lesson: moves make safe mutation and reversal natural.
- Representation: R C Node T. Ownership shape: shared, single-threaded. Main lesson: persistent tails can share structure.
- Representation: Arc Node T. Ownership shape: shared, thread-safe reference counts. Main lesson: atomic ownership is not automatic data mutation.
- Representation: R C RefCell Node T. Ownership shape: shared with runtime-checked mutation. Main lesson: cycles and borrow panics require deliberate care.
- Representation: raw pointers/NonNull Node T. Ownership shape: invariants enforced by the container. Main lesson: unsafe code must prove aliasing and lifetime laws.
- Representation: vector of T / vector deck T. Ownership shape: contiguous or ring-buffer allocation. Main lesson: usually faster and simpler for ordinary workloads.

The simplest owning link uses Option as the empty state instead of a sentinel node:

### Reverse a Singly Linked List

Reversal is a repeated ownership transfer. At every iteration, three regions are
well-defined: the reversed prefix, the current node, and the untouched suffix.

- Before one iteration: previous comes from current leads to rest. After one iteration: previous comes from current and head leads to rest.

The algorithm runs in big O of n time and uses big O of one auxiliary space. take() replaces
an Option with None while returning its old value, which makes the ownership change
explicit without cloning or unsafe pointers.

### Find the Middle Node with Slow and Fast References

Advance one reference by one edge and the other by two. When the fast reference reaches
the end, the slow reference is at the middle:

This is big O of n time and big O of one space. State the even-length convention because
“middle” is ambiguous for lists of length two, four, and so on.

### Detect a Cycle with Floyd's Tortoise and Hare

An exclusively owned Box list cannot safely point back to an ancestor, so use an
index-based arena to demonstrate cyclic topology without inventing unsafe aliases.
Each table entry stores the index of its next node:

The return type separates malformed edge (None) from valid acyclic list
(Some(false)). After a fast/slow collision, finding the cycle entry takes a second
phase: reset one pointer to the head and advance both one step until they meet.

### What the Linked-List Designs Teach

- Lesson from the list designs: recursive structure. General low-level consequence: recursive destruction may overflow on extreme depth.
- Lesson from the list designs: shared R C tails. General low-level consequence: cheap persistence trades mutation for shared identity.
- Lesson from the list designs: R C cycles. General low-level consequence: reference counts need Weak non-owning edges.
- Lesson from the list designs: doubly linked mutation. General low-level consequence: two-way aliases make safe ownership much harder.
- Lesson from the list designs: iterator families. General low-level consequence: consuming, shared, and mutable iteration differ.
- Lesson from the list designs: raw-pointer queue/deque. General low-level consequence: safety belongs to a small abstraction boundary.
- Lesson from the list designs: Miri/alias models. General low-level consequence: passing tests is not proof that unsafe code is valid.
- Lesson from the list designs: variance and Send/Sync. General low-level consequence: pointer representation affects type-system promises.
- Lesson from the list designs: panic safety. General low-level consequence: every intermediate mutation must remain droppable.
- Lesson from the list designs: cursor APIs. General low-level consequence: mutation location and iterator validity are contracts.

An iterative destructor avoids recursively dropping one node per stack frame:

Note. Practical default: linked lists are excellent for studying ownership and
specialized intrusive/persistent structures. For everyday stacks, queues, and
indexed data, start with Vec or vector deck and change only after measurement or a
structural requirement justifies it.

## 8. Breadth-First Search

BFS explores by increasing edge distance. Use vector deck, not Vec remove(0).

- BFS application: Unweighted shortest path. Why breadth-first order helps: First arrival uses the fewest edges.
- BFS application: Nearest state. Why breadth-first order helps: Explores states by increasing transition count.
- BFS application: Dependency distance. Why breadth-first order helps: Assigns layers from a starting node.
- BFS application: Level-order traversal. Why breadth-first order helps: Visits a tree one depth at a time.
- BFS application: Minimum transformations. Why breadth-first order helps: Finds the smallest number of uniform-cost steps.

Mark a node visited when enqueuing it, not when dequeuing, to avoid duplicate queue
entries. To reconstruct a shortest path, store parent[neighbor] = Some(node) at the
same moment that the neighbor is first discovered, then walk parents backward from the
goal.

## 9. Depth-First Search

DFS explores one path before backtracking. An explicit stack avoids overflowing the Rust
call stack on a deep graph:

- DFS application: A S T/syntax-tree walk. Useful traversal property: Follow a subtree before its siblings.
- DFS application: Reachability. Useful traversal property: Explore an entire connected region.
- DFS application: Cycle detection. Useful traversal property: Track active recursion/visit state.
- DFS application: Strongly connected components. Useful traversal property: Use DFS ordering in classic SCC algorithms.
- DFS application: Postorder code generation. Useful traversal property: Process children before parents.
- DFS application: G C marking. Useful traversal property: Trace object references to completion.

Choose recursive DFS when the input depth is strictly bounded and recursion clarifies
the algorithm. Otherwise prefer an explicit stack.

## 10. Backtracking and DFS with Memoization

DFS describes a traversal order. Two important refinements change what happens when a
state is revisited:

- Technique: plain DFS. Revisited state: skip if globally visited. Best fit: reachability and component traversal.
- Technique: backtracking. Revisited state: undo the current choice and try another. Best fit: enumerate constrained candidates.
- Technique: memoized DFS. Revisited state: return the cached answer for the same subproblem. Best fit: optimization/counting with overlap.
- Technique: BFS. Revisited state: keep the first discovery at minimum unweighted depth. Best fit: shortest number of uniform-cost steps.

### Backtracking Is Choose leads to Explore leads to Unchoose

Backtracking searches a decision tree and abandons branches that cannot produce a valid
answer. This example generates size-k combinations from 0..n:

Note. Backtracking invariant: after the recursive call returns, all mutable search
state must be exactly as it was before the choice.

Prune from constraints before branching. Useful compiler/reversing examples include
grammar alternatives, instruction-pattern selection, register assignments, symbolic
input constraints, and competing type or calling-convention hypotheses.

### Memoized DFS Turns a Repeated Search Tree into Dynamic Programming

Memoization caches a result by the complete state that determines future work. This
top-down coin-change example uses an outer Option for not computed yet and an
inner Option for computed but impossible:

Without the memo table, many amounts are recomputed along different DFS paths. With
memoization, there are at most amount plus 1 states and each tries every coin, giving
O(\textamount times \textcoins) time and O(\textamount) table space.

- Memoization design question: state key. Requirement: include everything that changes future answers.
- Memoization design question: base case. Requirement: terminate without consulting an invalid index.
- Memoization design question: progress. Requirement: recursive edges must approach a base case.
- Memoization design question: impossible result. Requirement: cache it too, or failed states will be recomputed.
- Memoization design question: cycles. Requirement: use an “in progress” state if the subproblem graph cycles.
- Memoization design question: depth. Requirement: prefer tabulation for adversarial or very deep state DAGs.

This is dynamic programming because the recursive call graph is a DAG of overlapping
subproblems. Top-down memoization evaluates only reachable states; bottom-up
tabulation avoids call-stack depth and makes dependency order explicit.

## 11. Topological Sorting

A directed acyclic graph can be ordered so every dependency appears before its users.
Kahn's algorithm repeatedly removes nodes with zero incoming edges:

If not every node is emitted, the graph contains a cycle.

- Topological-sort use: Module build order. Dependency represented by each edge: Imported module must be available first.
- Topological-sort use: Constant initialization. Dependency represented by each edge: Dependency value must be initialized first.
- Topological-sort use: I R scheduling. Dependency represented by each edge: Producer operation must precede its consumer.
- Topological-sort use: Pass/migration ordering. Dependency represented by each edge: Prerequisite step must run first.
- Topological-sort use: Cycle diagnosis. Dependency represented by each edge: Unemitted nodes reveal a dependency cycle.

## 12. Dynamic Programming

Dynamic programming applies when:

- the problem decomposes into subproblems;
- subproblems overlap;
- the best answer can be built from smaller answers.

Two styles:

- memoization: recursive, cache results on demand;
- tabulation: iterative, fill a table in dependency order.

Example: longest common subsequence length with rolling rows:

The full O(mn) table is unnecessary when each row depends only on the previous row.
This reduces extra space to big O of n.

- DP use in language tools: Sequence/tree diff. Optimized subproblem: Best alignment of smaller prefixes/subtrees.
- DP use in language tools: Parser recovery. Optimized subproblem: Lowest-cost repair path.
- DP use in language tools: Instruction selection. Optimized subproblem: Cheapest covering of I R patterns.
- DP use in language tools: Parenthesization. Optimized subproblem: Lowest-cost evaluation order.
- DP use in language tools: Diagnostic edit distance. Optimized subproblem: Minimum edits between names.
- DP use in language tools: Layout/line breaking. Optimized subproblem: Best formatting up to each position.

## 13. Weighted Graphs and Dijkstra's Algorithm

Use Dijkstra's algorithm when edge weights are non-negative and the goal is the
shortest distance from one source. The key operation is relaxation:

Rust's BinaryHeap is a max-heap, so reverse the comparison to make the cheapest
pending state come out first:

Important details:

- Skip stale heap entries instead of trying to decrease a key in place.
- Use checked addition when costs can come from untrusted input.
- None represents infinity without reserving a magic integer value.
- Dijkstra is incorrect for negative edges; use Bellman–Ford or reformulate the
problem.
- With an adjacency list and binary heap, the usual bound is
O((V + E) log V).

Compiler and reversing uses include weighted instruction selection, cheapest conversion
paths, control-flow navigation, and finding a minimum-cost sequence of state
transitions.

## 14. Disjoint Sets (Union-Find)

A disjoint-set structure maintains equivalence classes. It supports:

- find(x) — return the representative of x;
- union(a, b) — merge the two sets.

Path compression and union by rank make a long sequence of operations nearly linear:

- Union-find application: Kruskal's algorithm. Equivalence being maintained: Vertices already connected by selected edges.
- Union-find application: Type unification. Equivalence being maintained: Type variables known to be equal.
- Union-find application: Alias analysis. Equivalence being maintained: Values that may refer to the same region.
- Union-find application: Connected components. Equivalence being maintained: Nodes belonging to one component.
- Union-find application: Symbols/relocations. Equivalence being maintained: Records known to represent one entity.

The invariant is that every parent chain ends at a root whose parent is itself.

## 15. More Dynamic Programming: 0/1 Knapsack

The 0/1 knapsack problem chooses each item at most once while maximizing value under a
capacity limit. A one-dimensional table is enough if capacities are visited in reverse:

Reverse iteration is the essential invariant. Forward iteration would allow the newly
updated entry to be reused, silently changing the problem into unbounded knapsack.

Language-tool analogies:

- select optimizations under a compile-time budget;
- choose instructions under a byte-size limit;
- pack constants or sections under an alignment/size constraint;
- choose diagnostic repairs with bounded cost.

The table uses O(\textcapacity) memory and the algorithm uses
O(\textitems times \textcapacity) time. This is pseudo-polynomial: a numerically
large capacity can still make it impractical.

## 16. Cryptographic Algorithms: Learn the Shape, Use a Library

Cryptography combines algorithms with strict protocol rules. A mathematically correct
primitive can still be insecure because of nonce reuse, weak randomness, timing leaks,
bad serialization, missing authentication, or poor key handling.

- Goal: Detect accidental corruption. Appropriate primitive: Non-cryptographic checksum.
- Goal: Integrity without a key. Appropriate primitive: Cryptographic hash.
- Goal: Integrity with a shared key. Appropriate primitive: Message authentication code (M A C).
- Goal: Confidentiality + integrity. Appropriate primitive: Authenticated encryption with associated data.
- Goal: Store passwords. Appropriate primitive: Password K D F with salt and work factor.
- Goal: Establish a shared secret. Appropriate primitive: Authenticated key-exchange protocol.
- Goal: Prove who signed data. Appropriate primitive: Digital signature.

- Cryptographic rule: Authenticate encryption. Practical meaning: Confidentiality alone does not prevent tampering.
- Cryptographic rule: Respect nonce rules. Practical meaning: Public does not mean reusable.
- Cryptographic rule: Distinguish salts/keys. Practical meaning: A salt is neither a password nor an encryption key.
- Cryptographic rule: Hashes are one-way. Practical meaning: Hashing is not encryption.
- Cryptographic rule: Use a password K D F. Practical meaning: Fast general hashes are unsuitable for password storage.
- Cryptographic rule: Compare in constant time. Practical meaning: Use the library's authenticator comparison.
- Cryptographic rule: Minimize key exposure. Practical meaning: Keep keys scoped and never log them.
- Cryptographic rule: Version the format. Practical meaning: Record algorithms and parameters alongside ciphertext.
- Cryptographic rule: Use audited constructions. Practical meaning: Do not invent production ciphers or protocols.

### Educational Building Block: Modular Exponentiation

RSA and Diffie–Hellman use modular arithmetic, but real implementations also require
large integers, carefully generated parameters, padding/encoding rules, side-channel
resistance, and secure randomness. The following is only the square-and-multiply
arithmetic pattern:

This function is useful for learning exponentiation by squaring, but it is not
constant time and ordinary u128 multiplication can overflow before reduction. It is
not a production cryptographic implementation.

### Cryptography as a Parser and Protocol Problem

Treat authenticated data as a precisely encoded structure:

The decoder should:

1. bound all lengths before allocating;
2. reject unknown or forbidden algorithm identifiers;
3. authenticate before releasing plaintext;
4. reject duplicate/replayed nonces when the protocol requires it;
5. keep parsing errors from becoming a useful oracle;
6. preserve exact bytes used as associated data;
7. fail closed.

This is directly relevant to designing a language runtime: signed packages, bytecode
modules, plugin manifests, update files, and network messages are all binary formats
with trust boundaries.

## 17. Where Algorithms Appear in Language Tools

- Language-tool problem: Token lookup. Useful algorithm/data structure: Hash table or trie.
- Language-tool problem: Source-offset lookup. Useful algorithm/data structure: Binary search.
- Language-tool problem: A S T walk. Useful algorithm/data structure: DFS.
- Language-tool problem: Shortest unweighted path in C F G. Useful algorithm/data structure: BFS.
- Language-tool problem: Module dependency order. Useful algorithm/data structure: Topological sort.
- Language-tool problem: Recursive call groups. Useful algorithm/data structure: Strongly connected components.
- Language-tool problem: Type equivalence classes. Useful algorithm/data structure: Union-find/disjoint set.
- Language-tool problem: Data-flow analysis. Useful algorithm/data structure: Worklist fixed-point iteration.
- Language-tool problem: Register allocation. Useful algorithm/data structure: Graph coloring or linear scan.
- Language-tool problem: Constant propagation. Useful algorithm/data structure: Lattice + worklist.
- Language-tool problem: Garbage-collector marking. Useful algorithm/data structure: DFS/BFS over object graph.
- Language-tool problem: Diagnostic suggestions. Useful algorithm/data structure: Edit distance.
- Language-tool problem: Instruction scheduling. Useful algorithm/data structure: Dependency DAG.

## 18. Worklist Fixed-Point Pattern

Many compiler analyses repeatedly propagate facts until nothing changes:

- Fixed-point component: Fact domain. Requirement: Finite height or another convergence argument.
- Fixed-point component: Transfer function. Requirement: Monotone with respect to the fact ordering.
- Fixed-point component: Change detection. Requirement: Reliable equality or improvement test.
- Fixed-point component: Dependencies. Requirement: Precise successors to re-enqueue.
- Fixed-point component: Tests. Requirement: Deterministic expected final facts.

## 19. Algorithm Engineering Checklist

- Area: Standard tools. Preferred practice: Use library sorting/searching in production.
- Area: Invariants. Preferred practice: State them beside non-obvious loops.
- Area: Arithmetic. Preferred practice: Check indexes, capacities, and input-derived costs.
- Area: Depth. Preferred practice: Avoid recursion over unbounded adversarial input.
- Area: Allocation. Preferred practice: Preallocate only from trustworthy estimates.
- Area: Queues/heaps. Preferred practice: Use vector deck for FIFO and BinaryHeap for priority.
- Area: Membership. Preferred practice: Use a set or boolean vector instead of repeated scans.
- Area: Reproducibility. Preferred practice: Preserve stable ordering when output must be deterministic.
- Area: Edge cases. Preferred practice: Test empty, duplicate, cyclic, disconnected, and maximum inputs.
- Area: Performance. Preferred practice: Benchmark realistic workloads; big-O is not the whole result.
- Area: Generics. Preferred practice: Generalize only when reuse improves without hiding the algorithm.
- Area: Failure. Preferred practice: Return Option/Result when absence or failure is part of the contract.

# Machine Learning: Optimization, Probability, and Generalization

The Machine Learning Glossary
organizes the field around linear/logistic regression, calculus, linear algebra,
forward propagation, backpropagation, activations, losses, optimizers, regularization,
and model architectures. The goal here is a systems-level mental model, not a substitute
for a statistics course or a production ML framework.

## 1. Learning Means Fitting a Parameterized Function

- Term: feature. Meaning: measured or derived model input.
- Term: label/target. Meaning: desired output used for supervised learning.
- Term: parameter. Meaning: value learned during training.
- Term: hyperparameter. Meaning: configuration chosen outside training.
- Term: inference. Meaning: compute a prediction with fixed parameters.
- Term: training. Meaning: adjust parameters to reduce an objective.
- Term: epoch. Meaning: one pass over the training data.
- Term: batch. Meaning: subset used for one update.

## 2. Split Data by Purpose

- Split: training. Used for: fitting parameters. Must not be used for: final performance claim.
- Split: validation. Used for: selecting models and hyperparameters. Must not be used for: repeated unbiased final reporting.
- Split: test. Used for: final estimate after choices are frozen. Must not be used for: tuning.

Data leakage occurs when training receives information that would not exist at real
prediction time or indirectly sees validation/test targets. Split related samples by
user, machine, project, time, or other dependency when random rows would leak context.

## 3. Linear Regression in Small Rust

For one feature:

This example is intentionally small. Production training needs numerical checks,
vectorized/tensor operations, reproducibility controls, efficient batching, and mature
libraries.

## 4. Gradient Descent Is Local Iterative Optimization

- Symptom: loss explodes. Possible cause: rate too high, poor scaling, numerical issue.
- Symptom: loss barely moves. Possible cause: rate too low, saturation, weak features.
- Symptom: training good/test poor. Possible cause: overfitting or leakage.
- Symptom: training and test both poor. Possible cause: underfitting, bad representation, bad labels.
- Symptom: results vary wildly. Possible cause: seed, ordering, nondeterminism, small dataset.

Feature normalization changes optimization geometry. It does not create information
that the features do not contain.

## 5. Classification and Regression Need Different Outputs

- Task: regression. Output: continuous value. Common loss/metric examples: MSE, MAE, RMSE.
- Task: binary classification. Output: probability/logit for two classes. Common loss/metric examples: binary cross-entropy, precision/recall.
- Task: multiclass classification. Output: class distribution/logits. Common loss/metric examples: cross-entropy, top-k accuracy.
- Task: ranking. Output: relative ordering. Common loss/metric examples: pairwise/listwise objectives.
- Task: clustering. Output: unlabeled grouping. Common loss/metric examples: distance/internal validity measures.

Accuracy can be misleading on imbalanced data. A detector that predicts “safe” for
99.9% of inputs can score highly while missing every rare failure. Choose metrics from
the cost of false positives and false negatives.

## 6. Neural Networks Compose Differentiable Layers

- Concept: weight. Role: learned connection strength.
- Concept: bias. Role: learned offset.
- Concept: activation. Role: nonlinear transformation.
- Concept: forward pass. Role: compute predictions and intermediates.
- Concept: backpropagation. Role: apply chain rule from loss toward earlier layers.
- Concept: optimizer. Role: turn gradients into parameter updates.
- Concept: regularization. Role: discourage brittle overfit solutions.

ReLU is simple and common; sigmoid maps to (0, 1) but can saturate; softmax converts
logits into a normalized multiclass distribution. Match the final activation, loss, and
target representation.

## 7. Regularization Controls Generalization

- Technique: L1 penalty. Intuition: encourage sparse parameters.
- Technique: L2 penalty/weight decay. Intuition: discourage large parameters.
- Technique: dropout. Intuition: randomly omit activations during training.
- Technique: early stopping. Intuition: stop when validation performance stops improving.
- Technique: data augmentation. Intuition: encode valid input-preserving transformations.
- Technique: ensembling. Intuition: combine models to reduce some variance.

Regularization cannot repair mislabeled data, a broken split, or a metric unrelated to
the real objective.

## 8. Numerical and Systems Boundaries

- Boundary: data loader. Engineering question: Are order, decoding, and augmentation reproducible?
- Boundary: tensor shape. Engineering question: Are dimensions and broadcasting intentional?
- Boundary: numeric precision. Engineering question: Can overflow, underflow, or cancellation occur?
- Boundary: accelerator transfer. Engineering question: Is copy/synchronization cost measured?
- Boundary: checkpoint. Engineering question: Are model, optimizer, schema, and version recorded?
- Boundary: inference service. Engineering question: Are latency, batching, memory, and fallback bounded?
- Boundary: monitoring. Engineering question: Can drift and quality degradation be detected?

## 9. ML and Language Tools

- Possible use: code completion/ranking. Deterministic guardrail: parser/type checker rejects invalid output.
- Possible use: optimization heuristic. Deterministic guardrail: semantic equivalence tests.
- Possible use: decompiler naming suggestion. Deterministic guardrail: evidence and analyst confirmation.
- Possible use: fuzz-input prioritization. Deterministic guardrail: coverage and reproducible seeds.
- Possible use: anomaly detection in traces. Deterministic guardrail: retain raw evidence and explain uncertainty.
- Possible use: performance autotuning. Deterministic guardrail: benchmark budget and safe fallback.

Do not replace a cheap exact rule with a probabilistic model merely because ML is
available. Use ML when approximation creates value and its uncertainty can be contained.

Note. Next: formal-language and compiler chapters show how grammars, types, I R, and
runtimes turn human-readable rules into executable state transitions. The same
optimization and approximation ideas reappear there without statistical uncertainty.

# Formal Languages and Grammar: Defining Valid Structure

A formal grammar is a set of rules that define what makes valid code in a
programming language. Think of it like the grammar of English: “The cat sat” is valid,
but “Cat the sat” is not.

Note. Mental model: a grammar is the language's structural contract; the parser is
the program that checks that contract and turns matching text into structure.

For programming languages, a grammar specifies:

- What tokens are valid - keywords like def, if, return; operators like
plus, -, asterisk; literals like 42, true
- How tokens can be combined - 1 plus 2 is valid, plus plus 1 is not
- The structure of programs - functions contain statements, statements contain
expressions

# Parsing: From Tokens to Syntax Trees

A parser takes raw source code (a string) and converts it into a structured Abstract
Syntax Tree (A S T) that a computer can evaluate or compile. Think of source code as a
sentence and the A S T as its grammatical diagram. It captures structure, not just text.

Note. Parser invariant: every accepted input produces a well-formed structure, and
every rejected input produces a bounded, useful diagnostic rather than a crash.

Here is the step-by-step process of building one from scratch using Rust and the pest
crate:

## 1. Setting Up the Lexer/Grammar

Grammar rules are built from expressions (hence "parsing expression grammar"). These
expressions are a terse, formal description of how to parse an input string.

Expressions are composable: they can be built out of other expressions and nested
inside of each other to produce arbitrarily complex rules (although you should break
very complicated expressions into multiple rules to make them easier to manage).

P E G expressions are suitable for both high-level meaning, like "a function
signature, followed by a function body", and low-level meaning, like "a semicolon,
followed by a line feed". The combining form "followed by", the sequence operator, is
the same in either case.

### Macro Captures Used in Grammar Code

- Within Macros: $x:ty. Explanation: Macro capture (here a $x is the capture and ty means x must be type)..
- Within Macros: $x:block. Explanation: A block {} of statements or expressions, e.g., { let x = 5; }.
- Within Macros: $x:expr. Explanation: An expression, e.g., x, 1 plus 1, String new() or vec![].
- Within Macros: $x:ident. Explanation: An identifier, for example in let x = 0; the identifier is x..
- Within Macros: $x:item. Explanation: An item, like a function, struct, module, etc..
- Within Macros: $x:lifetime. Explanation: A lifetime (e.g., 'a, 'static, etc.)..
- Within Macros: $x:literal. Explanation: A literal (e.g., 3, "foo", b"bar", etc.)..
- Within Macros: $x:meta. Explanation: A meta item; the things that go inside number sign […] and number sign![…] attributes..
- Within Macros: $x:pat. Explanation: A pattern, such as Some(x), (17, 'a'), or an OR-pattern..
- Within Macros: $x:pat param. Explanation: A pattern accepted as a parameter without a top-level OR-pattern..
- Within Macros: $x:path. Explanation: A path (e.g., foo, std mem replace, transmute, int )..
- Within Macros: $x:stmt. Explanation: A statement, e.g., let x = 1 plus 1;, String new(); or vec![];.
- Within Macros: $x:tt. Explanation: A single token tree..
- Within Macros: $x:ty. Explanation: A type, e.g., String, you size or vector of u8..
- Within Macros: $x:vis. Explanation: A visibility modifier; pub, pub(crate), etc..

### Pest

First, you need rules that define what makes valid code. Using a parser generator (like
the pest crate in Rust), you define a grammar file (e.g., grammar.pest). The tool
uses this grammar to validate the text and tokenize the raw string into an iterator of
parsed "pairs" (tokens) — e.g., pulling out operators, numbers, and nested expressions.

- Syntax: "text". Meaning: Match exact text. Example: "def" matches the keyword def.
- Syntax: ~. Meaning: Sequence (then). Example: "if" ~ "(" ~ Expr ~ ")" matches if followed by (.
- Syntax: or. Meaning: Choice (or). Example: "true" or "false" matches either.
- Syntax: asterisk. Meaning: Zero or more (Kleene star). Example: Stmt asterisk matches any number of statements.
- Syntax: plus. Meaning: One or more. Example: ASCII DIGIT plus matches one or more digits.
- Syntax: ?. Meaning: Optional. Example: ReturnType? matches zero or one return type.
- Syntax: { }. Meaning: Rule definition. Example: Add = { " plus " } defines a rule.
- Syntax: { }. Meaning: Silent rule. Example: { Expr } matches but does not appear in A S T.
- Syntax: @{ }. Meaning: Atomic rule. Example: @{ ASCII DIGIT plus } matches as a single token.
- Syntax: SOI. Meaning: Start of input. Example: Beginning of the source code.
- Syntax: EOI. Meaning: End of input. Example: End of the source code.

#### Example: Calculator Grammar

### Cheat sheet

- Syntax: foo = {... }. Meaning: regular rule. Syntax: baz = @{... }. Meaning: atomic.
- Syntax: bar = {... }. Meaning: silent. Syntax: qux = ${... }. Meaning: compound-atomic.
- Syntax: number sign tag =.... Meaning: tags. Syntax: plugh =!{... }. Meaning: non-atomic.
- Syntax: "abc". Meaning: exact string. Syntax: ^"abc". Meaning: case insensitive.
- Syntax: 'a'..'z'. Meaning: character range. Syntax: ANY. Meaning: any character.
- Syntax: foo ~ bar. Meaning: sequence. Syntax: baz or qux. Meaning: ordered choice.
- Syntax: foo asterisk. Meaning: zero or more. Syntax: bar plus. Meaning: one or more.
- Syntax: baz?. Meaning: optional. Syntax: qux{n}. Meaning: exactly n.
- Syntax: qux{m, n}. Meaning: between m and n (inclusive). Syntax: not specified. Meaning: not specified.
- Syntax: shared reference to foo. Meaning: positive predicate. Syntax: !bar. Meaning: negative predicate.
- Syntax: PUSH(baz). Meaning: match and push. Syntax: PUSH LITERAL("a"). Meaning: push without match.
- Syntax: POP. Meaning: match and pop. Syntax: PEEK. Meaning: match without pop.
- Syntax: DROP. Meaning: pop without matching. Syntax: PEEK ALL. Meaning: match entire stack.

## 2. Defining the Abstract Syntax Tree (A S T)

The A S T captures the meaning and structure of the code, not just the raw text. In
Rust, this is typically done using enum to represent different node types:

- Terminal Nodes (Leaves): e.g., Node Int(i32) for raw numbers.
- Unary Expressions: e.g., Node UnaryExpr { op, child } for operations like -5.
- Binary Expressions: e.g., Node BinaryExpr { op, lhs, rhs } for operations like
1 plus 2. (Note: Box Node is used for the children to allow recursive nesting).

The tree naturally encodes the order of operations through its nesting.

Note. Note: Box Node is required for the children to allow recursive nesting. Without
Box, Rust wouldn't be able to calculate the size of the Node enum at compile time.

## 3. The Main Parsing Loop

The main entry point (often just parse(source: shared reference to str)) takes the raw string, feeds it
to the parser generator, and loops through the resulting top-level tokens. When it finds
a top-level expression, it passes that token to a recursive builder function to
construct the A S T.

## 4. Parsing Expressions and Associativity (Composite Nodes)

This is the core logic that converts generic pest tokens into your custom A S T nodes by
looking at the rule:

- Unary Expressions: Grab the operator and the single child term, then construct the
Unary node.
- Binary Expressions: Handling chained operations (like 1 plus 2 plus 3) requires
left-associativity so it evaluates as (1 plus 2) plus 3. Instead of using pure
recursion (which might result in right-associativity), this is often handled using a
loop:
1. Parse the initial Left-Hand Side (LHS), operator, and Right-Hand Side (RHS).
2. Build the first BinaryExpr node.
3. The Loop: If there is another operator immediately following in the token
stream, take the expression you just built and make it the new LHS, grab the
next number as the RHS, and build a new, nested BinaryExpr.

## 5. Parsing Terms (Leaf Nodes)

When the parser drills down to a basic term (Rule Term), it hits the bottom of the
tree. It expects one of two things:

1. A raw number: It parses the string into an i32 and returns a Node Int.
2. A nested expression: If it hits parentheses, it recurses back up to the
expression parsing logic to evaluate the inside of the parentheses first.

# Parsing Theory Through PEGs and Pest

Note. Library lens: Pest is used here to expose P E G recognition, ordered choice, lookahead, concrete parse trees, precedence, and the difference between accepted syntax and semantic meaning.

Pest turns a parsing expression grammar (P E G) into a Rust
parser. It is a good fit for a small language because the grammar remains readable while
Rust code handles A S T construction, validation, diagnostics, and later compiler phases.

Note. Mental model: a Pest grammar recognizes concrete syntax. Your Rust conversion
code decides which recognized details become meaningful A S T nodes.

## 1. P E G Choice Is Ordered

In a P E G, a | b means try a first; try b only if a fails. This differs from
thinking of alternatives as an unordered mathematical set.

- Operator: ~. Meaning: Sequence. Compiler use: let then name then = then value.
- Operator: or. Meaning: Ordered choice. Compiler use: statement form or expression form.
- Operator: asterisk. Meaning: Zero or more. Compiler use: arguments, statements, suffixes.
- Operator: plus. Meaning: One or more. Compiler use: digits, identifier characters.
- Operator: ?. Meaning: Optional. Compiler use: type annotation or trailing comma.
- Operator: shared reference to rule. Meaning: Positive lookahead. Compiler use: Require a prefix without consuming.
- Operator: !rule. Meaning: Negative lookahead. Compiler use: Reject reserved words or terminators.

Repetition binds more tightly than predicates, then sequence, then ordered choice. Add
parentheses when a reader might otherwise have to remember that precedence.

## 2. Whitespace Is a Grammar Policy

When rules named WHITESPACE or COMMENT exist, Pest can insert them implicitly
between sequence and repetition expressions. That is convenient for language-level
tokens, but dangerous inside indivisible tokens such as identifiers and numbers.

- Modifier: not specified. Parse-tree effect: Silent: omit this rule. Whitespace effect: Normal. Typical use: punctuation, whitespace.
- Modifier: @. Parse-tree effect: Atomic: inner rules become silent. Whitespace effect: Disable implicit whitespace. Typical use: identifiers, numbers.
- Modifier: $. Parse-tree effect: Compound atomic: keep inner rule pairs. Whitespace effect: Disable implicit whitespace. Typical use: structured string token.
- Modifier: !. Parse-tree effect: Non-atomic inside an atomic context. Whitespace effect: Re-enable implicit whitespace. Typical use: interpolation.

Atomic does not mean thread-safe. Here it means “match this grammar region as one
whitespace-sensitive unit.”

## 3. Anchor Complete Inputs

Without SOI and EOI, a parser may recognize only a valid prefix and leave unexpected
text behind.

- Entry rule: program. Appropriate contract: Must consume the whole source file.
- Entry rule: expression. Appropriate contract: May parse a nested region selected by another rule.
- Entry rule: editor fragment. Appropriate contract: May intentionally accept incomplete, recoverable text.

## 4. Pair Is a Concrete-Syntax View, Not the A S T

Each Pair Rule identifies the matched rule, matched text, byte span, and nested pairs.
Convert it promptly into your own A S T so the rest of the compiler is independent of
Pest's tree shape.

Pest spans are byte offsets into the original U T F eight input. If diagnostics also need
line and column, compute or cache that view deliberately; never treat a byte offset as
a character index.

## 5. Keep Syntax and Semantic Errors Separate

- Failure layer: Grammar. Example: Missing ). Best owner: Pest parse error.
- Failure layer: A S T lowering. Example: Integer does not fit in i64. Best owner: A S T conversion error.
- Failure layer: Name binding. Example: Unknown variable. Best owner: Resolver.
- Failure layer: Type checking. Example: Add integer to function. Best owner: Type checker.
- Failure layer: Codegen. Example: Unsupported target representation. Best owner: Backend.

## 6. Use Pratt Parsing for Expression Precedence

Pest's PrattParser maps a flat operator/operand stream into the intended precedence
and associativity. Define operators from lowest to highest precedence.
