- Expression: 2 plus 3 asterisk 4. Required shape: 2 plus (3 asterisk 4). Reason: Multiplication is higher.
- Expression: 10 - 3 - 2. Required shape: (10 - 3) - 2. Reason: Subtraction is left-associative.
- Expression: 2 ^ 3 ^ 2. Required shape: 2 ^ (3 ^ 2). Reason: Power is commonly right-associative.
- Expression: -x ^ 2. Required shape: Language-defined. Reason: Prefix precedence must be explicit.

Note. Invariant: after A S T construction, precedence is encoded by tree shape. Later
compiler phases should not need to remember the grammar's precedence table.

## 7. Test the Grammar at Three Scales

- Scale: Rule. Test input: one identifier, literal, or operator. Assertion: exact match and rejection cases.
- Scale: Construct. Test input: one expression or statement. Assertion: A S T shape, span, associativity.
- Scale: Program. Test input: a small file. Assertion: complete parse and diagnostics.
- Scale: Adversarial. Test input: truncation, deep nesting, huge literals. Assertion: bounded behavior and clear rejection.

Snapshotting a debug string is useful early, but mature compilers should compare typed
A S T values so formatting changes do not break semantic tests.

## 8. Rule Modifiers Control Both Whitespace and Tree Shape

Pest rule modifiers are not cosmetic. They change whether implicit whitespace is
allowed and which nodes appear in the concrete parse tree.

- Rule form: name = {... }. Meaning: normal rule; produces a pair. Typical use: meaningful syntax construct.
- Rule form: name = {... }. Meaning: silent rule; matches but produces no pair. Typical use: whitespace or grouping helper.
- Rule form: name = @{... }. Meaning: atomic; disables implicit whitespace and silences inner rules. Typical use: identifiers and simple literals.
- Rule form: name = ${... }. Meaning: compound atomic; disables whitespace but keeps inner pairs. Typical use: structured string/token forms.
- Rule form: name =!{... }. Meaning: non-atomic even inside an atomic caller. Typical use: interpolation with normal syntax.

The identifier is atomic because hello world must not become one identifier merely
because the grammar defines implicit whitespace. The interpolation rule becomes
non-atomic so an expression such as ${left plus right} can use ordinary spacing.

Note. Tree-shape trap: changing {... } to {... } can make correct matching
code fail later because the corresponding Pair disappears. Treat the concrete parse
tree as an interface and cover it with tests.

## 9. Predicates Inspect Without Consuming

Positive ( and ) and negative (!) predicates are P E G lookahead operations. They test the
next input but leave the parser at the same byte position.

- Predicate: shared reference to rule. Reads input?: yes. Consumes input?: no. Use: require a following shape.
- Predicate: !rule. Reads input?: yes. Consumes input?: no. Use: reject a following shape.
- Predicate: !terminator ~ ANY. Reads input?: yes. Consumes input?: one character. Use: consume until a delimiter.

Every successful repetition must make progress. A repeated expression that can succeed
without consuming input is a design smell because the parser cannot advance:

## 10. The Grammar Stack Handles Matched Delimiters

Pest's grammar stack stores matched text, not arbitrary semantic values. PUSH
records text; PEEK checks it without removing it; POP checks and removes it; DROP
removes it without matching.

One useful case is a raw string whose closing delimiter must contain the same number of
number sign characters as its opening delimiter:

- Good grammar-stack use: matching a repeated delimiter. Better handled elsewhere: symbol tables and name resolution.
- Good grammar-stack use: indentation prefix tracking. Better handled elsewhere: type checking.
- Good grammar-stack use: opener-dependent closing token. Better handled elsewhere: arbitrary runtime state.
- Good grammar-stack use: exact textual equality later. Better handled elsewhere: semantic equality of decoded values.

Keep the stack localized to one grammar feature. If many distant rules depend on it,
the grammar becomes difficult to reason about and error recovery becomes fragile.

Note. Next: the execution-pipeline section treats the A S T as the trusted input to an
interpreter or backend. Pest-specific details stop here; the language model continues.

# Abstract Syntax Trees and the Execution Pipeline

## The Pipeline

Walking through 1 plus 2:

1. Grammar — Rules defining valid syntax. 1 plus 2 matches; plus plus 1 doesn't.
2. Lexer (Tokenizer) — Breaks source into meaningful chunks: "1 plus 2" leads to
[1, plus, 2]. Tracks source location for error messages.
3. Parser — Builds a tree structure from tokens: plus at the root, 1 and 2 as
children — this is the A S T.
4. Interpreter/Evaluator — Walks the A S T recursively and computes the result.

Note. A S T analogy: Think of source code as a sentence and the A S T as its grammatical
diagram. Just as "The cat sat" is diagrammed into subject/verb, 1 plus 2 is diagrammed
into left/operator/right. The A S T captures structure, not just text.

We need a systematic way to turn any A S T into L L V M I R.

The answer is recursive tree traversal. We already do this in the interpreter - walk the
tree, evaluate each node. For code generation, we walk the tree and emit instructions
for each node instead of computing values.

Two common patterns help structure this:

- Builder pattern - Used for L L V M I R generation
- Visitor pattern - for A S T transformations

## Builder Pattern

Think of the L L V M builder like a cursor in a text editor. You position it somewhere in
your code, then “type” instructions at that position. The builder keeps track of where
you are and ensures instructions are added in the right place.

Let’s compare our interpreter’s recursive evaluation to the new J I T approach: the
interpreter walks the tree and computes values directly, while the builder walks the
tree and emits instructions instead.

## The Interpreter and Recursion

The C P U is the ultimate interpreter — it executes opcodes one at a time. Our software
interpreter does the same at a higher level by walking the A S T recursively:

To evaluate 1 plus 2:

1. Evaluate left (1) leads to 1
2. Evaluate right (2) leads to 2
3. Apply operator ( plus ) leads to 3

If the left side were (3 plus 4), we'd recursively evaluate it first. This is why trees
are powerful: structure determines evaluation order. Parse leads to A S T leads to recursive eval is
the foundation of every interpreter — Python, Ruby, JavaScript all do this with more
node types.

## State and Functions

A calculator is stateless — input goes in, output comes out, nothing persists. A
real language is a state machine:

- Language feature: Variables. Example: n. Role in execution: Named storage.
- Language feature: Functions. Example: fib. Role in execution: Abstraction and reuse.
- Language feature: Parameters. Example: passing n. Role in execution: Data flow into a call.
- Language feature: Conditionals. Example: if / else. Role in execution: Control-flow branching.
- Language feature: Operators. Example: , -. Role in execution: Computation.
- Language feature: Recursion. Example: fib calling fib. Role in execution: Self-reference.
- Language feature: Call stack. Example: one frame per call. Role in execution: Tracks local execution and returns.

The A S T becomes a program to execute, not just an expression to evaluate.

## Variables

Variables let us store values and refer to them by name. Without variables, every
computation would need to repeat its literal inputs — variables give us memory.

Reassigning a variable means any code referencing it automatically uses the new value,
which makes code reusable and readable.

Where variables live: the environment (storage) — a HashMap inside a frame.

- Local variables exist inside a function.
- Global variables exist outside all functions.
- Variables can be reassigned.

This simple mechanism — storing and looking up names — underlies all programming:

- Concept: Parameters. Role: Variables bound from function call arguments.
- Concept: Loop counters. Role: Variables that change each iteration.
- Concept: Object fields. Role: Variables attached to an object.

Note. The environment is one of the most important data structures in any interpreter.

## Functions

Functions are the heart of any programming language. Without them, code must be
copy-pasted every time it's reused. Functions let us:

1. Name a computation, to refer to it later
2. Parameterize it with inputs, so it works with different values
3. Reuse it — write once, call anywhere

Parameters: names for the inputs a function expects. When we bind parameters, we
are associating names with argument values.

Functions enable abstraction: calling fibonacci(10) hides how Fibonacci is
computed behind a simple name.

### How Function Calls Work

Evaluating add(3, 4) triggers a fixed sequence:

1. Look up the function by name
2. Evaluate the arguments (3, 4)
3. Create a new frame for local variables
4. Bind parameters to arguments (a = 3, b = 4)
5. Execute the function body
6. Return the result and pop the frame

## The Call Stack

Call Stack: Runtime data structure tracking function calls. Each call pushes a
frame; return pops it.

Each frame on the stack represents one in-progress function call. The stack
grows on call and shrinks on return — last in, first out (LIFO).

One frame per call: two calls to foo each get their own x. Call 1's x = 5
never interferes with call 2's x = 10, because they live in separate frames. This
isolation is essential for recursion, where the same function appears on the stack
multiple times, each with its own variables.

## Control Flow

Programs need to decide ("if logged in, show dashboard") and repeat ("while
items remain, sum their prices"). Without control flow, code only runs straight-line,
top to bottom.

### Conditionals: if / else

An if expression evaluates a condition and picks a branch:

1. Evaluate the condition (e.g. x 0)
2. Choose a branch — then if true, else if false
3. Return whatever that branch produces

### Loops: while

A while loop repeats its body while a condition holds true, using Rust's loop
construct with a condition check each iteration:

Note. After the body runs, execution returns to the top and re-checks the condition — this
is what creates repetition.

### Control Flow in Functions

- Nested conditionals — for when one condition isn't enough
- return in loops — exits the function immediately, even from deep inside a loop;
useful for "search" patterns

### Control Flow as Branching

Both if and while change the flow of execution, letting code:

- Skip code (untaken if branch)
- Repeat code (while body)
- Exit early (return inside a loop)

In compiled languages, these become branch instructions — the C P U jumps to different
memory locations. (In Secondlang, if compiles to L L V M I R's br instruction.)

# Recursion, Stacks, and Recursive Structure

Recursion is when a function calls itself. It seems paradoxical — how can a function
call itself mid-execution? — but the call stack makes it work cleanly.

Note. Analogy: Russian nesting dolls (matryoshka). Each doll contains a smaller version
of itself, down to the smallest doll, which contains nothing.

## The Key Insight

Every recursive function needs two parts:

- Part: Base case. Purpose: Returns directly, no further recursion — the "smallest doll".
- Part: Recursive case. Purpose: Calls itself on a smaller problem, then combines the result.

- No base case leads to recursion never stops leads to stack overflow
- No recursive case leads to not actually recursion, just a regular function

## Why Recursion Works

Three design decisions make it work:

1. Functions are stored globally — factorial can look itself up by name mid-call.
2. Each call gets its own frame — factorial(3) called from within factorial(4)
has an n fully independent of the outer n = 4.
3. Return values propagate correctly — factorial(1) returns 1 leads to used by
factorial(2) as 2 asterisk 1 = 2 leads to and so on up the stack.

Note. Using a single global n instead of per-call frames would break recursion: each call
would overwrite the shared n.

Mutual recursion: functions can call each other recursively.

Stack overflow risk: every recursive call consumes stack memory; very deep
recursion exhausts it.

Note. Some languages implement tail call optimization to run certain recursive functions
in constant stack space — not implemented here, but a notable optimization.

## When to Use Recursion

Recursion fits problems with recursive structure:

- Trees (each subtree is a smaller tree)
- Mathematical sequences (Fibonacci, factorial)
- Divide-and-conquer algorithms (merge sort, quicksort)
- Parsing nested structures (J S O N, H T M L, an A S T)

For simple loops, iteration is usually clearer — but for inherently recursive problems,
recursion is more natural.

## Operators

An operator tells us what to do. Values and operands are the things we operate on.

- Operator: !. Example: ident!(...), ident!{...}, ident![...]. Explanation: Macro expansion. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: !. Example: !expr. Explanation: Bitwise or logical complement. Overloadable?: Not.
- Operator: not equals. Example: expr not equals expr. Explanation: Nonequality comparison. Overloadable?: PartialEq.
- Operator: %. Example: expr % expr. Explanation: Arithmetic remainder. Overloadable?: Rem.
- Operator: %=. Example: var %= expr. Explanation: Arithmetic remainder and assignment. Overloadable?: RemAssign.
- Operator: and. Example: shared reference to expr, mutable reference to expr. Explanation: Borrow. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: and. Example: shared reference to type, mutable reference to type, and 'a type, and 'a mut type. Explanation: Borrowed pointer type. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: and. Example: expr and expr. Explanation: Bitwise AND. Overloadable?: BitAnd.
- Operator: and =. Example: var and = expr. Explanation: Bitwise AND and assignment. Overloadable?: BitAndAssign.
- Operator: and and. Example: expr and and expr. Explanation: Short-circuiting logical AND. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: asterisk. Example: expr asterisk expr. Explanation: Arithmetic multiplication. Overloadable?: Mul.
- Operator: asterisk =. Example: var asterisk = expr. Explanation: Arithmetic multiplication and assignment. Overloadable?: MulAssign.
- Operator: asterisk. Example: asterisk expr. Explanation: Dereference. Overloadable?: Deref.
- Operator: asterisk. Example: asterisk const type, asterisk mut type. Explanation: Raw pointer. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: plus. Example: trait plus trait, 'a plus trait. Explanation: Compound type constraint. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: plus. Example: expr plus expr. Explanation: Arithmetic addition. Overloadable?: Add.
- Operator: plus =. Example: var plus = expr. Explanation: Arithmetic addition and assignment. Overloadable?: AddAssign.
- Operator: ,. Example: expr, expr. Explanation: Argument and element separator. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: -. Example: - expr. Explanation: Arithmetic negation. Overloadable?: Neg.
- Operator: -. Example: expr - expr. Explanation: Arithmetic subtraction. Overloadable?: Sub.
- Operator: -=. Example: var -= expr. Explanation: Arithmetic subtraction and assignment. Overloadable?: SubAssign.
- Operator: -. Example: fn(...) - type, and vert;... and vert; -> type. Explanation: Function and closure return type. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: .. Example: expr.ident. Explanation: Field access. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: .. Example: expr.ident(expr,...). Explanation: Method call. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: .. Example: expr.0, expr.1, and so on. Explanation: Tuple indexing. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: ... Example: .., expr..,..expr, expr..expr. Explanation: Right-exclusive range literal. Overloadable?: PartialOrd.
- Operator: ..=. Example: ..=expr, expr..=expr. Explanation: Right-inclusive range literal. Overloadable?: PartialOrd.
- Operator: ... Example: ..expr. Explanation: Struct literal update syntax. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: ... Example: variant(x,..), struct type { x,.. }. Explanation: “And the rest” pattern binding. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: .... Example: expr...expr. Explanation: (Deprecated, use..= instead) In a pattern: inclusive range pattern. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: /. Example: expr / expr. Explanation: Arithmetic division. Overloadable?: Div.
- Operator: /=. Example: var /= expr. Explanation: Arithmetic division and assignment. Overloadable?: DivAssign.
- Operator: :. Example: pat: type, ident: type. Explanation: Constraints. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: :. Example: ident: expr. Explanation: Struct field initializer. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: :. Example: 'a: loop {...}. Explanation: Loop label. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: ;. Example: expr;. Explanation: Statement and item terminator. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: ;. Example: [...; len]. Explanation: Part of fixed-size array syntax. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: not specified. Example: expr expr. Explanation: Left-shift. Overloadable?: Shl.
- Operator: less than or equal to. Example: var less than or equal to expr. Explanation: Left-shift and assignment. Overloadable?: ShlAssign.
- Operator: not specified. Example: expr expr. Explanation: Less than comparison. Overloadable?: PartialOrd.
- Operator: less than or equal to. Example: expr less than or equal to expr. Explanation: Less than or equal to comparison. Overloadable?: PartialOrd.
- Operator: =. Example: var = expr, ident = type. Explanation: Assignment/equivalence. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: equals equals. Example: expr equals equals expr. Explanation: Equality comparison. Overloadable?: PartialEq.
- Operator: =. Example: pat = expr. Explanation: Part of match arm syntax. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: not specified. Example: expr expr. Explanation: Greater than comparison. Overloadable?: PartialOrd.
- Operator: greater than or equal to. Example: expr greater than or equal to expr. Explanation: Greater than or equal to comparison. Overloadable?: PartialOrd.
- Operator: not specified. Example: expr expr. Explanation: Right-shift. Overloadable?: Shr.
- Operator: greater than or equal to. Example: var greater than or equal to expr. Explanation: Right-shift and assignment. Overloadable?: ShrAssign.
- Operator: @. Example: ident @ pat. Explanation: Pattern binding. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: ^. Example: expr ^ expr. Explanation: Bitwise exclusive OR. Overloadable?: BitXor.
- Operator: ^=. Example: var ^= expr. Explanation: Bitwise exclusive OR and assignment. Overloadable?: BitXorAssign.
- Operator: and vert;. Example: pat and vert; pat. Explanation: Pattern alternatives. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: and vert;. Example: expr and vert; expr. Explanation: Bitwise OR. Overloadable?: BitOr.
- Operator: and vert;=. Example: var and vert;= expr. Explanation: Bitwise OR and assignment. Overloadable?: BitOrAssign.
- Operator: and vert; and vert;. Example: expr and vert; and vert; expr. Explanation: Short-circuiting logical OR. Overloadable?: not specified.
- Operator: ?. Example: expr?. Explanation: Error propagation. Overloadable?: not specified.

### State machine

If you pause a program in a debugger, you are looking at its current state. The call
stack tells you how the program got to this state and what local data it currently has
access to within that specific function frame. In computer science terms, adding a stack
to a finite state machine turns it into a Pushdown Automaton—allowing it to handle
nesting, recursion, and scoped memory.

- Concise definition: A mathematical model of computation representing a system that
can be in exactly one of a finite number of conditions (states) at any given time. The
machine changes from one state to another (a transition) in response to specific
inputs or events.
- Programming definition: A system that maintains a "memory" of its current
condition and changes that condition based on incoming events. Unlike a stateless
system (like a basic calculator), a state machine's output depends on its accumulated
history — which in a programming language is tracked via variables, memory heaps, and
the call stack.

Core concepts:

- State — the current condition or snapshot of the system's memory (e.g., current
variable values, active call stack).
- Inputs/Events — the actions or instructions fed into the system (e.g., executing
the next line of code).
- Transitions — the rules that dictate how the system moves from one state to the
next based on the input.

## Function Call Sequence

For add(3, 4):

1. Look up — Find the function value by name.
2. Evaluate arguments — Compute 3 and 4.
3. Push frame — Create a fresh environment (scope) on the call stack.
4. Bind parameters — a = 3, b = 4.
5. Execute body — Run function instructions.
6. Pop frame — Return result to the caller; the frame below becomes current again.

Note. Call stack analogy: A stack of sticky notes. Each call writes variables on a new
note and puts it on top. Returning tears it off. This is why inner()'s variables
don't overwrite outer()'s — they're on different notes.

Each frame is just a HashMap pushed and popped like any stack. Grammar grows, A S T
nodes multiply, but execution is still recursive tree traversal — now with scoped state.

## The REPL

A Read-Eval-Print Loop provides an interactive environment:

1. Read — Get a line of input.
2. Eval — Parse and execute it.
3. Print — Show the result.
4. Loop — Return to step 1.

A working REPL with variables, functions, conditionals, operators, recursion, and a call
stack means you have a real programming language. The culminating proof is recursive
Fibonacci:

Multi-line input: a REPL needs to know when a statement is incomplete (e.g., mid
function body) versus ready to evaluate. One approach: track bracket depth — count
unmatched {, (, [ (ignoring characters inside strings). While depth is positive,
keep reading lines and show a continuation prompt (...); once it returns to zero,
evaluate the accumulated input.

# Types, Constraints, and Type Inference

## Static vs. Dynamic Typing

Types act as contracts — when you write a: int, you promise a will always be an
integer. The compiler enforces that promise.

Note. Type-system goal: reject impossible or unsafe programs early while preserving
useful expressiveness.

- Approach: Static. When Checked: Compile time (before running). Examples: Rust, C, Java, Haskell.
- Approach: Dynamic. When Checked: Runtime (while running). Examples: Python, JavaScript, Ruby.

Trade-offs:

- Static — Catches bugs early, enables better performance, requires upfront
annotations.
- Dynamic — More flexible, faster to prototype, but bugs can hide until runtime.

## Types Enable Fast Code

When the compiler knows x and y are i64, it generates a single C P U instruction
for x asterisk y.

Without types, a dynamic interpreter must at runtime:

1. Check what type x is.
2. Look up the multiplication operation for that type.
3. Check operand compatibility.
4. Handle potential type errors.
5. Finally, multiply.

This overhead makes dynamically typed languages 10–100 times slower for numeric work.
This is why J I T compilers like V eight (JavaScript) and PyPy (Python) invest heavily
in type speculation — guessing types to generate fast paths.

## Type Inference

Inference means the compiler deduces types automatically — no annotations needed in
most cases. Types flow forward from known sources (literals, annotated parameters)
through operations into variables.

Note. Type inference analogy: Like solving a crossword puzzle. Some squares have letters
(explicit annotations); others are blank (Unknown). Constraints like "this is added
to an int, so it must be int" fill in the blanks.

Key mechanisms:

- Unification — Checks if two types are compatible and finds a common type.
Resolving Unknown with a concrete type is how the compiler learns what an unknown
type should be.
- Type Environment — A HashMap String, Type mapping names to types. Also called
symbol table or context

The environment is:

- Extended when we declare a variable or enter a function (adding new bindings)
- Queried when we reference a variable (looking up its type)
- Scoped — inner scopes can shadow outer bindings

## Two-Pass Function Type Checking

Functions are trickier because we need to handle:

- Parameters (types come from annotations)
- Local variables (types are inferred)
- Return value (must match declared return type)

Functions can call each other (mutual recursion), requiring two passes:

1. Pass 1 — Collect Signatures — Scan all function definitions and record their type
signatures before checking any bodies. This ensures foo can call bar even if
bar is defined later.
2. Pass 2 — Check Bodies — Walk each function body, inferring and unifying types
throughout.

Per-expression pattern: recursively type-check sub-expressions leads to apply typing rule leads to
set type on this node.

Type inference works by:

1. Starting with known types — literals (42 leads to Int, true leads to Bool) and
annotated parameters.
2. Flowing types through expressions — operators, calls, assignments.
3. Recording types in the environment so variables can be looked up later.
4. Unifying types — checking compatibility and resolving Unknown.
5. Reporting errors when types conflict.

## Type Inference Approaches

- Approach: Hindley-Milner. Description: Infers polymorphic types like fn identity T (x: T) - T without any annotations.
- Approach: Local Inference. Description: Requires annotations at function boundaries; infers types within function bodies.

## The Typed (Decorated) A S T

Secondlang's type checker doesn't just validate types — it produces a new tree where
every expression carries its inferred type. This is called a decorated or
annotated A S T:

Nodes start as Type Unknown and get filled in during inference. Grammar-wise,
Secondlang barely changes from Firstlang — the expression rules (Expr, Comparison,
Additive,...) are identical. Only Function, TypedParam, ReturnType, Type, and
Assignment gain optional type annotations; the real growth happens in the compiler,
not the grammar.

# Bytecode and Virtual Machines

Bytecode is another technique for translating source code toward machine code: it
emulates the instruction set with a new, simplified, human-friendly encoding — an
intermediate representation that sits between source and assembly, lower-level than
source but higher-level than assembly language. It's executed by a V M.

## Why Bytecode?

- Approach: Direct A S T interpretation. Trade-off: Simple to implement, but slow.
- Approach: Bytecode + V M. Trade-off: Moderate complexity, significantly faster, highly portable.
- Approach: J I T to native machine code. Trade-off: Fastest, but complex (L L V M dependency, platform-specific).

- Simpler than J I T — No L L V M dependency; works everywhere.
- Faster than A S T — Bytecode is compact and cache-friendly.
- Portable — Same bytecode runs on any machine with your V M.

This is how Python (CPython), Java (JVM), and Ruby (YARV) work: compile source to
bytecode once, run the bytecode interpreter wherever you need it.

Is Python (or a language X) Compiled or Interpreted?

Being A O T compiled, J I T compiled or interpreted is implementation-dependent. For
example, the standard Python implementation is CPython which compiles a Python source
code (in CPython V M) to CPython Bytecode (contents of.pyc) and interprets the Bytecode.
However, another implementation of Python is PyPy which (more or less) compiles a Python
source code (in PyPy V M) to PyPy Bytecode and J I T compiles the PyPy Bytecode to the
Machine Code (and is usually faster than CPython interpreter).

### Bytecode Structure

- instructions - A flat array of bytes. Opcodes and their arguments, all mixed
together.
- constants - A table of literal values. Instead of encoding 42 in the instruction
stream, we store it in the constants table and reference it by index.

## The Stack Machine

We've seen two ways to execute code: interpret the A S T directly, or J I T compile to
native machine code. There's a third approach that sits in between: compile to
bytecode, then interpret that. Our V M is a Stack Machine — it keeps intermediate
values on a stack.

A stack machine has three components:

- Bytecode — the program to execute.
- A stack array supporting push/pop, used to hold intermediate values. A fixed-size
array is used for speed.
- An Instruction Pointer (IP) and Stack Pointer (SP), tracking which instruction
is executing and what's next.

Why stacks? They handle any nesting automatically. For (1 plus 2) asterisk (3 plus 4):

- Push 1, push 2, add leads to stack: [3]
- Push 3, push 4, add leads to stack: [3, 7]
- Multiply leads to stack: [21]

Every operation pops its inputs and pushes its output. The stack naturally tracks what's
"in progress."

Think of bytecode as a simplified assembly language designed for our V M.

- OpConstant(index) Push a constant onto the stack
- OpAdd Pop two values, push their sum
- OpSub Pop two values, push their difference
- OpPop Pop and discard the top value

Real assembly has hundreds of instructions.

V M execution of 1 plus 2 (fetch-decode-execute):

These four carry the arithmetic (OpSub not used in this example):

The V M reads instructions left-to-right with the Instruction Pointer (ip):

1. Fetch — Read next byte from bytecode.instructions[ip]
2. Decode — Match on the opcode
3. Execute — Manipulate the stack
4. Repeat — Increment IP; continue until out of instructions

# Runtime Architecture Through Rust-Hosted Languages

Note. Library lens: Rust-hosted language designs expose arenas, handles, call frames, roots, allocation, and host/runtime boundaries. The crate shape matters only because it makes those runtime mechanisms inspectable.

This section draws from
Writing Interpreters in Rust: a Guide.
Its central problem is deeper than parsing: how can a Rust host safely implement a
dynamic guest language whose objects, aliases, and garbage-collection rules are not
known to Rust's borrow checker?

Note. Host/guest boundary: Rust owns the runtime implementation; the guest language
defines the behavior of values that live inside it.

## 1. Host Language Versus Guest Language

- The host language implements the runtime. Here, the host is Rust.
- The guest language is parsed, compiled, and executed by that runtime.
- The mutator is ordinary guest-program execution that allocates and changes
objects.
- The collector traces, moves, marks, or reclaims guest objects.

Rust statically enforces Rust ownership. It cannot automatically prove the guest
language's runtime ownership model.

This means runtime authors must design a safe public abstraction around a small,
carefully reviewed unsafe core.

## 2. Separate Runtime Layers

A useful architecture has explicit boundaries:

Each layer should depend on the narrowest interface below it:

- Layer: Parser. Should understand: tokens, grammar, A S T nodes. Should not depend on: heap-block layout.
- Layer: Compiler. Should understand: A S T, constants, bytecode. Should not depend on: raw-pointer manipulation.
- Layer: V M. Should understand: instructions, values, handles. Should not depend on: parser internals.
- Layer: Allocator. Should understand: sizes, alignments, object kind. Should not depend on: source-language grammar.
- Layer: Collector. Should understand: roots and tracing metadata. Should not depend on: syntax or source-level declarations.

## 3. Allocation Is More Than Getting Bytes

A runtime allocation request must answer:

- Allocation property: Size. Question the runtime must answer: How many bytes are required?
- Allocation property: Alignment. Question the runtime must answer: At which address boundary may the object begin?
- Allocation property: Type. Question the runtime must answer: Which runtime object layout is being allocated?
- Allocation property: Tracing. Question the runtime must answer: Does it contain references to other managed objects?
- Allocation property: Initialization. Question the runtime must answer: When do all fields become safe for the G C to observe?
- Allocation property: Header lookup. Question the runtime must answer: How does a payload locate its metadata?
- Allocation property: Movement. Question the runtime must answer: May collection change the object's address?
- Allocation property: Failure. Question the runtime must answer: Does failure trigger G C, return an error, or terminate?

Conceptually:

The real header layout is part of the runtime A B I. Changing it affects allocation,
tracing, debugging, and possibly serialized heap images.

## 4. Bump Allocation Mental Model

A bump allocator keeps a cursor inside a block:

To allocate:

1. round the cursor up to the required alignment;
2. check whether aligned cursor plus size fits;
3. reserve that range;
4. advance the cursor;
5. initialize the object before exposing it.

Bump allocation is fast because the common path is arithmetic plus a bounds check. It
does not individually reclaim objects; a collector or arena reset handles reclamation.

Checked alignment helper:

## 5. Runtime Values

A dynamically typed language binds type information to values at runtime:

This handle-based sketch keeps raw heap addresses out of most code. A real runtime may
use direct pointers, indices, generations, tagged words, or a mixture.

- Representation decision: Small integers. Common choices: Inline/tagged or boxed.
- Representation decision: Booleans and nil. Common choices: Immediate singleton values or heap objects.
- Representation decision: Strings. Common choices: Interned, uniquely allocated, or mixed.
- Representation decision: Object addresses. Common choices: Stable or movable during collection.
- Representation decision: Equality. Common choices: Identity, contents, or type-dependent.
- Representation decision: Stale handles. Common choices: Generation counters, tables, or validation.

## 6. Tagged Values and Tagged Pointers

Aligned pointers have low bits that are normally zero. Some runtimes use those bits as a
small type tag:

Possible tags might mean:

- Tagged-value advantage: Identify common types without reading an object header. Tagged-value cost: Masking and shifting require carefully reviewed unsafe code.
- Tagged-value advantage: Keep small integers out of the heap. Tagged-value cost: The inline integer range becomes smaller.
- Tagged-value advantage: Fit a complete dynamic value in one machine word. Tagged-value cost: Available tag bits depend on guaranteed alignment.
- Tagged-value advantage: Reduce indirection for common values. Tagged-value cost: Debuggers and sanitizers see encoded pointers.
- Tagged-value advantage: Improve locality for value stacks and register arrays. Tagged-value cost: Portability and pointer-provenance rules need deliberate design.

Keep the encoded representation private. Expose safe constructors and accessors that
validate tags before decoding.

## 7. Object Headers Versus Inline Tags

Inline tags cannot represent unlimited types. A hybrid runtime may use:

The header is also a natural place for:

- Header field: Mark bits. Purpose: Record tracing state.
- Header field: Size or size class. Purpose: Locate boundaries and choose reclamation bin.
- Header field: Generation or age. Purpose: Support generational collection.
- Header field: Forwarding information. Purpose: Redirect references after movement.
- Header field: Immutable/frozen state. Purpose: Enforce guest-language mutation rules.
- Header field: Identity hash. Purpose: Preserve stable identity-based hashing.

Every extra header field costs memory for every object, so header design is a workload
trade-off.

## 8. Mutator Scopes and Rooting

The mutator and collector have conflicting views:

- normal execution wants stable access to individual objects;
- a moving collector may need exclusive access to the whole heap and must update every
reference.

A safe design ensures they cannot run simultaneously.

Long-lived guest references should normally be represented as traceable roots or
handles, not as unconstrained Rust references.

- Root source: V M value stack/register array. Why it keeps objects alive: Contains currently executing values.
- Root source: Active call frames. Why it keeps objects alive: Hold locals, closures, and return state.
- Root source: Globals and modules. Why it keeps objects alive: Remain reachable by name.
- Root source: Interned symbols. Why it keeps objects alive: Are retained by the runtime's intern table.
- Root source: Host-visible native handles. Why it keeps objects alive: Represent guest objects borrowed by native code.
- Root source: Pending exceptions. Why it keeps objects alive: Carry values until handled.
- Root source: Executable compiler constants. Why it keeps objects alive: Remain reachable from live functions.

If one live reference is absent from the root graph, a collector may reclaim a reachable
object.

## 9. Garbage-Collection Invariants

The exact collector can vary, but these invariants transfer:

- G C invariant: Reachability. Required property: Every live object is reachable through traceable edges.
- G C invariant: Initialization. Required property: Traceable fields are initialized before G C can observe them.
- G C invariant: Layout knowledge. Required property: The collector knows each object's layout or tracing procedure.
- G C invariant: Write barriers. Required property: Mutator writes preserve the collector's required metadata.
- G C invariant: Movement. Required property: Every reference is updated when an object moves.
- G C invariant: Reclamation. Required property: No valid handle can reach reclaimed memory.
- G C invariant: Finalization. Required property: Objects cannot resurrect through an untracked reference.
- G C invariant: Collection failure. Required property: The heap remains in a defined state.

Basic tracing shape:

Do not hold a normal Rust reference across an operation that may allocate if allocation
can trigger a moving collection.

## 10. Register-Based Bytecode

The hosted-languages guide uses a register-oriented V M influenced by Lua. Each compiled
function owns bytecode and literals, while call frames provide that function's
registers.

The compiler sees a construction interface:

- append an instruction;
- append/deduplicate a literal;
- reserve a register;
- patch a forward jump after its destination is known.

The V M sees an execution interface:

- fetch the instruction at the instruction pointer;
- advance or replace the instruction pointer;
- access frame registers;
- switch bytecode when calling or returning.

These are two views of the same data, much like ELF's linking and execution views.

## 11. Call Frames

A register V M frame may contain:

Real implementations often store all registers in one contiguous V M stack and let each
frame refer to a window. That avoids one Vec allocation per call.

- Frame invariant: Instruction pointer. Required relationship: Refers to the frame's own bytecode.
- Frame invariant: Register index. Required relationship: Is below the function's declared register count.
- Frame invariant: Arguments. Required relationship: Occupy the calling convention's expected registers.
- Frame invariant: Return destination. Required relationship: Belongs to the caller and remains valid.
- Frame invariant: Live values. Required relationship: Are visible to the garbage collector.

## 12. Safe-Unsafe Boundary Checklist

- Area: Isolation. Review requirement: Keep raw allocation and pointer arithmetic in one small module.
- Area: Safety contract. Review requirement: State alignment, initialization, aliasing, and lifetime requirements.
- Area: Tagged pointers. Review requirement: Never expose an encoded value as directly dereferenceable.
- Area: Domain types. Review requirement: Use newtypes for IDs, sizes, offsets, handles, and registers.
- Area: G C coordination. Review requirement: Make collection and mutation mutually exclusive.
- Area: Edge tests. Review requirement: Cover zero, maximum, overflow, and alignment-edge allocations.
- Area: Dynamic checks. Review requirement: Run Miri and sanitizers where applicable.
- Area: Fuzzing. Review requirement: Fuzz bytecode verification and object decoding.
- Area: Stable identity. Review requirement: Prefer handles when raw addresses may move.
- Area: Allocation audit. Review requirement: Treat every allocation point as a possible collection point.

## 13. Keep V M State Explicit and Split by Responsibility

The hosted-languages guide models one execution thread with distinct structures for
call frames, register values, open upvalues, globals, and the current instruction
stream. Keeping these roles separate prevents one overloaded “stack” from hiding
different invariants.

- State: frames. Primary invariant: top frame owns the active return context.
- State: registers. Primary invariant: active window fits within the allocated register stack.
- State: stack base. Primary invariant: points to the first register of the active call.
- State: open upvalues. Primary invariant: each captured stack slot has shared intermediary state.
- State: globals. Primary invariant: keys obey the language's name/environment policy.
- State: instruction location. Primary invariant: belongs to the active function's bytecode.

A register V M can reserve a fixed-width window for each call:

This is still a stack of activation storage, even though bytecodes name registers
instead of pushing and popping every operand.

### Call and Return Are Coordinated State Transitions

- Failure mode: bytecode names register 250 of 8. Required defense: verify register operands before execution.
- Failure mode: malformed return underflows frames. Required defense: verifier plus runtime boundary check.
- Failure mode: captured local dies with frame. Required defense: open/closed upvalue intermediary.
- Failure mode: collection misses a live register. Required defense: trace active windows and saved frame values.
- Failure mode: native call re-enters the V M. Required defense: document re-entrancy and rooting discipline.

Bytecode verification can move many checks out of the dispatch loop, but only if
unverified bytecode can never reach the fast path.

# Language Semantics Through a Minimal Lua Interpreter

Note. Library lens: The small Lua implementation is a semantic microscope: each Rust type corresponds to a value representation, environment rule, call transition, or runtime invariant.

This section follows the incremental idea from
Build a Lua Interpreter in Rust:
implement one tiny program through the complete pipeline, then grow the language without
discarding the architecture.

Note. Growth strategy: make one tiny program work end to end, then expand one
language feature and one test at a time.

## 1. A Small Program Contains Many Concepts

Even this one line crosses the complete language pipeline:

- Stage: Tokenizer. Work required for print "hello, world!": Recognize an identifier and string literal.
- Stage: Parser. Work required for print "hello, world!": Build a function-call expression.
- Stage: Value model. Work required for print "hello, world!": Represent a dynamic string and callable value.
- Stage: Name resolution. Work required for print "hello, world!": Resolve the global name print.
- Stage: Compiler. Work required for print "hello, world!": Store the string constant and emit call instructions.
- Stage: Calling convention. Work required for print "hello, world!": Place the argument where the callee expects it.
- Stage: Native boundary. Work required for print "hello, world!": Invoke the standard-library implementation.
- Stage: V M. Work required for print "hello, world!": Fetch, decode, and execute the generated bytecode.

This is a good vertical slice: tiny surface area, complete pipeline.

## 2. Define Bytecode Before Filling in the Pipeline

Bytecode becomes a contract between compiler and V M:

For the tiny program:

Semantic trace:

The exact bytecode is an implementation decision, not part of the Lua language
specification. Another compatible Lua implementation may use different instructions.

## 3. Chunk, Constants, and Globals Are Different

- The constant table belongs to compiled code and is addressed by compact indexes.
- The global table belongs to execution state and is addressed by language-level
names.
- Bytecode can first load a name from constants, then use that name to query globals.

Keeping these separate prevents compile-time representation from being confused with
runtime state.

## 4. Dynamic Values as an Enum

The enum makes every operation explicitly handle the dynamic type. Later, heap-backed
strings, tables, closures, and userdata may use reference-counted or garbage-collected
handles instead of owned String.

## 5. Keep the First V M Loop Obvious

This version favors clarity over optimization. Before adding features, add a bytecode
verifier so malformed instructions cannot index arbitrary registers or constants.

## 6. Module Layout That Can Grow

Split by runtime domain and responsibility. Do not make every internal type public.

## 7. Lexer and Parser Contract

The tiny grammar can be:

Tokens:

Even at this stage, retain source spans:

Spans let later compiler and runtime errors point back to the original source.

## 8. Grow by Preserving Invariants

- Feature: Multiple statements. Compiler/runtime addition: Loop until end-of-file.
- Feature: Local variables. Compiler/runtime addition: Scope table + register allocation.
- Feature: Assignment. Compiler/runtime addition: L-value representation + store instruction.
- Feature: Arithmetic. Compiler/runtime addition: Numeric coercion rules + arithmetic opcodes.
- Feature: Tables. Compiler/runtime addition: Heap object + indexed/name lookup.
- Feature: Conditionals. Compiler/runtime addition: Conditional jump + patching.
- Feature: Loops. Compiler/runtime addition: Backward jumps + break targets.
- Feature: Functions. Compiler/runtime addition: Prototypes, frames, call/return.
- Feature: Closures. Compiler/runtime addition: Upvalues and open/closed capture state.
- Feature: Garbage collection. Compiler/runtime addition: Roots, tracing, reclamation.

A jump offset may begin as forward-only and later need to become signed when loops add
backward edges. Representation choices should evolve when language semantics demand it.

## 9. Closures and Escaping Upvalues

An inner function may outlive the outer frame it references:

While the outer call is active, count can be an open upvalue referring to a stack
slot. When that frame returns, the runtime must close the upvalue by moving or
copying the captured value into heap-managed storage.

All closures sharing the same captured local must share the same upvalue cell, not
independent copies.

## 10. Differential and Layered Testing

- Test layer: Lexer. Assertion: Exact token and span sequence.
- Test layer: Parser/compiler. Assertion: Exact instructions and constants.
- Test layer: Verifier. Assertion: Malformed register, constant, and jump indexes are rejected.
- Test layer: V M. Assertion: Hand-built chunks execute independently of parsing.
- Test layer: End-to-end. Assertion: Source text produces expected output.
- Test layer: Differential. Assertion: Visible behavior matches reference Lua where intended.
- Test layer: Snapshot. Assertion: Bytecode listings and diagnostics remain readable/stable.

When a test fails, ask which boundary first differs:

## 11. Lua Tables Combine Array and Hash Representations

Lua presents one table abstraction, but an implementation can split storage:

- Key shape: dense positive integer sequence. Likely representation: array part.
- Key shape: sparse integer. Likely representation: hash part.
- Key shape: string or other permitted key. Likely representation: hash part.
- Key shape: nil or invalid numeric key. Likely representation: reject according to semantics.

The split is an optimization hidden behind one semantic interface. Reads and writes
must behave consistently even when a key migrates between representations.

- Source form: t.name. Semantic operation: t["name"].
- Source form: t[i]. Semantic operation: evaluate t, evaluate i, then index.
- Source form: t.x.y. Semantic operation: chained reads with intermediate values.
- Source form: t.x = v. Semantic operation: l-value/indexed store, not a local assignment.

Table objects have reference identity. Assigning a table value normally copies a
reference, not all entries. That affects equality, hashing, mutation visibility, and
garbage-collector edges.

Note. Implementation rule: specify table semantics first; only then optimize dense
integer keys, constant fields, inline caches, or hash layouts.

## 12. Control Flow Turns Labels into Patched Offsets

The compiler often emits a jump before it knows the destination:

- Construct: if. Patch information to retain: false branch and optional end branch.
- Construct: while. Patch information to retain: exit branch plus backward loop edge.
- Construct: break. Patch information to retain: list of exits for the nearest loop.
- Construct: goto. Patch information to retain: label, scope depth, and unresolved jump.
- Construct: short-circuit op. Patch information to retain: true/false target and whether a value is required.

Forward-only offsets stop being sufficient once loops add backward edges. Store signed,
range-checked displacements and define whether they are relative to the current
instruction or the following instruction.

Logical expressions have two compilation modes:

Compiling a and b directly as branches can avoid materializing a temporary boolean,
but the result must still preserve Lua's value semantics when the expression is used as
a value.

# Object Models, Dispatch, and Classes

## Why Classes?

Without classes, related data is scattered. This works, but causes problems:

- No semantic grouping — nothing says x1 and y1 belong together; they're just
two independent integers.
- Easy to mix up — accidentally using x1 with y2 isn't caught by the compiler.
- Can't pass as a unit — you can't write distance(p1, p2); you need
distance(x1, y1, x2, y2).
- No encapsulation — the distance formula is scattered across your code, so changing
it means finding every place it was computed.

We need a way to group related data and attach behavior to it — that's what classes give
us.

Note. Classes analogy: Think of a filing cabinet. Without classes you have loose papers
(x1, y1). Classes are folders that group related papers together — and know what
operations to perform on them.

## O O P Vocabulary

- Concept: Class. Description: Blueprint for creating objects. Example: class Point {... }.
- Concept: Object. Description: An instance of a class. Example: p = new Point(1, 2).
- Concept: Field. Description: Data stored in an object. Example: self.x, self.y.
- Concept: Method. Description: Function attached to a class. Example: def distance(self).
- Concept: Constructor. Description: Initializes a new object. Example: def init (self).
- Concept: Destructor. Description: Cleans up before deletion. Example: def del (self).

## Classes as Custom Types

Classes define new types in a nominal type system — types are identified by
their names:

## Design Decisions

We implement a subset of O O P — deliberately simple:

No Inheritance — Many O O P languages support class B extends class A. We skip this
because it adds significant complexity (vtables, dynamic dispatch), and composition over
inheritance is often preferred anyway. The core concepts are clearer without it.

Explicit Memory Management — Instead of G C, we use explicit delete:

This mirrors C++ and teaches how memory actually works. Understanding manual management
helps you appreciate what G C, reference counting, and ownership models solve.

What we include vs exclude:

- Included: Class definition with fields. Excluded: Inheritance (vtables, dynamic dispatch).
- Included: Methods with self. Excluded: Interfaces / Traits.
- Included: Constructor init. Excluded: Visibility (public/private).
- Included: Destructor del. Excluded: Static methods.
- Included: new / delete. Excluded: Operator overloading.
- Included: Field access p.x / self.x. Excluded: not specified.
- Included: Method calls p.method(). Excluded: not specified.
- Included: Classes as types other: Point. Excluded: not specified.

## Memory Model: Stack vs Heap

In most primitive/variable contexts, values live on the stack. Objects live on the
heap because they must outlive the function that created them and can be shared
across references.

- Field 1: Management. Stack: Automatic (LIFO with call frames). Heap: Manual (new/delete).
- Field 1: Speed. Stack: Fast (just move a pointer). Heap: Slower (system call to OS).
- Field 1: Size. Stack: Limited (~few MB). Heap: Large (all available RAM).
- Field 1: Lifetime. Stack: Tied to function scope. Heap: Until explicitly freed.

Note. Analogy: The stack is a stack of cafeteria trays — same size, add/remove from top
only. The heap is a parking lot — park anywhere, leave as long as you want, but you
must retrieve it or it stays forever (memory leak).

## Constructors and new

The constructor ( init ) initializes a new object. It always takes self as the
first parameter.

When you write p = new Point(10, 20):

1. Calculate size — Point has two i64 fields leads to 16 bytes.
2. Call malloc — Ask the OS for 16 bytes; get back a pointer.
3. Initialize fields — Zero-initialize all fields as a safety baseline.
4. Call init — Runs with the new pointer as self; sets self.x = 10,
self.y = 20.
5. Return pointer — p now holds the heap address of the object.

Memory layout:

Field order (tracked via field order in ClassInfo) determines the memory layout —
order matters!

## Constructor Patterns

Default values:

Computed initialization:

Validation (clamping, since we have no exceptions):

Failure: Our constructors cannot fail. Real languages handle this via exceptions
(Java, Python), Result/Option (Rust), or factory methods. We keep it simple —
constructors always succeed.

## The self Parameter

self is the receiver for the object the method was called on. At the machine-code
boundary it becomes an ordinary pointer or reference argument. Rust exposes the
receiver explicitly and therefore makes the lowering easy to see:

- Language: Python. Self/This: def method(self): — explicit.
- Language: Rust. Self/This: fn method(shared reference to self) — explicit.
- Language: Java / C++. Self/This: this — implicit.
- Language: Thirdlang. Self/This: def method(self) — explicit.

Explicit self makes it clear: methods are just functions that receive the object as
their first argument.

In codegen, self is just stored as a local variable pointer, exactly like any
other parameter — there's no special-casing:

## Methods and Field Access

Methods compile to regular functions with the naming convention ClassName methodName
— avoiding collisions between classes and making ownership clear.

Field access via getelementptr (GEP):

GEP calculates the memory address of a struct field without reading memory — it is
pointer arithmetic that knows struct layouts:

Method call p.get x() leads to call @Point get x(ptr %p) — the object is passed as the
first argument.

Note. Important: When you pass an object as a parameter, you pass a pointer — not a
copy. Modifying other.x inside a method modifies the original object. This is
reference semantics.

## Method Patterns

Calling methods on self — methods can call other methods on the same object:

Returning self's type — enables builder pattern:

Method naming convention (ClassName methodName in L L V M):

- Method: Point. init. L L V M Function: @Point init.
- Method: Point.get x. L L V M Function: @Point get x.
- Method: Counter.increment. L L V M Function: @Counter increment.

This avoids name collisions between classes and lets the J I T find the right function at
call sites.

## Destructors and delete

The destructor ( del ) is called automatically when you delete an object:

Behind the scenes:

1. Call Point del(p) — runs any cleanup code.
2. Call free(p) — returns memory to the OS.

After delete, p still holds the old address — but accessing it is undefined
behavior.

## L L V M Codegen for Classes — Summary

- Thirdlang: class Point { x: int }. L L V M I R: %Point = type { i64 }.
- Thirdlang: new Point(10). L L V M I R: call @malloc + call @Point init.
- Thirdlang: p.x. L L V M I R: getelementptr + load.
- Thirdlang: p.x = 5. L L V M I R: getelementptr + store.
- Thirdlang: p.method(). L L V M I R: call @Point method(ptr %p).
- Thirdlang: delete p. L L V M I R: call @Point del + call @free.

Class compilation happens in six phases:

1. Declare libc functions — malloc and free.
2. Create class struct types — define an L L V M struct for each class.
3. Declare methods — create function signatures (enables cross-method and forward
calls).
4. Compile class bodies — generate method implementations.
5. Compile top-level code — generate the @ main wrapper.
6. Verify module — check the I R is well-formed.

## Complete Codegen Example

Tracing Counter from source to L L V M I R:

Generated I R:

Note. The code that runs isn't interpreted — it's real compiled machine code, the same as if
you'd written it in C or Rust. Objects really live on the heap. Methods really jump to
function addresses. When this calls malloc, it's calling the actual C malloc.

Performance considerations:

- What We Do: Direct field access via GEP (fast). What Real Compilers Add: Inline method calls when possible.
- What We Do: Static method calls — no vtable lookup. What Real Compilers Add: Escape analysis (stack-allocate short-lived objects).
- What We Do: Objects laid out contiguously in memory. What Real Compilers Add: Field alignment optimization, dead field elimination.

## Memory Layout Trace

Tracing new Point(10, 20) followed by delete p at the address level:

new Point(10, 20):

1. malloc(16) leads to returns pointer 0x1000
2. Point init (0x1000, 10, 20) — sets x=10 at offset 0, y=20 at offset 8
3. p holds 0x1000

delete p:

1. Point del (0x1000) — runs destructor cleanup
2. free(0x1000) — returns memory to OS
3. p still holds 0x1000 — but it is invalid (dangling pointer)

## Best Practices (Manual Memory)

### 1. Delete What You Allocate

Every new has a matching delete.

### 2. Keep One Clear Owner

Have one clear owner responsible for deletion:

### 3. Clear or Invalidate After Release

Set the reference to null after deletion when the language supports it:

## Memory Safety Bugs

Languages without G C (C, C++) require explicit new/delete, introducing high-risk
bugs:

- Bug: Memory Leak. Description: Forgot delete; memory consumed until exit. Code: p = new Point(1,2) — never freed.
- Bug: Use After Free. Description: Access after delete — undefined behavior. Code: delete p; p.x.
- Bug: Double Free. Description: delete twice — corrupts allocator. Code: delete p; delete p.
- Bug: Dangling Pointer. Description: Two refs to same object; one deletes it. Code: q = p; delete p; q.x.

## Memory Management Approaches

- Approach: Manual (C, C++). Pros: Fast, predictable, teaches fundamentals. Cons: Error-prone.
- Approach: Garbage Collection (Java, Python). Pros: Safe, convenient. Cons: Runtime overhead, G C pauses.
- Approach: Reference Counting (Swift, Python). Pros: Predictable cleanup. Cons: Cycle leaks, overhead.
- Approach: Ownership (Rust). Pros: Safe with no runtime cost. Cons: Complex ownership rules.

# Compiler Optimization: Preserving Meaning While Changing Form

Optimizations simplify the A S T before code generation, producing faster output, cleaner
debug trees, and less work for the backend. They are chained into a pass pipeline —
order matters!

Note. Optimization invariant: the transformed program must preserve every behavior
the language specification says is observable.

- Optimization: Constant Folding. Description: 1 plus 2 leads to 3 at compile time.
- Optimization: Algebraic Simplification. Description: x asterisk 0 leads to 0, strength reduction.
- Optimization: Dead Code Elimination. Description: Remove unreachable branches.
- Optimization: Common Subexpression Elimination. Description: Compute identical sub-expressions once.
- Optimization: Loop Unrolling. Description: Replace loops with repeated sequential code.
- Optimization: Inlining. Description: Substitute function body directly at call site.
- Optimization: Tail Call Optimization. Description: Convert tail recursion into a flat loop.

Note. Even when L L V M will optimize downstream, custom passes improve compile speed, debug
output readability, and can exploit language-specific knowledge L L V M can't.

# Intermediate Representation and Native Code with L L V M

Note. L L V M is like a universal translator for CPUs. You speak L L V M I R; L L V M translates it to
x86, ARM, Web Assembly — whatever you need. Write your compiler frontend once; L L V M
gives you every platform for free.
Backend boundary: your frontend must produce valid, well-typed I R; L L V M
cannot repair incorrect language semantics or undefined assumptions.

L L V M I R is a universal, low-level assembly language not tied to any specific C P U. It
is used by Rust, Swift, Julia, Kotlin/Native, and more. By targeting L L V M I R, you get
world-class optimizations for free.

## Core I R Mechanics

### S S A (Static Single Assignment)

In L L V M I R, every variable is assigned exactly once. This makes optimizations like
dead code elimination and constant propagation trivial — the compiler always knows
exactly where each value was defined.

### Mutability: Alloca / Load / Store

Because S S A values are immutable, mutable variables use stack slots:

L L V M's mem2reg pass promotes these stack slots to fast registers automatically.

Why store parameters too? For add(%a, %b), the pattern is: (1) alloca stack
space for %a.addr/%b.addr, (2) store the incoming parameters into those slots, (3)
load the values back, (4) add i64 the loaded values, (5) ret the result. This
looks wasteful — why not use %a and %b directly? Because in L L V M I R, S S A values like
%a are immutable, but in most source languages variables can change. Storing every
variable in a stack slot up front handles mutability uniformly, and mem2reg cleans it
up afterward when a variable is never actually reassigned.

### Basic Blocks and Branching

Conditionals require separate basic blocks (entry, then, else, merge). Each
block ends with a terminator — either ret (return) or br (branch):

### Phi Nodes (φ)

In S S A form, if a variable's value depends on which branch was taken, a phi node
selects the correct value based on the incoming basic block:

"If we came from %then, use %x.then. If we came from %else, use %x.else." Phi
nodes are the only way to merge values from different control flow paths in S S A.

### Recursion

Recursive functions use the standard call instruction:

### Function Calls and Return Values

To compile a call: look up the function, compile each argument, then emit a call
instruction. L L V M calls return either a basic value (an int or pointer you can use)
or nothing (void). The pattern try as basic value().unwrap basic() extracts the
basic-value case — safe here since our functions always return int — and
.into int value() converts the result to the specific integer type needed downstream.

## L L V M State

- Component: Context. Role: Workspace for all L L V M objects.
- Component: Module. Role: Container for functions (one per compilation unit).
- Component: Builder. Role: Inserts I R instructions into a basic block.
- Component: variables map. Role: Maps variable names leads to stack pointers (alloca results).
- Component: functions map. Role: Maps function names leads to L L V M function objects.
- Component: current fn. Role: The function being compiled (needed to create new basic blocks).

## The Three-Pass Compilation Process

The Prestage stage:

- Context create() - The top-level container. All L L V M objects belong to a
context. It manages memory and ensures thread safety.
- context.create module("addition") - Creates a module (like a compilation unit).
Our add function will live here.
- context.i32 type() - Gets the 32-bit integer type. L L V M is explicitly typed - we
need to declare that our function works with i32.

### Pass 1 — Declare Functions

Announce all function signatures before compiling any body. This enables mutual
recursion through forward references.

- i32 type.fn type( and [i32 type.into(), i32 type.into()], false) - Creates a
function type: returns i32, takes two i32 parameters. The false means it's not
variadic (doesn't take variable arguments like printf).
- module.add function("add", fn type, None) - Adds a function called "add" with
this signature to our module.
- context.append basic block(add fn, "entry") - Creates a basic block named
"entry". A basic block is a sequence of instructions with no branches in the middle -
execution flows straight through.
- context.create builder() - The builder is our "cursor" for adding instructions.
We position it at a basic block, then build instructions there.
- builder.position at end(entry) - Point the builder at our entry block. New
instructions will go here.

### Pass 2 — Compile Bodies

Generate I R instructions for each function body using the alloca/load/store pattern.

- add fn.get nth param(0) - Get the first parameter. L L V M functions have an array
of parameters, indexed from 0.
- .unwrap().into int value() - Parameters come as generic "basic values." We know
ours are integers, so we convert them.
- builder.build int add(x, y, "result") - This emits an add instruction. The
"result" is just a name for the output (helps when reading I R).
- builder.build return(Some(shared reference to sum)) - Emit a ret instruction to return our sum.

### Pass 3 — Create the @ main Wrapper

Wrap top-level expressions, such as fib(10), in main as the J I T entry point, then
verify the module.

- module.create jit execution engine(OptimizationLevel None) - Creates a J I T
compiler. L L V M takes our I R and compiles it to native x86/ARM code right now, in
memory.
- execution engine.get function unsafe extern "C" fn(i32, i32) - i32 ("add") -
Look up our compiled function. The type signature tells Rust how to call it.
- add.call(1, 2) - Call the native function! This jumps directly to machine code -
no interpretation, no overhead.

## Running a Program: Source to Result

What happens when you run cargo run -- examples/fibonacci.sl:

1. Parse the source file leads to Typed A S T (with Unknown types).
2. Type check leads to Typed A S T (all types resolved).
3. Optimize (optional) leads to Simplified A S T.
4. Compile leads to L L V M I R.
5. J I T leads to Native machine code.
6. Execute leads to Result.

All in a fraction of a second. Each step transforms the program into a different
representation, getting closer and closer to something the C P U can execute directly.

Calling the J I T-compiled code:

1. Create a code generator — sets up the L L V M context, module, and builder (the
workspace).
2. Compile the program to I R.
3. Create a J I T execution engine from the verified module.
4. Get a pointer to @ main — our entry point.
5. Call it and return the result.

The J I T engine compiles I R to native machine code on the fly, then executes it — this is
much faster than interpretation, because the C P U is running actual machine code, not
walking a tree.

Note. The unsafe block required when calling J I T output signals that we are invoking raw
machine code — we must trust that our code generator produced valid I R.

## L L V M Optimization Passes

After code generation, L L V M passes transform naive I R into efficient code. Passes are
chained in a pipeline string (e.g., "mem2reg,dce,instcombine,simplifycfg").

Why optimize ourselves if L L V M will do it anyway?

- Reason: Learning. Benefit: Implementing optimizations teaches how production compilers actually work.
- Reason: Simplicity. Benefit: A simpler A S T means simpler, less error-prone code generation.
- Reason: Debug output. Benefit: Optimized I R is easier to read when printed for debugging.
- Reason: Specialized optimizations. Benefit: You may know language-specific facts L L V M cannot infer.
- Reason: Compile time. Benefit: A simpler A S T means less work for L L V M, so compilation is faster.

- Pass: mem2reg. What It Does: Promotes alloca stack slots to S S A registers — the most impactful pass.
- Pass: dce. What It Does: Dead Code Elimination — removes instructions whose results are never used.
- Pass: instcombine. What It Does: Merges redundant instructions; constant folds; strength-reduces (mul x, 2 leads to shl x, 1).
- Pass: simplifycfg. What It Does: Removes empty blocks, merges single-predecessor blocks, simplifies trivial branches.

Before/after example — increment method (14 leads to 4 instructions):

Each pass in isolation:

mem2reg — promotes an alloca'd variable straight to a value:

dce — drops instructions whose results are never read:

instcombine — simplifies arithmetic patterns: sub i64 %x, 1 leads to add i64 %x, -1;
mul i64 %x, 2 leads to shl i64 %x, 1 (shift left); constant-folds add i64 3, 4 leads to 7.

simplifycfg — removes empty basic blocks, merges blocks with a single predecessor, and
simplifies trivially-true/false branches.

L L V M preset pipelines:

- Level: default O0. Description: No optimization (verification only).
- Level: default O1. Description: Light optimization.
- Level: default O2. Description: Standard optimization (recommended).
- Level: default O3. Description: Aggressive optimization.

C L I usage:

Setting up the pass manager requires:

1. Initialize Native Target — required before creating a TargetMachine.
2. Get Target Triple — the host machine description (e.g., x86 64-apple-darwin).
3. Create TargetMachine — needed for target-specific optimizations.
4. run passes — takes the comma-separated pass list (or a preset like
"default O2 ") and applies it to the module.

# Compiler Correctness Through Inkwell and L L V M Verification

Note. Library lens: Inkwell is used as a typed view over L L V M concepts. The lesson is dominance, S S A, terminators, verification, A B I shape, and semantic equivalence—not memorizing builder calls.

The tests/all suite in Inkwell
is useful as executable A P I documentation. Its files are organized around L L V M
concepts—builders, blocks, contexts, modules, targets, types, values, passes, debug
information, object files, and the execution engine—rather than around one large demo.

## 1. Read Upstream Tests as a Capability Map

- Upstream test area: test types.rs / test values.rs. Question for your compiler: Are guest types represented exactly as intended?
- Upstream test area: test builder.rs / test basic block.rs. Question for your compiler: Is every block well formed and terminated?
- Upstream test area: test module.rs. Question for your compiler: Can modules verify, link, clone, and serialize?
- Upstream test area: test execution engine.rs. Question for your compiler: Does generated I R compute the correct result?
- Upstream test area: test targets.rs / test object file.rs. Question for your compiler: Can the backend emit for the selected target?
- Upstream test area: test passes asterisk.rs. Question for your compiler: Does optimization preserve behavior?
- Upstream test area: test debug info.rs. Question for your compiler: Can source spans survive into debugger metadata?
- Upstream test area: test attributes.rs. Question for your compiler: Are calling and optimization contracts attached?
- Upstream test area: test intrinsics.rs. Question for your compiler: Are target-independent L L V M operations declared?
- Upstream test area: test memory buffer.rs. Question for your compiler: Can I R/bitcode enter through a byte-oriented A P I?

Inkwell's tests/all/main.rs declares these as modules so Cargo builds them into a
single integration-test binary. It also uses cfg conditions for L L V M-version-sensitive
tests. That is a strong model for a backend supporting several L L V M versions:

## 2. Verify Every Constructed Module

L L V M I R is typed, but a sequence of individually valid builder calls can still produce
an invalid function or module. Run the verifier immediately after the smallest useful
construction.

Note. Backend invariant: each reachable basic block ends with exactly one terminator,
every S S A use is dominated by its definition, and instruction/result types agree.

## 3. Test at Increasing Levels of Meaning

- Test level: Builder result. What it catches: Missing insertion point or invalid operation. Typical assertion: Result is Ok.
- Test level: Function verify. What it catches: Local control-flow/type defects. Typical assertion: function.verify(true).
- Test level: Module verify. What it catches: Cross-function/module defects. Typical assertion: module.verify().is ok().
- Test level: I R inspection. What it catches: Names, structure, attributes, calling form. Typical assertion: selected normalized I R fragments.
- Test level: J I T execution. What it catches: Wrong semantics despite valid I R. Typical assertion: returned value equals oracle.
- Test level: Object emission. What it catches: Target/data-layout/relocation mistakes. Typical assertion: object parses and symbols exist.
- Test level: Differential run. What it catches: Optimization or backend disagreement. Typical assertion: interpreter result equals J I T/A O T.

Do not snapshot an entire L L V M module for every test. Full snapshots are noisy across
L L V M versions. Prefer semantic execution tests plus focused I R assertions for features
whose representation matters.

## 4. J I T Lookup Is an Unsafe A B I Boundary

ExecutionEngine get function is unsafe because Rust must promise that the requested
function-pointer type matches the generated function's A B I and signature. The engine
must also outlive the pointer.

Wrap this unsafe lookup behind a small, typed backend A P I. Do not let arbitrary strings
and arbitrary Rust function-pointer types spread through the compiler.

## 5. Build a Compiler Compatibility Matrix

- Dimension: L L V M version. Minimum matrix: each version/feature combination you claim.
- Dimension: Optimization. Minimum matrix: unoptimized plus the production pass pipeline.
- Dimension: Target. Minimum matrix: host plus every cross-target you distribute.
- Dimension: Execution mode. Minimum matrix: interpreter/reference, J I T, and A O T where applicable.
- Dimension: Numeric edges. Minimum matrix: zero, extrema, overflow policy, NaN where applicable.
- Dimension: Control flow. Minimum matrix: both branch arms, loop zero/one/many iterations.
- Dimension: Debug mode. Minimum matrix: verifier and debug-info checks enabled.

For your own language, a high-value oracle is:

When those disagree, preserve the source, seed, I R before/after optimization, L L V M
version, target triple, and data layout. That turns a mysterious codegen bug into a
reproducible backend test.

Note. Next: debugging treats every compiler stage as a hypothesis with observable
inputs, outputs, invariants, and failure evidence.

# Debugging as Evidence-Guided Model Correction

## Compiler Pipeline Debugging

Note. Your language is a pipeline: Source leads to Tokens leads to A S T leads to Output. When something breaks,
find which stage produced the wrong output.

Systematic approach:

1. Reproduce — Find the smallest input that triggers the bug.
2. Isolate — Which stage is producing wrong output?
3. Inspect — Print the data at that stage.
4. Fix — Change the code.
5. Verify — Re-run the test.

- Symptom: Parse error. Action: Simplify input; inspect lexer tokens.
- Symptom: Wrong result. Action: Print the A S T; check grammar operator precedence.
- Symptom: Program crash. Action: Add debug prints; run cargo clippy.
- Symptom: Infinite loop. Action: Add print statements inside the eval loop.
- Symptom: Precedence wrong. Action: Print A S T and verify grammar rule order.

Debugging tips:

- Test each feature in isolation — don't write 100 lines then debug.
- Use the REPL for quick experiments.
- Print the A S T — structure reveals bugs that output doesn't.
- Check operator precedence by inspecting which node is the A S T root.

## Testing the Pipeline

Test at multiple levels:

- Level: Unit. What to Test: Individual functions — parser output, type checker rules.
- Level: Integration. What to Test: Multiple components — parse + typecheck + codegen.
- Level: End-to-end. What to Test: Full programs from source to result.
- Level: Snapshot. What to Test: I R and error message output — catch regressions.

Start with integration tests for full programs, then add unit tests for complex logic.
Add a regression test for every bug fixed.

## Reverse Engineering and Debugging Compiled Binaries

Everything above is about debugging your own compiler pipeline while you have the
source. A different (and complementary) skill is understanding someone else's
already-compiled binary, where you only have opcodes — this is the mirror image of
everything in Hardware Fundamentals: instead of going source
leads to assembly leads to opcodes, you're going opcodes leads to assembly leads to (partial) understanding.

Static vs. dynamic analysis:

- Approach: Static analysis. What it means: Disassemble a binary without running it — read the opcodes as text. When it's used: Auditing a binary before executing it (e.g. static inspection).
- Approach: Dynamic analysis. What it means: Attach a debugger to a running process and observe it live. When it's used: Understanding behavior that only appears at runtime.
- Approach: Decompiling. What it means: Attempt to reconstruct high-level source from disassembly. When it's used: Best-effort only — decompilers guess at structure and can mislead.

Note. Disassembly always reflects what's actually executing; decompiling does not — it's a
reconstruction, not a fact.

Debugger fundamentals (same core loop regardless of tool — gdb, LLDB, x64dbg,
WinDbg):

- Attach — connect the debugger to a running process (or launch the process under
it).
- Breakpoint — mark an address so execution pauses when the C P U reaches it.
Breakpoints can be code breakpoints (pause on an instruction) or memory/data
breakpoints (pause when a memory address is read or written).
- Step — advance execution one instruction (or line) at a time once paused.
- Step into a call — follow execution into the called function.
- Step over a call — run the whole function without descending into it, landing
on the next instruction after it returns.
- Execute until return — run until the current function's ret, useful for
"bubbling up" from a low-level instruction to the higher-level function that led
there.
- Inspect — read registers, the call stack, and memory (typically shown as hex +
ASCII) while paused.

Reading assembly you didn't write: the general strategy is to establish context
first — find a landmark (a known string, a known memory value, a function you already
understand) — then work outward from it one instruction at a time. mov, add, sub,
cmp, and jmp/je/jne (conditional branches) cover the majority of what you'll
need to trace through simple control flow; not every instruction needs to be understood
to follow the logic.

Why this connects back to compilers: a debugger is doing, by hand, exactly what your
interpreter's eval loop and the
