C P U's fetch-decode-execute cycle do automatically — stepping through
instructions one at a time and tracking state. Understanding one deepens the others.

## Following Unfamiliar Call Chains

A function rarely acts alone — it calls other functions, which call others still.
Visualizing this as a function chain makes a deep call stack easier to hold in your
head:

When stepping through a debugger, working from a low-level instruction back up through
a chain like this — using execute-until-return repeatedly — is sometimes called
bubbling up: you keep surfacing to the next caller until you reach the higher-level
function that actually matters to you. It's the same recursive-structure idea as
the call stack, just observed from the outside instead of written
from the inside.

A general four-step approach for making sense of any unfamiliar system's behavior — a
program, an A P I, a bug report — mirrors this:

1. Identify what you're trying to understand or verify.
2. Understand what data or code path is actually involved.
3. Locate it — find the concrete function, variable, or address.
4. Observe/verify — confirm your understanding matches what actually happens.

## Pointers and Shared Libraries

Two more building blocks that show up constantly once you're reading real systems code:

- Pointers — a variable that stores the address of another value rather than the
value itself. int asterisk y = shared reference to x; makes y point at x; dereferencing ( asterisk y) reads or
writes through that address. Pointers are how languages like C/C++/Rust let code
directly reference and manipulate memory rather than only working with copies.
- Shared/dynamic libraries (.dll on Windows,.so on Linux,.dylib on macOS) —
compiled code that's loaded into a program at runtime rather than baked into the
executable at compile time, so multiple programs can share one copy of common
functionality (e.g. a UI toolkit) without each program bundling its own copy. This is
the runtime cousin of the Assembler/linker relationship — a
linker resolves static dependencies at build time, while a loader resolves dynamic
ones each time the program starts.

# System Design: Queues, Feedback, Failure, and Scale

DesignDeck presents system-design decisions as a
connected set of trade-offs: traffic, data, consistency, caching, partitioning,
messaging, and failure handling. This section translates several of those decisions
into Rust-shaped interfaces.

Note. Design principle: begin with quantities, guarantees, and failure behavior;
only then choose components.

## 1. Start with Quantities and Guarantees

Before drawing services and databases, write down:

- Design input: Traffic. Questions to answer: Peak reads and writes per second?
- Design input: Payloads. Questions to answer: Typical and maximum request/response sizes?
- Design input: Storage. Questions to answer: Daily growth and retention period?
- Design input: Latency. Questions to answer: Acceptable median and tail latency?
- Design input: Reliability. Questions to answer: Availability, recovery time, and recovery point targets?
- Design input: Consistency. Questions to answer: What does each operation guarantee?
- Design input: Message behavior. Questions to answer: Is loss, duplication, or reordering acceptable?
- Design input: Security. Questions to answer: Where are trust and authorization boundaries?
- Design input: Dependency failure. Questions to answer: What happens when a dependency is slow or unavailable?

Useful rough estimates:

These are estimates, not capacity promises. Validate them with measurement and leave
headroom for bursts, retries, replication, metadata, indexes, and uneven partitions.

## 2. Cache-Aside

In cache-aside, the application checks the cache, loads a miss from the source of truth,
and then fills the cache. The cache does not independently query storage.

A small interface keeps policy separate from storage:

- Cache decision: Staleness. Question: How long may an old value remain visible?
- Cache decision: Negative caching. Question: Should missing values be cached?
- Cache decision: Stampede control. Question: What protects a hot key during a miss?
- Cache decision: Fill failure. Question: Does cache failure fail the request or only reduce speed?
- Cache decision: Write policy. Question: Write-through, write-back, or invalidation?
- Cache decision: Eviction. Question: LRU, LFU, FIFO, or a domain-specific policy?

The database remains the source of truth unless the design explicitly chooses a
different consistency model.

## 3. Token-Bucket Rate Limiting

A token bucket allows controlled bursts while enforcing a long-term rate. Tokens refill
over time up to a fixed capacity; each accepted operation spends tokens.

- Rate-limit decision: Identity. Choices to define: User, IP, token, tenant, endpoint, or resource.
- Rate-limit decision: Scope. Choices to define: Per process or coordinated across replicas.
- Rate-limit decision: Store failure. Choices to define: Fail open, fail closed, or use a local fallback.
- Rate-limit decision: Over-limit action. Choices to define: Reject, queue, or degrade.
- Rate-limit decision: Client feedback. Choices to define: Retry timing, remaining quota, and reset metadata.

A leaky-bucket queue smooths output at a regular rate. It is useful when work can wait;
a token bucket is useful when short bursts are legitimate.

## 4. Circuit Breakers, Timeouts, and Backoff

A circuit breaker prevents repeated calls to a dependency that is already failing:

State transitions:

Use the patterns together:

- Resilience pattern: Timeout. Responsibility: Limit one attempt.
- Resilience pattern: Retry budget. Responsibility: Limit total extra work.
- Resilience pattern: Exponential backoff. Responsibility: Increase spacing between attempts.
- Resilience pattern: Jitter. Responsibility: Prevent synchronized retry storms.
- Resilience pattern: Circuit breaker. Responsibility: Stop calls during sustained dependency failure.
- Resilience pattern: Load shedding. Responsibility: Reject low-priority work before global collapse.

Retry only operations that are safe to repeat, or attach an idempotency key. Never let
every layer retry independently without a shared budget; multiplicative retries can
turn a small outage into overload.

## 5. Delivery Is Not Processing

Networks and processes can fail between any two observable steps. Messages may be
delayed, duplicated, dropped, or reordered.

- Delivery model: At most once. What the consumer must expect: Loss is possible; duplicates are suppressed.
- Delivery model: At least once. What the consumer must expect: Duplicates are possible; retry covers some losses.
- Delivery model: Effectively once. What the consumer must expect: Deduplication makes repeated processing harmless.

Exactly-once network delivery is not a general guarantee. Systems usually approach
exactly-once effects through idempotency, durable deduplication, and atomic state
changes.

This in-memory example explains the invariant but is not crash safe. In a real system,
the deduplication record and business update must be committed atomically in durable
storage, or a crash can occur between them.

An event log retains ordered records for replay and multiple consumers. A queue
typically distributes work so one consumer handles each message. Choose based on replay,
fan-out, ordering, and retention needs.

## 6. Consistent Hashing and Partition Movement

Ordinary hash(key) % node count remaps most keys when the number of nodes changes.
Consistent hashing places keys and nodes in a hash space so adding or removing a node
moves a smaller portion of the data.

Virtual nodes give each physical node multiple positions, which can improve balance.
Jump consistent hashing is another option when a compact computation is preferable to
maintaining a ring.

- Partition concern: Hash stability. Required decision: Is the mapping stable across versions and languages?
- Partition concern: Hot keys. Required decision: How are skewed workloads isolated or replicated?
- Partition concern: Replication. Required decision: How many owners does each partition have?
- Partition concern: Membership. Required decision: How are node changes proposed and observed?
- Partition concern: Rebalancing. Required decision: Can reads and writes continue while data moves?
- Partition concern: Recovery. Required decision: How are partially moved partitions detected and repaired?

If the key is a compiler module, bytecode package, or analysis artifact, stable
partitioning can also support distributed builds and content-addressed caches.

## 7. Consistency Is Per Operation

Do not label an entire system merely "strong" or "eventual." State what each operation
guarantees:

- Consistency guarantee: Read-your-writes. Meaning: A client observes its own completed writes.
- Consistency guarantee: Monotonic reads. Meaning: A client does not move backward to older state.
- Consistency guarantee: Causal ordering. Meaning: Causally related events are observed in order.
- Consistency guarantee: Linearizable compare-and-set. Meaning: The update behaves as one atomic real-time operation.
- Consistency guarantee: Bounded staleness. Meaning: Reads lag by no more than a defined time/version bound.
- Consistency guarantee: Eventual convergence. Meaning: Replicas agree once updates and failures stop.

The CAP trade-off matters during a network partition: a distributed system cannot
simultaneously guarantee both availability for every request and linearizable
consistency across the partition. Different operations may make different choices.

## 8. System-Design Review Checklist

- Area: Data ownership. Review criterion: Source of truth and derived copies are explicit.
- Area: Remote calls. Review criterion: Every call has a timeout.
- Area: Resource bounds. Review criterion: Queues, retries, bodies, fan-out, and concurrency are capped.
- Area: Overload. Review criterion: Degraded and rejected behavior is defined.
- Area: Idempotency. Review criterion: Repeated messages are safe or durably detected.
- Area: Versioning. Review criterion: Wire formats and stored schemas carry versions.
- Area: Deployment. Review criterion: Migration, rollback, and mixed-version behavior are defined.
- Area: Observability. Review criterion: Saturation, errors, tail latency, and dropped work are measured.
- Area: Privacy. Review criterion: Logs and cache keys exclude secrets and sensitive data.
- Area: Failure testing. Review criterion: Slowness and partial failure are tested.
- Area: Recovery. Review criterion: Recovery point and recovery time objectives are stated.
- Area: Simplicity. Review criterion: Operators can explain the important failure modes.

# Data Models, APIs, Caches, and Distributed Consistency

A storage engine turns logical operations into bytes on media. A distributed system
adds copies, messages, clocks, and partial failure. The useful question is never merely
“S Q L or no S Q L?” It is which operations, invariants, access paths, failure behavior,
and consistency guarantees are required?

## 1. Database Families Optimize Different Access Patterns

- Family: relational. Natural model: tables, rows, keys, constraints. Strength: joins, transactions, integrity. Typical cost: schema and query planning discipline.
- Family: document. Natural model: nested records such as BSON/J S O N. Strength: aggregate-shaped reads and flexible fields. Typical cost: duplicated data and cross-document work.
- Family: key-value. Natural model: opaque value by key. Strength: predictable direct lookup. Typical cost: secondary relationships are application work.
- Family: wide-column. Natural model: sparse rows partitioned by key. Strength: large distributed write/read workloads. Typical cost: queries must follow partition design.
- Family: graph. Natural model: vertices, edges, properties. Strength: relationship traversal. Typical cost: partitioning and global traversal cost.
- Family: time-series. Natural model: timestamped measurements. Strength: range aggregation, retention, compression. Typical cost: specialized query shape.
- Family: search index. Natural model: tokens, postings, ranking. Strength: text search and relevance. Typical cost: usually not source of truth.
- Family: object/blob store. Natural model: named immutable-ish byte objects. Strength: durable large-file storage. Typical cost: limited transactional/query semantics.
- Family: embedded. Natural model: library and local file, often S Q L/KV. Strength: simple deployment and local durability. Typical cost: one-machine concurrency boundaries.

“no S Q L” is a broad label for several of these models, not a single consistency or schema
policy. Document databases still have schemas—the schema may be enforced by the
application, validation rules, or migration code rather than a traditional DDL.

## 2. Relational Design Connects Identity and Constraints

- Concept: primary key. Meaning: stable row identity inside a relation.
- Concept: foreign key. Meaning: declared reference to another relation.
- Concept: unique constraint. Meaning: database-enforced non-duplication.
- Concept: index. Meaning: auxiliary structure that accelerates selected access paths.
- Concept: transaction. Meaning: group of operations with an atomicity boundary.
- Concept: isolation. Meaning: which concurrent intermediate states can be observed.
- Concept: M V C C. Meaning: keep versions so readers and writers can overlap under defined rules.
- Concept: migration. Meaning: versioned transformation of schema and stored data.

An index trades write amplification and space for faster lookup. A foreign key is not
an index in every database. Query plans are algorithms over cardinality estimates; use
the database's plan inspector rather than assuming a query is “obviously fast.”

## 3. NULL, Missing, Empty, and Unknown Are Different

S Q L NULL represents an absent/unknown marker and uses three-valued logic. x = NULL
does not test nullness; S Q L uses IS NULL. At an application boundary, distinguish:

- State: missing field. Example meaning: client made no statement.
- State: explicit null. Example meaning: client asks to clear or declares unknown.
- State: empty string/list. Example meaning: present value with zero content.
- State: default. Example meaning: value inferred by policy.
- State: tombstone. Example meaning: record intentionally deleted.

This enum is also a language-design lesson: one nullable pointer cannot express every
business state without conventions.

## 4. Graph Q L Is an A P I Language, Not a Database

Graph Q L defines a typed schema and lets a client request a response shape. Resolvers
may read S Q L, documents, caches, services, or generated values.

- Benefit: client selects fields. Required discipline: depth/complexity limits.
- Benefit: schema enables validation and tooling. Required discipline: authorization at every resolver/data edge.
- Benefit: one request can traverse relationships. Required discipline: batching to avoid N+1 lookups.
- Benefit: types document possible values. Required discipline: explicit nullability and error semantics.
- Benefit: schema evolves additively. Required discipline: deprecation and usage measurement.

REST, RPC, and Graph Q L are A P I styles, not mutually exclusive transport protocols.
Graph Q L commonly travels over H T T P, while the database behind it remains an independent
choice. The Graph Q L overview explicitly describes it as a query
language and runtime that is not tied to a storage engine.

## 5. Serialization Is a Versioned Boundary

Serialization maps typed state to bytes; deserialization attempts the inverse. It is
also a parser for untrusted input.

- Decision: framing. Questions: where does one value end and the next begin?
- Decision: schema. Questions: which fields, types, tags, and defaults exist?
- Decision: encoding. Questions: J S O N, X M L, CBOR, Protobuf, custom binary?
- Decision: compatibility. Questions: can old readers skip new fields?
- Decision: limits. Questions: maximum depth, size, collection count, and allocation?
- Decision: canonicalization. Questions: is there exactly one byte representation for signing/hashing?
- Decision: validation. Questions: which semantic invariants follow syntactic parsing?

X M L adds namespaces, attributes, mixed text, and entity-processing policy. Disable
external entity resolution unless a tightly controlled use case requires it; bind
values to a domain model instead of interpreting arbitrary object types.

## 6. UUIDs Are Identifiers, Not Authentication

A U U I D is 128 bits with a version/variant structure. Different versions make different
tradeoffs:

- Kind: random-style. Useful property: decentralized generation. Caution: collisions are improbable, not impossible.
- Kind: time-ordered. Useful property: index locality and rough order. Caution: may reveal timing; ordering is not trust.
- Kind: name-derived. Useful property: deterministic for namespace/name. Caution: changes when either input changes.

Use a current implementation of RFC 9562.
A U U I D does not prove identity, authorization, secrecy, or unpredictability unless the
specific version and generator provide the property—and even then it should not replace
a cryptographic session token.

## 7. Cache Layers Trade Freshness for Time

- Layer: C P U cache. Near: processor core. Typical contents: memory cache lines. Invalidation signal: coherence protocol.
- Layer: OS page cache. Near: kernel/filesystem. Typical contents: file-backed pages. Invalidation signal: writes, reclaim.
- Layer: in-process. Near: one service instance. Typical contents: parsed/computed values. Invalidation signal: TTL/version/event.
- Layer: Redis/network cache. Near: several service instances. Typical contents: shared hot records. Invalidation signal: TTL/delete/event.
- Layer: H T T P browser/proxy. Near: user or intermediary. Typical contents: responses. Invalidation signal: validators/directives.
- Layer: C D N edge. Near: geographically near users. Typical contents: cacheable web/media objects. Invalidation signal: TTL/purge/versioned U R L.

Every cache needs:

- a key and namespace;
- a value plus version/expiry;
- a miss/loading policy;
- an invalidation or freshness model;
- stampede protection;
- size/eviction limits;
- observability for hits, misses, stale results, and origin load.

Redis provides typed structures—not only strings—including hashes, lists, sets, sorted
sets, streams, and probabilistic structures. It can persist, but treating it as
disposable cache or durable primary storage are different architectures. Review its
data types and
persistence choices
before relying on recovery behavior.

## 8. H T T P Caching Is a Protocol Between Participants

- Mechanism: Cache-Control: max-age=N. Meaning: response is fresh for a defined time.
- Mechanism: no-store. Meaning: do not store this response.
- Mechanism: no-cache. Meaning: storage may occur, but revalidate before reuse.
- Mechanism: private. Meaning: shared caches must not store it.
- Mechanism: E tag / If-None-Match. Meaning: validator for a selected representation.
- Mechanism: Last-Modified / If-Modified-Since. Meaning: time-based validator.
- Mechanism: Vary. Meaning: request headers that select different representations.
- Mechanism: 304 Not Modified. Meaning: reuse stored body after successful validation.

Cache keys must include every representation selector described by policy. Personalized
responses accidentally stored in shared caches are security incidents, not merely
staleness bugs. See RFC 9111 and the
H T T P caching guide.

## 9. CDNs Combine Reverse Proxying, Caching, and Routing

A C D N places edge servers closer to clients. On a cache hit, the edge answers without
origin work; on a miss, it retrieves or forwards to the origin and may store a copy.

- Benefit: lower latency. New concern: cache-key correctness.
- Benefit: origin offload. New concern: purge/propagation delay.
- Benefit: traffic absorption. New concern: provider and control-plane dependency.
- Benefit: T L S termination near users. New concern: certificate/key lifecycle.
- Benefit: geographic presence. New concern: data residency and logging.

Versioned immutable asset names make invalidation simple: deploy a new U R L instead of
trying to retract an ambiguous old value. Cloudflare's
caching overview provides a
useful edge/origin mental model.

## 10. Consistency Is a Per-Operation Promise

- Model: strong / linearizable. Reader may observe: latest completed write in one global order. Suitable when: locks, ownership, critical coordination.
- Model: sequential. Reader may observe: one order consistent with each client's program order. Suitable when: some replicated state machines.
- Model: causal. Reader may observe: causally related operations in order. Suitable when: collaboration and messaging.
- Model: eventual. Reader may observe: replicas converge after writes stop. Suitable when: caches, feeds, derived views.
- Model: weak. Reader may observe: few ordering/freshness promises. Suitable when: telemetry or best-effort data.
- Model: read-your-writes. Reader may observe: a client sees its own accepted updates. Suitable when: user-facing settings.

“The database is eventually consistent” is too vague. Name the operation, object,
failure case, and session guarantee. The consistency patterns guide is a useful
comparison; verify the actual database configuration and A P I semantics.

## 11. Consensus Is Agreement Under Failure

A consensus algorithm lets a group agree on a sequence/value despite delays and some
failures. A leader-based replicated log usually separates:

- Term: quorum. Meaning: overlapping majority or configured voting set.
- Term: term/epoch. Meaning: logical leadership generation.
- Term: commit index. Meaning: highest entry known to meet the commit rule.
- Term: state machine. Meaning: deterministic application of committed commands.
- Term: split brain. Meaning: more than one side incorrectly acts authoritative.
- Term: fencing token. Meaning: monotonically increasing proof that rejects stale leaders.

Consensus does not make a service automatically available, fast, or correct. The state
machine can contain bugs; clients need retry/idempotency semantics; quorum loss may
choose safety over progress.

## 12. Fault Tolerance Starts with a Failure Model

- Failure: process crash. Design response: supervisor, durable checkpoint, restart budget.
- Failure: request timeout. Design response: deadline, cancellation, bounded retry.
- Failure: duplicate delivery. Design response: idempotency key and deduplication window.
- Failure: message loss. Design response: acknowledgement and redelivery policy.
- Failure: reordering. Design response: sequence/term numbers where order matters.
- Failure: network partition. Design response: explicit availability/consistency choice.
- Failure: slow dependency. Design response: bulkhead, circuit breaker, backpressure.
- Failure: corrupt data. Design response: checksum/signature, validation, replica repair.
- Failure: region loss. Design response: independent failure domains and tested recovery.

Reliability is the probability a system meets its specified behavior over time.
Availability is the fraction of time it can serve according to the contract.
Durability concerns accepted data surviving. Do not merge all three into “uptime.”

## 13. Heartbeats Detect Silence, Not Death

A heartbeat periodically reports liveness or lease renewal. Missing heartbeats means
“the observer did not receive timely evidence,” which could be a crashed peer, network
delay, scheduler pause, overload, or clock issue.

Use several missed intervals, generation/lease numbers, and a suspect leads to verify leads to
failed state transition. A heartbeat alone must not authorize destructive failover.

## 14. Event-Driven and Stream Processing

- Concept: event. Meaning: immutable statement that something happened.
- Concept: command. Meaning: request that something should happen.
- Concept: event loop. Meaning: dispatches readiness/messages to handlers.
- Concept: broker/log. Meaning: stores and distributes ordered records.
- Concept: stream processor. Meaning: transforms unbounded event sequences.
- Concept: event time. Meaning: time occurrence claims to have happened.
- Concept: processing time. Meaning: time the processor handled it.
- Concept: watermark. Meaning: estimate that earlier event-time data is mostly complete.
- Concept: window. Meaning: finite grouping over an unbounded stream.

Event-driven development decouples producers and consumers, but it introduces schema
evolution, duplicates, ordering domains, backpressure, poison messages, and
observability needs. “Exactly once” usually describes a scoped combination of broker,
state store, and sink protocol—not a magic end-to-end guarantee.

## 15. Synchronous, Asynchronous, Futures, and Promises

- Term: synchronous. Meaning: caller's control flow waits for completion.
- Term: asynchronous. Meaning: operation can make progress while caller does other work.
- Term: blocking. Meaning: thread cannot do other work while waiting.
- Term: nonblocking. Meaning: operation returns without waiting for unavailable progress.
- Term: future. Meaning: value representing a computation that may complete later.
- Term: promise. Meaning: writable/completion side of a future in some ecosystems.
- Term: task. Meaning: schedulable async state machine.

Rust's async fn compiles into a state machine implementing Future; polling must not
block. Cancellation often occurs by dropping the future, so partial-effect and cleanup
contracts matter. Concurrency allows interleaving; parallelism means simultaneous
execution.

## 16. Load Balancing Selects a Destination

- Strategy: round robin. Good for: similar healthy instances. Watch for: unequal work.
- Strategy: least connections. Good for: long-lived sessions. Watch for: connections differ in cost.
- Strategy: latency-aware. Good for: geographically/disparately performing backends. Watch for: noisy feedback loops.
- Strategy: consistent hash. Good for: affinity and partitioned caches. Watch for: skew and rebalancing.
- Strategy: weighted. Good for: mixed capacity or gradual rollout. Watch for: stale weights.

Health checking, connection draining, overload signals, retry budgets, and session
affinity matter as much as the selection algorithm. A retry routed to another backend
can duplicate a non-idempotent operation.

## 17. T D D and Reliability Tests Operate at Different Scales

Test-driven development uses a short feedback loop:

- Test: unit. Proves: local transformation/invariant.
- Test: property. Proves: behavior over generated input classes.
- Test: integration. Proves: boundary adapters cooperate.
- Test: contract. Proves: client/provider assumptions match.
- Test: concurrency/model. Proves: allowed interleavings preserve invariants.
- Test: fault-injection. Proves: timeouts, loss, retries, and crashes are handled.
- Test: load/soak. Proves: behavior under volume and time.
- Test: recovery. Proves: backups, failover, and replay actually restore service.

Tests are evidence about specified cases, not proof of absence of defects. Keep clocks,
randomness, storage, and network adapters injectable so unusual states become
repeatable.

Database sources: Postgres Q L tutorial,
Postgres Q L concurrency control,
MongoDB data modeling, and
MongoDB embedded documents.

# Networking: Packets, Protocols, Ports, and Wire Formats

Networking is low-level input and output plus a shared language. A protocol has syntax (frame
layout), semantics (what messages mean), and state (which messages are valid
now). That makes protocol design closely related to compiler design.

Note. Mental model: a network protocol is a language spoken over an unreliable,
partial-input and output boundary.

## 1. Layered Mental Model

An application usually reads and writes through a socket; the OS networking stack
handles the lower layers.

- Concept: Abstraction. T C P: Ordered byte stream. U D P: Individual datagrams.
- Concept: Delivery. T C P: Retransmitted while connection lives. U D P: Best effort.
- Concept: Boundaries. T C P: Not preserved. U D P: Preserved per datagram.
- Concept: Connection state. T C P: Yes. U D P: No transport connection.
- Concept: Typical use. T C P: Web, shells, databases, file transfer. U D P: D N S, telemetry, games, real-time media.

T C P does not deliver "messages." One write may be split across many reads, and
several writes may arrive in one read. Your protocol must provide framing.

## 2. Common Framing Strategies

- Strategy: Fixed size. Example: exactly 32 bytes. Trade-off: Simple, wastes space.
- Strategy: Delimiter. Example: line ending \n. Trade-off: Human-readable, escaping needed.
- Strategy: Length prefix. Example: u32 length + payload. Trade-off: Efficient, must bound length.
- Strategy: Type-length-value. Example: tag + length + value. Trade-off: Extensible.
- Strategy: Self-describing. Example: J S O N/CBOR-like value. Trade-off: Flexible, more overhead.

Example binary frame:

## 3. Reading a Length-Prefixed T C P Frame

read exact loops until the requested buffer is filled or an error occurs. It is
appropriate for a fixed header and a validated payload length.

Real services should also use timeouts, connection limits, authentication when needed,
and resource budgets. Portable code should not assume every operating system reports
socket timeouts with exactly the same error kind.

## 4. A Safe Loopback Protocol Lab

Bind test services to loopback so they are not exposed to the local network:

- Lab concept: Endpoint. What the example demonstrates: An address combined with a port.
- Lab concept: Server lifecycle. What the example demonstrates: Listen, accept, read, write, close.
- Lab concept: Partial input and output. What the example demonstrates: Use helpers that complete or report failure.
- Lab concept: Protocol semantics. What the example demonstrates: Map a request to a defined response.
- Lab concept: Error isolation. What the example demonstrates: One connection failure need not stop the listener.

## 5. Socket Lifecycle and Client/Server Roles

Beej's Guide to Network Programming frames a socket
as an operating-system handle used for network input and output. On Unix-like systems it is a file
descriptor, so familiar resource and blocking-input and output ideas apply.

Typical T C P server:

Typical T C P client:

Rust wraps the resource in TcpStream, TcpListener, and UdpSocket. Their destructors
close the underlying socket when ownership ends, but protocol shutdown may still need an
explicit shutdown.

shutdown(Write) sends an end-of-stream signal for the writing direction while leaving
the reading direction open. This is useful only when the application protocol defines
end-of-stream as the request boundary. Many protocols keep the connection open and use
lengths or delimiters instead.

## 6. Names, Addresses, and Ports

An endpoint combines an IP address with a transport port:

- Network name/value: IP address. Role: Identifies an interface or routing destination.
- Network name/value: Port. Role: Identifies a transport endpoint on a host.
- Network name/value: Host name. Role: Resolves to one or more IPv4/IPv6 addresses.
- Network name/value: Client local port. Role: Usually assigned ephemerally by the OS.
- Network name/value: Server port. Role: Bound to a known local address/service port.

Do not resolve a name and then use only the first address forever. Try the returned
candidates according to policy:

Resolution is not authentication. A successful D N S lookup says where to connect, not
whether the peer is trusted. Secure protocols still authenticate the remote identity.

### Rust Demonstration: Observe a Bounded Set of T C P Ports

A T C P port scanner is conceptually a repeated connection-state experiment:

The last three states must not be collapsed into “closed.” A timeout does not prove why
no response arrived.

A harmless local experiment can inspect a short, explicit list:

Use network observation only on systems you own or are authorized to assess. The
bounded slice is intentional: it makes work, connection count, and audit scope visible.

- Computer-science concept: Cartesian product. What the code exposes: A scan chooses endpoints from hosts times ports.
- Computer-science concept: State classification. What the code exposes: Results are modeled as an enum rather than a Boolean.
- Computer-science concept: Time. What the code exposes: A deadline turns unlimited waiting into a bounded observation.
- Computer-science concept: Evidence versus inference. What the code exposes: NoResponse records evidence without claiming a cause.
- Computer-science concept: Ownership. What the code exposes: Each successful TcpStream is closed when its value is dropped.
- Computer-science concept: Complexity. What the code exposes: One host and p ports performs O(p) connection attempts.
- Computer-science concept: Backpressure. What the code exposes: A concurrent version must cap in-flight sockets and result buffering.

Parallel scanning changes latency, not the amount of work. Spawning one thread or task
per possible port can exhaust file descriptors, ephemeral ports, scheduler capacity, or
the target. A bounded worker pool or semaphore makes concurrency an explicit resource
policy:

This is the same producer–consumer model used by crawlers, compilers with parallel work
queues, and servers that limit simultaneous requests.

## 7. Encapsulation and Byte Order

Each layer wraps the data from the layer above:

On receipt, the layers remove their respective headers in reverse order. This
separation lets the application use the same socket interface across different link
technologies.

Multi-byte integers need a specified wire order. Network protocols commonly use
big-endian, traditionally called network byte order:

Never transmit an in-memory Rust struct directly:

- In-memory property: Padding. Wire-format problem: Bytes may be unstable or uninitialized.
- In-memory property: Alignment/layout. Wire-format problem: Can vary by target and compiler settings.
- In-memory property: Integer representation. Wire-format problem: May use the wrong byte order.
- In-memory property: Pointers/references. Wire-format problem: Have no meaning in another process.
- In-memory property: Enum representation. Wire-format problem: Is unstable without an explicit representation contract.

Serialize each field deliberately, or use a documented format and implementation.

## 8. Partial input and output, EOF, and Blocking

A successful socket read or write may transfer fewer bytes than requested.

- Operation/result: read leads to Ok(0). Meaning: T C P end-of-stream.
- Operation/result: read exact. Meaning: Fill the buffer or report EOF/error.
- Operation/result: write all. Meaning: Continue until all bytes are accepted or an error occurs.
- Operation/result: Timeout. Meaning: Bound waiting; does not create a message boundary.

Blocking sockets put the calling thread to sleep until progress or an error is possible.
Non-blocking sockets instead report that the operation would block. An event loop uses
an OS readiness facility to learn which sockets may make progress.

Readiness is a hint, not a promise that an arbitrarily large operation will finish.
Drain or fill only until the operation would block again, and keep per-connection
buffers bounded.

For T C P framing, test all of these:

## 9. U D P Is Message-Oriented but Not Reliable

U D P preserves datagram boundaries, but an individual datagram may be lost, duplicated,
or reordered. If reliability matters, the application protocol must define sequence
numbers, acknowledgments, retransmission, deduplication, and congestion behavior—or use
an existing transport that already does.

This example binds to loopback for a local lab. In a real U D P protocol:

- U D P concern: Fragmentation. Defensive design: Keep datagrams within a conservative size.
- U D P concern: Authenticity. Defensive design: Authenticate before acting.
- U D P concern: Response matching. Defensive design: Use unpredictable request IDs.
- U D P concern: Amplification. Defensive design: Bound response size relative to request size.
- U D P concern: Retransmission. Defensive design: Model timeout/retry as finite protocol state.

## 10. Protocol State Machines

A parser answers "is this frame shaped correctly?" A state machine answers "is this
message valid now?"

A compiler is also a protocol state machine:

## 11. Reverse Engineering an Unknown Protocol Safely

packet-analysis workflow generalizes well to your own local server, interoperability
research, and authorized captures:

1. identify the transport and endpoints;
2. capture a baseline exchange;
3. change one controllable input;
4. compare packet lengths and changed byte regions;
5. look for magic values, counters, timestamps, strings, and length prefixes;
6. test byte order and compression hypotheses;
7. infer the session state machine;
8. write a decoder that rejects malformed input;
9. validate against fresh captures you did not use to form the hypothesis.

- Observed clue: First 2/4 bytes match remaining length. Plausible interpretation: Length-prefixed framing.
- Observed clue: Many readable bytes. Plausible interpretation: Text or lightly structured data.
- Observed clue: Tiny change affects most later bytes. Plausible interpretation: Compression, encryption, or integrity data.
- Observed clue: Repeated fixed prefix. Plausible interpretation: Magic value, version, or header.
- Observed clue: Monotonically changing field. Plausible interpretation: Sequence number or timestamp.
- Observed clue: Same message differs each time. Plausible interpretation: Nonce, timestamp, randomization, or encryption.

Do not assume traffic is plaintext merely because a few bytes are readable. Do not
attempt to defeat encryption or access systems without permission.

## 12. Designing a Protocol for Your Own Language

Suppose your language has a remote REPL. Separate framing, message syntax, and
semantics:

- Protocol area: Versioning. Design requirement: Include a version from the beginning.
- Protocol area: Integers. Design requirement: Define widths and byte order.
- Protocol area: Text. Design requirement: Specify U T F eight validity and normalization.
- Protocol area: Resource limits. Design requirement: Cap frames, strings, collections, and nesting.
- Protocol area: Identifier domains. Design requirement: Use newtypes for request IDs and session IDs.
- Protocol area: Extensibility. Design requirement: Define optional/mandatory unknown-field behavior.
- Protocol area: Authentication. Design requirement: Authenticate before evaluating code.
- Protocol area: Sandboxing. Design requirement: Bound C P U, memory, and execution time.
- Protocol area: Diagnostics. Design requirement: Use structured fields, not scraped prose.
- Protocol area: Compatibility tests. Design requirement: Publish input bytes with expected decoded values.

## 13. Networking Failure Modes

- Failure mode: Assume full read/write. Safer design: Loop with read exact/write all or buffered state.
- Failure mode: Trust a length field. Safer design: Validate against protocol and resource limits.
- Failure mode: Omit timeouts. Safer design: Bound connect, read, write, and idle time.
- Failure mode: Recurse without a limit. Safer design: Enforce structural depth.
- Failure mode: Treat T C P close as a frame. Safer design: Use explicit framing semantics.
- Failure mode: Parse before trust checks. Safer design: Place authentication/authorization deliberately.
- Failure mode: Trust client type tags. Safer design: Validate every tag, offset, and enum value.
- Failure mode: Log entire payloads. Safer design: Redact and cap diagnostic data.
- Failure mode: Use host endianness. Safer design: Specify and encode a wire byte order.
- Failure mode: Change layouts silently. Safer design: Version messages and define compatibility.

Tests should fragment a frame at every possible byte boundary, combine several frames
into one read, truncate every field, and exercise maximum permitted sizes.

## 14. Address Resolution Produces Candidates, Not One Answer

Beej's socket workflow begins with getaddrinfo: a host/service name can resolve to
multiple address-family, socket-type, and protocol candidates. Robust clients try
compatible candidates until one connects.

- Resolution concern: IPv4 and IPv6. Design requirement: avoid hard-coding one address family.
- Resolution concern: multiple records. Design requirement: define candidate ordering and connection timeout.
- Resolution concern: service names. Design requirement: separate human service input from numeric port.
- Resolution concern: canonical/display name. Design requirement: do not treat reverse lookup as authentication.
- Resolution concern: D N S change. Design requirement: cache with an explicit lifetime, not forever.
- Resolution concern: untrusted hostname. Design requirement: cap length and log safely.

In Rust, ToSocketAddrs is convenient, but the same mental model applies:

A production client should usually apply one overall deadline, not a full timeout
for an unbounded number of candidates.

## 15. Readiness Is Not Completion

poll/select-style APIs report that an operation can make progress without blocking;
they do not promise that a whole logical message can be transferred.

- Readiness event: readable. Possible interpretation: data, orderly EOF, or an error is available.
- Readiness event: writable. Possible interpretation: at least some send-buffer capacity may exist.
- Readiness event: error/hangup. Possible interpretation: inspect the socket result; buffered data may remain.

For a nonblocking connection, keep explicit state:

- Bug: discard suffix after partial send. Consequence: truncated protocol message.
- Bug: treat WouldBlock as fatal. Consequence: disconnect under ordinary load.
- Bug: parse before full frame exists. Consequence: false malformed-input error.
- Bug: keep growing read buffer. Consequence: memory exhaustion.
- Bug: ignore EOF with partial frame. Consequence: ambiguous truncation.
- Bug: assume writable means peer is alive. Consequence: delayed failure or lost application semantics.

shutdown can close one direction while leaving the other usable. Protocols that rely
on half-close must define whether EOF means “end of request,” “end of response,” or an
unexpected truncation; explicit length framing is usually easier to validate.

# Cryptography, Identity, Network Services, and Web Security

This chapter connects wire protocols to trust. The recurring rule is:

Note. Parse and bound bytes first, authenticate the peer and message second, authorize
the requested action third, and only then perform a side effect.

## 1. Client, Server, Proxy, and Load Balancer Are Roles

- Role: client. Initiates/accepts: initiates a request/connection. Main responsibility: asks for a service.
- Role: server. Initiates/accepts: accepts and responds. Main responsibility: owns a service contract.
- Role: forward proxy. Initiates/accepts: client connects to it. Main responsibility: reaches destinations for clients.
- Role: reverse proxy. Initiates/accepts: appears to be the server. Main responsibility: fronts one or more origins.
- Role: load balancer. Initiates/accepts: selects a healthy destination. Main responsibility: distributes connections/requests.
- Role: gateway. Initiates/accepts: translates policy/protocol/domain boundary. Main responsibility: mediates unlike systems.
- Role: C D N edge. Initiates/accepts: reverse proxy near users. Main responsibility: caches/serves/forwards content.

One process can play several roles. A browser is an H T T P client and may also accept
local debugging connections; a reverse proxy is a server to the client and a client to
the origin. The proxy-server guide compares
forward and reverse placement.

## 2. Ports Identify Transport Endpoints

An IP address selects an interface/host route; a transport port helps select an
endpoint within that network stack. A socket is identified by protocol and addressing
state, often summarized for T C P by:

Ports are 16-bit numbers, not security identities. A familiar port suggests an
application protocol but does not prove it; T L S, authentication, and application
parsing determine what the peer is actually speaking.

- Protocol: IP. Typical role: packet addressing/routing. Important distinction: best effort; no application message boundary.
- Protocol: T C P. Typical role: reliable ordered byte stream. Important distinction: no record boundaries.
- Protocol: U D P. Typical role: message-oriented datagrams. Important distinction: loss, duplication, and reordering possible.
- Protocol: quick. Typical role: secure multiplexed transport over U D P. Important distinction: application still defines messages.
- Protocol: H T T P. Typical role: request/response semantics. Important distinction: may run over T C P/T L S or quick.
- Protocol: D N S. Typical role: names and typed resource records. Important distinction: caching and authority are core.
- Protocol: S S H. Typical role: secure remote shell/tunneling protocol. Important distinction: not the same protocol as T L S.
- Protocol: F T P. Typical role: legacy file-transfer control/data channels. Important distinction: plaintext unless separately secured.

## 3. H T T P Is a Typed Message Exchange

Simplified H T T P/1.1 shape:

The blank line ends headers. Content-Length counts body octets, not Unicode
characters. H T T P/2 and H T T P/3 encode frames differently but retain H T T P method, status,
field, representation, and caching semantics.

- Header: Host /:authority. Boundary question: which virtual service is addressed?
- Header: Content-Type. Boundary question: how should body bytes be interpreted?
- Header: Content-Encoding. Boundary question: which representation coding must be decoded?
- Header: Accept. Boundary question: which response media types can the client process?
- Header: Authorization. Boundary question: what credential accompanies this request?
- Header: Cookie / Set-Cookie. Boundary question: which browser-managed state is sent or stored?
- Header: Origin. Boundary question: which web origin initiated the request?
- Header: Cache-Control. Boundary question: where/how long may the response be reused?
- Header: Forwarded. Boundary question: what proxy-origin information is asserted, and by whom?

Trust forwarding headers only from proxies you control and after stripping untrusted
copies at the edge.

## 4. D N S Resolves Typed Names Through Delegation

- Record: A / AAAA. Purpose: IPv4 / IPv6 address.
- Record: CNAME. Purpose: alias to another canonical name.
- Record: NS. Purpose: authoritative server delegation.
- Record: MX. Purpose: mail exchanger.
- Record: TXT. Purpose: arbitrary text used by several higher-level protocols.
- Record: SRV. Purpose: service location with port/priority/weight.
- Record: CAA. Purpose: certificate-authority issuance policy.

A TTL bounds how long a cached record should be reused; it is not a propagation
guarantee or lease on the whole Internet. A recursive resolver answers from cache or
pursues referrals; an authoritative server answers from the zones it serves. D N S sec
authenticates signed D N S data and denial proofs, but it does not encrypt ordinary D N S
queries. See RFC 7719 D N S terminology.

## 5. F T P, F T P S, S F T P, and File APIs Are Not Synonyms

- Name: F T P. Built on: separate T C P control and data channels. Security note: credentials/data are plaintext by default.
- Name: F T P S. Built on: F T P protected with T L S. Security note: still retains F T P's channel/firewall complexity.
- Name: S F T P. Built on: S S H file-transfer subsystem. Security note: not F T P over S S H.
- Name: SCP. Built on: S S H-based copying protocol/tooling. Security note: different semantics from S F T P.
- Name: HTTPS object A P I. Built on: H T T P/T L S. Security note: common for cloud/object storage.

For new systems, prefer a maintained secure protocol with explicit authentication,
integrity, limits, resumability, atomic destination behavior, and path containment.

## 6. V P N, Tunnel, and Shadowsocks Cover Different Traffic

- Mechanism: application proxy. Usually captures: traffic from configured applications. Endpoint sees: proxy as network peer.
- Mechanism: SOCKS proxy. Usually captures: supported T C P/U D P destinations. Endpoint sees: proxy as relay.
- Mechanism: Shadowsocks. Usually captures: traffic explicitly sent through local secure split proxy. Endpoint sees: remote relay.
- Mechanism: V P N. Usually captures: selected IP routes or an interface. Endpoint sees: V P N gateway as routed hop.
- Mechanism: S S H tunnel. Usually captures: explicitly forwarded ports/connections. Endpoint sees: S S H server as relay.

Shadowsocks describes itself as a
secure split proxy loosely based on socks five: local and remote components protect the
relay leg, then the remote connects to the target. It is not automatically a
device-wide V P N, anonymity system, or substitute for end-to-end T L S.

A tunnel changes routing and observation points. It does not make a malicious endpoint
safe, and D N S or other traffic can escape if routing policy is incomplete.

## 7. Encoding, Compression, Encryption, and Obfuscation

- Transformation: encoding. Goal: represent data under a syntax. Key required?: no. Reversible?: yes. Security property: none.
- Transformation: decoding. Goal: recover represented data. Key required?: no. Reversible?: yes. Security property: none.
- Transformation: compression. Goal: remove redundancy. Key required?: no. Reversible?: yes/lossy. Security property: none.
- Transformation: encryption. Goal: confidentiality under a key. Key required?: yes. Reversible?: yes with key. Security property: confidentiality; integrity only with authenticated mode.
- Transformation: hashing. Goal: fixed-size one-way digest. Key required?: no. Reversible?: not generally. Security property: integrity building block.
- Transformation: M A C. Goal: authenticate bytes with shared key. Key required?: yes. Reversible?: verification. Security property: integrity/authenticity to key holders.
- Transformation: digital signature. Goal: authenticate bytes with private/public keys. Key required?: private to sign. Reversible?: verification. Security property: integrity, signer-key authenticity.
- Transformation: obfuscation. Goal: make understanding harder. Key required?: usually no. Reversible?: intentionally difficult. Security property: delay, not a trust boundary.

Base64, hex, U R L encoding, and character encodings are not encryption. Packing,
minification, symbol stripping, control-flow flattening, and string hiding are forms of
obfuscation, not permission checks.

For data that will be both compressed and encrypted, protocols generally compress
before encryption, because good ciphertext should not retain compressible patterns.
Compression of attacker-influenced data beside secrets can leak through output length,
so security protocols need a threat-specific policy.

## 8. Symmetric and Asymmetric Cryptography Work Together

- Family: symmetric encryption. Key relationship: same secret protects/recovers. Good at: fast bulk confidentiality. Main operational risk: safely sharing/rotating the secret.
- Family: M A C. Key relationship: shared secret authenticates. Good at: fast message integrity. Main operational risk: every verifier can also forge.
- Family: asymmetric encryption/KEM. Key relationship: public/private pair. Good at: establishing a shared secret. Main operational risk: key validation and algorithm misuse.
- Family: digital signature. Key relationship: private signs; public verifies. Good at: software/document identity and integrity. Main operational risk: private-key compromise.

Real protocols are commonly hybrid: authenticate peers and establish a fresh shared
secret using asymmetric techniques, then protect bulk records with an authenticated
symmetric cipher. Use a maintained protocol/library and an A E A D construction; do not
invent a cipher mode, nonce scheme, padding scheme, or certificate validator.

## 9. Signing Protects Exact Bytes and Context

A safe signing design defines:

The domain label prevents a valid signature for one protocol from being replayed as a
different kind of authorization. Canonical encoding prevents multiple byte
representations of the “same” logical value. Verification must also check purpose,
identity, time policy, revocation/status policy, and key trust—not only the math.

## 10. Certificates Bind Keys to Claims

A digital certificate is a signed structure binding a public key to names/attributes
under an issuer and validity policy.

T L S certificate validation commonly checks:

- a chain to a trusted anchor;
- signatures and allowed algorithms;
- current validity interval;
- requested hostname/IP against subject alternative names;
- key usage and extended key usage;
- basic constraints/path limits;
- status/revocation policy when applicable.

Installing a certificate does not itself prove control of the corresponding private
key; T L S proves possession during the handshake. A self-signed certificate can encrypt
a connection but provides no external identity unless its fingerprint/key is trusted
through another channel.

## 11. T L S and S S H Protect Different Application Boundaries

T L S provides a secure transport for protocols such as H T T P, database connections, and
custom services. S S H defines secure remote login, channels, port forwarding, and file
transfer.

- Question: common identity. T L S: certificate name/key. S S H: host key plus user credential.
- Question: common trust store. T L S: public/private CAs. S S H: known hosts, enterprise C A, explicit key.
- Question: application. T L S: layered beneath an application protocol. S S H: includes channel/subsystem model.
- Question: first-use risk. T L S: P K I/domain validation policy. S S H: host-key verification/T O F U policy.

Both require correct host identity verification. Disabling certificate or host-key
checks turns active interception into expected behavior. See
T L S 1.3 and the
T L S-versus-S S H guide.

## 12. Authentication, Authorization, and A P I Keys

- Step: identification. Question: which identity is being claimed?
- Step: authentication. Question: what evidence supports that claim?
- Step: session establishment. Question: how is repeated proof represented safely?
- Step: authorization. Question: may this identity perform this action on this object now?
- Step: accounting/audit. Question: what security-relevant decision occurred?

An A P I key is usually a bearer secret identifying an integration/project; it is not
automatically an end-user identity. Store only a verifier/hash when possible, show the
secret once, scope it, rotate it, rate-limit it, and keep it out of URLs and logs.

Authorization belongs at the object/action boundary. “User is logged in” does not imply
“user may read record 42.”

## 13. Password Storage Uses Slow Verification, Salt, and Policy

Passwords should be stored with a maintained password-hashing function, not
reversible encryption or a fast general-purpose hash.

- Component: per-password salt. Purpose: makes equal passwords produce different stored verifiers.
- Component: memory-hard work factor. Purpose: makes each offline guess expensive.
- Component: pepper (optional). Purpose: separate server-held secret outside the database.
- Component: algorithm/version fields. Purpose: allow verification and future upgrades.
- Component: rehash-on-login. Purpose: migrate accepted old parameters to current policy.
- Component: rate limiting/M F A. Purpose: reduce online-guessing and credential-reuse damage.

A salt is not secret and must be unique enough for each password. A pepper is secret
and must be rotatable with an incident plan. Follow current
OWASP password-storage guidance
rather than copying fixed parameters forever.

### Rust Demonstration: Hash and Verify a Password With Argon two I D

Note. Terminology correction: authentication systems normally hash passwords;
they do not encrypt them. Encryption is reversible with a key. Password verification
should compare against a deliberately expensive, one-way verifier.

The current RustCrypto argon2 password A P I demonstrates the full data flow:

The stored string contains enough public information to repeat verification. It does
not contain the original password or a decryption key.

Example lifecycle:

Do not log either the password or the full authentication request. In a production
service, use generic externally visible failure messages and separately rate-limit
attempts.

- P H C field: Algorithm identifier. Why verification needs it: Selects Argon two I D rather than an unrelated function.
- P H C field: Version. Why verification needs it: Reconstructs the algorithm definition used originally.
- P H C field: Memory/time/parallelism costs. Why verification needs it: Repeats the intended amount of work.
- P H C field: Salt. Why verification needs it: Makes equal passwords produce unrelated stored values.
- P H C field: Derived verifier. Why verification needs it: Value against which the candidate is checked.

The salt prevents attackers from amortizing one precomputed table across many accounts.
The memory-hard cost makes each guess consume meaningful memory and time. Neither salt
nor cost makes a weak password strong, so online rate limits, M F A, breach detection,
and password screening remain separate controls.

#### Rehash-on-Login Is a Format Migration

Because the P H C string carries its parameters, old records remain verifiable after the
service adopts stronger parameters:

This is the same versioned-decoding pattern used for network messages, filesystem
records, bytecode, and serialized ASTs: decode the old representation, validate it,
then deliberately emit the current representation.

Note. Boundary: fixed cost parameters copied from an old example are not a permanent
security policy. Benchmark current guidance on the deployment hardware, cap concurrent
hashing work, and plan how parameter upgrades affect service capacity.

## 14. Sessions, Cookies, Tokens, and JWTs

- Item: session. Meaning: server-recognized continuity of authenticated state.
- Item: cookie. Meaning: browser storage/attachment mechanism scoped by attributes.
- Item: bearer token. Meaning: possession grants the represented authority.
- Item: refresh token. Meaning: longer-lived credential used to obtain new access tokens.
- Item: J W T. Meaning: signed and/or encrypted claims container with defined registered claims.

J W T is a format, not an authentication architecture. A signed J W T is normally
readable; the signature prevents undetected modification but does not encrypt claims.
Validate the allowed algorithm, signature, issuer, audience, time claims, token type,
key selection, and application-specific authorization. See
RFC 7519.

For browser session cookies:

- use Secure so HTTPS is required;
- use HttpOnly to reduce script access;
- choose SameSite deliberately;
- set narrow Path/Domain;
- rotate the session ID after authentication/privilege changes;
- enforce idle and absolute expiry server-side;
- revoke or version sensitive sessions.

Follow the OWASP session-management guide.

## 15. C O R S, C S R F, and X S S Solve Different Problems

- Risk/control: same-origin policy. What it concerns: browser script reading another origin. Core defense: browser-enforced isolation.
- Risk/control: C O R S. What it concerns: server opts selected origins into cross-origin reads. Core defense: exact origin/method/header policy.
- Risk/control: C S R F. What it concerns: victim's browser sends authenticated unwanted request. Core defense: SameSite, C S R F token, origin checks, safe methods.
- Risk/control: X S S. What it concerns: attacker-controlled content executes as trusted page script. Core defense: contextual output encoding, safe D O M APIs, C S P.

C O R S is not authentication, and it does not stop non-browser clients from sending
requests. C S R F primarily matters when credentials are attached automatically, such as
cookies. X S S can often defeat C S R F tokens by acting inside the trusted origin, so
preventing injection remains essential. Use the OWASP C S R F guide,
OWASP X S S guide,
and MDN C O R S guide.

## 16. Fingerprinting and Canvas Are Privacy Boundaries

Fingerprinting combines observable features—headers, screen properties, fonts,
rendering differences, timing, device capabilities, and storage state—to correlate a
browser/device. Canvas fingerprinting renders controlled content and hashes or analyzes
the resulting pixels, whose small differences may reflect graphics and font stacks.

Defensive/product guidance:

- collect only what a documented purpose requires;
- prefer authenticated account/device keys over probabilistic fingerprints;
- obtain consent where required and define retention;
- treat a fingerprint as a noisy risk signal, not identity proof;
- give users recovery and appeal paths;
- avoid silently expanding the attributes collected.

## 17. Random Numbers Must Match the Domain

- Need: simulation/test reproducibility. Appropriate source: explicitly seeded deterministic pseudo random number generator.
- Need: randomized algorithm. Appropriate source: fast pseudo random number generator with documented seed.
- Need: session/token/key/nonce. Appropriate source: operating-system cryptographic randomness through a maintained library.
- Need: unique database ID. Appropriate source: U U I D/sequence according to collision/order policy.

Never use timestamps, process IDs, ordinary hashes, or a non-cryptographic pseudo random number generator for
secrets. Never reuse a nonce where the selected A E A D/signature scheme forbids it.
Randomness tests cannot prove that a secret generator is unpredictable; entropy source,
state protection, reseeding, and construction matter. NIST maintains current
cryptographic standards and random-bit-generation guidance.

### Rust Demonstration: Reproducible and Secret Randomness Are Different APIs

Random-number generation is a state machine:

The same seed reproduces a pseudorandom sequence for the same generator definition.
That is useful for tests and simulations, but it is precisely why a public fixed seed
cannot protect a token or cryptographic key.

The current rand A P I makes the generator choice visible:

Modulo bias occurs when the raw generator range is not an exact multiple of the desired
range. For a byte mapped with % 6, the 256 possible bytes cannot be divided evenly
among six outcomes, so some results occur more often. A maintained range sampler uses a
correct reduction strategy such as rejection sampling.

For secret material, request bytes from the operating system and handle failure:

SysRng is the rand 0.10 name for the system source formerly exposed as OsRng.
Password-hashing crates may re-export a compatible OsRng through their own
rand core dependency, as the Argon2 example above does. Follow the imports documented
for the exact crate version rather than assuming names from a different version compose.

- Property: Reproducible. Seeded simulation pseudo random number generator: Yes, within the generator's stability contract. Operating-system secret source: No.
- Property: Failure. Seeded simulation pseudo random number generator: Construction can often be infallible. Operating-system secret source: Entropy access can fail.
- Property: Fast bulk sampling. Seeded simulation pseudo random number generator: Yes. Operating-system secret source: Often seed a cryptographically secure pseudo random number generator for bulk work.
- Property: Appropriate for test replay. Seeded simulation pseudo random number generator: Yes. Operating-system secret source: Usually no.
- Property: Appropriate for keys/tokens. Seeded simulation pseudo random number generator: Only when securely seeded cryptographically secure pseudo random number generator policy says so. Operating-system secret source: Yes.
- Property: Seed may be logged. Seeded simulation pseudo random number generator: Only for non-secret simulations. Operating-system secret source: Never.

#### Distribution Is Separate From Entropy

An RNG produces raw unpredictable or pseudorandom state. A distribution maps that
state into the domain the program needs:

- Desired sample: Fair die. Distribution problem: Uniform integers 1 through 6.
- Desired sample: Random array index. Distribution problem: Uniform integers 0 through length minus one.
- Desired sample: Simulation noise. Distribution problem: Often normal, exponential, or domain-specific.
- Desired sample: Weighted choice. Distribution problem: Probability mass proportional to weights.
- Desired sample: Shuffle. Distribution problem: Unbiased permutation, normally Fisher–Yates.

This separation mirrors compilers:

The left side supplies undifferentiated input; the right side interprets it under a
mathematical contract.

#### A Token Also Needs an Encoding

Random bytes are not necessarily printable or safe in URLs. Encoding changes their
representation, not their entropy:

Hex would use 64 characters for the same 256 bits. Do not “improve” randomness by
hashing a timestamp or concatenating a process ID. Those values can be predictable even
when their encoded output looks irregular.

## 18. Sandboxing Is Authority Reduction

A sandbox restricts what code can observe or mutate. Mechanisms include:

- Mechanism: process boundary. Restricts: address space and crash containment.
- Mechanism: OS user/ACL. Restricts: files, objects, services.
- Mechanism: capability handle. Restricts: specific resource operations.
- Mechanism: syscall filter. Restricts: kernel entry points.
- Mechanism: namespace. Restricts: resource view.
- Mechanism: V M/hypervisor. Restricts: guest machine boundary.
- Mechanism: language/Wasm runtime. Restricts: instructions, memory, imports, fuel.
- Mechanism: broker process. Restricts: privileged operations through validated messages.

A sandbox is only as strong as its escape surface: syscalls, parsers, shared memory,
G P U/driver interfaces, JITs, I P C brokers, and configuration. Start with no authority and
grant narrow handles rather than passing ambient global access.

# T C P/IP State Machines and Bounded Buffers Through small T C P

Note. Library lens: small T C P removes the hosted socket illusion and exposes packet buffers, polling, timers, device tokens, socket sets, and T C P state. That makes protocol mechanics and resource bounds directly observable.

small T C P is a standalone, event-driven T C P/IP
stack designed for bare-metal and real-time systems. It can operate without heap
allocation, making queues, socket buffers, timing, and packet work visible rather than
hiding them behind an operating-system socket A P I.

## 1. Stack Architecture

- Layer: Device. Owns or describes: frame receive/transmit capability, MTU, medium.
- Layer: Interface. Owns or describes: IP addresses, routes, neighbor/protocol state.
- Layer: SocketSet. Owns or describes: heterogeneous sockets identified by stable handles.
- Layer: socket. Owns or describes: protocol state plus explicitly supplied buffers.
- Layer: application. Owns or describes: when to poll, consume, produce, retry, and time out.

This resembles a language runtime: the device is the host boundary, the interface is
runtime-wide state, sockets are managed objects, and poll is the scheduler step.

## 2. Memory Is Part of the Network Contract

In hosted code, examples may provide vector of u8 storage. In heapless code, the same
buffer abstractions can borrow fixed slices.

Prefer linker- or platform-provided single-init cells where available; the snippet makes
the low-level ownership contract visible, not idealizes static mut as an everyday A P I.

- Fixed resource: T C P receive buffer. Behavior when exhausted: advertised window shrinks; application must read.
- Fixed resource: T C P transmit buffer. Behavior when exhausted: send capacity disappears until packets advance.
- Fixed resource: U D P packet metadata/storage. Behavior when exhausted: datagram send/receive can fail.
- Fixed resource: neighbor cache. Behavior when exhausted: entries are replaced or resolution is delayed.
- Fixed resource: routes/addresses. Behavior when exhausted: insertion may fail at configured capacity.
- Fixed resource: reassembly storage. Behavior when exhausted: oversized/excess fragmented packets are dropped.

Note. Invariant: buffer size is observable protocol behavior. Treat it as a deliberate
capacity and backpressure decision, not an incidental allocation.

## 3. Polling Makes Scheduling Explicit

Interface poll(now, device, sockets) transmits queued socket data and processes
queued device packets. poll at or poll delay tells an event loop when protocol timers
want attention.

- Timing mistake: Poll too late. Consequence: retransmissions and quality of service suffer.
- Timing mistake: Busy-poll continuously. Consequence: wasted C P U and energy.
- Timing mistake: Use a non-monotonic clock. Consequence: broken timeout/retransmission reasoning.
- Timing mistake: Drain unlimited ingress. Consequence: other real-time work may starve.
- Timing mistake: Ignore application backpressure. Consequence: buffers fill and useful work is displaced.

The current interface A P I warns that a full poll() may process an unbounded number of
queued ingress packets. In a cooperative or real-time scheduler, use
poll ingress single, poll egress, and poll maintenance to create explicit yield
points and bounded work.

## 4. Socket State Is a Protocol State Machine

- Readiness/state query: is open(). Meaning for application logic: socket is not fully closed.
- Readiness/state query: is active(). Meaning for application logic: connection has active protocol state.
- Readiness/state query: can recv(). Meaning for application logic: receive would produce application data.
- Readiness/state query: may recv(). Meaning for application logic: peer may still send data in the future.
- Readiness/state query: can send(). Meaning for application logic: transmit buffer currently has capacity.
- Readiness/state query: may send(). Meaning for application logic: connection may still permit future sends.

Do not equate can recv() equals equals false with EOF. Like a V M or coroutine, a socket can be
temporarily unable to progress while remaining live.

## 5. A Networked Language Runtime

- Runtime feature: remote REPL. small T C P integration decision: frame parser, source limit, authentication, timeout.
- Runtime feature: debugger protocol. small T C P integration decision: request IDs, cancellation, bounded diagnostic output.
- Runtime feature: package transfer. small T C P integration decision: chunking, hash verification, storage budget.
- Runtime feature: actor/message runtime. small T C P integration decision: U D P loss policy or T C P backpressure.
- Runtime feature: embedded web endpoint. small T C P integration decision: H T T P parser limits and one/many-connection policy.
- Runtime feature: telemetry. small T C P integration decision: sampling, queue eviction, reconnect/backoff.

Keep protocol parsing independent from the socket. Feed it byte slices and test it with
fragmentation, coalescing, truncation, invalid lengths, and capacity exhaustion. Then
test the stack in a loopback or TAP-based lab you control.

## 6. Test Faults, Not Only Happy Packets

The upstream hosted examples support packet dropping, corruption, size limits, rate
limits, and PCAP capture. These are valuable language-runtime tests too:

- Injected condition: packet loss. Runtime property to verify: retry/timeout does not duplicate semantic work.
- Injected condition: corruption. Runtime property to verify: checksums and parsers reject damaged input.
- Injected condition: tiny MTU. Runtime property to verify: fragmentation assumptions are not baked in.
- Injected condition: tiny socket buffer. Runtime property to verify: backpressure reaches the producer.
- Injected condition: slow polling. Runtime property to verify: deadlines fail predictably.
- Injected condition: connection reset. Runtime property to verify: pending requests finish with structured errors.

Run raw-interface and fault-injection experiments only on an isolated lab interface or
network you are authorized to control.

Note. Next: quick keeps U D P's user-space flexibility while adding secure connections,
reliable streams, loss recovery, and congestion control.

# Reliable Multiplexed Transport Through quick and Quinn

Note. Library lens: Quinn exposes quick's actual model: one encrypted connection, independent ordered streams, datagrams, congestion/loss feedback, flow control, and cancellation.

The Quinn networking introduction
compares T C P, U D P, and quick, then motivates quick through latency and head-of-line
blocking. Quinn is an async Rust implementation of quick.

## 1. Transport Comparison

- Property: connection. T C P: yes. U D P: no. quick: yes.
- Property: reliability/order. T C P: one reliable ordered byte stream. U D P: neither provided. quick: reliable streams; optional datagrams.
- Property: implementation. T C P: usually kernel. U D P: usually kernel primitive. quick: mainly user space over U D P.
- Property: security. T C P: separate T L S. U D P: application-defined. quick: T L S integrated into the protocol.
- Property: multiplexing. T C P: application protocol must add it. U D P: application-defined. quick: multiple independent streams.
- Property: migration. T C P: connection tied to address tuple. U D P: application-defined. quick: connection IDs can support migration.

quick is not “reliable U D P.” It is a connection protocol built over U D P with its own
handshake, encryption, stream, loss-recovery, and congestion-control behavior.

## 2. Head-of-Line Blocking Has Layers

T C P exposes one ordered stream. If a packet containing earlier bytes is lost, later
bytes cannot be delivered to the application even if they belong to an unrelated
logical request.

Loss on one quick stream does not prevent complete data on another stream from becoming
available to the application. Congestion control still couples traffic at the
connection/path level, so “independent” does not mean resource-free.

## 3. Quinn's Core Objects

- Object/A P I: Endpoint. Responsibility: bind U D P socket, accept/connect, own quick state.
- Object/A P I: Connection. Responsibility: secure peer session and shared transport state.
- Object/A P I: open bi/accept bi. Responsibility: create/accept bidirectional streams.
- Object/A P I: open uni/accept uni. Responsibility: create/accept one-way streams.
- Object/A P I: datagram A P I. Responsibility: bounded unreliable messages.
- Object/A P I: connection stats. Responsibility: observe loss, RTT, congestion, and traffic.

## 4. A Bounded Request on an Existing Connection

The connection setup is deliberately separate because certificate trust, server name,
transport configuration, socket address, timeouts, and resumption policy belong to a
larger security contract.

## 5. Streams Need Application Framing

A quick stream is still a byte stream. Opening one stream per request can provide a
natural boundary, but a long-lived stream still needs explicit framing.

- Choice: one stream per request. Appropriate when: independent request/response exchanges.
- Choice: long-lived framed stream. Appropriate when: ordered session, REPL, incremental protocol.
- Choice: unidirectional stream. Appropriate when: logs, asset push, one-way result.
- Choice: datagram. Appropriate when: stale data is worse than missing data.

Never allocate directly from an untrusted length field. Cap stream count, total buffered
bytes, message length, decompressed size, and work per connection.

## 6. Security and Replay

- Concern: certificate trust. Design requirement: verify the intended identity; do not disable verification.
- Concern: ALPN. Design requirement: negotiate the exact application protocol.
- Concern: 0-RTT. Design requirement: early data may be replayed; allow only replay-safe work.
- Concern: authentication. Design requirement: transport identity may not equal application user.
- Concern: migration. Design requirement: authorization must not rely only on source IP.
- Concern: key logging/tracing. Design requirement: keep diagnostic secrets out of production logs.

A safe 0-RTT operation is idempotent or otherwise replay-tolerant. “Charge account,”
“delete record,” and “run build” are not safe merely because the transport accepts them
early.

## 7. Backpressure and Concurrency

The semaphore bounds active stream handlers. A production server also needs connection
limits, idle timeouts, cancellation, task supervision, per-peer policy, and a strategy
for expected connection closure.

## 8. quick for a Language Runtime

- Runtime feature: compile request. Useful quick mapping: one bidirectional stream.
- Runtime feature: diagnostic stream. Useful quick mapping: server-initiated unidirectional stream.
- Runtime feature: debugger session. Useful quick mapping: long-lived framed bidirectional stream.
- Runtime feature: cancellation. Useful quick mapping: reset/stop the request stream plus request ID.
- Runtime feature: live telemetry. Useful quick mapping: datagrams when old samples have no value.
- Runtime feature: package/object transfer. Useful quick mapping: independent reliable streams with hashes.

Define semantic cancellation separately from transport reset: stopping byte delivery
does not automatically roll back compilation, database work, or external effects.

Note. Next: graphics and media systems explain the vectors, transforms, sampling,
buffers, G P U queues, and timing models that browsers later use to produce pixels,
audio, and video.

# Graphics and Media Systems: 3D, GPUs, Audio, and Video

Note. Library lens: W G P U and S D L are used to make linear algebra, resource layouts, G P U queues, audio timing, and presentation contracts concrete.

This chapter applies the earlier mathematical foundations to systems that must
produce perceptual output under strict memory, bandwidth, latency, and device
constraints. The equations describe the intended transformation; APIs such as W G P U
and S D L expose the resource ownership, queues, formats, and synchronization that make
the transformation run on real hardware.

Note. Prerequisite: read Mathematical Foundations
first. Here, vectors, matrices, sampling, approximation, and probability become
concrete graphics and media pipelines.

## 1. The 3D Transform Pipeline

- Stage: model. Question: Where is a vertex relative to its object?. Frequent reversing clue: mesh vertex/index buffers.
- Stage: world. Question: Where is the entity in the scene?. Frequent reversing clue: transform/component structures.
- Stage: view. Question: Where is it relative to the camera?. Frequent reversing clue: camera basis or view matrix.
- Stage: projection. Question: How does depth map to a viewing volume?. Frequent reversing clue: field of view, near/far planes.
- Stage: clipping. Question: Which primitives intersect the visible volume?. Frequent reversing clue: clip-space w, plane tests.
- Stage: rasterization. Question: Which pixel samples does a triangle cover?. Frequent reversing clue: viewport and depth state.
- Stage: shading. Question: What color/material result is produced?. Frequent reversing clue: shader constants, textures.
- Stage: compositing. Question: How are layers combined for display?. Frequent reversing clue: render targets, blend state.
