Matrices compose transforms, and order matters. With column-vector notation,
projection asterisk view asterisk model asterisk position applies model first. An engine using row vectors
may write the apparent reverse. Recover convention by testing a known translation, not
by guessing from storage order.

Homogeneous coordinates add w: points usually use w = 1, directions use w = 0,
and perspective projection makes the later divide possible. A quaternion compactly
represents orientation and composes rotations without the same singularity problems as
Euler angles, but it still needs normalization and a documented multiplication order.

## 2. Scene Entities Are Relationships, Not Just Coordinates

An entity/component system often separates identity from storage:

- Part: entity ID. Purpose: stable logical identity. Low-level pattern: index plus generation counter.
- Part: component pool. Purpose: dense values of one type. Low-level pattern: contiguous array / sparse set.
- Part: transform hierarchy. Purpose: parent-child spatial relation. Low-level pattern: graph or indexed parent.
- Part: system. Purpose: behavior over a component query. Low-level pattern: loop or scheduled function.
- Part: resource. Purpose: shared world-level state. Low-level pattern: singleton handle or typed store.

When reversing an authorized build, repeated access patterns such as
base plus index asterisk stride plus field offset suggest an array of fixed-layout records.
Pointers from one record to several component pools suggest indirection. Do not name a
field from one observation: vary position, health, animation, and ownership separately
and record which bytes change.

For your own language, an entity declaration can lower to ordinary component schemas
and queries. The compiler can validate component names while the runtime chooses dense
storage for cache-friendly iteration.

## 3. A G P U Is an Asynchronous Throughput Machine

The C P U records work; the driver/runtime validates and translates it; the G P U consumes
commands later.

- G P U concept: shader. Mental model: small program executed across many invocations. Failure mode: divergent control flow.
- G P U concept: buffer. Mental model: typed interpretation over bytes. Failure mode: wrong stride/alignment/lifetime.
- G P U concept: texture. Mental model: multidimensional sampled storage. Failure mode: wrong format/color space/layout.
- G P U concept: command buffer. Mental model: recorded work for later execution. Failure mode: resource freed too early.
- G P U concept: pipeline state. Mental model: compiled collection of fixed and programmable stages. Failure mode: incompatible formats.
- G P U concept: barrier. Mental model: visibility/order constraint between uses. Failure mode: stale or racing data.
- G P U concept: fence. Mental model: completion signal visible to the host. Failure mode: C P U reads before completion.
- G P U concept: staging memory. Mental model: host-friendly transfer area. Failure mode: excess copies and bandwidth.

Synchronization is part of correctness. “The submit call returned” usually means
the queue accepted work, not that rendering finished. The same distinction appears in
async input and output, D M A, databases, and distributed acknowledgements.

### W G P U as an Executable Model of G P U Work

Current W G P U APIs make the abstract G P U pipeline visible as a set of separate
capabilities and timelines:

- W G P U object: Instance. Computer-science model it exposes: discovery of available graphics backends.
- W G P U object: Adapter. Computer-science model it exposes: one implementation's capabilities and limits.
- W G P U object: Device. Computer-science model it exposes: validated resource and pipeline factory.
- W G P U object: Queue. Computer-science model it exposes: ordered submission timeline for asynchronous work.
- W G P U object: CommandEncoder. Computer-science model it exposes: a mutable builder for a later immutable command buffer.
- W G P U object: render/compute pipeline. Computer-science model it exposes: compiled state machine describing legal execution.
- W G P U object: bind-group layout. Computer-science model it exposes: type-like contract for shader-visible resources.
- W G P U object: bind group. Computer-science model it exposes: concrete resources satisfying that contract.
- W G P U object: Surface. Computer-science model it exposes: finite pool of presentation images owned with the window system.

The C P U does not call a shader once per vertex. It records commands and resource
relationships, submits them to a queue, and continues while the G P U consumes that work.
Validation checks that bindings, formats, usage flags, and pipeline stages agree before
the backend reaches a less forgiving driver A P I.

This conceptual enum shows why “the Rust function returned” and “the G P U finished” are
different events. Correct resource reuse needs a completion signal or an A P I guarantee
that internally provides the same ordering.

The render pipeline also demonstrates separation of static and dynamic state:
shader entry points, vertex formats, color formats, depth rules, and primitive topology
are compiled into a reusable pipeline; buffers, textures, offsets, and draw counts vary
per submission. Moving stable validation out of the hot loop is the same idea used by
prepared database statements, compiled regular expressions, and bytecode VMs.

## 4. Digital Audio Is Timed Sampled Data

- Term: sample. Meaning: one amplitude value for one channel at one instant.
- Term: sample rate. Meaning: audio frames per second, such as 48,000.
- Term: channel. Meaning: one signal lane: left, right, center, and so on.
- Term: audio frame. Meaning: one sample for every channel at a time point.
- Term: bit depth / format. Meaning: integer or float representation of each sample.
- Term: buffer. Meaning: contiguous frames exchanged with a device or codec.
- Term: latency. Meaning: time from production/input to audible output/observation.
- Term: underrun. Meaning: consumer needs frames that the producer has not supplied.
- Term: clipping. Meaning: amplitude exceeds the representable range.
- Term: resampling. Meaning: change sample rate by interpolation/filtering.

Audio callbacks may run on a real-time-sensitive thread. Avoid blocking input and output,
unbounded allocation, slow locks, and logging in the callback. Feed it from a bounded
ring buffer and decide whether overload drops old data, inserts silence, or applies
backpressure.

## 5. Frequency, Windows, and Compression

A time-domain waveform says amplitude over time. A Fourier transform expresses the
same finite window as weighted frequencies. The sampling theorem links the highest
representable frequency to sample rate, while a reconstruction/anti-alias filter makes
the physical boundary practical.

- Operation: windowing. Purpose: reduce edge discontinuities in a finite analysis block.
- Operation: F F T. Purpose: compute a discrete Fourier transform efficiently.
- Operation: convolution. Purpose: apply an impulse response/filter.
- Operation: dynamic range compression. Purpose: reduce amplitude range; not file compression.
- Operation: lossless codec. Purpose: reproduce original samples exactly.
- Operation: lossy codec. Purpose: discard perceptually less important information.

“Compression” is overloaded. Always specify data compression, audio dynamics
compression, or geometric compression.

## 6. Video Separates Frames, Codecs, and Containers

- Layer: raw frame. Contains: pixels plus dimensions, format, stride, timestamp. Example concern: R G B vs Y C b C r.
- Layer: codec. Contains: compressed sequence syntax. Example concern: keyframes and inter-frame references.
- Layer: elementary stream. Contains: encoded chunks for one track. Example concern: decoder configuration.
- Layer: container. Contains: tracks, timestamps, metadata, seek indexes. Example concern: MP4, WebM, Matroska.
- Layer: transport. Contains: how chunks move between systems. Example concern: file, H T T P, RTP, quick.

A codec compresses/decompresses media; a container multiplexes tracks and
metadata. Demuxing finds encoded chunks; decoding turns chunks into frames. Seeking
often starts at a keyframe and decodes forward because predicted frames depend on other
frames.

Y C b C r separates luma from chroma. Formats such as 4:2:0 store color at lower spatial
resolution than brightness, reducing size. A correct frame parser must track:

- coded and display dimensions;
- pixel format and bit depth;
- per-plane stride and offset;
- color primaries, transfer function, and range;
- timestamps and time base;
- ownership while hardware decoding is in flight.

## 7. Media Reversing Is Layer Identification

For files and owned captures, begin with structure:

1. identify magic bytes and the container;
2. parse bounded lengths and indexes;
3. enumerate tracks, formats, and time bases;
4. hand encoded payloads to a maintained codec library;
5. compare decoded frames or samples, not only file hashes;
6. preserve the original and fuzz only disposable copies.

Do not mistake obfuscated metadata for a new codec. First test ordinary causes:
endianness, alignment, compression, encryption, checksums, and versioned headers.

Note. Language-design connection: arrays, sim-dee vectors, numeric conversions,
foreign-function interfaces, async device queues, and resource lifetimes all become
concrete when a language can safely express a small renderer or audio graph.

Sources: SDL3 audio model,
SDL3 pixel formats,
digital-audio concepts,
digital-video concepts,
and frames, codecs, and containers.

# How Chrome Works: Processes, Parsing, JavaScript, and Pixels

Chrome is not one parser attached to one window. It is an operating environment for
untrusted network programs. It combines networking, process isolation, compilers,
garbage collectors, databases, event loops, graphics, media, accessibility, and device
APIs behind the abstraction of a page.

Note. Core mental model: a browser repeatedly transforms untrusted bytes into more
structured representations while moving work across security and scheduling
boundaries. Each representation exists because the next stage needs different facts.

JavaScript can mutate earlier stages, so this is not a one-way batch compiler. It is an
incremental, concurrent pipeline with invalidation: a mutation marks affected derived
state dirty, and later work reconstructs only what is needed.

## 1. Navigation Is a Distributed State Machine

Entering a U R L begins a navigation whose state is coordinated by the privileged browser
process:

1. Parse and normalize the U R L.
2. Check policy, existing service workers, caches, and navigation history.
3. Resolve a host name through D N S when a network connection is needed.
4. Establish or reuse a transport connection; HTTPS authenticates the server and
negotiates encryption with T L S.
5. Send an H T T P request and validate response headers.
6. Choose or create a renderer process allowed to host the destination's site.
7. Commit the navigation, stream response data, and start parsing.
8. Discover and fetch dependent scripts, style sheets, fonts, images, and media.

The real browser supports redirects, authentication challenges, downloads, back/forward
cache, speculative preloading, cancellation, process swaps, error pages, and races
between them. The useful lesson is that an async operation is a state machine whose
late results must be checked against current identity and generation.

## 2. Chrome Uses Processes as Protection Domains

Chromium separates major responsibilities into operating-system processes. Exact
placement varies by platform and configuration, but the conceptual boundaries remain:

- Process/service: browser process. Main responsibility: windows, tabs, navigation, permissions, process policy. Why isolate it: privileged coordinator and authority broker.
- Process/service: renderer process. Main responsibility: Blink, page D O M/layout, V eight execution for hosted documents. Why isolate it: contains untrusted site code.
- Process/service: network service. Main responsibility: H T T P, proxy, D N S integration, cache, socket/T L S work. Why isolate it: central policy and fault isolation.
- Process/service: Viz/G P U process. Main responsibility: raster, compositing, G P U command submission, presentation. Why isolate it: isolates complex drivers and aggregates surfaces.
- Process/service: utility processes. Main responsibility: decoding and narrowly scoped services. Why isolate it: least privilege and crash containment.

A process has a separate virtual address space. A renderer cannot directly dereference a
browser-process object. It must request an operation through an I P C interface, which
creates a place to validate identity, ownership, size, and permission.

Multi-process design trades memory and I P C cost for:

- fault isolation — one renderer crash need not terminate every tab;
- security isolation — a compromised renderer receives limited operating-system
authority;
- parallelism — unrelated sites can run on different C P U cores;
- resource accounting — memory and C P U use can be attributed and reclaimed.

Threads solve scheduling within a protection domain; processes also change what memory
and operating-system capabilities are reachable.

## 3. Origin, Site, Frame, and Process Are Different Boundaries

An origin is normally the tuple (scheme, host, port). A site is a broader
grouping used by parts of the process model. A page contains a tree of frames, and a
cross-site iframe may live in a different renderer process from its parent.

- Object: document. Meaning: one parsed H T M L document with script and rendering state.
- Object: frame. Meaning: a position in the page's nested browsing-context tree.
- Object: origin. Meaning: principal used by the same-origin security model.
- Object: site instance. Meaning: group of related documents that may need synchronous access.
- Object: renderer process. Meaning: OS protection domain hosting one or more permitted site instances.

Site Isolation tries to prevent one renderer from receiving sensitive documents
belonging to another site. The browser process retains authoritative frame and security
state, and uses remote-frame proxies when related frames reside in different renderers.

Note. Invariant: a process locked to one site must not gain document data or
privileged capabilities belonging to another principal merely by forging an I P C
request.

## 4. Mojo I P C Makes Cross-Process Authority Explicit

Chromium commonly uses Mojo interfaces for typed inter-process communication. An
interface defines legal messages; endpoints grant the capability to send those
messages. Payloads are serialized because sender and receiver do not share ordinary
object pointers.

The enum is only a teaching model. The important ideas are transferable:

- deserialize into bounded, validated data;
- authenticate the sender's process/frame identity out of band;
- do not trust the sender's claim about its own origin or permissions;
- grant narrow endpoints rather than global ambient authority;
- handle endpoint closure, process death, cancellation, and version skew.

## 5. Blink and V eight Cooperate but Solve Different Problems

The renderer contains two major language systems:

- Component: Blink. Model: implements H T M L, C S S, D O M, layout, painting, accessibility, and web-platform bindings.
- Component: V eight. Model: parses, interprets, compiles, executes, and garbage-collects JavaScript/Web Assembly.

Blink owns host objects such as D O M nodes. V eight owns JavaScript values and execution.
Bindings translate calls such as element.appendChild(child) across the JavaScript and vice versa
Blink boundary while preserving identity, lifetime, exceptions, and security policy.

This is an F F I problem inside one process: two runtimes have different object models and
garbage collectors but expose one apparent programming environment.

## 6. H T M L Parsing Is Tokenization Plus Error-Recovering Tree Construction

H T M L parsing does more than split on angle brackets:

The tokenizer has states because the meaning of a character depends on context. in
ordinary text, a script, a comment, or an attribute does not always begin the same token.
The tree builder uses insertion modes and a stack of open elements to recover a
deterministic D O M even from malformed markup.

- Mechanism: encoding detection/decoding. Why it exists: bytes must become Unicode input.
- Mechanism: tokenizer state. Why it exists: characters have context-dependent meaning.
- Mechanism: insertion mode. Why it exists: tokens mean different tree operations in tables, body, templates, etc..
- Mechanism: stack of open elements. Why it exists: track nesting and recovery.
- Mechanism: speculative preload scanner. Why it exists: discover likely resources before the main parser reaches them.

Parser-blocking scripts can pause tree construction because they may inspect or mutate
the partially built document. Stylesheets can delay script execution when computed
style must be available. Therefore network order, parser order, and script semantics
interact.

## 7. D O M, Style, Layout, Paint, and Layers Are Separate Representations

One tree cannot efficiently answer every rendering question.

- Representation: D O M. Stores: document nodes and attributes. Omits or differs from: final visual geometry.
- Representation: style data. Stores: winning computed property values. Omits or differs from: pixel placement.
- Representation: layout objects/fragments. Stores: boxes, line fragments, sizes, positions. Omits or differs from: final painted pixels.
- Representation: paint records/display items. Stores: drawing operations in order. Omits or differs from: physical screen surfaces.
- Representation: property trees/layers. Stores: transform, clip, effect, scroll relationships. Omits or differs from: document semantics.
- Representation: raster tiles. Stores: pixel data for regions. Omits or differs from: high-level D O M meaning.

C S S processing includes selector matching, cascade origin, importance, specificity,
inheritance, custom properties, and computed-value resolution. Layout then solves box
geometry under constraints such as available width, formatting context, intrinsic size,
font metrics, and fragmentation.

A D O M node may produce no layout object (display: none), one or many fragments, or a
relationship that differs from D O M ancestry. This is why “the render tree” is a useful
introductory phrase but not one universal data structure.

## 8. Invalidation Makes Rendering Incremental

When state changes, Chrome records which derived stages are no longer valid:

The exact invalidation rules are far more selective. The model explains performance:

- changing geometry can require layout, paint, raster, and composite;
- changing color may require paint without layout;
- changing a compositor-managed transform may avoid main-thread layout and paint;
- reading layout immediately after a write can force pending work synchronously.

## 9. Rendering N G Splits Main-Thread and Compositor Work

The renderer's main thread runs scripts, document lifecycle work, style, layout, paint,
and event dispatch. A compositor thread can handle eligible scrolling and animations
without waiting for page JavaScript. The Viz process aggregates compositor frames,
rasters tiles, submits G P U work, and presents the final image.

Compositing does not mean every element owns a permanent texture. Layerization is a
resource decision balancing independent movement against memory, raster work, and
composition overhead.

- Stage: style. Typical bottleneck: broad selector invalidation and large affected subtrees.
- Stage: layout. Typical bottleneck: geometry dependencies and forced synchronous reads.
- Stage: paint. Typical bottleneck: many or expensive display items.
- Stage: raster. Typical bottleneck: large/complex tiles and image decode.
- Stage: composite. Typical bottleneck: too many surfaces/layers or G P U synchronization.
- Stage: present. Typical bottleneck: missed frame deadline or display backpressure.

## 10. Input and the Event Loop Are Scheduling Systems

Raw mouse, touch, keyboard, and wheel events begin in platform input systems. The
browser routes them to the correct frame and hit-tested target. Some scrolling can run
on the compositor thread; events requiring JavaScript reach the renderer main thread.

The web event loop coordinates several queues and checkpoints:

1. take one runnable task;
2. execute JavaScript to completion for that task;
3. drain the microtask queue at the specified checkpoint;
4. perform rendering work when the document and scheduler allow;
5. wait for more input, timers, network completion, or other work.

“Run to completion” prevents two JavaScript callbacks on one agent from interleaving at
arbitrary instructions, but callbacks can observe mutations made by earlier tasks, and
await divides a function into multiple scheduled continuations.

## 11. V eight Uses Tiered Execution to Balance Startup and Peak Speed

Current V eight uses multiple execution tiers because no single compiler optimizes
simultaneously for fastest startup, lowest compilation cost, and highest peak speed:

- Mechanism: bytecode. Computer-science idea: compact instruction set independent of host C P U.
- Mechanism: interpreter. Computer-science idea: fetch/decode/execute loop over virtual instructions.
- Mechanism: inline cache. Computer-science idea: remember shapes/types observed at an operation.
- Mechanism: hidden class / map. Computer-science idea: shared structural description of object properties.
- Mechanism: speculative optimization. Computer-science idea: generate fast code under observed assumptions.
- Mechanism: deoptimization. Computer-science idea: reconstruct a less-specialized execution state when an assumption fails.
- Mechanism: tiering. Computer-science idea: spend optimization cost only on code that becomes hot.

For object.x, V eight can observe repeated objects with the same shape and cache where x
is stored. Optimized code checks the shape, loads from the known offset, and takes a
slow path or deoptimizes when the shape differs.

Note. Compiler connection: this combines parsing, bytecode, profiling, S S A/C F G
optimization, machine-code generation, stack maps, garbage-collector metadata, and
reversible mappings needed to reconstruct interpreter-visible state.

## 12. Garbage Collection Preserves Reachability Across Runtimes

V eight uses a generational heap because most newly allocated objects die young. Young
objects can be collected frequently; surviving objects move or promote into older
storage. Major collection traces reachable objects, reclaims unreachable storage, and
may compact fragmented pages.

- G C mechanism: roots. Why it is needed: begin the reachability graph from stacks, globals, and runtime handles.
- G C mechanism: young/old generations. Why it is needed: exploit different object lifetime distributions.
- G C mechanism: remembered sets. Why it is needed: find old-to-young references without scanning all old objects.
- G C mechanism: write barriers. Why it is needed: record graph mutations during generational/incremental/concurrent G C.
- G C mechanism: marking. Why it is needed: discover reachable objects.
- G C mechanism: sweeping. Why it is needed: return unreachable regions to free storage.
- G C mechanism: compaction. Why it is needed: move live objects together and update references.

Blink also manages garbage-collected objects. Cross-runtime bindings must ensure that a
live JavaScript wrapper keeps the corresponding host object valid when required, and
that cycles crossing runtime boundaries do not become invisible to tracing.

“Garbage collected” does not mean “memory is free immediately.” Reachable caches,
listener registrations, pending tasks, D O M references, and native resources can retain
large graphs indefinitely.

## 13. Caches and Storage Have Different Consistency and Lifetime Rules

Chrome coordinates multiple forms of state:

- Storage: memory cache. Purpose: reuse decoded or fetched resources quickly. Important boundary: process/document lifetime.
- Storage: H T T P cache. Purpose: reuse responses under protocol freshness rules. Important boundary: request method, headers, validators.
- Storage: cookies. Purpose: small request-associated state. Important boundary: domain/path/SameSite/Secure policy.
- Storage: Cache Storage. Purpose: service-worker-controlled response storage. Important boundary: origin and application policy.
- Storage: IndexedDB. Purpose: transactional structured object storage. Important boundary: origin and schema/version.
- Storage: local/session storage. Purpose: small synchronous key/value APIs. Important boundary: origin and browsing-session rules.
- Storage: back/forward cache. Purpose: preserve an entire page for fast history navigation. Important boundary: page eligibility and lifecycle.

A service worker is a programmable request intermediary, not a magical offline flag. It
has an event-driven lifetime, can intercept in-scope requests, and must coordinate
version changes and durable state without assuming it runs forever.

H T T P caching is a protocol between client, intermediary, and origin server. Freshness,
validators, request headers, response headers, partitioning, and invalidation determine
whether a stored response is reusable.

## 14. Browser Security Is Layered Boundary Enforcement

- Boundary: site and vice versa site. Mechanism: same-origin policy, Site Isolation, frame/process policy.
- Boundary: renderer and vice versa operating system. Mechanism: sandbox and brokered capabilities.
- Boundary: document and vice versa network response. Mechanism: C O R S, CORB/ORB, content-type and fetch rules.
- Boundary: text and vice versa executable script. Mechanism: C S P, Trusted Types, parsing contexts.
- Boundary: user and vice versa site. Mechanism: permission prompts, gestures, profile policy.
- Boundary: JavaScript and vice versa native engine. Mechanism: checked bindings, V eight sandboxing, memory safety defenses.

No one layer proves the browser safe. The model assumes a parser, J I T, renderer, or G P U
component can contain bugs, then limits what a compromise can reach. This is defense
in depth: validation, least privilege, process isolation, origin policy, memory
protection, and exploit mitigations overlap.

## 15. Diagnose Browser Performance by Finding the Delayed Stage

- Symptom: slow initial content. Likely investigation: D N S, connection, T L S, response timing, parser-blocking resources.
- Symptom: long input delay. Likely investigation: main-thread task length and event scheduling.
- Symptom: animation jank. Likely investigation: layout/paint invalidation, raster cost, compositor eligibility.
- Symptom: memory growth. Likely investigation: retained D O M/listeners, caches, JS heap, images, G P U resources.
- Symptom: slow script after warm-up. Likely investigation: unstable object shapes, deoptimization, allocation/G C.
- Symptom: visual update appears late. Likely investigation: frame deadline, compositor commit, raster, G P U/presentation.

Measure before naming the cause. A “G P U problem” can originate in main-thread layout;
a “JavaScript problem” can be network blocking; a “memory leak” can be intentional cache
retention. Performance tools are useful because their tracks correspond to real queues,
threads, representations, and transitions in the architecture.

## 16. Chrome Is a Capstone for Computer Science

- Earlier concept: grammars and automata. Chrome manifestation: H T M L/C S S/JavaScript parsing.
- Earlier concept: compilers and VMs. Chrome manifestation: V eight bytecode, tiering, speculation, deoptimization.
- Earlier concept: graph algorithms. Chrome manifestation: D O M traversal, dependency graphs, G C reachability.
- Earlier concept: operating systems. Chrome manifestation: processes, virtual memory, scheduling, permissions.
- Earlier concept: networking. Chrome manifestation: D N S, H T T P, T L S, proxying, caching, quick.
- Earlier concept: databases. Chrome manifestation: origin-scoped durable storage and transactions.
- Earlier concept: distributed systems. Chrome manifestation: I P C, failure, cancellation, versioning, partial results.
- Earlier concept: graphics and linear algebra. Chrome manifestation: transforms, clipping, raster, compositing.
- Earlier concept: security. Chrome manifestation: sandboxing, principals, capabilities, validation.
- Earlier concept: real-time reasoning. Chrome manifestation: frame deadlines, input latency, media synchronization.

For your own language or runtime, do not copy Chrome's scale. Copy the separation of
models:

Current primary references: Chromium multi-process architecture,
process model and Site Isolation,
Rendering N G architecture,
Chromium network service,
V eight high-level overview,
and V eight garbage collection.

Note. Next: G U I and T U I architectures generalize the same event, state,
invalidation, layout, rendering, and presentation models outside the browser.

# User Interfaces: Events, State, Layout, Windows, G U I, and T U I

Note. Library lens: win it, E G U I, Rat a too ee, and W G P U are used to compare event sources, retained versus immediate state, layout, diffing, G P U command recording, and OS presentation.

A user interface is a feedback loop over state:

A G U I targets windows and pixels. A T U I targets a terminal's character-cell protocol.
Their output devices differ, but both need event handling, state, layout, focus,
incremental redraw, accessibility, testing, and cleanup.

## 1. A G U I Sits on Several Layers

- Layer: application model. Responsibility: domain state and commands. Rust examples: your structs, enums, reducers.
- Layer: UI toolkit. Responsibility: widgets, layout, focus, accessibility, styling. Rust examples: E G U I, iced, Slint, GTK4.
- Layer: text/2D scene. Responsibility: shaping, paths, images, glyphs, clipping. Rust examples: Skia, Cairo, tiny-skia.
- Layer: graphics A P I. Responsibility: buffers, textures, shaders, command queues. Rust examples: W G P U, Vulkan, Metal, D3D12, OpenGL.
- Layer: window/event layer. Responsibility: windows, monitors, input, I M E, D P I, lifecycle. Rust examples: win it, SDL3.
- Layer: compositor/window system. Responsibility: combines application surfaces on screen. Rust examples: Wayland/X11, D W M, Quartz.
- Layer: driver/G P U/display. Responsibility: executes commands and scans completed images out. Rust examples: platform hardware stack.

A full toolkit may own most layers; a focused library may own one. win it creates
windows and dispatches events but is not a widget toolkit. W G P U submits graphics work
but does not provide buttons, layout, or text editing.

## 2. The Event Loop Owns Platform Lifecycle

Desktop platforms deliver resize, scale-factor, keyboard, pointer, touch, I M E, theme,
focus, suspend/resume, redraw, and close events. The event loop often has thread and
lifecycle restrictions, so create windows only when the platform says it is valid.

Current win it uses ApplicationHandler with EventLoop run app; windows are commonly
created through ActiveEventLoop during resumed. A redraw request means “produce a
frame when dispatched,” not “draw immediately inside every input callback.”

Separate raw platform events from domain messages:

This pure update core can be driven by a G U I, T U I, tests, network command, or your own
language runtime.

## 3. Retained, Immediate, and Declarative UI

- Model: retained mode. Programmer describes: create/update a persistent widget/object tree. Toolkit retains: widgets and invalidation state. Good fit: document/form-style applications.
- Model: immediate mode. Programmer describes: rebuild desired widgets each frame/pass. Toolkit retains: interaction memory keyed by IDs. Good fit: tools, editors, debug panels.
- Model: declarative/reactive. Programmer describes: desired UI as a function of state. Toolkit retains: dependency graph or virtual/retained representation. Good fit: data-driven apps.
- Model: Elm/reducer style. Programmer describes: messages, pure update, view. Toolkit retains: runtime effects/subscriptions/tree. Good fit: explicit state transitions.

“Immediate” does not mean the G P U redraws every pixel for every method call. A toolkit
can collect a frame's UI description, tessellate once, and repaint only when requested.
“Retained” does not eliminate application state; it means the toolkit retains UI
objects and schedules updates.

Stable widget IDs matter in immediate-mode systems: focus, drag state, open/closed
state, and text editing must remain attached to the same logical control across frames.

## 4. Layout Is Constraint Solving Over a Tree

Most layout engines perform some variation of:

- Input: constraints. Examples: minimum/maximum width and height.
- Input: intrinsic size. Examples: text, image, control content.
- Input: policy. Examples: row/column/grid, flex, alignment, padding.
- Input: environment. Examples: D P I scale, font metrics, locale, theme.
- Input: output. Examples: logical rectangles plus clip and transform.

Use logical units for layout and convert to physical pixels at the rendering
boundary. Fractional scale factors mean rounding each child independently can create
gaps or drift; distribute rounding with a documented policy.

Cycles require rules. “Parent sizes to child while child fills parent” cannot be solved
by naive recursion. Real toolkits use bounded passes, priority constraints, or
well-defined flex/intrinsic algorithms.

## 5. Painting Produces a Display List

Widgets should usually emit drawing primitives rather than talk directly to the G P U:

The renderer then:

1. resolves clips/transforms and z-order;
2. shapes text and locates glyphs;
3. batches compatible primitives;
4. uploads changed buffers/textures;
5. records graphics commands;
6. submits and presents;
7. retains resources until the G P U has finished.

Dirty-region/damage tracking avoids repainting unaffected areas. Immediate-mode
toolkits may still cache fonts, meshes, textures, and unchanged windows.

## 6. Text Is the Hardest “Simple” Widget

Text input/rendering involves:

- Concern: shaping. Why bytes or scalar values are insufficient: several characters may become one glyph or contextual forms.
- Concern: grapheme clusters. Why bytes or scalar values are insufficient: one perceived character may contain several scalars.
- Concern: bidi. Why bytes or scalar values are insufficient: visual order can differ from logical storage order.
- Concern: line breaking. Why bytes or scalar values are insufficient: language and punctuation rules affect breaks.
- Concern: font fallback. Why bytes or scalar values are insufficient: one run may require several fonts.
- Concern: cursor selection. Why bytes or scalar values are insufficient: byte, scalar, grapheme, glyph, and visual positions differ.
- Concern: I M E. Why bytes or scalar values are insufficient: composition text is provisional until committed.
- Concern: accessibility. Why bytes or scalar values are insufficient: semantic label/value/role differs from painted pixels.

Keep source text and semantic selection separate from shaped glyph positions. A glyph
atlas is a cache of rasterized glyph images, not the authoritative text.

## 7. Input Becomes Routing, Focus, and Gestures

- Mechanism: hit testing. Purpose: choose visual target under a point.
- Mechanism: focus. Purpose: choose keyboard/I M E target.
- Mechanism: pointer capture. Purpose: keep receiving drag events outside original bounds.
- Mechanism: event bubbling/capture. Purpose: route through ancestors under toolkit policy.
- Mechanism: gesture recognition. Purpose: convert temporal pointer/touch sequences into intent.
- Mechanism: command mapping. Purpose: turn shortcuts/menu actions into domain messages.

Do not identify keyboard shortcuts only by produced text; physical key, logical key,
modifiers, keyboard layout, and I M E composition are different representations.

## 8. Accessibility Is a Parallel Semantic Tree

A painted rectangle does not tell assistive technology that it is a checked,
disabled button named “Mute.” Expose a semantic tree containing:

- role, label, value, description, and state;
- parent/child relationships and reading order;
- focus and selection;
- available actions;
- live-region/change notifications;
- bounds when the platform requires them.

Keyboard operation, visible focus, contrast, scalable text, reduced motion, and screen
reader semantics are architectural requirements. Test the accessibility tree and real
assistive technology; a mouse-only visual test is insufficient.

## 9. G U I and Graphics Library Selection

- Library: win it. Layer/model: cross-platform windows/events. Choose it when: building a custom renderer or toolkit. It does not replace: widgets, text, renderer.
- Library: W G P U. Layer/model: safe cross-platform G P U A P I. Choose it when: custom 2D/3D/compute rendering. It does not replace: window/event loop and UI.
- Library: tiny-skia. Layer/model: small pure-Rust C P U 2D rasterizer. Choose it when: paths and pixels without a G P U stack. It does not replace: full widgets and windowing.
- Library: Skia / Cairo bindings. Layer/model: mature 2D graphics/text ecosystem. Choose it when: complex vector/text rendering or integration. It does not replace: application UI architecture.
- Library: E G U I / eframe. Layer/model: immediate-mode Rust G U I. Choose it when: tools, inspectors, editors, rapid native/web UI. It does not replace: native platform widgets.
- Library: iced. Layer/model: typed Elm-inspired G U I. Choose it when: message/update/view architecture and async tasks. It does not replace: OS-native widget toolkit.
- Library: Slint. Layer/model: declarative UI language plus Rust A P I. Choose it when: designer-friendly declarative apps, embedded/desktop targets. It does not replace: arbitrary platform-native widgets.
- Library: gtk4-rs / Relm4. Layer/model: bindings / Elm-like layer over GTK4. Choose it when: established toolkit, accessibility, GNOME integration. It does not replace: small dependency footprint.
- Library: Tauri. Layer/model: system webview plus Rust backend/capabilities. Choose it when: H T M L/C S S UI with a Rust desktop boundary. It does not replace: custom native/G P U widget toolkit.
- Library: SDL3. Layer/model: window/input/audio/2D/G P U-oriented platform layer. Choose it when: games, media tools, custom loops. It does not replace: complete application widget set.
- Library: Bevy. Layer/model: ECS game/application engine. Choose it when: data-oriented scenes, games, visual tools. It does not replace: traditional desktop widget semantics.

No toolkit is universally “best.” Decide using:

- Requirement: target platforms. Verify before choosing: desktop, web, mobile, embedded, remote.
- Requirement: look/behavior. Verify before choosing: native toolkit, custom brand, game/tool style.
- Requirement: accessibility/I M E. Verify before choosing: real support on every required platform.
- Requirement: renderer. Verify before choosing: C P U, G P U, webview/D O M, remote.
- Requirement: application model. Verify before choosing: immediate, retained, reducer, reactive.
- Requirement: packaging. Verify before choosing: runtime libraries, binary size, system dependencies.
- Requirement: licensing. Verify before choosing: toolkit and transitive/native dependencies.
- Requirement: testability. Verify before choosing: headless/test backend, event injection, accessibility snapshots.
- Requirement: maturity. Verify before choosing: releases, migration cost, issue responsiveness.

## 10. A Small Immediate-Mode E G U I View

The function describes the current frame's controls. E G U I remembers interaction state
through stable IDs and returns responses such as clicked. Long-running work belongs
in a task/thread; send a result back and request repaint rather than blocking the UI
thread.

## 11. How a T U I Reaches the Screen

A terminal user interface usually writes a stream of characters and control sequences
to a terminal emulator:

- Terminal concept: cell grid. Meaning: rows/columns containing glyph, style, and width state.
- Terminal concept: escape/control sequence. Meaning: protocol command for cursor, color, erase, mode, and more.
- Terminal concept: raw mode. Meaning: deliver key bytes/events without ordinary line editing/echo.
- Terminal concept: alternate screen. Meaning: temporary full-screen buffer used by many TUIs.
- Terminal concept: cursor state. Meaning: position, visibility, and style.
- Terminal concept: terminal size. Meaning: current rows/columns; can change asynchronously.
- Terminal concept: P T Y. Meaning: bidirectional pseudo-device connecting program and terminal/remote session.

The terminal ultimately draws pixels too, but the T U I normally controls cells, not
the terminal emulator's font rasterizer or compositor.

## 12. T U I Rendering Uses a Back Buffer and Diff

For each frame:

1. query the current terminal area;
2. lay out widgets in cell coordinates;
3. render all visible widgets into an in-memory cell buffer;
4. compare with the previous buffer;
5. emit control sequences only for changed runs;
6. flush, then wait for events/ticks.

Wide Unicode graphemes may occupy two cells; combining marks and terminal capability
differences complicate width. Test with the terminal families and locales you support.

## 13. T U I Library Selection

- Library: Rat a too ee. Role: immediate-rendered widgets, layout, terminal/frame abstraction. Use: build the T U I.
- Library: Cross term. Role: cross-platform terminal modes, cursor, style, input events. Use: common Rat a too ee backend and lower-level control.
- Library: Termion. Role: Unix-oriented terminal control/backend. Use: alternative backend in Unix-focused apps.
- Library: Termwiz. Role: terminal model/input/rendering utilities. Use: advanced terminal behavior or alternate backend.
- Library: tui-textarea and widget crates. Role: reusable higher-level widgets. Use: avoid rewriting complex editing behavior.

Rat a too ee 0.30's main crate is the recommended application entry point, and its run
helper initializes and restores the terminal around the application closure.

## 14. A Small Rat a too ee Application

For periodic animation or background work, poll with a deadline or use an event channel
instead of blocking forever on event read. Keep the channel bounded and coalesce
frequent progress updates.

## 15. Terminal Cleanup and Escape-Sequence Safety

If a T U I enables raw mode, hides the cursor, or enters the alternate screen, restore
the terminal on normal return, errors, and panics where practical. A stuck terminal is
usually recoverable, but it is an avoidable failure.

Untrusted text may contain control sequences that change colors, titles, hyperlinks,
clipboard-related controls, or apparent log content. A widget string A P I that escapes
or treats content as text is safer than printing raw bytes. Bound input length and
filter control characters according to the display contract.

## 16. UI Performance and Correctness Checklist

- Symptom: idle C P U/G P U usage. Inspect: repaint requests, polling control flow, animations.
- Symptom: typing lag. Inspect: synchronous work on UI thread, shaping/layout invalidation.
- Symptom: resize flicker. Inspect: surface reconfiguration and retained frame policy.
- Symptom: blurry text. Inspect: logical-to-physical scale and half-pixel transforms.
- Symptom: lost clicks. Inspect: hit-test transforms, clipping, pointer capture.
- Symptom: wrong key input. Inspect: physical/logical key and I M E composition handling.
- Symptom: T U I corruption. Inspect: terminal restore, width calculation, partial writes.
- Symptom: screen-reader gaps. Inspect: missing role/name/state/action semantics.
- Symptom: memory growth. Inspect: texture/glyph/widget caches and stale IDs.
- Symptom: G P U validation errors. Inspect: resource lifetime, format, alignment, barriers.

Measure event-to-present latency separately from frames per second. A form that redraws
only on input may feel excellent at zero idle FPS; a game may need continuous,
well-paced frames.

## 17. Designing a UI System for Your Own Language

A small UI language can lower through familiar compiler stages:

- Language feature: component/function. Runtime representation: constructor plus state slot.
- Language feature: property. Runtime representation: typed field or reactive value.
- Language feature: event handler. Runtime representation: closure/function reference plus environment.
- Language feature: conditional/list. Runtime representation: tree diff or immediate iteration with stable key.
- Language feature: async command. Runtime representation: future/task that sends a typed message.
- Language feature: style. Runtime representation: resolved rules/tokens converted to paint/layout values.
- Language feature: accessibility. Runtime representation: semantic nodes emitted beside visuals.
- Language feature: backend. Runtime representation: D O M, native toolkit, G P U display list, or terminal cells.

Define whether updates are synchronous, batched, or transactional; whether event
handlers may re-enter layout; how stable identity works; and which thread owns UI
objects. Make resource handles and backend capabilities explicit.

When reversing a UI runtime, look for the same stages in reverse: event dispatch tables,
widget/component trees, string/font caches, layout rectangles, display lists, G P U
buffers, and platform accessibility/I M E calls.

## 18. Native Window A P I Basics: Win thirty-two as a Concrete Example

“Window A P I” can mean a portable layer such as win it, or the operating system's
native windowing interface. Win thirty-two makes the lower-level pieces unusually explicit:

- Win thirty-two concept: HINSTANCE. Meaning: handle identifying the loaded application/module instance.
- Win thirty-two concept: window class. Meaning: registered metadata shared by windows, including a window procedure.
- Win thirty-two concept: H W N D. Meaning: opaque handle identifying one live window; it is not an owned Rust reference.
- Win thirty-two concept: window procedure. Meaning: A B I callback that receives messages for a window.
- Win thirty-two concept: message queue. Meaning: per-thread queued input/lifecycle work retrieved by the pump.
- Win thirty-two concept: sent message. Meaning: synchronous call into a target procedure; may cause re-entrancy.
- Win thirty-two concept: client area. Meaning: application-painted content inside the non-client frame.
- Win thirty-two concept: update region. Meaning: invalid portion that needs repainting.
- Win thirty-two concept: device context. Meaning: G D I drawing state/target used during painting.

The basic lifetime is:

### Window Classes and H W N D

RegisterClassW registers a name plus behavior such as the W N D proc. A window class is
not an object-oriented class and is not one visible window. CreateWindowExW creates
an instance and returns its H W N D.

Handles are opaque tokens owned under platform rules. After destruction, an H W N D
must not be used; the numeric value may eventually be reused for another object.

### The Window Procedure and Message Pump

GetMessageW has three outcomes: positive means a message was retrieved, zero means
W M QUIT, and negative means an error. Do not write while GetMessageW(...).as bool()
without preserving the error case.

The window procedure receives (H W N D, message ID, WPARAM, LPARAM). The meaning and
ownership of both parameter-sized values depend entirely on the message. Delegate
unhandled messages to DefWindowProcW; default behavior includes ordinary activation,
non-client interaction, and close processing.

### Paint, Resize, D P I, and Close

- Message: W M NCCREATE / W M CREATE. Typical responsibility: attach and initialize instance state.
- Message: W M SIZE. Typical responsibility: update logical size and resize/reconfigure render targets.
- Message: W M PAINT. Typical responsibility: validate the update region and paint the requested client area.
- Message: W M DPICHANGED. Typical responsibility: update scale and usually apply the suggested window rectangle.
- Message: W M CLOSE. Typical responsibility: confirm/save policy or allow default destruction.
- Message: W M DESTROY. Typical responsibility: release window resources and post quit for a single-window app.
- Message: W M NCDESTROY. Typical responsibility: final point to detach per-window state.

BeginPaint and EndPaint define the ordinary W M PAINT validation scope. Continuous
renderers may use a swap chain and explicit frame scheduling, but they still need to
respect invalidation, occlusion, resize, and device-loss behavior.

Set D P I awareness through the supported manifest/context policy before creating
windows. Use logical layout units, query the window's current D P I, and handle
W M DPICHANGED when crossing monitors rather than assuming 96 D P I forever.

### Minimal windows-rs Skeleton

This example exposes the raw boundary for learning. A production application will
usually prefer win it, windows-window, GTK, E G U I/eframe, iced, Slint, or
another wrapper that owns more lifecycle invariants.

The windows crate needs only the Win thirty-two feature groups used by the adapter. Prefer the
Unicode W APIs, keep UTF-16 conversion at the boundary, and use
windows core Error from win32() immediately when a sentinel-returning function
fails so a later call cannot overwrite the thread's last-error value.

### Attaching Rust State Safely

Raw Win thirty-two programs often pass a boxed state pointer through CreateWindowExW, receive
it during W M NCCREATE, store it with window-associated user data, and recover it in
later messages. That adapter must guarantee:

- the allocation outlives every message that can reference it;
- it is initialized once and detached during final destruction;
- callbacks never create simultaneous mutable Rust references;
- re-entrant sent messages cannot observe a half-mutated state;
- a panic never unwinds across the foreign callback A B I;
- worker threads communicate by messages/channels rather than directly mutating UI
state.

A safe wrapper can encode those invariants in one owner type. Keep the rest of the
application on the Message leads to update leads to render architecture shown earlier.

Win thirty-two references: Microsoft's first-window walkthrough,
W M PAINT,
high-D P I APIs,
and the current windows-rs Win thirty-two bindings.

Current references: win it event loop,
W G P U architecture,
E G U I immediate-mode G U I,
iced typed Elm-inspired G U I,
Slint's Rust A P I,
GTK4 Rust book, and
Rat a too ee application model.

# Web Execution Boundaries: Actix Web, Web Assembly, and the D O M

Note. Library lens: Actix Web and Waz-em bind-gen expose opposite host boundaries: server request/response execution and browser-owned objects reached through Web Assembly bindings.

Actix Web and Waz-em bind-gen sit on opposite sides of the web:

Both are boundary-design problems. Bytes and dynamic values enter Rust, become typed
data, pass through application logic, and are converted back into an external
representation.

## 1. Boundary Comparison

- Concern: External peer. Actix Web boundary: H T T P client. Waz-em bind-gen boundary: JavaScript host.
- Concern: Input conversion. Actix Web boundary: Extractors (Path, Query, Json). Waz-em bind-gen boundary: Generated JS/Wasm bindings.
- Concern: Error form. Actix Web boundary: H T T P status + response body. Waz-em bind-gen boundary: Result of T or JsValue / JS exception.
- Concern: Shared state. Actix Web boundary: web Data T. Waz-em bind-gen boundary: Exported Rust object or Wasm linear memory.
- Concern: Trust boundary. Actix Web boundary: Network request. Waz-em bind-gen boundary: Host calls and JS values.
- Concern: Main resource risk. Actix Web boundary: Connections, bodies, tasks, backend calls. Waz-em bind-gen boundary: Copies, memory growth, handles, JS G C.

## 2. Actix Web Application Shape

Current Actix Web 4 applications construct an App inside an HttpServer factory:

Note. Naming distinction: actix-web is the H T T P framework; actix is the actor
framework described in Actors: Owned State Behind a Typed Mailbox.
Actix Web applications do not require you to model handlers as Actix actors.

The server factory may run once per worker. Create intentionally shared state outside
the closure and clone its web Data handle into each app.

## 3. Extractors Turn Requests into Typed Inputs

- Extractor: web Path T. Source in the H T T P request: Variables captured by the route.
- Extractor: web Query T. Source in the H T T P request: U R L query string.
- Extractor: web Json T. Source in the H T T P request: J S O N request body.
- Extractor: web Data T. Source in the H T T P request: Application state.
- Extractor: HttpRequest. Source in the H T T P request: Headers, peer data, extensions, method, URI.
- Extractor: web Bytes. Source in the H T T P request: Buffered raw body.

Note. Extraction is parsing: a handler should receive values that already satisfy
transport-level syntax, then perform application validation such as source-size,
authorization, and allowed-language checks.

## 4. Bound Request Resources

- Resource: Request body. Boundary to define: Maximum compressed and decoded size.
- Resource: J S O N nesting. Boundary to define: Parser/format limits when configurable.
- Resource: Path/query values. Boundary to define: Length and allowed character policy.
- Resource: Connections. Boundary to define: Worker, backlog, keep-alive, and timeout policy.
- Resource: Backend calls. Boundary to define: Timeout and retry budget.
- Resource: Spawned tasks. Boundary to define: Concurrency limit and cancellation behavior.
- Resource: Response. Boundary to define: Maximum buffered or streamed output.

Do not hold a synchronous mutex guard across.await. Prefer an atomic for small
independent counters, a short critical section for local compound state, or an
async-aware/resource-specific client pool for input and output.

## 5. Typed H T T P Errors

- Error layer: Internal error. Suitable information: Source chain, operation, IDs, private diagnostics.
- Error layer: Log/trace event. Suitable information: Correlation ID, sanitized context, latency.
- Error layer: Client response. Suitable information: Stable code, safe message, appropriate H T T P status.

Avoid exposing filesystem paths, database errors, backtraces, secrets, or parser
internals to an untrusted client.

## 6. Middleware and Handler Responsibilities

- Concern: Correlation/request ID. Best location: Middleware.
- Concern: Access logging and timing. Best location: Middleware.
- Concern: Authentication context. Best location: Middleware/extractor.
- Concern: Route-specific authorization. Best location: Handler or domain service.
- Concern: Input extraction. Best location: Typed extractor.
- Concern: Business rules. Best location: Domain function independent of Actix.
- Concern: H T T P response mapping. Best location: Handler / ResponseError.

Body-consuming extractors need special care in middleware because the body is a stream.
A middleware that consumes it must deliberately restore or replace what downstream
handlers receive.

## 7. Test the Service Without Opening a Port

Test routing and H T T P conversion at the Actix layer, but keep most language parsing,
type checking, and compilation tests independent of the web framework.

## 8. What Waz-em bind-gen Generates

Web Assembly itself has a deliberately small boundary based on numeric values, linear
memory, functions, tables, imports, and exports. Waz-em bind-gen generates glue that maps
more ergonomic Rust and JavaScript values across that lower-level interface.

- Layer: Rust compiler. Responsibility: Produce the Wasm module.
- Layer: Waz-em bind-gen macro. Responsibility: Describe imported/exported boundary items.
- Layer: Binding generator. Responsibility: Produce JS glue and metadata.
- Layer: JavaScript loader. Responsibility: Instantiate the module and satisfy imports.
- Layer: Wasm linear memory. Responsibility: Store Rust stacks, heaps, strings, and buffers.

## 9. Export Rust Functions and Types

An exported Rust struct appears to JavaScript through a generated wrapper/handle. Its
fields remain private unless explicitly exported through methods or accessors.

## 10. Import JavaScript Functions

An imported JavaScript function is an environmental dependency just like an OS system
call or host function in a custom V M. Keep imports narrow so the module's capabilities
are easy to audit and replace in tests.

## 11. Value and Ownership Boundaries

- Rust-side value: Numbers/booleans. Boundary behavior to remember: Converted directly when supported.
- Rust-side value: String / shared reference to str. Boundary behavior to remember: Encoded/decoded through Wasm memory.
- Rust-side value: vector of u8 / and [u8]. Boundary behavior to remember: Typically copied to/from typed-array-compatible data.
- Rust-side value: JsValue. Boundary behavior to remember: Dynamic escape hatch; loses Rust type precision.
- Rust-side value: Exported Rust struct. Boundary behavior to remember: JavaScript wrapper holds a Rust-side allocation.
- Rust-side value: Imported JS object. Boundary behavior to remember: Rust holds a generated handle to a host object.
- Rust-side value: Option of T. Boundary behavior to remember: Often maps to a nullable/optional host value.

Note. Memory-view hazard: Wasm memory can grow. JavaScript typed-array views into old
memory may become stale after growth, so reacquire views according to the generated
A P I instead of retaining them indefinitely.

## 12. Error Design Across JavaScript

Use Result of T or JsValue when a Rust export can fail for a JavaScript caller:

- Boundary choice: JsValue error. Trade-off: Natural JS interop, weak Rust-side structure.
- Boundary choice: Numeric error code. Trade-off: Compact A B I, requires an external error table.
- Boundary choice: Serialized error. Trade-off: Stable schema, conversion overhead.
- Boundary choice: Exported error type. Trade-off: Rich A P I, more bindings and lifetime management.

Do not expose Rust panics as normal validation. Convert expected failure into Result
and install deliberate panic diagnostics only for unexpected bugs.

## 13. Using Wasm as a Language Target

For your own language, Web Assembly can be:

- Role: Compilation target. Architecture: Source leads to typed I R leads to Wasm.
- Role: Sandboxable plugin format. Architecture: Host supplies a small import capability set.
- Role: Browser runtime. Architecture: Language V M compiled to Wasm, UI supplied by JS.
- Role: Portable bytecode. Architecture: Server/C L I embeds a Wasm engine.

- Wasm language-target contract: Imports. Decision to define: Module and function names.
- Wasm language-target contract: Representation. Decision to define: Value and memory layouts.
- Wasm language-target contract: Allocation. Decision to define: Ownership across host/module calls.
- Wasm language-target contract: Strings/errors. Decision to define: Encoding and failure representation.
- Wasm language-target contract: Memory growth. Decision to define: Whether growth is permitted and view invalidation.
- Wasm language-target contract: Resource limits. Decision to define: Fuel, wall time, stack, and memory.
- Wasm language-target contract: Capabilities. Decision to define: Deterministic/nondeterministic host functions.

This is the same design work as an F F I A B I and a network protocol: representation,
ownership, versioning, and trust must all be explicit.

## 14. The D O M Is a Host-Owned Object Graph

As the
Waz-em bind-gen D O M examples
demonstrate, the browser's Document Object Model is not stored inside Wasm linear
memory. web-sys exposes typed Rust handles to JavaScript-managed objects.

- Type: Window. Role: browser global, timers, location, event target.
- Type: Document. Role: element creation and document-wide lookup.
- Type: Node. Role: common tree operations such as append child.
- Type: Element. Role: attributes, selectors, classes, element content.
- Type: HtmlElement. Role: H T M L-specific style, focus, and common properties.
- Type: EventTarget. Role: add/remove event listener boundary.
- Type: JsValue. Role: dynamic JavaScript value and error carrier.

web-sys APIs are feature-gated. Enable only the browser types and methods your crate
uses; a compile error about a missing method may mean a missing Cargo feature rather
than a missing browser A P I.

## 15. Create and Attach D O M Nodes

Prefer set text content when displaying untrusted text. set inner html parses H T M L,
so using it with untrusted source, diagnostics, names, or network data can create an
injection vulnerability.

- Lookup/creation A P I: web sys window(). Result shape: Option of Window. Design response: host environment may differ.
- Lookup/creation A P I: window.document(). Result shape: Option of Document. Design response: worker has no Document.
- Lookup/creation A P I: get element by id. Result shape: Option of Element. Design response: missing markup is expected.
- Lookup/creation A P I: query selector. Result shape: Result of Option of Element or. Design response: selector may be invalid/missing.
- Lookup/creation A P I: create element. Result shape: Result of Element or JsValue. Design response: browser operation may throw.
- Lookup/creation A P I: append child. Result shape: Result of Node or JsValue. Design response: tree mutation may fail.

## 16. Cast Only at a Checked Boundary

A C S S selector returns a general Element. Use JsCast dyn into when an operation
requires a more specific browser type.

- Cast style: dyn ref T (). Use: checked borrowed view; keep original handle.
- Cast style: dyn into T (). Use: checked consuming conversion.
- Cast style: unchecked ref T (). Use: glue boundary only when type is already proven.
- Cast style: unchecked into T (). Use: rare; caller assumes the complete type contract.

Unchecked casting is analogous to unsafe: keep it local and document what establishes
the runtime JavaScript type.

## 17. D O M Event Listeners Need Lifetime Design

Browser callbacks are JavaScript functions, while Rust closures live in Wasm-managed
state. Dropping a Closure while JavaScript still holds its callback is invalid.
Calling forget() keeps it alive forever, which is acceptable only for a truly
page-lifetime listener.

The application must retain the ClickListener for as long as the UI is active. This
R A I I shape pairs registration with removal and works well inside a Rust application
struct exported through Waz-em bind-gen.

- Callback strategy: local Closure, dropped. Lifetime/result: listener becomes invalid.
- Callback strategy: closure.forget(). Lifetime/result: page-lifetime leak by design.
- Callback strategy: store in app state. Lifetime/result: listener lives with component/application.
- Callback strategy: R A I I listener wrapper. Lifetime/result: automatically unregisters when owner is dropped.

## 18. UI State Crosses Two Memory Managers

- Ownership edge: Rust state captured by closure. What keeps it alive: Wasm closure environment.
- Ownership edge: Closure callback wrapper. What keeps it alive: Rust owner or deliberate forget().
- Ownership edge: D O M node. What keeps it alive: browser D O M tree and JS references.
- Ownership edge: Rust handle to D O M node. What keeps it alive: generated JS reference table/glue.
- Ownership edge: typed-array view of Wasm memory. What keeps it alive: JS value, but backing memory may grow.

R C Cell T is appropriate for small single-threaded browser state. Use
R C RefCell T for more complex mutable state, but keep borrows short and never call
unknown JavaScript while a mutable RefCell borrow is held: JavaScript can re-enter
Rust through another callback.

## 19. D O M Work Is Also Performance Work

- Expensive pattern: alternate layout reads and writes. Better direction: group reads, compute, then group writes.
- Expensive pattern: rebuild a large subtree for one text edit. Better direction: mutate the smallest stable node.
- Expensive pattern: copy huge buffers for every event. Better direction: batch, reuse, or expose bounded views carefully.
- Expensive pattern: compile on every keystroke immediately. Better direction: debounce/cancel stale work.
- Expensive pattern: block the main thread with language work. Better direction: use a Web Worker or incremental execution.

For a browser-hosted language:

Keep the compiler core independent of web-sys. A browser adapter should translate D O M
events into plain Rust inputs and translate structured compiler results back into UI
updates. That preserves C L I, test, server, and browser front ends over one language
implementation.

Note. Next: S D L exposes native windows, input, timing, audio, and drawing without the
browser's document and JavaScript host layers.

# Event Loops, Input, Audio, and Rendering Through SDL3

Note. Library lens: SDL3 exposes the portable platform layer beneath games and media: window ownership, event polling, clocks, audio buffers, input state, and presentation.

The SDL3 A P I by category is best read as a
map of platform services. S D L does not impose a game architecture; it gives a portable
boundary over windows, input, graphics, audio, timing, files, threads, and devices.

## 1. Read the A P I by Responsibility

- A P I family: initialization. What it gives the program: subsystem startup/shutdown. Boundary to design carefully: partial failure and cleanup.
- A P I family: video. What it gives the program: windows, displays, surfaces. Boundary to design carefully: OS-owned handles.
- A P I family: events. What it gives the program: keyboard, mouse, touch, window/device events. Boundary to design carefully: queue draining and latency.
- A P I family: 2D render. What it gives the program: draw state, textures, primitives, present. Boundary to design carefully: frame lifetime and backend state.
- A P I family: G P U. What it gives the program: explicit modern graphics work. Boundary to design carefully: synchronization and resource lifetime.
- A P I family: audio. What it gives the program: devices, streams, formats. Boundary to design carefully: real-time callback constraints.
- A P I family: gamepad/joystick. What it gives the program: controllers, axes, buttons, hotplug. Boundary to design carefully: unstable device identities.
- A P I family: time. What it gives the program: ticks, delay, high-resolution timing. Boundary to design carefully: units, overflow, frame pacing.
- A P I family: threads/synchronizing. What it gives the program: worker threads, mutexes, conditions. Boundary to design carefully: ownership and shutdown.
- A P I family: input and output/storage. What it gives the program: streams, paths, filesystem helpers. Boundary to design carefully: untrusted bytes and platform paths.
- A P I family: properties/logging. What it gives the program: extensible metadata and diagnostics. Boundary to design carefully: type agreement and observability.

Note. SDL3 migration note: do not paste SDL2 error checks blindly. Many SDL3
operations—including S D L Init and S D L CreateWindowAndRenderer—return bool, with
true meaning success.

## 2. Resource Lifetime Is the First Invariant

Every successful acquisition must have one cleanup path. Destroy resources in an order
compatible with their dependencies.

## 3. Minimal SDL3 Window and Render Loop

Check the return value of fallible drawing operations in production code. A concise
tutorial may omit checks to keep the lifecycle visible; an engine should surface errors
with operation, resource, and frame context.

## 4. Separate Platform Events from Simulation State

- Layer: raw event. Example data: key code, device ID, window event.
- Layer: normalized command. Example data: MoveLeft(true), Resize { width, height }.
- Layer: simulation state. Example data: position, velocity, editor selection.
- Layer: presentation snapshot. Example data: visible sprites, boxes, glyphs.

Do not let gameplay or language semantics depend directly on platform key codes. An
input-mapping layer makes tests deterministic and lets keyboard, gamepad, touch, and
replay files produce the same commands.

## 5. Variable Rendering, Fixed Simulation

- Timing mistake: simulation speed tied to frame count. Result: behavior changes across machines.
- Timing mistake: unbounded catch-up after a pause. Result: spiral of death.
- Timing mistake: mixed milliseconds/nanoseconds. Result: enormous or tiny time steps.
- Timing mistake: nondeterministic input inside update. Result: replays/tests diverge.

S D L provides timing primitives such as S D L GetTicksNS; convert once into a
well-named duration type. Clamp unusually large elapsed intervals after breakpoints,
suspend/resume, or window dragging.

## 6. Rendering APIs Form a State Machine

The renderer remembers draw color, blend mode, target, viewport, clip rectangle, and
texture state. Make state transitions explicit rather than relying on whatever the
previous draw left behind.

- Representation: surface. Best use: C P U-side pixels, loading/manipulation.
- Representation: texture. Best use: renderer-owned image used for drawing.
- Representation: 2D renderer. Best use: portable sprites, rectangles, simple UI.
- Representation: G P U A P I. Best use: explicit pipelines, buffers, shaders.

## 7. Audio and Device Events Need Special Discipline

Audio work runs under tight real-time constraints. Avoid blocking input and output, long locks,
allocation spikes, and logging in the time-critical path. Feed an audio stream from
bounded buffers and define underrun behavior.

Controllers can appear and disappear while the program runs:

Use a logical player/device handle rather than treating a temporary enumeration index
as permanent identity.

## 8. Wrap S D L Handles at a Safe Language Boundary

The full wrapper must also define:

- Contract: nullability. Question: Can creation or lookup return null?
- Contract: thread affinity. Question: Which thread may call this operation?
- Contract: aliasing. Question: Can two safe wrappers believe they uniquely own one handle?
- Contract: callbacks. Question: How long do captured values live, and can calls re-enter?
- Contract: destruction order. Question: Which child resources must be dropped first?
- Contract: strings/buffers. Question: Who owns bytes, and for how long are pointers valid?

Keep raw pointers private. A custom language runtime should expose stable integer
handles or capability objects, validate every lookup, and never let untrusted scripts
forge native addresses.

## 9. S D L as a Backend for a Small Language

The host adapter should enforce limits on windows, textures, audio buffers, file paths,
and per-frame commands. This turns S D L from an unrestricted F F I surface into a
capability-based standard library.

Note. Next: Bevy builds a data-oriented scheduler and application framework over the
same event/update/render lifecycle.

# Application Architecture Through Bevy, Diesel, and Tauri

Note. Library lens: Bevy, Diesel, and Tauri serve as case studies for ECS/data-oriented design, relational algebra and transactions, and capability-checked native/web process boundaries.

These frameworks solve different problems, but they all reward the same habits:
explicit data models, narrow boundaries, scheduled work, bounded resources, and
testable domain logic.

- Framework: Bevy. Primary problem: data-driven games/visual applications. Architectural lesson: behavior as scheduled systems.
- Framework: Diesel. Primary problem: typed relational database access. Architectural lesson: schema/query contract in Rust types.
- Framework: Tauri 2. Primary problem: cross-platform webview applications. Architectural lesson: least-privilege frontend/backend I P C.

## 1. Bevy: Data-Oriented Application Structure

The Bevy quick start introduces a
modular, data-focused Rust engine based on the Entity Component System (ECS) paradigm.

- ECS concept: entity. Compiler/runtime analogy: stable arena ID or object handle.
- ECS concept: component. Compiler/runtime analogy: fact attached to an ID.
- ECS concept: query. Compiler/runtime analogy: select IDs possessing a required fact combination.
- ECS concept: system. Compiler/runtime analogy: compiler pass over selected data.
- ECS concept: resource. Compiler/runtime analogy: target configuration, interner, diagnostic sink.
- ECS concept: schedule. Compiler/runtime analogy: pass pipeline and dependency ordering.
- ECS concept: plugin. Compiler/runtime analogy: optional language feature/backend package.

### A Small Scheduled Model

Bevy can parallelize systems when their parameter access is compatible. A system that
reads Res T can overlap with other readers; a ResMut T or mutable component query
declares exclusive access to that data.

### ECS Design Questions

- Question: component or resource?. Better direction: per-entity fact vs one world-wide value.
- Question: component or enum field?. Better direction: queried independently vs always part of one state.
- Question: event/message or component?. Better direction: transient notification vs persistent state.
- Question: one huge system?. Better direction: separate transformations with explicit ordering.
- Question: every entity has everything?. Better direction: compose only the data each behavior requires.

Do not use ECS merely to avoid ordinary structs. It is strongest when large collections
need different combinations of data-oriented behavior.

## 2. Diesel: Typed S Q L Boundary

The Diesel guides separate setup, selects, updates, inserts,
relations, schema generation, application composition, and extension. Diesel turns many
schema/query mismatches into compile-time type errors, but runtime data, connectivity,
transactions, constraints, and authorization still require deliberate handling.

### Separate Row, Insert, and Domain Types

- Type: generated schema. Owns which contract: S Q L table/column types.
- Type: JobRow. Owns which contract: selected database row shape.
- Type: NewJob 'a. Owns which contract: allowed insert fields, borrowing inputs.
- Type: domain CompileJob. Owns which contract: validated application invariants.
- Type: A P I response. Owns which contract: intentionally exposed external fields.

Do not use one struct for every layer merely because the fields currently match.

### Transactions Protect Multi-Step Invariants

The sketch still needs an affected-row check or database constraint to distinguish
“claimed” from “already unavailable.” Transactions provide atomicity; they do not invent
the business invariant.

### Diesel Boundary Checklist

- Area: migrations. Decision: versioned, reviewed, reversible strategy.
- Area: connection. Decision: pool size, timeout, health, transaction lifetime.
- Area: async application. Decision: avoid blocking executor threads; use appropriate adapter/pool.
- Area: query cardinality. Decision: first, optional, one, many, or paginated.
- Area: updates. Decision: distinguish absent field from explicit S Q L NULL.
- Area: errors. Decision: map not-found, constraint, transient, and internal failures.
- Area: observability. Decision: record sanitized query context and latency.

## 3. Tauri 2: A Desktop Capability Boundary

Tauri 2 combines a web frontend with native/mobile
application code. JavaScript calls registered Rust commands across I P C; this makes every
command a trust and serialization boundary.

### Register a Narrow Command Surface

The outer expect handles an unrecoverable application-startup failure. Input-dependent
command failures remain structured Result values.

### Capabilities and Permissions

- Boundary: command registration. Least-privilege question: Does this command need to be callable at all?
- Boundary: capability configuration. Least-privilege question: Which windows/webviews receive which permissions?
- Boundary: filesystem. Least-privilege question: Which paths and operations are allowed?
- Boundary: shell/process. Least-privilege question: Can fixed programs replace arbitrary commands?
- Boundary: network. Least-privilege question: Which destinations and protocols are necessary?
- Boundary: plugin. Least-privilege question: Which plugin commands are exposed?
- Boundary: updater/deep links. Least-privilege question: How are authenticity and untrusted arguments checked?

Never turn a frontend string into an unrestricted shell command, path, S Q L fragment, or
U R L fetch. Parse it into a typed request and authorize the resulting operation.

### Events, Channels, and Commands

- Mechanism: command. Best fit: bounded request/response.
- Mechanism: event. Best fit: broadcast-style notification.
- Mechanism: channel. Best fit: ordered streaming progress/data.
- Mechanism: managed state. Best fit: shared native service/configuration handle.

For long compilation, download, or indexing jobs, return a request ID promptly and send
bounded progress events. Define cancellation and terminal states explicitly.

## 4. Zero to Production: Service Engineering

The Zero to Production repository
is the evolving reference application for an opinionated Rust backend book. Its
newsletter service connects Actix Web, Postgres Q L/SQLx, Redis-backed sessions,
configuration, authentication, email delivery, telemetry, migrations, and A P I tests.

Note. Production mental model: an H T T P handler is only the visible edge. A service is
the complete system that can be configured, observed, migrated, tested, deployed,
restarted, and operated without guessing.

### Grow the System in Dependency Order

- Layer: health endpoint. Question to settle: Can orchestration tell whether the process serves?
- Layer: configuration. Question to settle: How do local, test, and production values differ?
- Layer: database migration. Question to settle: How does persistent state evolve with the program?
- Layer: request extraction. Question to settle: Which bytes become trusted domain inputs?
- Layer: domain/use-case logic. Question to settle: Which invariant must hold independent of H T T P?
- Layer: outbound dependency. Question to settle: How are email/cache calls bounded and retried?
- Layer: authentication/session. Question to settle: Who is acting, and how is that identity established?
- Layer: telemetry. Question to settle: Can an operator reconstruct a failed request?
- Layer: deployment. Question to settle: Can the artifact start reproducibly in a fresh setting?

Build a thin vertical slice first:

Each added dependency should arrive behind a narrow interface and with a test strategy.

### Bind the Listener Outside the Application Factory

A testable server accepts a listener chosen by its caller. Tests can bind port 0 and
ask the OS for an unused port instead of racing over a hard-coded one.

- Design choice: caller supplies TcpListener. Testing/operations benefit: ephemeral ports and pre-bound sockets.
- Design choice: construction returns Server. Testing/operations benefit: test can spawn and control lifetime.
- Design choice: startup is separate from routes. Testing/operations benefit: configuration errors fail before serving.
- Design choice: health check avoids dependencies. Testing/operations benefit: distinguishes process health from deep readiness.

Define liveness and readiness deliberately. A liveness probe should not restart
a healthy process just because a downstream database briefly fails; readiness can stop
new traffic when the service cannot fulfill requests.
