The four stages of building a language from scratch:

Note. Progression principle: grow the language in vertical slices so every stage
remains runnable, testable, and explainable.

- Feature: Grammar size. Calculator: ~18 lines. Firstlang: ~70 lines. Secondlang: ~77 lines. Thirdlang: ~140 lines.
- Feature: Type System. Calculator: None. Firstlang: Dynamic. Secondlang: Static. Thirdlang: Static + Classes.
- Feature: Variables. Calculator: No. Firstlang: Yes. Secondlang: Yes. Thirdlang: Yes.
- Feature: Functions. Calculator: No. Firstlang: Yes. Secondlang: Yes. Thirdlang: Yes + Methods.
- Feature: Classes. Calculator: No. Firstlang: No. Secondlang: No. Thirdlang: Yes.
- Feature: Memory. Calculator: Stack. Firstlang: Stack. Secondlang: Stack. Thirdlang: Stack + Heap.
- Feature: Execution. Calculator: Interpreter / V M / J I T. Firstlang: Interpreter. Secondlang: L L V M J I T. Thirdlang: L L V M J I T.

Each stage adds one layer of abstraction:

1. Calculator — Learn the basics: parsing, A S T, evaluation.
2. Firstlang — Add programming: variables, functions, control flow.
3. Secondlang — Add types: static checking, L L V M compilation.
4. Thirdlang — Add O O P: classes, objects, heap memory management.

Grammar growth, side by side — each stage layers new pest rules onto the last
without disturbing the expression rules underneath:

Note. The expression rules (Expr, Comparison, Additive, etc.) barely change across all
four stages — growth comes from statements and declarations, not from how
arithmetic is parsed.

## What to Explore Next

- Direction: Language Features. Topics: Inheritance (vtables, dynamic dispatch), interfaces/traits, generics (List T ), closures, pattern matching, algebraic data types.
- Direction: Type System. Topics: Nullability (Point?), reference vs owned types, Hindley-Milner full inference.
- Direction: Memory Management. Topics: Garbage collection (mark-and-sweep, generational), reference counting, Rust-style ownership.
- Direction: Execution Models. Topics: A O T compilation to executables, bytecode VMs, transpilation to JS/C/WASM.
- Direction: Optimizations. Topics: Inlining, escape analysis (stack-allocate short-lived objects), devirtualization.
- Direction: Tooling. Topics: Debuggers, formatters, Language Server Protocol (L S P) for I D E support.

Sketching a few of these directly on Thirdlang:

Note. Generics require either monomorphization (generate specialized code per concrete
type — Rust's approach) or type erasure (use runtime type info instead — Java's
approach).

A minimal debugger works the same way as our interpreter, just paused: it steps
through the A S T/bytecode one node at a time and lets you inspect the environment at each
break:

Real-world Rust language projects worth reading, roughly in order of complexity:
start with smaller, approachable codebases like Koto or
Rhai, then graduate to more advanced implementations like
Gleam or Boa (a JS engine). They use
the same techniques covered here — grammars, ASTs, type systems — at production scale.

The concepts you've learned — grammars, ASTs, type systems, code generation — appear
everywhere: S Q L, Graph Q L, YAML, regex, C S S, template engines. You now have the
foundation to understand, modify, or create any of them.

All paths lead to the same truth: computing is structured transformation — from text,
to trees, to bytes, to machine code, to electrons.

# Cross-Linked Computer Science and Low-Level Glossary

Use this glossary to decode a term quickly, then follow Learn more for the full
mental model, examples, invariants, and failure modes.

## A–D

- Term: 3D fundamentals. Low-level meaning: Coordinates, vectors, transforms, clipping, rasterization, depth, and shading turn geometry into pixels.. Learn more: 3D transform pipeline.
- Term: accessibility tree. Low-level meaning: A semantic parallel to painted UI exposes roles, names, values, focus, and actions to assistive technology.. Learn more: G U I accessibility.
- Term: allocator. Low-level meaning: A policy over finite storage chooses aligned free ranges while preserving non-overlap, ownership, and reuse invariants.. Learn more: Kernel allocator models.
- Term: A P I. Low-level meaning: An application programming interface is a source/behavior contract; it may sit above a different binary A B I or wire protocol.. Learn more: A B I versus A P I.
- Term: asymmetric cryptography. Low-level meaning: A public/private key relationship supports key establishment or signatures without sharing the private key.. Learn more: Symmetric and asymmetric cryptography.
- Term: async / asynchronous. Low-level meaning: Work is represented so the caller can make progress before that work completes.. Learn more: Async, futures, and promises.
- Term: audio. Low-level meaning: Timed frames of per-channel samples flow through buffers, devices, resamplers, mixers, and codecs.. Learn more: Digital audio.
- Term: authentication. Low-level meaning: Evidence is checked for a claimed identity before a session is established.. Learn more: Authentication and authorization.
- Term: authorization. Low-level meaning: Policy decides whether an authenticated identity may perform an action on a particular resource.. Learn more: Authentication and authorization.
- Term: backend. Low-level meaning: Server-side policy, persistence, computation, and integrations behind a client-facing boundary.. Learn more: Platform and application layers.
- Term: bitmap. Low-level meaning: One bit per member compactly represents allocation or membership in a known finite set.. Learn more: Filesystem allocation maps.
- Term: bootloader. Low-level meaning: Early privileged code initializes enough hardware to choose, verify, load, and enter a kernel.. Learn more: Firmware-to-application chain.
- Term: cache. Low-level meaning: A nearer copy trades space and possible staleness for lower access time and origin work.. Learn more: Cache layers.
- Term: call logger. Low-level meaning: Instrumentation records approved metadata around calls so behavior and timing can be reconstructed.. Learn more: Safe call logger.
- Term: canvas fingerprinting. Low-level meaning: Rendered pixel differences contribute a probabilistic browser/device correlation signal.. Learn more: Fingerprinting and canvas.
- Term: C D N. Low-level meaning: A distributed reverse-proxy edge serves cached content nearer users and forwards misses to an origin.. Learn more: CDNs.
- Term: certificate / digital certificate. Low-level meaning: A signed structure binds a public key to names or attributes under an issuer and policy.. Learn more: Certificates.
- Term: client–server. Low-level meaning: The client initiates a service interaction; the server accepts and answers under a protocol contract.. Learn more: Network roles.
- Term: command-line environment. Low-level meaning: A terminal, shell, environment, streams, paths, and process model compose text-oriented tools.. Learn more: Terminal, shell, and program.
- Term: compositor. Low-level meaning: A system combines application surfaces/layers into the final display image under clipping, transform, and z-order rules.. Learn more: G U I layers.
- Term: compression. Low-level meaning: A transform reduces redundant representation; it may be lossless or deliberately lossy.. Learn more: Encoding, compression, and encryption.
- Term: consensus algorithm. Low-level meaning: Replicas agree on a value or ordered log despite delays and a defined set of failures.. Learn more: Consensus.
- Term: consistency: eventual. Low-level meaning: Replicas are allowed to differ but should converge after updates stop and communication succeeds.. Learn more: Consistency models.
- Term: consistency: strong. Low-level meaning: An operation observes a tightly ordered, current view such as linearizable behavior.. Learn more: Consistency models.
- Term: consistency: weak. Low-level meaning: The system gives few freshness or ordering promises for the operation.. Learn more: Consistency models.
- Term: container / containerization. Low-level meaning: A packaged process receives restricted resource views while normally sharing the host kernel.. Learn more: Containers.
- Term: cookie. Low-level meaning: Browser-managed state is stored and attached to matching requests under security/scope attributes.. Learn more: Sessions, cookies, and tokens.
- Term: C O R S. Low-level meaning: H T T P response headers let a server opt selected web origins into browser-readable cross-origin responses.. Learn more: C O R S, C S R F, and X S S.
- Term: cryptographic signing. Low-level meaning: A private key signs exact context-bound bytes; a public key verifies integrity and signer-key possession.. Learn more: Signing.
- Term: crash consistency. Low-level meaning: Persistent write ordering constrains which metadata and data states may remain after power loss or a crash.. Learn more: Filesystem recovery.
- Term: C S R F. Low-level meaning: A browser may attach ambient credentials to an unwanted cross-site request.. Learn more: C O R S, C S R F, and X S S.
- Term: database. Low-level meaning: A storage engine provides a data model, access paths, concurrency rules, and durability/failure semantics.. Learn more: Database families.
- Term: decoding. Low-level meaning: Bytes in an encoding are parsed back into another representation under explicit validity rules.. Learn more: Encoding distinctions.
- Term: deserialization. Low-level meaning: A byte/text representation is parsed into typed values and must then pass semantic validation.. Learn more: Serialization boundary.
- Term: D L L injection. Low-level meaning: A process is induced to map or execute code it did not select; defenders correlate loader, memory, thread, and handle evidence.. Learn more: D L L loading and injection signals.
- Term: D M A / D M A card. Low-level meaning: A device reads or writes mapped memory without C P U copying; an I O M M U can translate and restrict those accesses.. Learn more: D M A and I O M M U.
- Term: D N S. Low-level meaning: A delegated, cached database maps names to typed resource records through resolvers and authoritative servers.. Learn more: D N S.
- Term: Docker. Low-level meaning: A container platform that builds images and runs isolated processes sharing the host kernel by default.. Learn more: Containers.
- Term: document database. Low-level meaning: Records naturally store nested aggregate-shaped documents and may duplicate related data for access locality.. Learn more: Database families.
- Term: driver. Low-level meaning: Concurrent privileged code translates an OS resource contract into device registers, queues, interrupts, and D M A.. Learn more: Driver model.

## E–M

- Term: emulator. Low-level meaning: Software reproduces another I S A, machine, or device closely enough to run target software.. Learn more: Execution environments.
- Term: encoding. Low-level meaning: A syntax maps information into representable bytes or characters without providing secrecy.. Learn more: Encoding distinctions.
- Term: encryption. Low-level meaning: A keyed transformation provides confidentiality; authenticated encryption also detects modification.. Learn more: Cryptography families.
- Term: entity structure. Low-level meaning: Identity and component storage form data relationships inferred from strides, offsets, pointers, and controlled changes.. Learn more: Recovering entity structures.
- Term: event loop. Low-level meaning: A platform/runtime dispatches input, lifecycle, timers, tasks, and redraw work while owning thread-sensitive UI state.. Learn more: G U I event loop.
- Term: event-driven development. Low-level meaning: Commands/events are dispatched to handlers, decoupling producers while introducing ordering and backpressure concerns.. Learn more: Event-driven and stream processing.
- Term: fault tolerance. Low-level meaning: The design continues safely or recovers under an explicit crash, delay, loss, corruption, or partition model.. Learn more: Fault tolerance.
- Term: file storage. Low-level meaning: Filesystems or object stores retain named byte sequences plus metadata under durability and atomicity rules.. Learn more: Database and storage families.
- Term: filesystem. Low-level meaning: Metadata layers turn numbered storage blocks into allocated objects, directories, paths, byte ranges, and recovery rules.. Learn more: Filesystem model.
- Term: fingerprinting. Low-level meaning: Multiple observable attributes are combined into a noisy correlation signal, not cryptographic identity.. Learn more: Fingerprinting and canvas.
- Term: firmware. Low-level meaning: Software stored with a device/platform initializes or controls hardware below ordinary application boundaries.. Learn more: Firmware-to-application chain.
- Term: floating point. Low-level meaning: Significand/exponent values approximate real numbers with finite precision and special non-finite states.. Learn more: Floating-point contract.
- Term: forward proxy. Low-level meaning: A relay acts on behalf of clients when reaching destinations.. Learn more: Network roles.
- Term: frontend. Low-level meaning: User-facing presentation and interaction code consuming host/backend capabilities.. Learn more: Platform and application layers.
- Term: F T P / F T P S / S F T P. Low-level meaning: Distinct file-transfer protocols: legacy F T P, T L S-protected F T P, and S S H's file subsystem.. Learn more: File-transfer protocols.
- Term: function pointer. Low-level meaning: A value selects callable code under a specific A B I and signature.. Learn more: Function pointers.
- Term: future. Low-level meaning: A pollable value represents computation that may produce a result later.. Learn more: Async, futures, and promises.
- Term: graphics A P I. Low-level meaning: A command/resource interface submits buffers, textures, shaders, and synchronization to a G P U or renderer.. Learn more: G U I and graphics libraries.
- Term: G P U. Low-level meaning: An asynchronous throughput processor consumes queued shader/compute/transfer work over explicit memory contracts.. Learn more: G P U pipeline.
- Term: Graph Q L. Low-level meaning: A typed A P I query language/runtime that resolves requested fields over arbitrary backing systems; it is not a database.. Learn more: Graph Q L.
- Term: G U I. Low-level meaning: A graphical user interface maps input events and application state through layout/paint into window-system surfaces.. Learn more: G U I architecture.
- Term: headers, H T T P. Low-level meaning: Metadata fields select representations, credentials, caching, routing context, and body interpretation.. Learn more: H T T P messages and headers.
- Term: heartbeat. Low-level meaning: Periodic liveness evidence supports suspicion and leases but cannot distinguish every cause of silence.. Learn more: Heartbeats.
- Term: heuristic scanning. Low-level meaning: Weighted structural or behavioral signals generalize beyond exact signatures but require false-positive management.. Learn more: Scanner comparison.
- Term: H T M L canvas. Low-level meaning: A scriptable pixel surface used for graphics whose outputs can also become a fingerprinting signal.. Learn more: Fingerprinting and canvas.
- Term: H T T P. Low-level meaning: An application protocol defines request methods, status responses, fields, representations, and caching semantics.. Learn more: H T T P messages.
- Term: H T T P caching. Low-level meaning: Freshness directives and validators coordinate reuse of stored responses across browsers and intermediaries.. Learn more: H T T P caching.
- Term: H W N D. Low-level meaning: An opaque Win thirty-two handle identifies one live window under OS lifetime rules; it is not a Rust reference or ownership proof.. Learn more: Native Window A P I basics.
- Term: hardware I D. Low-level meaning: A hardware-derived identifier is mutable, privacy-sensitive, and unsuitable as the sole proof of identity.. Learn more: Platform identity.
- Term: Hyper V. Low-level meaning: Microsoft's hypervisor architecture organizes a root partition and child guest partitions with synthetic device channels.. Learn more: Hypervisors and Hyper V.
- Term: hypervisor. Low-level meaning: Privileged software virtualizes C P U, memory, interrupts, and devices for guest machines.. Learn more: Hypervisors and Hyper V.
- Term: immediate-mode UI. Low-level meaning: The application describes the desired controls each frame/pass while the toolkit retains interaction state by identity.. Learn more: UI models.
- Term: inode. Low-level meaning: A stable filesystem object record stores metadata and block references separately from the directory names that reach it.. Learn more: Inodes and names.
- Term: J tag / S W D. Low-level meaning: Hardware debug transports can expose scan, halt, registers, memory, trace, and programming capabilities.. Learn more: J tag and S W D.
- Term: J W T. Low-level meaning: A standardized claims container that may be signed/encrypted; it is a format, not an authentication design.. Learn more: Sessions, cookies, and tokens.
- Term: kernel. Low-level meaning: The privileged resource manager controls processes, virtual memory, scheduling, devices, files, and isolation.. Learn more: Operating systems.
- Term: layout engine. Low-level meaning: Constraints, intrinsic sizes, policy, and scale become rectangles, clips, and transforms for a UI tree.. Learn more: G U I layout.
- Term: lifetime. Low-level meaning: A type-level relationship bounds how long borrowed access may remain valid; an annotation does not extend storage.. Learn more: Lifetimes as relationships.
- Term: load balancing. Low-level meaning: A policy selects a healthy destination while accounting for capacity, affinity, draining, and retries.. Learn more: Load balancing.
- Term: memory scanner. Low-level meaning: A bounded query compares typed values across authorized immutable memory snapshots.. Learn more: Memory scanners.
- Term: middleware. Low-level meaning: Reusable translation/policy behavior sits between application logic and a platform, protocol, or data service.. Learn more: Platform and application layers.

## N–Z

- Term: no S Q L. Low-level meaning: An umbrella for non-relational models such as document, key-value, wide-column, and graph—not one consistency policy.. Learn more: Database families.
- Term: NULL / nullability. Low-level meaning: Absence/unknown is a distinct state whose logic and wire/update meaning must be modeled explicitly.. Learn more: Null, missing, and empty.
- Term: obfuscation. Low-level meaning: Transformations raise analysis cost but do not provide cryptographic trust or authorization.. Learn more: Obfuscation analysis.
- Term: ownership. Low-level meaning: A static resource protocol assigns responsibility for a value and its eventual release, transfer, or controlled sharing.. Learn more: Ownership model.
- Term: password salting. Low-level meaning: A unique non-secret salt ensures equal passwords do not produce equal stored verifiers.. Learn more: Password storage.
- Term: password storage. Low-level meaning: A slow memory-hard password-hashing verifier replaces plaintext, reversible encryption, or fast hashes.. Learn more: Password storage.
- Term: pattern scanner. Low-level meaning: A bounded matcher locates exact/wildcard byte sequences in an owned file or snapshot.. Learn more: Pattern scanning.
- Term: permissions. Low-level meaning: ACLs, ownership, capabilities, page protections, and handles restrict specific operations on resources.. Learn more: Sandbox authority.
- Term: plist. Low-level meaning: Apple's property-list model serializes structured configuration in X M L or binary form.. Learn more: Platform storage.
- Term: port. Low-level meaning: A 16-bit transport endpoint selector used with addresses and protocol state.. Learn more: Ports.
- Term: port observation / scanning. Low-level meaning: Bounded connection attempts classify endpoint evidence as connected, refused, timed out, or another OS error.. Learn more: Bounded T C P port observation.
- Term: promise. Low-level meaning: In some async models, the completion/writable counterpart to a future.. Learn more: Async, futures, and promises.
- Term: protocol. Low-level meaning: A shared syntax, state machine, timing model, and error contract between participants.. Learn more: Layered networking.
- Term: proxying. Low-level meaning: A relay terminates one participant role and initiates another, changing trust and observation points.. Learn more: Network roles.
- Term: R A I I. Low-level meaning: Resource acquisition is tied to an owning value whose destructor performs structural cleanup at scope exit.. Learn more: Ownership beyond memory.
- Term: random number. Low-level meaning: A value comes from a generator selected for either reproducibility/performance or cryptographic unpredictability.. Learn more: Random-number domains.
- Term: Redis. Low-level meaning: A networked typed data-structure server often used for caching, coordination, queues, or fast derived state.. Learn more: Cache layers and Redis.
- Term: Registry. Low-level meaning: Windows' typed hierarchical configuration database has hives, views, permissions, and platform-specific semantics.. Learn more: Platform storage.
- Term: relational database. Low-level meaning: Tables, keys, constraints, indexes, joins, and transactions model and protect relationships.. Learn more: Relational design.
- Term: reliability. Low-level meaning: The probability a system satisfies its behavior contract over time, distinct from availability and durability.. Learn more: Fault tolerance and reliability.
- Term: retained-mode UI. Low-level meaning: A toolkit keeps a persistent widget/object tree and invalidates or redraws it when properties change.. Learn more: UI models.
- Term: reverse proxy. Low-level meaning: A relay appears to clients as the service and forwards selected requests to origins.. Learn more: Network roles.
- Term: sandbox. Low-level meaning: Several mechanisms reduce ambient authority and isolate a component's address, syscall, resource, or runtime view.. Learn more: Sandboxing.
- Term: serialization. Low-level meaning: Typed state is encoded into a versioned byte/text representation with framing and compatibility rules.. Learn more: Serialization boundary.
- Term: session management. Low-level meaning: A service creates, rotates, expires, and revokes continuity of authenticated state.. Learn more: Sessions, cookies, and tokens.
- Term: Shadowsocks. Low-level meaning: A secure split proxy protects traffic between local and remote relay components; it is not automatically a full V P N.. Learn more: VPNs and Shadowsocks.
- Term: signature-based scanning. Low-level meaning: Exact hashes or stable byte/structural signatures recognize known artifacts efficiently.. Learn more: Scanner comparison.
- Term: simulator. Low-level meaning: An abstract behavioral model reproduces selected system properties without necessarily running the exact target machine.. Learn more: Execution environments.
- Term: S S H. Low-level meaning: A secure protocol provides remote login, authenticated host/user channels, forwarding, and subsystems such as S F T P.. Learn more: T L S and S S H.
- Term: stream processing. Low-level meaning: Operators transform an unbounded event sequence using ordering, time, windows, state, and recovery policy.. Learn more: Event-driven and stream processing.
- Term: superblock. Low-level meaning: A filesystem's root format record declares its version, geometry, counts, and the locations needed to decode other regions.. Learn more: On-disk layout.
- Term: symmetric cryptography. Low-level meaning: Participants share a secret for fast encryption or message authentication.. Learn more: Cryptography families.
- Term: sync / synchronous. Low-level meaning: Control flow waits for an operation to complete before proceeding.. Learn more: Async, futures, and promises.
- Term: test-driven development (T D D). Low-level meaning: A failing behavioral example precedes the smallest implementation change, followed by refactoring.. Learn more: T D D and reliability tests.
- Term: terminal emulator. Low-level meaning: A G U I program interprets terminal control sequences and renders a character-cell model into pixels.. Learn more: How a T U I reaches the screen.
- Term: threat model. Low-level meaning: A structured statement identifies controlled inputs, trust boundaries, assets, attacker capabilities, and acceptable failures.. Learn more: Threat modeling.
- Term: T L S. Low-level meaning: A handshake and record protocol authenticates endpoints and protects application data in transit.. Learn more: T L S and S S H.
- Term: token. Low-level meaning: A credential or typed symbol represents authority or syntax; security tokens must be scoped and protected as secrets.. Learn more: Sessions, cookies, and tokens.
- Term: T U I. Low-level meaning: A terminal user interface lays out widgets in cells and emits only the changed terminal state through a backend.. Learn more: T U I rendering.
- Term: UI toolkit. Low-level meaning: A library owns widgets, input routing, layout, styling, accessibility, and rendering integration at some chosen level.. Learn more: G U I library selection.
- Term: U U I D. Low-level meaning: A 128-bit versioned identifier supports decentralized or ordered/name-derived generation; it is not proof of identity.. Learn more: UUIDs.
- Term: video. Low-level meaning: Timestamped frames are compressed into codec streams and multiplexed with metadata/tracks in containers.. Learn more: Video.
- Term: virtual machine (V M). Low-level meaning: Virtualized hardware runs a guest OS with its own kernel under a hypervisor.. Learn more: Execution environments.
- Term: V P N. Low-level meaning: A tunnel/routing policy carries selected IP traffic through a gateway; it changes paths, not endpoint trust.. Learn more: VPNs and Shadowsocks.
- Term: widget. Low-level meaning: A logical UI element contributes semantics, layout, event handling, and paint/cell output.. Learn more: G U I architecture.
- Term: window A P I / Win thirty-two. Low-level meaning: A native windowing A P I registers window behavior, creates opaque handles, dispatches events/messages, and schedules paint.. Learn more: Native Window A P I basics.
- Term: window procedure. Low-level meaning: A Win thirty-two system-A B I callback receives typed-by-message parameters and must delegate unhandled behavior to the OS default.. Learn more: Window procedure and pump.
- Term: X S S. Low-level meaning: Untrusted content executes as script in a trusted origin unless contextual encoding and safe D O M APIs prevent injection.. Learn more: C O R S, C S R F, and X S S.
- Term: X M L. Low-level meaning: A structured text format with elements, attributes, namespaces, mixed content, and security-sensitive entity policy.. Learn more: Serialization boundary.

Note. Glossary habit: if two terms sound interchangeable, follow both links and
compare their boundaries. Common costly confusions include encoding versus
encryption, container versus V M, Graph Q L versus database, S F T P versus F T P S, J W T
versus session, and C O R S versus C S R F.

## How These Domains Feed Your Own Language

- Domain: numeric/media. When building a language: define integer overflow, floating point, sim-dee/vector types, units, and foreign buffers. When reversing a language/runtime: identify numeric width, matrix convention, buffer layout, and hardware calls.
- Domain: memory/OS. When building a language: define ownership, allocation, page permission, thread, and file/socket abstractions. When reversing a language/runtime: recover object layout, lifetimes, mappings, syscalls, and synchronization.
- Domain: bytecode/native code. When building a language: define instruction encoding, call frames, A B I, imports, and debug metadata. When reversing a language/runtime: reconstruct opcodes, dispatch, indirect calls, symbols, and control flow.
- Domain: serialization/protocols. When building a language: define stable schemas, framing, versioning, and bounded parsing. When reversing a language/runtime: infer message boundaries, state machines, checksums, and field meaning.
- Domain: async/events. When building a language: define task, future, cancellation, channel, and backpressure semantics. When reversing a language/runtime: recognize compiler-generated state machines, event loops, queues, and callbacks.
- Domain: database/cache. When building a language: define persistence APIs, null/missing states, transactions, and deterministic encoding. When reversing a language/runtime: distinguish source of truth from cache, index, journal, or derived state.
- Domain: cryptography/identity. When building a language: expose safe library-backed types and keep keys/nonces unforgeable in ordinary code. When reversing a language/runtime: locate trust decisions, canonical bytes, certificate use, and failure paths.
- Domain: sandbox/V M. When building a language: restrict imports, syscalls, memory, time, and execution fuel. When reversing a language/runtime: identify host/guest boundaries and the authority of each escape edge.
- Domain: G U I/T U I. When building a language: define components, stable identity, events, layout, tasks, and backend capabilities. When reversing a language/runtime: recover widget trees, dispatch, layout boxes, display lists, and terminal output.
- Domain: observability. When building a language: emit source maps, spans, structured events, and stable IDs. When reversing a language/runtime: correlate addresses with modules, calls, data flow, and external effects.

Note. Design principle: give a programmer safe, typed access to the concept—not raw
authority by default. A Socket, File, Future, SecretKey, or GpuBuffer should
carry ownership, lifetime, state, and permission rules that the compiler/runtime can
enforce.
